Men require a fiction to begin anything. Even science, that rigorous measurer of reality, must invent a starting point; it selects a specific star in its endless journey and declares, "Here, at this precise moment, Time is Zero." Its less precise counterpart, Poetry, is traditionally said to begin in the middle. Yet, upon closer inspection, their methods are remarkably similar. Science counts backward as well as forward, dividing its units into billions, and even with its clock hand pointing to Zero, it truly launches *in medias res*. No amount of looking back can reach the absolute origin. Whether our prologue unfolds in heaven or on earth, it remains merely a fragment of the vast, presupposed fact from which our narrative springs.

Was she beautiful, or not? What secret combination of form and expression lent such dynamic power to her glance? Did the good or the evil genius reign within those beams? Likely the latter; otherwise, why did her presence evoke unrest rather than serene charm? Why did the desire to look again feel like coercion rather than a willing surrender of the soul? These were the questions swirling in Daniel Deronda's mind as he observed the woman before him. She was currently engaged in gambling—not in the open air beneath a southern sun, tossing copper coins against a crumbling wall with ragged limbs, but within one of those magnificent establishments that centuries of enlightenment have crafted for such pleasures at a steep price. The venue was adorned with heavy gilt moldings, dark tones, and plump nudes, creating a dense condenser for the breath of humanity's elite, a mixture rarely available to those of lesser station.

It was nearly four o'clock on a September afternoon, the air thick with a visible haze. Deep silence hung over the room, punctuated only by the soft clatter of chips, a gentle sweep of the dealer's cloth, and the occasional monotone murmur of French, sounding as if spoken by a sophisticated automaton. Two long tables hosted packed crowds, all eyes fixed downward except for one. That solitary exception was a melancholy little boy, dressed in a fantastical costume save for his bare legs and feet. He stood behind a lady deeply engrossed at a roulette table, staring blankly toward the doorway with the vacant gaze of a costumed child advertising an itinerant show.

Around the table, fifty or sixty people were gathered. Spectators sat in the outer rows, occasionally dropping a five-franc note with a simpering air, merely curious about the thrill of the game. Those fully immersed in high-stakes play displayed a remarkable variety of European types: Livonian, Spanish, Greek-Italian, and various German strains, alongside English aristocrats and commoners alike. Here was a stark admission of human equality. The jeweled, manicured fingers of an English countess hovered inches from a bony, yellow, claw-like hand gripping a stack of coins. That hand belonged to a figure with a square, gaunt face, deep-set eyes, grizzled brows, and ill-combed hair that resembled a slight mutation of a vulture. Nearby sat a prematurely aged woman, withered like an artificial flower after its brief bloom, clutching a shabby velvet reticule and occasionally popping the tip of her card-pricker into her mouth. Close to the countess also stood a respectable London tradesman, blonde and soft-handed, his hair meticulously parted. Conscious of circulars addressed to the nobility, he enjoyed fashionable holidays and the company of the gentry. Unlike the true gambler whose passion overrides appetite, this man sought only a well-fed leisure, alternating between making money in business and spending it ostentatiously in play. He reasoned that Providence approved of his amusement, ready to quit if the sweet taste of winning turned sour. To him, the vice of gambling lay solely in losing money. Though he retained the bearing of a merchant, in his pleasures he ranked with ancient titled families.

Beside him stood a handsome Italian, calm and statuesque, reaching across to place a fresh pile of napoleons onto the table. Within minutes, the pile was pushed toward an elderly woman wearing glasses that pinched her nose. A faint, gleaming smile touched her lips, but the Italian remained impassive, confident in his infallible system that placed his foot firmly on the neck of Chance, already preparing the next wager. Similarly, a man resembling an emaciated beau or a spent libertine, peering at life through a single eyeglass, extended his trembling hand for change. Perhaps it was not a rigid system, but a delusion of white crows or the superstition that the eighth of the month was lucky, driving his fierce yet tottering impulses.

Despite their individual differences, every player wore a uniform negativity of expression, like a mask. It was as if they had all consumed a root that forced their minds into a narrow, monotonous loop of action. Deronda's initial reaction to this scene of dull, gas-choked absorption was that the gambling of Spanish shepherd boys seemed far more enviable. One might almost justify Rousseau's claim that art and science had served mankind poorly.

Suddenly, however, the atmosphere became dramatic. Deronda's attention seized by a young lady standing nearby, the last object of his gaze. She was bending to speak English to a middle-aged woman seated at the table, then straightening up to resume play. She revealed the full stature of a graceful figure, possessing a face that might not immediately inspire admiration but certainly demanded notice. An internal debate sparked in Deronda, sharpening his scrutiny until it drifted far beyond the vague warmth of undefined attraction.

At moments, his eyes tracked the movement of her arms and hands as this enigmatic sylph deposited her stake with an air of decisive choice. Then they returned to her face, steady and focused on the game, seemingly unaffected by observers. The sylph was winning. As her slender, pale-gray gloved fingers adjusted the coins pushed toward her, she glanced around with a survey so cold and neutral it suggested artifice masking inner triumph. But during this scan, her eyes met Deronda's. Instead of averting them as she intended, she felt an unpleasant awareness that he was arresting her gaze.

A fleeting sensation told her he was measuring her, looking down upon her as an inferior, distinct from the human dross surrounding her. He appeared to belong to a realm outside and above her, examining her as a specimen of a lower order. This provoked a tingling resentment that stretched the moment in conflict. It did not flush her cheeks but drained the color from her lips. Controlling herself with an inward defiance, she offered no further sign of emotion save for that pallor before turning back to the game. Yet, Deronda's gaze seemed to act as an evil eye. Her stake vanished.

It mattered little; she had been winning since arriving with a few napoleons and possessed a substantial reserve. She began to believe in her own luck, as others had begun to believe in hers. Visions arose of a *cortège* following her, worshipping her as a goddess of fortune and watching her play as a divine omen. While such tales existed for male gamblers, why not for a woman seeking supremacy? Her friend and chaperon, initially opposed to her playing, now approved, offering prudent advice to stop and return to England. Gwendolen Harleth dismissed this, stating she cared for the excitement, not the winnings. Under this assumption, the current moment should have marked the peak of her gambling fervor.

Yet, when her next stake was swept away, she felt her eyes heating up. The certainty of that man still watching her felt like a tightening pressure, becoming increasingly torturous. This only fueled her resolve not to flinch, to continue playing as if indifferent to loss or gain. Her friend touched her elbow, suggesting they leave. In response, Gwendolen placed ten louis on the same spot. She entered a mood of defiance where the mind ignores all ends beyond the satisfaction of enraged resistance, foolishly including luck itself as an object of that defiance. Since she wasn't winning spectacularly, the next best option was to lose spectacularly. She controlled her muscles, showing no tremor in her mouth or hands. Every time her stake disappeared, she doubled it.

Many now watched her, but the only observation she registered was Deronda's. Though she never looked directly at him, she was certain he hadn't moved away. Such dramas unfold quickly; development and catastrophe can often be measured by nothing clumsier than the ticking of the clock hand.

"Faites votre jeu, mesdames et messieurs," announced the automatic voice of destiny from behind the croupier's mustache and powdered wig. Gwendolen stretched her arm to deposit her final, meager heap of napoleons. "Le jeu ne va plus," declared destiny. Within five seconds, Gwendolen turned from the table, but she turned resolutely with her face toward Deronda, locking gazes with him.

An ironic smile played in his eyes as their目光s met, yet it was preferable that he had kept his attention fixed on her than ignored her as one of an anonymous insect swarm. Moreover, despite his superciliousness and irony, it was hard to believe he didn't admire both her spirit and her person. He was young, handsome, and distinguished in appearance—not one of those ridiculous, dowdy Philistines who felt compelled to frown with sour disapproval as they passed the gaming tables. The general conviction that we are admirable rarely yields to a single negative; when members of Vanity's large family find their performance received coldly, they tend to believe that adding more of themselves will sway the dissenting observer. In Gwendolen's mindset, it was assumed she knew what was admirable and that she herself was admired. This foundation had suffered a disagreeable shock, reeling slightly, but proving difficult to overthrow.

"This man contrives a secret 'twixt us two, / That he may quell me with his meeting eyes / Like one who quells a lioness at bay."

These lines were inscribed above a letter Gwendolen discovered upon her table.

"DEAREST CHILD,—I have been expecting to hear from you for a week. In your last correspondence, you mentioned that the Langens contemplated leaving Leubronn for Baden. How could you be so thoughtless as to leave me in uncertainty regarding your address? I am in the utmost anxiety lest this missive fail to reach you.

In any event, you were to return home by the end of September; however, I must urgently implore you to come back as soon as possible. Should you exhaust your funds, I shall be powerless to provide further assistance, and you must absolutely refrain from borrowing from the Langens, for I possess no means to repay them.

This is the sad truth, my child—I wish I could soften the blow, yet a dreadful calamity has befallen us all. You know nothing of business affairs and will likely struggle to comprehend the gravity of the situation, but Grapnell & Co. have failed to the tune of a million pounds, and we are utterly ruined—your Aunt Gascoigne included, save for your Uncle's benefice. Consequently, the family must subsist by selling the carriage and investing the proceeds for the boys' education. Every asset our poor father labored to secure for us is now destined to settle our liabilities. I possess nothing that can truly be called my own.

It is preferable that you learn this immediately, though it rends my heart to deliver such news. Naturally, we cannot help wondering how ill-timed your departure proved. Yet, I shall never reproach you, my dear child; I would shield you from all distress if I could. On your journey home, you will have ample time to prepare for the drastic changes awaiting you. We may depart Offendene forthwith, hoping Mr. Haynes, who previously expressed interest, is ready to purchase the rectory. Regrettably, we cannot move into the rectory itself, as there is not a single corner available. We shall be forced to seek some humble dwelling to shelter us, relying on your Uncle Gascoigne's charity until further provisions can be arranged. I shall be unable to settle debts with tradesmen or cover servants' wages.

Summon your fortitude, my dear child; we must resign ourselves to God's will. It is agonizing to submit to Mr. Lassman's wicked recklessness, which rumors attribute as the cause of this failure. Your poor sisters can only weep alongside me, offering no practical aid. Were you here, there might be a break in the clouds—I always felt it impossible that you were destined for poverty. If the Langens choose to remain abroad, perhaps you can place yourself under someone else's care for the remainder of the trip. But please, come as soon as you can to your afflicted and loving mother,

The initial impact of this letter upon Gwendolen was half-stupefying. The implicit confidence that her destiny awaited her—a life of luxurious ease where any trouble would be elegantly clothed and comfortably provided for—had been stronger in her own mind than in her mother's, nourished by her youthful vitality and that innate sense of superior claim that occupied a vast portion of her consciousness. It was nearly as difficult for her to instantly accept that her station had plummeted into poverty and humiliating dependence as it would have been to suddenly feel the chilling reality of her own mortality.

She stood motionless for several moments, then casually tossed aside her hat and mechanically glanced into the mirror. The coils of her smooth, light-brown hair remained perfectly arranged, suitable for a ballroom. On other evenings, Gwendolen might have lingered before her reflection with pleasure—an allowable indulgence—but now she took no conscious note of her beauty. She simply stared straight ahead, as if jarred by a hateful noise, waiting for confirmation of its source.

By and by, she collapsed into the corner of the red velvet sofa, picked up the letter, and read it twice deliberately. Finally, she let it drop to the floor, resting her clasped hands on her lap and sitting perfectly still, shedding no tears. Her instinct was to survey and resist the situation rather than to wail over it. There was no inward exclamation of "Poor mamma!" Her mother had rarely seemed to derive enjoyment from life; if Gwendolen felt pity at this moment, she would have directed it toward herself—for was she not naturally and rightfully the primary object of her mother's anxiety?

What possessed her was anger, resistance, and bitter vexation. She resented having lost her winnings at roulette. Had her luck held true for just one more day, she would have secured a handsome sum to bring home, or she could have continued playing until she amassed enough to support them all. Was it not still possible? She possessed only four napoleons in her purse, but she owned ornaments she could sell—a practice commonplace among stylish society at German spas, requiring no shame. Even without her mother's letter, she likely would have decided to liquidate an Etruscan necklace she hadn't worn since arriving, doing so with an agreeable sense of living intensely and escaping the mundane. With ten louis at her disposal and a return of her former fortune, which seemed probable, what better course existed than to continue gambling for a few days?

If her friends at home disapproved of her methods of acquiring funds—as they certainly would—the money would nonetheless be hers. Gwendolen's imagination dwelled on this scenario, conjuring agreeable consequences, though not with the unbroken confidence and rising certainty characteristic of a true gambler's mania. She had approached the roulette table not out of passion, but in search of it. Her mind remained sanely capable of calculating balanced probabilities. While the allure of winning tempted her, the specter of losing thrust itself forward with alternating strength, creating a vision from which her pride sank sensitively.

Resolved never to inform the Langens of her family's misfortune, nor to incur any debt to their compassion, she calculated that parting with significant jewelry would invite intrusive inquiries and remonstrations. The path posing the least risk of intolerable annoyance was to pawn her necklace early the next morning, inform the Langens that her mother demanded her immediate return without specifying reasons, and board the train for Brussels that evening.

She had no maid accompanying her, and the Langens might make difficulties regarding her solitary return, but her will was peremptory. Refusing to retire to bed, she lit the brightest fire she could manage and began to pack, working diligently. Throughout the process, she was haunted by visions of the coming day: now by tedious explanations and farewells, the whirlwind journey toward a transformed home; now by the alternative of staying just one more day and standing once again at the roulette table. Yet, in every latter scene, the presence of Deronda loomed, watching her with exasperating irony. These two keen experiences were inevitably revived together, beholding her again forsaken by luck. This importunate image undeniably swayed her resolve toward immediate departure, urging her packing to a point where changing her mind became inconvenient.

When she finally entered her room, the clock had struck twelve. By the time she verified that she had packed only the essentials, the faint dawn was filtering through the white blinds, dimming her candles. What purpose served sleep? Her cold bath had been sufficient refreshment, and she noted that a slight trace of fatigue around her eyes only enhanced her allure. Before six o'clock, she was fully equipped in her gray traveling dress, complete with her felt hat, intending to walk out as soon as she could expect to encounter other ladies heading to the springs.

Happening to sit sideways before the long strip of mirror between her two windows, she turned to gaze at herself, leaning her elbow on the chair's back in an attitude seemingly chosen for a portrait. It is possible to harbor a strong self-love devoid of self-satisfaction, indeed accompanied by a self-discontent that is more intense because one's own core of egoistic sensibility becomes a supreme preoccupation. However, Gwendolen knew nothing of such internal strife. She possessed a *naïve* delight in her fortunate self, a trait for which any but the harshest saintliness would grant indulgence in a girl who daily witnessed a pleasant reflection of that self in her friends' flattery and in the looking-glass.

Even amidst this onset of troubles, while she sat gazing at her image in the growing light, lacking anything else to occupy her, her face gathered a complacency gradual as the morning's cheerfulness. Her beautiful lips curled into a more and more decisive smile until, finally, she removed her hat, leaned forward, and kissed the cold glass, which had appeared so warm. How could she believe in sorrow? If it attacked her, she felt the strength to crush it, defy it, or flee from it, as she had already done. Anything seemed more plausible than enduring miseries, great or small.

Madame von Langen never ventured out before breakfast, allowing Gwendolen to safely conclude her early walk by returning home through the Obere Strasse, where the necessary shop was certain to be open after seven. At that hour, any observers she cared to avoid would either be strolling in the spring regions or still tucked away in their bedrooms. Certainly, there was one grand exception: the *Czarina*, a hotel from whose vantage points eyes might follow her up to Mr. Wiener's door. This was a risk worth taking; might she not be entering to purchase something that had caught her fancy? This implicit falsehood passed through her mind as

"Let no flower of the spring pass by us; let us crown ourselves with rosebuds before they be withered." — *Book of Wisdom*.

It is a pity that Offendene was not the home of Miss Gwendolen Harleth's childhood, or a place endeared to her by family memories! A human life, I believe, should be firmly rooted in some corner of a native land. There, it may learn to love the face of the earth, the labors men undertake, the sounds and accents that haunt it, and whatever gives that early home an unmistakable familiarity amidst the widening scope of future knowledge. It is a spot where the definiteness of early memories becomes woven with affection, and where kindly acquaintance with all neighbors—even to dogs and donkeys—spreads not through sentimental effort, but as a sweet habit of the blood. At five years old, mortals are not yet prepared to be citizens of the world, nor stimulated by abstract nouns to soar above personal preference into impartiality. The blind prejudice in favor of milk with which we begin is merely a type of the way body and soul must be nourished, at least for a time. The best introduction to astronomy is to imagine the nightly heavens as a little lot of stars belonging to one's own homestead.

But this blessed persistence, in which affection can truly take root, had been absent from Gwendolen's life. It was only a year prior to her return from Leubronn that Offendene was chosen as her mother's home, selected simply for its proximity to Pennicote Rectory. On a late October afternoon, when rooks crowed loudly overhead and yellow elm leaves whirled in the wind, Mrs. Davilow, Gwendolen, and her four half-sisters drove along the avenue for the first time, accompanied by the governess and the maid in a separate vehicle.

The season suited the aspect of the old oblong red-brick house, which was rather too anxiously ornamented with stone at every line—not excepting the double row of narrow windows and the large square portico. The stone encouraged a greenish lichen, while the brick wore a powdery gray hue. Thus, although the building was rigidly rectangular, there was no harshness in its physiognomy as it faced the three avenues cutting through the hundred yards of old plantation encircling the immediate grounds. One would have wished the house to stand upon a knoll, allowing it to look beyond its own little domain to the long thatched roofs of distant villages, the church towers, the scattered homesteads, the gradual rise of surging woods, and the green breadths of undulating park that formed the beautiful face of the earth in that part of Wessex. Yet, standing behind a screen amid flat pastures, it possessed on one side a glimpse of the wider world in the lofty curves of the chalk downs, where grand, steadfast forms were played over by the changing days.

The house was just large enough to be called a mansion, moderately rented, lacking a manor attachment, and somewhat difficult to let due to its sombre furniture and faded upholstery. However, inside and out, it presented an image that no beholder could suppose to be inhabited by retired tradespeople—a certainty worth many conveniences to tenants who possessed the taste that shrinks from new finery and occupied that border-territory of rank where annexation is a burning topic. To take up residence in a house that had once sufficed for dowager countesses gave a perceptible tinge to Mrs. Davilow's satisfaction in finally having an establishment of her own. This arrangement appeared suddenly possible to Gwendolen upon the death of her stepfather, Captain Davilow, who had for the last nine years joined his family only in brief, fitful bursts, enough to reconcile them to his long absences. Yet, Gwendolen cared much more for the fact than for the explanation. All her prospects had become more agreeable in consequence. She had disliked their former way of life, roving from one foreign watering-place or Parisian apartment to another, always feeling new antipathies toward new suites of hired furniture and meeting new people under conditions that made her feel of little importance. The variation of spending two years at a showy school, where she was invariably placed foremost on all occasions of display, had only deepened her conviction that someone as exceptional as herself could hardly remain in ordinary circumstances or in a social position less than advantageous. Any fear of this latter evil was banished now that her mother was to have an establishment. Regarding her birth, Gwendolen was quite easy; she had no notion how her maternal grandfather acquired the fortune inherited by his two daughters, but he had been a West Indian, which seemed to exclude further questioning. She knew that her father's family was so high-born that it took no notice of her mother, who nevertheless preserved with much pride the miniature of a Lady Molly in that connection. She would probably have known much more about her father but for a little incident that occurred when she was twelve years old. Mrs. Davilow had brought out, as she did only at wide intervals, various memorials of her first husband. While showing his miniature to Gwendolen, she recalled with a fervor that seemed to count on a peculiar filial sympathy the fact that "dear papa" had died when his little daughter was in long clothes. Gwendolen, immediately thinking of the unlovable stepfather whom she had known for the greater part of her life while her frocks were short, said, "Why did you marry again, mamma? It would have been nicer if you had not." Mrs. Davilow colored deeply, a slight convulsive movement passing over her face, and straightway shutting up the memorials, she said, with a violence quite unusual in her, "You have no feeling, child!" Gwendolen, who was fond of her mother, felt hurt and ashamed and had never since dared to ask a question about her father.

This was not the only instance in which she had brought upon herself the pain of some filial compunction. It was always arranged, when possible, that she should have a small bed in her mother's room, for Mrs. Davilow's motherly tenderness clung chiefly to her eldest girl, who had been born in her happier time. One night, under an attack of pain, she found that the specific regularly placed by her bedside had been forgotten, and begged Gwendolen to get out of bed and reach it for her. That healthy young lady, snug and warm as a rosy infant in her little couch, objected to stepping out into the cold. Lying perfectly still, she grumbled a refusal. Mrs. Davilow went without the medicine and never reproached her daughter; but the next day, Gwendolen was keenly conscious of what must be in her mother's mind and tried to make amends by caresses which cost her no effort. Having always been the pet and pride of the household, waited upon by mother, sisters, governess, and maids as if she were a princess in exile, she naturally found it difficult to think her own pleasure less important than others made it. When it was positively thwarted, she felt an astonished resentment apt, in her cruder days, to vent itself in one of those passionate acts which look like a contradiction of habitual tendencies. Though never even as a child thoughtlessly cruel—nay, delighting to rescue drowning insects and watch their recovery—there remained a disagreeable silent remembrance of her having strangled her sister's canary-bird in a final fit of exasperation at its shrill singing, which had again and again jarringly interrupted her own. She had taken pains to buy a white mouse for her sister in retribution, and though inwardly excusing herself on the ground of a peculiar sensitiveness which was a mark of her general superiority, the thought of that infamous murder had always made her wince. Gwendolen's nature was not remorseless, but she liked to make her penances easy. Now that she was twenty and more, some of her native force had turned into a self-control by which she guarded herself from penitential humiliation. There was more show of fire and will in her than ever, but there was more calculation underneath it.

On this day of arrival at Offendene, a place neither Mrs. Davilow nor anyone else had seen before—it having been secured for her by her brother-in-law, Mr. Gascoigne—all had dismounted from the carriage and were standing under the porch in front of the open door. So they could have a general view of the place and a glimpse of the stone hall and staircase hung with sombre pictures, but enlivened by a bright wood fire, no one spoke. Mamma, the four sisters, and the governess all looked at Gwendolen, as if their feelings depended entirely on her decision. Of the girls, from Alice in her sixteenth year to Isabel in her tenth, hardly anything could be said on a first view save that they were girlish and that their black dresses were getting shabby. Miss Merry was elderly and altogether neutral in expression. Mrs. Davilow's worn beauty seemed the more pathetic for the look of entire appeal which she cast at Gwendolen, who was glancing round at the house, the landscape, and the entrance hall with an air of rapid judgment. Imagine a young racehorse in the paddock among untrimmed ponies and patient hacks.

"Well, dear, what do you think of the place?" said Mrs. Davilow at last, in a gentle, deprecatory tone.

"I think it is charming," said Gwendolen, quickly. "A romantic place; anything delightful may happen in it; it would be a good background for anything. No one need be

*"Gorgibus"—* "I tell you that marriage is a holy and sacred thing; and it is proper for honest folk to begin with it."

*"Madame"*—"Good heavens! If everyone were like you, a novel would soon be over! What a fine thing it would be if Cyrus married Mandane right off, and Aronce wedded Clélie straightaway! Let us take our time weaving the fabric of our romance, and do not rush its conclusion so." —Molière, *The Pretentious Ladies*.

It would be somewhat harsh to fault the Rector of Pennicote for viewing matters from every angle, including seeing Gwendolen as a candidate destined for a dazzling marriage. Was he to be expected to diverge from his peers in wishing his niece a fate inferior to what society deemed optimal for her charming youth? Rather, it speaks to his kindness that his sentiments were purely benevolent. Indeed, to advise her to dress in rags like Griselda's merely to attract a Marquis, or to insist that a fair maiden seeking a husband must hide away, would have been sheer folly. Mr. Gascoigne's plans were rational; he did not scheme to throw her upon a dangerous horse so a wealthy suitor might rescue her. He genuinely wished her well and intended to present her to the finest society in the neighborhood. His intentions aligned perfectly with Gwendolen's own desires.

Yet, let no one mistake this alignment. Gwendolen did not envision marriage as the ultimate goal of her existence, nor did she imagine herself conquering the world solely through equestrian grace or other accomplishments. While she acknowledged that she would eventually marry—and that it would certainly not be a mediocre union like those accepted by most women—she felt this quietly, without argument. Marriage was never the climax of the dramas where she pictured herself as a heroine. Being courted fervently or sighing over hopelessly was indeed a delightful assurance of female power; but becoming a wife, bound by the domestic chains of that state, seemed largely a tedious necessity. Her observations led her to view matrimony as a dreary confinement where a woman could do nothing as she pleased, bore more children than desired, grew dull, and sank irretrievably into the mundane.

Of course, marriage represented social elevation; she could not foresee a single life. Yet, promotions sometimes require swallowing bitter herbs—a title does not replace the need for actual leadership for a man intent on leading. This delicate, ethereal figure of twenty years old meant to lead. For such passions reside within feminine hearts as well. In Gwendolen's case, however, they dwelled exclusively among strictly feminine pursuits, holding no disruptive connection to the progress of learning or the stability of the state; her knowledge was such that no leverage, short or long, could ever move the world. She intended to act pleasantly for herself in a striking manner—or rather, anything she could do to strike others with admiration, thereby gaining a more intense sense of living through their reflection, seemed delightful to her imagination.

"Gwendolen will not rest until she has the world at her feet," declared Miss Merry, the gentle governess. These hyperbolic words had long since acquired their most moderate meanings; after all, who has not heard of private individuals having the world at their feet in the form of a dozen items of flattering attention, commonly known in a genteel suburb? Words could scarcely be broad or vague enough to describe the hazy grandeur that surrounded poor Gwendolen atop the heights of her youthful self-exaltation. Others allowed themselves to be enslaved, their lives tossed like empty vessels with no will steering them. Not so with her; she would no longer be sacrificed to beings lesser than herself. Instead, she would seize the best opportunities life offered and conquer circumstances through her exceptional cleverness.

Certainly, settling at Offendene, with the patronage of Lady Brackenshaw, the Archery Club, and invitations to dine with the Arrowpoints, did not seem to promise extraordinary prospects. Yet, Gwendolen's confidence rested chiefly in herself. She felt fully equipped to master life. Regarding much of her past, she believed she had been treated unfairly; yet concerning her "education," she admitted it had left her with no disadvantages. In the classroom, her quick mind had eagerly absorbed the strong starch of unexplained rules and disconnected facts, shielding her from any painful sense of inadequacy. Whatever remained of knowable things, she felt sufficiently acquainted with through novels, plays, and poetry. Concerning French and music—the two essential accomplishments of a young lady—she felt no anxiety. Adding to these qualifications, both negative and positive, was the spontaneous sense of capability some fortunate souls are born with: the belief that any subject they touch impresses them with their own power to judge it correctly. Who could wonder then that Gwendolen felt ready to steer her own destiny?

There were many subjects in the world—perhaps the majority—in which she felt no interest, simply because they were stupid. Subjects often appear dull to the young as light appears dim to the elderly; yet she would not have felt helpless regarding them if they arose in conversation. It must be remembered that no one had ever disputed her power or her general superiority. Just as upon her arrival at Offendene, so always, the first thought of those around her was, *What will Gwendolen think?* Whether the footman tread heavily in creaking boots, or the laundrywoman's work was unsatisfactory, or the maid remarked, "This will never do for Miss Harleth"; if the wood smoked in the bedroom fireplace, Mrs. Davilow, whose own weak eyes suffered greatly from the irritation, apologized to Gwendolen. If, during travel, she did not appear at the breakfast table until everyone else had finished, the sole concern was ensuring her coffee and toast remained the hottest and crispest. When she finally arrived, with her freshly brushed light-brown hair streaming back, awaiting her mother's hand to coil it, her large brown eyes gleaming brightly like a wave-washed onyx beneath long lashes, it was always she who had to exercise tolerance—begging Alice not to stiffen her shoulders in that dreadful manner, and urging Isabel to leave her alone rather than pressing questions.

Always she was the princess in exile, entitled to have her breakfast roll made of the finest flour from the seven thin ears of wheat in times of famine, and to have her silver fork preserved from the baggage during a general retreat. How was this to be explained? The answer may seem obvious: in her beauty, a certain unusual quality, and a decisive will that manifested in her graceful movements and clear, unhesitating tone. So, if she entered a room on a rainy day when everyone else was listless and the purpose of things seemed unclear, there seemed a sudden, sufficient reason to maintain the forms of life. Even hotel waiters displayed greater alacrity in removing crumbs, creases, and dregs mixed with struggling flies.

This potent charm, combined with the fact that she was the eldest daughter, toward whom her mother had always maintained an apologetic attitude regarding the hardships inflicted by a stepfather, may seem a complete explanation for Gwendolen's domestic empire. To seek another cause would be to ask why there is daylight when the sun shines. But beware of drawing conclusions without comparison. I recall witnessing similar diligent, apologetic attention bestowed upon persons who were neither beautiful nor unusual, whose firmness lacked grace or euphony, and who were not eldest daughters with tender, timid mothers filled with remorse. Some were merely common men. The only similarity among them was a strong determination to obtain what was pleasant, coupled with a total fearlessness in becoming disagreeable or dangerous when denied. Who is cajoled and served with trembling by the weak females of a household more than the unscrupulous male—capable, if he lacks freedom at home, of causing far worse trouble elsewhere? Thus, I am compelled to doubt whether, even without her potent charm and unique filial position, Gwendolen might not still have played the queen in exile, provided she retained her innate energy of egoistic desire and her power to inspire fear regarding what she might say or do.

However, she possessed the charm, and those who feared her were also fond of her; the fear and affection perhaps heightened by what may be termed the iridescence of her character—the interplay of various, nay, contradictory tendencies. For Macbeth's rhetoric regarding the impossibility of being many opposite things simultaneously referred to the clumsy necessities of action, not the subtler possibilities of feeling. We cannot speak a loyal word and remain meanly silent; we cannot kill and refrain from killing in the same instant. But a moment is wide enough to hold both loyal and base desires, the outburst of a murderous thought, and the sharp, backward stroke of repentance.

*"Her wit values itself so highly, that to her all matter else seems weak."* — *Much Ado About Nothing.*

Gwendolen's arrival in the neighborhood proved exactly what her uncle had anticipated. From the imposing Brackenshaw Castle down to the cozy Firs at Wanchester, where Mr. Quallon the banker maintained a notably generous household, she was greeted with open admiration. Even among those ladies whose hearts were not entirely won by her charms, there existed a practical comfort in hosting a new, striking figure; for hostesses who entertain frequently must assemble their gatherings with the strategic acumen of ministers forming a cabinet, prioritizing balance over pure affection. Consequently, securing Gwendolen as a guest required no difficult diplomacy regarding disagreeable personalities, for Mrs. Davilow always presented a quiet, picturesque silhouette as a chaperone, and Mr. Gascoigne was universally sought after for his own considerable merits.

Among the households where Gwendolen was tolerated rather than loved, yet still invited, was Quetcham Hall. Her first summons there led to a grand dinner party that served as a de facto general introduction to the local society. In a select gathering of thirty, carefully balanced across ages, it was impossible to omit any visitable family. Yet, no youthful figure present could compare to hers. As she glided through the long suites of rooms, illuminated by light and blooming flowers, she appeared initially as a slender shape drifting in white drapery, becoming increasingly defined and radiant with each passing doorway. She had never experienced such a promenade before, and she felt a surge of exultation that it suited her perfectly; anyone observing her for the first time might reasonably assume that long galleries and serving staff had always been the backdrop of her existence. Meanwhile, her cousin Anna, who possessed a far greater familiarity with such formalities, felt nearly as awkward as a rabbit suddenly dropped into a brightly lit clearing.

"Who is that walking with Gascoigne?" asked the Archdeacon, abandoning a discussion of military maneuvers on which, as a clergyman, he was naturally consulted. Simultaneously, his son—a promising young scholar who had recently proposed "not less elegant than ingenious" corrections to Greek texts—remarked, "By George! Who is that girl with the awfully well-set head and jolly figure?"

However, to a mind of general benevolence that wished everyone to appear their best, it was somewhat exasperating to witness how Gwendolen eclipsed all others. Even the handsome Miss Lawe, identified as the daughter of Lady Lawe, seemed to shrink, appearing suddenly broad, heavy, and inanimate beside her. Similarly, Miss Arrowpoint, unfortunately clad in white as well, instantly resembled a *carte-de-visite* where one suspected the price had been inflated solely for the length of the skirt. Since Miss Arrowpoint was generally cherished for her amiable, unpretentious manner of wearing her fortune and for acting as a softening buffer for her mother's eccentricities, Gwendolen's overwhelming display of social importance seemed strangely unfitting.

"She is not actually so beautiful if you examine her features closely," Mrs. Arrowpoint confided later in the evening to Mrs. Vulcany. "It is a certain style she possesses that creates a tremendous initial effect, but afterwards she becomes less agreeable."

Indeed, Gwendolen, without intending to, had offended her hostess. Though Mrs. Arrowpoint was neither splenetic nor vindictive, she held strong susceptibilities. Various circumstances converged in the Lady of Quetcham that neighbors deemed essentially connected: she was occasionally remembered as the heiress to a fortune amassed through obscure urban ventures, supposedly explaining her squat figure, her harsh, parrot-like voice, and her systematically elevated head-dress. Since these traits rendered her externally ridiculous, it seemed logical to many that she should harbor literary tendencies. A closer analysis, however, reveals that these attributes exist independently; daughters of aldermen are often well-proportioned and featured, pretty women may possess harsh voices, and the creation of mediocre literature is compatible with virtually any physique, masculine or feminine.

Gwendolen, possessing a keen sense of absurdity in others but a kindly disposition toward anyone capable of making life agreeable, intended to win Mrs. Arrowpoint's favor through exceptional interest and attention. Yet, self-confidence often addresses an imaginary dullness in others; the wealthy cajole the poor, and the vigorous speak artificially to the elderly, hastily assuming deafness or imbecility. Gwendolen, despite her cleverness and desire to be agreeable, fell prey to this form of stupidity. Unreflectingly, she concluded that because Mrs. Arrowpoint was ridiculous, she must also lack penetration. Thus, she enacted her little scenes without suspecting that every nuance of her behavior was being meticulously observed.

"You are fond of books as well as music, riding, and archery, I hear," Mrs. Arrowpoint said, approaching her for a *tête-à-tête* in the drawing-room after dinner. "Catherine will be delighted to have such a sympathetic neighbor."

This remark, delivered in a low, melodious tone, carried a fatal twang that made Gwendolen feel she was exercising patronage. She replied gracefully, "It is I who am fortunate. Miss Arrowpoint will teach me what good music is. I shall be entirely a learner. I hear she is a thorough musician."

"Catherine has certainly enjoyed every advantage. We have a first-rate musician in the house now—Herr Klesmer; perhaps you know all his compositions. You must allow me to introduce him. You sing, I believe? Catherine plays three instruments but does not sing. I hope you will let us hear you. I understand you are an accomplished singer."

"Oh, no!" Gwendolen exclaimed, quoting Mephistopheles. "'Die Kraft ist schwach, allein die Lust ist gross.' 'The strength is weak, but the delight is great.'"

"Ah, you are a student of Goethe. Young ladies are so advanced nowadays. I suppose you have read everything."

"No, really. I shall be so glad if you tell me what to read. I have examined all the books in the library at Offendene, but there is nothing readable. The leaves stick together and smell musty. I wish I could write books to amuse myself, as you can! How delightful it must be to write books according to one's own taste instead of reading other people's! Home-made books must be so nice."

For an instant, Mrs. Arrowpoint's gaze sharpened, but the dangerous edge of satire vanished when Gwendolen added, "I would give anything to write a book!"

"And why should you not?" Mrs. Arrowpoint encouraged. "You need only begin as I did. Pen, ink, and paper are within everyone's reach. But I will send you all I have written with pleasure."

"Thank you. I shall be so happy to read your writings. Being acquainted with authors must grant a peculiar understanding of their works; one would be able to distinguish the humorous from the serious. I am sure I often laugh in the wrong places."

Here, Gwendolen sensed danger and quickly added, "In Shakespeare, you know, and other great writers we can never fully comprehend. But I always want to know more than exists in the books."

"If you are interested in any of my subjects, I can lend you many extra sheets in manuscript," offered Mrs. Arrowpoint. Gwendolen felt painfully like the young lady who professes to enjoy potted sprats. "These are things I dare say I shall publish eventually; several friends have urged me to do so, and one dislikes being obstinate. My *Tasso*, for instance—I could have made it twice the size."

"I dote on Tasso," said Gwendolen.

"Well, you shall have all my papers if you wish. So many have written about Tasso, but they are all wrong. Regarding the specific nature of his madness, his feelings for Leonora, the real cause of his imprisonment, and the character of Leonora—who, in my opinion, was a cold-hearted woman, else she would have married him in spite of her brother—all accounts are incorrect. I differ from everybody."

"How very interesting!" said Gwendolen. "I like to differ from everybody. I think it so stupid to agree. That is the worst of writing your opinions; you make people agree with you."

This speech revived a slight suspicion in Mrs. Arrowpoint, causing her glance to turn examining once more. However, Gwendolen appeared utterly innocent and continued with a docile air: "I know nothing of Tasso except the *Gerusalemme Liberata*, which we read and memorized at school."

"Ah, his life is more fascinating than his poetry. I have constructed the early part of his life as a sort of romance. When one considers his father, Bernardo, and so on, there is much that must be true."

"Imagination is often truer than fact," Gwendolen stated decisively, unable to explain these glib words any better than if they were Coptic or Etruscan. "I shall be so glad to learn all about Tasso—and his madness especially. I suppose poets are always a little mad."

"To be sure—" Mrs. Arrowpoint quoted, "'the poet's eye in a fine frenzy rolling'; and someone says of Marlowe, 'For that fine madness still he did maintain, Which always should possess the poet's brain.'"

"But it was not always discovered, was it?" Gwendolen asked innocently. "I suppose some rolled their eyes in private. Mad people are often very cunning."

A shadow crossed Mrs. Arrowpoint's face, but the arrival of the gentlemen

"Do you think I've humiliated myself by telling you that the earth revolves around the sun?" Fontenelle once asked, adding a note of defiant pride. "I swear I hold myself no less esteemed."

That very sentiment echoed in Gwendolen's mind, sparking a fresh, peculiar ache within her. She would never willingly admit that she felt ill-equipped compared to Miss Arrowpoint, lacking the musical training necessary to critique Herr Klesmer with authority. Even less could she confess to herself that meeting Miss Arrowpoint invariably stirred a sharp, unwonted jealousy. It wasn't merely because the rival was an heiress; it was because it was genuinely infuriating that a girl of slight build, average height, small features, tolerable eyes, and a sallow complexion possessed a mental superiority that defied explanation. Miss Arrowpoint displayed an exasperating thoroughness in her musical accomplishments and a fastidious discernment in her tastes that commanded awe rather than admiration. This seemingly insignificant twenty-four-year-old, whom anyone might glance over if not for her name, seemed to harbor a quiet conviction that Miss Harleth's talents were merely common. Such a thought was unbearable, especially when cloaked beneath Miss Arrowpoint's perpetual, perfect kindness.

Gwendolen refused to dwell on facts that cast such an unfavorable light on her own character. Since the Musical Magus who had so dramatically expanded her horizons was frequently traveling between London and Quetcham, she seized upon his absences as opportunities to coax him into a more admiring frame of mind. In the meantime, the evident pleasure her singing brought to Brackenshaw Castle, the Firs, and other gatherings restored her composure. She preferred to believe that approval was more reliable than objection, and unlike those few individuals who crave a perfection their neighbors do not demand, she was content. Perhaps it would have been rash to claim she was exceptionally complex inwardly; her uniqueness lay mostly in her rare grace of movement, her bearing, and a certain daring that added spice to a very common, egoistic ambition. Many hide such ambitions behind clumsy exteriors, unnoticed by the world. After all, the tilt of one's head does not dictate the hunger of the inner self for supremacy, only perhaps *how* that supremacy is pursued and to what degree it can be achieved. This was particularly true for a girl whose passion for distinction is bounded by ideals of breeding and free from the sordid need for income.

Gwendolen was internally rebellious against the constraints of her family's circumstances, ready to look past obligations to her own fundamental lack of feeling for them, as if fueled by bold speculation. Yet, she possessed no such theories. She would have immediately distanced herself from any theoretical or practical reformer by mocking them. She delighted in feeling exceptional, yet her horizon remained confined to the realm of genteel romance: a heroine whose soul pours out in journals filled with vague power and rebellion, while her actual life moves strictly within the sphere of fashion. Should she wander into a swamp, the tragedy lies partly in the fact that she wears satin shoes. Nature and society provide this restraint on the pursuit of striking adventure, holding a soul burning with a sense of what the universe is not—and eager to consume all existence as fuel—captive within the ordinary wirework of social forms, leaving her to achieve nothing particular.

This mundane fate was what Gwendolen feared even amidst the novelty of her first winter at Offendene. She was certain she did not wish to live the life of ordinary young ladies, yet she was unclear on how to begin living differently or what specific acts would assert her freedom. Offendene served as a promising backdrop if events were to unfold, but largely, the neighborhood failed to inspire.

Beyond the initial impact of her beauty, her early invitations offered little excitement. Returning home after brief sallies of satire and knowingness—which had previously offended Mrs. Arrowpoint—she filled the idle days with childish schemes. Her strongest assertion of individuality involved skipping Alice's lessons (operating on the principle that ignorance bred excellence) to spend time with Miss Merry and the maid rumored to serve all the ladies. Together, they arranged dramatic costumes Gwendolen had prepared for future charades or theatrical pieces, projects she intended to launch through sheer will or contrivance. Though she had never truly acted beyond posing in *tableaux vivants* at school, she was convinced of her ability. Having visited the Théâtre Français and heard her mother speak of Rachel, her reverie often turned to becoming an actress like the famous Jewish tragedienne, reasoning that she was far more beautiful than the "thin Jewess."

The wet days preceding Christmas were spent pleasantly preparing Greek, Oriental, and composite costumes. Gwendolen posed and delivered lines before a domestic audience, even pressuring the housekeeper to swell the applause. However, when the housekeeper pointed out that Miss Harleth looked far more regal in her own attire than in the baggy costume exposing her arms, she was politely dismissed from further participation.

"Do I look as well as Rachel, mamma?" Gwendolen asked one day, displaying her Greek garb to Anna and enacting fragments of scenes with heavy tragic intent.

"You have better arms than Rachel," Mrs. Davilow replied lazily. "Your arms would suffice for anything, Gwen. But your voice lacks her tragic depth; it isn't deep enough."

"I can deepen it if I wish," Gwendolen said provisionally, then decided firmly, "I believe a higher voice is more tragic. It is more feminine. And the more feminine a woman is, the more tragic she appears when performing desperate deeds."

"There may be truth in that," Mrs. Davilow murmured. "But I don't see the benefit in making one's blood crawl. If anything terrible must be done, I'd prefer it left to the men."

"Oh, mamma, you are dreadfully prosaic!" Gwendolen retorted. "As if all great poetic criminals weren't women! Men are poor, cautious creatures."

"Well, dear, and you—who are afraid to be alone at night—I don't think you'd be very bold in committing crimes, thank God."

"I'm not speaking of reality, mamma," Gwendolen snapped impatiently. Once her mother left the room, she turned quickly to her cousin. "Anna, please ask Uncle to organize some charades at the rectory. Mr. Middleton and Warham could act with us, just for practice. Mamma says it's improper for Mr. Middleton to consult and rehearse here. He is stiff, but we could give him suitable roles. Please ask, or I will."

"Oh, not until Rex arrives," Anna interjected. "He is so clever and dear; he'll act as Napoleon gazing over the sea. He looks exactly like Napoleon. Rex can do anything."

"I don't believe in your Rex, Anna," Gwendolen laughed. "He'll turn out like those wretched blue-and-yellow watercolors you hang in your bedroom and worship."

"Very well, you'll see," Anna countered. "It's not that I know what constitutes cleverness, but he has a scholarship already, and Papa says he'll get a fellowship. Nobody is better at games. He is cleverer than Mr. Middleton, and everyone but you calls Mr. Middleton clever."

"So he may shine in a dim-lantern sort of way. But he *is* stiff. If he had to say, 'Perdition catch my soul, but I do love her,' he'd deliver it in the exact same tone as, 'Here endeth the second lesson.'"

"Oh, Gwendolen!" Anna exclaimed, shocked by these casual insults. "It's unkind to speak so of him, for he admires you greatly. I heard Warham tell Mama, 'Middleton is regularly spoony upon Gwendolen.' She was furious, but I know what it means—it's the college term for being in love."

"How can I help it?" Gwendolen said with disdain. "Perdition catch my soul if I loved *him*."

"No, of course; Papa wouldn't wish it. And he leaves soon. But it saddens me when you ridicule him."

"What will you do to me when I ridicule Rex?" Gwendolen teased wickedly.

"Now, Gwendolen, dear, you *won't*?" Anna pleaded, her eyes filling with tears. "I couldn't bear it. But there's really nothing to ridicule. Only you might discover things. No one ever laughed at Mr. Middleton before you. Everyone said he was good-looking with perfect manners. I was always frightened by his learning, his square-cut coat, and his status as the Bishop's nephew. But you won't ridicule Rex—promise me." Anna finished with a beseeching look that touched Gwendolen.

"You're a dear little cousin," Gwendolen said softly, tapping the tip of Anna's chin. "I never want to do anything that vexes you. Especially if Rex is to make everything succeed—the charades and all."

When Rex finally arrived, the animation he brought to Offendene and the rectory, along with his enthusiastic partnership in Gwendolen's plans, erased any inclination for ridicule unless it was open and flattering. He was a fine, open-hearted youth with a handsome face resembling both his father and Anna's, yet softer in expression and larger in scale. His nature was bright, healthy, and loving, finding joy in innocent pursuits so readily that vice held no temptation for him. Any knowledge he possessed of darker matters resided in the remote corners of his mind, too distant to cause repulsion. Vicious habits were simply "what some fellows did"—"stupid stuff" he preferred to avoid. He returned Anna's affection as fully as a brother could, given that his pleasures outside of her

"Perigot. As the bonny lass passed by, Willie. Hey, ho, bonny lass! P. She roode at me with glauncing eye, W. As clear as the crystal glass. P. All as the sunny beam so bright, W. Hey, ho, the sunbeam! P. Glaunceth from Phoebus' face forthright, W. So love into thy heart did stream." —SPENSER: *Shepherd's Calendar*.

"The kindliest symptom, yet the most alarming crisis in the ticklish state of youth; the nourisher and destroyer of hopeful wits; ... the servitude above freedom; the gentle mind's religion; the liberal superstition." —CHARLES LAMB.

The first sign of the approaching snowstorm was a transparent white cloud that seemed to heighten the brilliance of the blue sky. Anna understood the turmoil in Rex's heart; for the first time, he had remained silent about what weighed most heavily upon him, assuming she shared his burden. Conversely, for the first time, Anna found herself unable to voice the constant thoughts swirling in her own mind. Perhaps it was a pain she would have hidden, that he cared so deeply for someone else before her, but this feeling was largely neutralized by her profound anxiety for his safety.

Anna admired her cousin. She would have spoken simply and sincerely, "Gwendolen is always very good to me," accepting her total subordination to this relative as natural. Yet, she viewed Gwendolen with a mixture of fear and distrust, contemplating her as a wondrous, beautiful creature whose nature remained a mystery—a beast that might, for all Anna knew, hunger to devour the small pets she cherished. Now, Anna's heart sank under the heavy, unspoken conviction that Gwendolen would never care for Rex. What Anna held in tender reverence seemed utterly indifferent to Gwendolen; it was easier to imagine her scorn than to accept any return of affection. Furthermore, Gwendolen was perpetually striving to be extraordinary. Poor Rex! His father would be furious if he knew. Of course, Rex was too young to love in such a manner; Anna had believed it would be years before anything of the sort occurred, and that she would remain his housekeeper forever. But what a heart must be that failed to reciprocate his love! In the prospect of his suffering, Anna began to dislike her cousin's overwhelming fascination.

To both Anna and Rex, the weeks seemed filled with a tumultuous life visible to all observers. Had he been questioned, Rex would have claimed he wished to conceal nothing regarding an engagement he intended to announce to his father immediately. Yet, for the first time in his life, he was reserved—not only about his feelings but, more remarkably to Anna, about his actions. On her side, Anna grew nervous every time her parents spoke privately, fearing they might mention Rex and Gwendolen. However, the elders were entirely unaware of this agitating drama, which unfolded mostly in a pantomime lucid only to those expressing it internally, easily missed by spectators busy scanning the *Guardian* or the *Clerical Gazette*, interpreting the young ones' trivialities no differently than the movements of lively ants.

"Where are you going, Rex?" Anna asked one gray morning. Her father had departed in his carriage with Mrs. Gascoigne, and she noticed her brother wearing his *antigropelos*, his closest approximation to hunting attire.

"I'm going to see the hounds throw off at the Three Barns," Rex replied.

"Are you going to take Gwendolen?" Anna asked timidly.

"She told you, did she?"

"No, but I thought... Does Papa know you're going?"

"Not that I'm aware of. I don't suppose he'd trouble himself."

"You're going to use his horse?"

"He knows I do that whenever I can."

"Don't let Gwendolen ride after the hounds, Rex," Anna pleaded, her fears granting her a strange second sight.

"Why not?" Rex smiled, somewhat provocatively.

"Papa, Mama, and Aunt Davilow all wish her not to. They think it isn't right for her."

"Why should you suppose she intends to do what is wrong?"

"Gwendolen minds nobody sometimes," Anna said, growing bolder through a spark of anger.

"Then she wouldn't mind me," Rex retorted, turning her anxiety into a perverse joke.

"Oh, Rex, I can't bear it. You'll make yourself very unhappy." Anna burst into tears.

"Nannie, Nannie, what on earth is the matter with you?" Rex exclaimed, slightly impatient, hat in hand and whip in grip.

"She won't care for you one bit—I know she never will!" the poor child whispered, sobbing uncontrollably.

Rex flushed and hurried out of the hall door, leaving her to the misery of realizing she had been disagreeable in vain.

As he rode, he considered her words. They carried the unwelcome weight of all unfavorable prophecy, even when laughed at. Yet, he quickly rationalized them as stemming from little Anna's tenderness, regretting only that he had to leave without soothing her. Every other feeling on the subject was swiftly submerged by a resistant belief contrary to hers, accompanied by a new determination to prove her wrong. This certainty possessed just enough kinship to doubt and unease to hasten a confession that an untouched security might have delayed.

Gwendolen was already mounted, pacing up and down the avenue when Rex arrived at the gate. She had prepared for his tardiness by having the groom stand ready behind her, unwilling to wait beyond a reasonable time. Now, the groom was dismissed, and the two rode away in delightful freedom. Gwendolen was in high spirits; Rex thought she had never looked lovelier before. Her figure, her long white throat, and the curves of her cheek and chin were perfected by the compact simplicity of her riding dress. To a youthful lover like Rex, the fundamental unity of the good, the true, and the beautiful seemed already manifest in the object of his affection. Most observers would agree that a girl should hold similar impressions of Rex, for in his handsome face lay nothing corresponding to the indefinable, stinging quality—as if a trace of demonic ancestry—that caused some to hesitate in admiring Gwendolen.

It was an exquisite January morning, free from rain threats, with a gray sky providing the calmest backdrop for a mild winter scene: grassy lane borders, hedgerows dotted with red berries and low bird calls, the purple bareness of elm trees, and the rich brown of plowed furrows. The horses' hooves created a musical chime, echoing their young voices. Gwendolen laughed at his lack of dandyish equipment, and he enjoyed her laughter; the freshness of the morning mingled with the freshness of their youth. Every sound from their clear throats, every glance exchanged, flowed like water from a spring of joy. It was all morning to them, inside and out. One is tempted to futile wishes in such moments—if only things could have been slightly otherwise, leading to vastly different outcomes later; if only these two beautiful youths could have pledged themselves then and there, never swerving from that vow throughout their lives! Some of the goodness Rex believed in was indeed present. Goodness is a vast, often prospective word; like harvest, it lies underground at one stage, an indeterminate future, asking if the germ prospers in the dark. At another, it sends forth delicate green blades, and soon trembling blossoms await the dash of a rough wind or rain. Each stage has its peculiar blight, capable of choking healthy life through the foul actions of the soil that raises it or brings damage from afar.

"Anna had got it into her head that you wanted to ride after the hounds this morning," Rex said, his secret association with Anna's earlier words making this remark feel perilously close to the most momentous topic.

"Did she?" Gwendolen laughed. "What a little clairvoyant she is!"

"Shall you?" Rex asked. He hadn't believed she intended to go despite the elders' objections, trusting instead that she had good reasons.

"I don't know. I can't tell what I'll do until I get there. Clairvoyants are often wrong; they foresee what is likely. I'm not fond of what is likely; it's always dull. I do what is unlikely."

"There, you've told me a secret. Once I knew what people generally would likely do, I'd know you'd do the opposite. So you'd come around to a likelihood of your own sort. I'll be able to calculate on you. You couldn't surprise me."

"Yes, I could. I'd turn round and do what was likely for people in general," Gwendolen said with a musical laugh.

"You see, you can't escape some sort of likelihood. And contradictoriness makes the strongest likelihood of all. You must give up a plan."

"No, I shan't. My plan is to do what pleases me." (If any young lady inclined to imitate Gwendolen, she should consider the set of her head and neck: had the angle been different, the chin protrusive, and the cervical vertebrae slightly more curved, ten to one Gwendolen's words would have jarred the sweet-natured Rex. But everything odd in her speech was merely humor and pretty banter, which he eagerly turned toward a single point.)

"Can you manage to feel only what pleases you?" he asked.

"Of course not; that comes from what others do. But if the world were pleasanter, one would only feel what was pleasant. Girls' lives are so stupid; they

"To-morrow" is the name joy borrows when life is fairest; "To-morrow" fits sorrow best in young despair. Yet at the rectory, there existed a trouble far more enduring and heavy.

When Rex returned, he threw himself upon his bed, sinking into a state of apparent apathy that lasted unbroken until the following day. Only then did more positive signs of illness begin to interrupt his stillness. No mention was made of his journey to Southampton. Instead, the paramount thoughts of his mother and Anna became how to tend this patient—a boy who seemingly desired no recovery. Once the brightest, most grateful spirit in the household, he had been metamorphosed into an irresponsive, dull-eyed creature who met every affectionate attempt with a murmuring, "Let me alone."

His father looked beyond the immediate crisis, viewing it merely as the shortest exit from an unlucky affair. Still, he felt the weight of the inevitable suffering. Now and then, he would sit by Rex's side in silence for a few moments, parting with a gentle pressure of his hand upon the boy's blank brow and whispering, "God bless you, my boy."

Warham and the younger children used to peep around the door edges to witness this incredible spectacle of their lively brother laid low, but fingers were immediately raised to shoo them back. The constant guardian was Anna. Her little hand was permitted to rest within her brother's, though he never offered a welcoming squeeze. Her soul was torn between anguish for Rex and a sharp reproach directed at Gwendolen. "Perhaps it is wicked of me," she confessed to herself in her recurring inner monody, "but I think I never *can* love her again." Even Mrs. Gascoigne harbored an angry feeling toward her niece, which she could not refrain from expressing (with apologies) to her husband.

"I know, of course, it is better, and we ought to be thankful she is not in love with the poor boy," Mrs. Gascoigne said to Henry. "But really, Henry, I think she is hard. She has the heart of a coquette. I cannot help thinking she must have led him to believe something, or the disappointment would not have seized him so completely. And some blame attaches to poor Fanny; she is quite blind regarding that girl."

Mr. Gascoigne answered imperatively: "The less said on that point the better, Nancy. I ought to have been more awake myself. As to the boy, be thankful if nothing worse ever happens to him. Let the matter die out as quickly as possible; and especially concerning Gwendolen—let it be as if it had never been."

The rector's dominant feeling was that they had escaped disaster. If Gwendolen had loved Rex in return, it would have created a much harder problem, a solution potentially beyond his control. However, he faced further difficulties.

One fine morning, Rex requested his bath and prepared for the day as usual. Anna, filled with excitement at this change, could do nothing but listen for his descent. Finally hearing his step, she ran to the foot of the stairs to meet him. For the first time, he gave her a faint smile; yet, it looked so melancholy upon his pale face that she could hardly restrain her tears.

"Nannie!" he said gently, taking her hand and leading her slowly along to the drawing-room.

His mother was present, and when she came to kiss him, he exclaimed, "What a plague I am!" Then he sat still, gazing out of the bow-window at the lawn and shrubs covered in hoarfrost, across which the sun sent faint, occasional gleams. Anna watched him, thinking of that sad smile on Rex's face. He felt as though he had undergone a resurrection into a new world, unsure of his purpose there, with his old interests left far behind.

Anna sat near him, pretending to work, while truly watching him with yearning looks. Beyond the garden hedge lay a road where wagons and carts occasionally passed during field-work. A railed opening had been cut in the hedge because the upland, bordered by woods and a clump of ash-trees against the sky, presented a lovely sight. Soon, a wagon laden with timber rolled past. The horses strained their grand muscles, and the driver, cracking his whip, ran anxiously alongside to guide the lead horse's head, fearing a swerve.

Rex seemed shaken into attention. He rose and watched until the last quivering trunk of timber disappeared, then paced the room once or twice. Mrs. Gascoigne was no longer there. When Rex sat down again, Anna, sensing a return of speech in her brother's eyes, could not resist bringing a small stool and seating herself against his knee. She looked up at him with an expression that seemed to plead, "Do speak to me."

And he spoke.

"I'll tell you what I'm thinking of, Nannie. I will go to Canada, or somewhere of that sort." (Rex had not yet studied the character of our colonial possessions.)

"Oh, Rex, not for always!"

"Yes, to get my bread there. I should like to build a hut, work hard at clearing, and have everything wild about me, with a great wide quiet."

"And not take me with you?" said Anna, big tears rushing fast.

"How could I?"

"I should like it better than anything; and settlers go with their families. I would sooner go there than stay here in England. I could make the fires, mend the clothes, cook the food; and I could learn how to bake bread before we went. It would be nicer than anything—like playing at life over again, as we used to do when we made our tent with the drugget, and had our little plates and dishes."

"Father and mother would not let you go."

"Yes, I think they would, when I explain everything. It would save money; and Papa would have more to bring up the boys with."

Further talk of this practical nature continued at intervals, ending with Rex consenting that Anna accompany him when he spoke to his father on the subject. Naturally, this discussion took place only when the rector was alone in his study. Their mother would eventually reconcile herself to whatever decision he made, but mentioning the question to her first would have distressed her.

"Well, my children!" said Mr. Gascoigne, cheerfully, as they entered. It was comforting to see Rex about again.

"May we sit down with you a little, Papa?" said Anna. "Rex has something to say."

"With all my heart."

It was a noticeable trio these three creatures formed, each possessing faces of the same structural type: the straight brow, the nose abruptly straightened from an intention of being aquiline, the short upper lip, and the short, strong, well-hung chin. There was even the same tone of complexion and set of the eye. The gray-haired father was both massive and keen-looking; a perpendicular line on his brow deepened whenever he spoke with force of interest, and the habit of ruling gave him an air of reserved authority.

Rex would have seemed a vision of his father's youth, if one could imagine Mr. Gascoigne stripped of distinct plans and command, smitten with heart-sorrow, and lacking the capacity for concealment that a sick animal possesses. Anna was a tiny copy of Rex, her hair drawn back and knotted, her face mirroring his expressions of change as if they shared one soul.

"You know all about what has upset me, Father," Rex began, and Mr. Gascoigne nodded.

"I am quite done up for life in this part of the world. I am sure it will be no use my returning to Oxford. I couldn't do any reading. I should fail, and cause you expense for nothing. I want your consent to take another course, sir."

Mr. Gascoigne nodded more slowly; the vertical line on his brow deepened, and Anna's trembling increased.

"If you will allow me a small outfit, I should like to go to the colonies and work on the land there." Rex considered the vagueness of the phrase prudent; "the colonies" necessarily embraced more advantages and were less susceptible to rebuttal on a single specific ground than any particular settlement.

"Oh, and with me, Papa," said Anna, unable to bear being excluded from the proposal even temporarily.

"Rex would want someone to take care of him, you know—someone to keep house. And we shall never, either of us, be married. And I should cost nothing, and I should be so happy. I know it would be hard to leave you and Mamma; but there are all the others to bring up, and we two should be no trouble to you anymore."

Anna had risen from her seat, employing the feminine argument of moving closer to her father as she spoke. He did not smile, but he drew her onto his knee and held her there, as if to gently remove her from the equation while he addressed Rex.

"You will admit that my experience gives me some power of judging for you, and that I can probably guide you in practical matters better than you can guide yourself?"

Rex was obliged to say, "Yes, sir."

"And perhaps you will admit—though I do not wish to press that point—that you are bound in duty to consider my judgment and wishes?"

"I have never yet placed myself in opposition to you, sir."

In his secret soul, Rex could not feel that he was bound not to go to the colonies, but rather to return to Oxford—that was the true point in question.

"I'll tell thee, Berthold, what men's hopes are like: A silly child that, quivering with joy, Would cast its little mimic fishing-line Baited with loadstone for a bowl of toys In the salt ocean."

Eight months after the family arrived at Offendene—that is, by the end of the following June—a rumor swept through the neighborhood that sparked excitement among many. While unrelated to the outcome of the American War, this news resonated deeply across all social strata within a specific radius around Wanchester. Corn factors, brewers, horse dealers, and saddlers alike hailed it as a praiseworthy development, celebrating it on abstract grounds as proof of the value of an aristocracy in a free nation like England. Even the blacksmith in the hamlet of Diplow felt a sense of impending prosperity; the wives of laborers dreamed that their agile sons of ten or twelve would soon find employment serving gentlemen in livery; and the local farmers, though tinged with bitterness and reservation, admitted that a man might finally find an easier market for a rick of old hay or a wagon-load of straw.

If these were the aspirations of those outside society, it follows that those "betters" possessed even stronger reasons for satisfaction, driven perhaps more by the pleasures of life than its business. Yet, marriage falls under both categories. Just as the announcement of a royal visit ignites dreams of knighthood or a baronetcy among common folk, this news conjured a vague, floating vision of matrimony in the minds of several well-bred individuals.

The news was that Diplow Hall—the estate of Sir Hugo Mallinger, which for two years had stared its white, shuttered windows in a painfully cross-eyed fashion at its fine elms, beeches, lilied pools, and deer-speckled acres—was being prepared for a new tenant. It was destined to be inhabited in proper style throughout the remainder of the summer and the hunting season. However, not by Sir Hugo himself, but by his nephew, Mr. Mallinger Grandcourt, the presumptive heir to the baronetcy, since his uncle's marriage had yielded only daughters.

Nor was this the only stroke of fortune favoring young Grandcourt, as he was pleasantly called. While his claim to the baronetcy stemmed from his father, his mother had bestowed a baronial streak upon his bloodline. Should certain intermediaries in the distant background pass away, he would ascend to become a Baron and Peer of the Realm. Nature's uneven allotment grants the tuft only to the male bird, yet we need not blindly follow hasty philosophers who urge us to replicate nature in all its quirks. If Mr. Mallinger Grandcourt secured a baronetcy or a peerage, his wife would share the title. Combined with his existing fortune, this prospect made the choice of a spouse particularly significant; indeed, being currently unmarried, he was already the subject of sympathetic interest from many who viewed him as a woman destined for security.

Some readers might deem it incredulous that people would build matrimonial prospects solely on the report of a wealthy bachelor approaching maturity, dismissing such thoughts as mere gall. They would argue that neither they nor their kin possess such unbridled minds, asserting that human nature knows better than to speculate on fallacies. However, let it be noted that this narrative does not concern human nature in general, but rather a select group in a corner of Wessex—people of impeccable reputation, all of whom maintained visiting ties with persons of rank.

Consider the Arrowpoints, residing in their beautiful home at Quetcham. No one could accuse them of sordid motives regarding their daughter Catherine's marriage, given that they could leave her an inheritance of at least half a million. Yet, harboring affectionate anxieties about her position—especially after she had resolutely rejected Lord Slogan, an unexceptionable Irish peer whose estate lacked both drainage and population—they wondered, perhaps motivated by more than charity, whether Mr. Grandcourt was good-looking, of sound constitution, virtuous, or at least reformed.
