Were he a liberal conservative, or perhaps not *too* liberal-conservative, his succession to the title would be an event eagerly desired.

If the Arrowpoints engaged in such rumination, it is less surprising that Mr. Gascoigne, a clergyman nonetheless subject to parental and guardian anxieties, felt similarly stirred. We have witnessed how both he and Mrs. Gascoigne began to feel overwhelmed by the management of young creatures who seemed impossible to hold with bit or bridle, or any metaphor suggesting judicious advice.

Naturally, people did not openly discuss their true feelings regarding Mr. Grandcourt's arrival. On no subject is such candor prudently practiced—not even concerning the generation of acids or the destination of fixed stars. One's contemporary might find one's ideas ingenious and preemptively apply them, or conversely, hold opposing views and judge accordingly. Mr. Gascoigne did not inquire of Mr. Arrowpoint for trustworthy sources on Grandcourt as a potential husband for a charming girl; nor did Mrs. Arrowpoint whisper to Mrs. Davilow that if the prospective peer sought a wife locally, the only logical candidate was Catherine, who, however, would refuse unless he proved capable of securing her happiness.

Indeed, even to his wife, the rector remained silent about the possibility of a matrimonial match, anticipating that Mr. Grandcourt might appear at the next Archery Meeting alongside Gwendolen. Though Mrs. Gascoigne's mind was undoubtedly more active in this direction, she had only interjected casually to her sister, "It would be a mercy, Fanny, if that girl were well married!" Mrs. Davilow, sensing a critique of her darling in the fervor of that wish, offered no audible reply, though inwardly she thought, "You will not get her to marry for your pleasure"; the mild mother growing somewhat saucy when identifying herself with her daughter.

To her husband, Mrs. Gascoigne remarked, "I hear Mr. Grandcourt has two places of his own, but he comes to Diplow for the hunt. It is to be hoped he sets a good example in the neighborhood. Have you heard what sort of a young man he is, Henry?"

Mr. Gascoigne claimed ignorance; at least, if his male acquaintances had gossiped in his hearing, he was unwilling to repeat it or emphasize it in his own mind. He deemed it futile, even if appropriate, to show curiosity about the past of a young man whose birth, wealth, and leisure rendered many habits venial under other circumstances. Whatever Grandcourt had done, he had not ruined himself. It is well known that in gambling, whether professional or recreational, a man with the strength of mind to stop before ruining others is considered reformed. This was merely an illustration: Mr. Gascoigne had not heard that Grandcourt was a gambler. We can hardly label him singular for feeling that a landed proprietor with noble blood was not an object for suspicious inquiry like a reformed character offering himself as a butler or footman. Where a man can afford to dispense with reformation, it is rarely anything but genuine. Furthermore, there was no evidence suggesting Mr. Grandcourt needed reform more than other youths of twenty-five, and ultimately, the significance of his past must be judged by his present reality.

Mrs. Davilow, though outwardly unmoved by her sister's suggestive comment, could not remain indifferent internally to an advent promising a brilliant fate for Gwendolen. Speculation on "what may be" arises naturally, inevitably forming images when strangers are mentioned. First, Mr. Grandcourt's name evoked in Mrs. Davilow's mind the picture of a handsome, accomplished, excellent young man, perfectly suited as a husband. Then came the further question: Would Gwendolen be satisfied with him? Who knew what would appeal to that girl's taste or touch her affections? It might be something other than mere excellence. Thus, the image of the perfect suitor gave way to a fluctuating blend of qualities imagined to win Gwendolen's heart.

In struggling to identify the precise combination ensuring success, the mother told herself, "It would not signify about her being in love, if she would only accept the right person." For whatever marriage had been for herself, how could she desire less for her daughter? The difference lay in her own misfortunes; she never dared dwell extensively on the desirability of marriage with Gwendolen, fearing an answer akin to that of the future Madame Roland. When her gentle mother urged the acceptance of a suitor, saying, "Tu seras heureuse, ma chère," the response had been, "Oui, maman, comme toi." Regarding the problematic Mr. Grandcourt, Mrs. Davilow would least of all willingly drop hints of the airy castle-building she felt ashamed of, for such a hint might tilt Gwendolen's thoughts adversely, causing her to detest the desirable husband beforehand.

Since the scene following poor Rex's farewell visit, the mother had felt a renewed sense of peril in touching the mystery of her child's feelings and rashly determining her welfare; she could conceive of welfare only in the shape of marriage. Discussions about Gwendolen's attire for the Archery Meeting provided a relevant topic. Once it was decided that, as a touch of color on her white cashmere, nothing compared to pale green—a feather she had been testing in her hat before the mirror—Mrs. Davilow felt her ears tingle when Gwendolen suddenly threw herself into the pose of drawing her bow, saying with a look of comic delight, "How I pity all the other girls at the Archery Meeting—all thinking of Mr. Grandcourt! And they have not a shadow of a chance."

Mrs. Davilow lacked

"What sort of woman ought one to be?" the first gentleman asked. "Sir, consult the tastes of marriageable men. On this planet, stores of iron, cotton, wool, and chemicals—all matter shaped by our skilled hands—are forged into forms that answer demand. The market's pulse dictates value, high or low, by some grand rule. Our daughters must become wives, and whatever men choose becomes the standard for women; men's taste is woman's test. Do you catch the phrase? 'Tis excellent, I believe—the meaning perfectly balanced by the rhythm of the words."

"Nay, but turn it around," replied the second gentleman. "Give us the test of taste. Consider a fine menu—is it today what Roman epicures insisted a gentleman must eat to earn the dignity of dining well?"

Brackenshaw Park overlooked the scene from its gentle heights, gazing far across the neighboring valley to the distant eastern downs and the broad, slow rise of cultivated land stretching westward like a vast curtain. Upon the highest platform of the clustered hills stood the castle, constructed of rough-hewn limestone, its surface alive with shifting lights and shadows cast by dark lichen dust and the washings of rain. Masses of beech and fir trees sheltered it to the north, spreading down the green slopes like flocks seeking the water that gleamed below.

At the farthest end of the park lay the archery ground, a carefully tended enclosure on a patch of tableland. Protected to the southwest by towering elms and a thick hedge of holly, it offered agreeable afternoon shade for the gravel walks and the freshly mown turf where targets stood. The Archery Hall, with its arcade facing forward, resembled a white temple against the northern greenery. It served as a perfect backdrop for clusters of ladies moving, bowing, and turning their necks with the leisurely grace of lilies suddenly given the ability to move.

Even when the military band from Wanchester fell silent, the atmosphere remained delightful. Musical laughter rang through all registers, mingling with a harmony of friendly conversation that rose to mild excitement before sinking back into a pleasant murmur. Few outdoor amusements escaped the noisy, crowding conditions that ruin modern pleasures; few archery meetings were so select. Tickets were limited to ensure the gathering remained convenient for the subsequent dinner and ball held within the castle. Inside the enclosure, no common spectators were admitted except Lord Brackenshaw's tenants and their families. Among these, it was chiefly the women who exercised the privilege, bringing their children or younger siblings. While the young men among them relieved the monotony of the event with imaginative betting—"anything you like" being the stake on their favorite archers—the young maidens operated on a different principle of discrimination, wondering which of the sweetly dressed ladies they would choose to emulate if given the option.

Probably, these rural souls would have strived for a form unlike Gwendolen's—one flushed with more pink and crowned with the most fashionable yellow hair. Yet, among the male judges standing closest to her, there was unusual unanimity in declaring her the finest girl present. No wonder she reveled in that July day. Pre-eminence is sweet to those who cherish it, even under mediocre circumstances. Perhaps it was not purely mythical that a slave takes pride in being bought first; nor that a barnyard fowl, unaware of its status, might strut with a self-aware consciousness of being the best of a bad lot, finding comfort in its relative importance. But for true enjoyment, the outer and inner selves must align. And for Gwendolen, this alignment was occurring.

Who could deny that bows and arrows are among the fairest weapons for feminine forms to wield? They invite attitudes brimming with grace and power, releasing the fine concentration of energy found in marksmanship from any association with bloodshed. The time-honored British pastime of "killing something" was no longer pursued with bow and quiver; bands defending their passes fought under different skies, and poisoned darts existed only as harmless remnants in rhetoric or distant lands. Archery possessed no foul odor of brimstone; it broke no shins and bred no monstrous athletes. Its sole danger was failure, a prospect sufficient to mold skillful action in generous hearts. Furthermore, the prizes awarded by the Brackenshaw Archery Club were of noble symbolism: not parcels to degrade honor into gain, but gold and silver arrows, stars to be worn proudly on the chest as tokens of achievement before passing to the next worthy competitor. These signs of excellence held the virtue of wreaths without their inconvenience, avoiding the potential melancholy of heavy floral arrangements in the heat of a ballroom.

Altogether, the Brackenshaw Archery Club was an institution crafted with impeccable taste, designed to avoid ridiculous incidents. Today, every element favored it. The air was mildly warm, devoid of wind to disturb hair, drapery, or the flight of an arrow. Skillful preparation met fair play, and when the general march began to retrieve arrows, the procession of joyful young creatures in light speech and laughter, moving gracefully toward a common goal, was a sight worth witnessing. Here, Gwendolen seemed a Calypso among her nymphs. Every observer admitted her surpassing charm in her attitudes and movements.

"That girl is like a high-mettled racer," Lord Brackenshaw remarked to young Clintock, one of the invited guests.

"First chop! Tremendously pretty too," agreed the elegant Greek, who had paid her assiduous attention. "I never saw her look better."

Perhaps she had never looked so well. Her face beamed with youthful pleasure, free of any malignant ray of discontent. Satisfied with her own prospects, she felt kindly toward everyone and content with the universe. Lacking the highest rank or the label of heiress like Miss Arrowpoint, she derived an added triumph from eclipsing those advantages. Regarding personal recommendation, she would not trade her family group for any other: her mother's appearance suited an amiable duchess, while her Uncle and Aunt Gascoigne, accompanied by Anna, made equally gratifying figures. Gwendolen was too full of joyous self-belief to feel the slightest jealousy, even knowing Miss Arrowpoint was one of the finest archeresses.

Even the unexpected reappearance of the formidable Herr Klesmer seemed merely to suit Gwendolen's inclination to be amused. Short of Apollo himself, what greater musical maestro could possibly command attention at an archery meeting? A satirical light danced in Gwendolen's eyes as she glanced toward the Arrowpoint party upon their entrance, noting the stark contrast between Klesmer and the average assembly of English country folk. This juxtaposition reached its peak intensity near his hosts—or patrons, as Mrs. Arrowpoint preferred to call them, hoping thereby to deny any lingering notion of patronizing genius, whose royalty was universally acknowledged. Such a contrast might have amused a graver soul, but Gwendolen found it delightful.

"We English are a miscellaneous people," she observed internally. Any fifty of us might display varied architectures of character or facial ornamentation, yet our prevailing expression lacks the liveliness of an impassioned race preoccupied with ideals. The strong point of the pure English gentleman lies in his easy style of figure and clothing; he objects to marked irregularities in his attire and to appearing inspired. Imagine an assembly of such men watching Herr Klesmer enter: his mane of hair floating backward in massive contradiction to the chimney-pot hat perched comically atop his pronounced, well-modeled features and powerful, clean-shaven jaw. His tall, thin frame was clad in a manner that, lacking strict English convention, emphasized his intent too heavily. Draped in a loose garment with a Florentine *berretta*, he would have been fitting beside Leonardo da Vinci. But how did he fare in trousers that defied English expectations regarding the knee? How did the fire in his glances and the curious sweep of his head transform into comedy when capped by a hat demanding well-cropped hair and staid demeanor, such as Mr. Arrowsmith's, whose nullity of face and perfect tailoring passed unnoticed anywhere?

One understands why it is often better for greatness to be dead, shedding the burden of the outward man. Many present knew Klesmer, or heard of him, but only from candlelit evenings where he appeared simply as a musician. He lacked yet that supreme, worldwide celebrity that renders an artist great to the ordinary public through sheer expense and renown. It was a new light for them to see him in—unexpectedly presented on a July afternoon within an exclusive society. Some laughed; others felt a touch of disgust at the Arrowpoints' lack of judgment in using an introductory card.

"What extreme types those artistic fellows usually are!" young Clintock told Gwendolen. "Look at the figure he cuts, bowing with his hand on his heart to Lady Brackenshaw—and Mrs. Arrowpoint's feather just brushing his shoulder."

"You are one of the profane," Gwendolen retorted. "You are blind to the majesty of genius. Herr Klesmer smites me with awe; I feel crushed in his presence; my courage oozes away."

"Ahh, you understand all about his music."

"No, indeed," Gwendolen laughed lightly. "It is he who understands all about mine and finds it pitiable."

Klesmer's verdict on her singing had become an easier joke since he had been struck by her *plastic* artistry. "It is not addressed to the ears of the future, I suppose. I'm glad of that: it suits mine."

"Oh, you are very kind. But how remarkably well Miss Arrowpoint looks today! She would make quite a fine picture in that gold-colored dress."

"Too splendid, don't you think

Getting to know someone—or something—is simply the process of turning a blank page into a defined sketch. For Gwendolen Harleth, the desire to be formally introduced to Mr. Grandcourt had been building slowly, a quiet anticipation rather than a sudden impulse. Yet, when Lord Brackenshaw stepped aside to make way for the anticipated stranger, and Gwendolen found herself face-to-face with the reality of the man, a sharp jolt ran through her. It flushed her cheeks and burned deeper as she became acutely aware of the sensation. The shock stemmed entirely from the reversal of her expectations; Grandcourt was far removed from every imaginary portrait she had constructed.

He stood slightly taller than she did, their eyes seemingly level. There was not a hint of a smile upon his face as he regarded her, nor any trace of self-consciousness or anxiety in his posture. As he lifted his hat, revealing an extensive baldness framed only by a sparse fringe of reddish-blonde hair, she also noted a perfect hand. The line of his features, from brow to chin, was undeniably handsome, stripped of beard and marked by angles that deviated only moderately from the vertical. Even his slight moustache hung perpendicularly. It was impossible for a human countenance to be so utterly free of grimaces or nervous wriggles; equally, it seemed impossible for a living, awake man to appear so devoid of animation.

One might argue that the quintessential Englishman, stiffening himself after a bow into rigid formality, agrees with severity and appears internally drilled, suggesting a suppressed vitality that might explode violently once released from parade. But Grandcourt possessed no such rigidity; his bearing leaned instead toward the flaccid. His complexion held a faded fairness, reminiscent of an actress's skin once the artificial rouge and powder had washed away. His long, narrow gray eyes expressed nothing but indifference.

Attempts at precise description are foolish; who can capture a human being in a single instant? Even when presented to us, we merely begin the study of their appearance, a knowledge to be completed by countless impressions gathered under varying circumstances. We learn the alphabet, but we remain unsure of the language. What Gwendolen grasped in the first moments of their meeting was summarized by a simple contrast: *He is not ridiculous.*

Immediately, however, Lord Brackenshaw departed, and what is termed "conversation" commenced. Its primary and constant element was that Grandcourt looked at Gwendolen with a persistent, slightly exploratory gaze, his expression unchanged, while she occasionally met his stare with a flash of observation, softened just enough by coquetry. Following her replies, there would invariably be a pause, longer or shorter, before he spoke again.

"I used to think archery was a great bore," Grandcourt began. He spoke with a fine accent, though delivered with a certain broken drawl, as if a distinguished personage suffered from a distinguished cold lodged in his chest.

"Are you converted today?" asked Gwendolen.

*(A pause ensued, during which she imagined various degrees and modes of opinion regarding herself that Grandcourt might hold.)*

"Yes, since I saw you shooting. In matters of this sort, one generally observes people missing and simpering."

"I suppose you are a first-rate shot with a rifle."

*(Another pause. Having taken a rapid assessment of Grandcourt, Gwendolen mentally drafted a brief, graphic description of him for an indefinite audience.)*

"I have left off shooting."

"Oh, then you are a formidable person. Those who accomplish things once and then abandon them make one feel very contemptible, as if one were wearing cast-off fashions. I hope you haven't abandoned all follies, for I practice a great many."

"What do you call follies?"

"Well, in general, I believe whatever is agreeable is classified as a folly. But I hear you haven't left off hunting."

*(Here, Gwendolen recalled rumors concerning Grandcourt's status and concluded he was the most aristocratically appearing man she had ever encountered.)*

"One must do something."

"And do you care about the turf? Or is that among the things you've discarded?"

*(In this interval, Gwendolen reasoned that a man of such cold, calm manners might actually be preferable as a husband—he wouldn't likely interfere with her preferences.)*

"I run a horse now and then, but I don't pursue it as fervently as some men do. Are you fond of horses?"

"Yes, indeed. I never enjoy my life more than when I am on horseback, having a great gallop. I think of nothing. I only feel myself strong and happy."

*(Gwendolen paused, wondering if Grandcourt approved of her sentiments, yet reassuring herself that she had no intention of disguising her tastes.)*

"Do you like danger?"

"I don't know. When I am riding, I never consider danger. It seems to me that if I broke my bones, I shouldn't feel it. I would go at anything that came in my path."

"You would perhaps enjoy tiger-hunting or pig-sticking. I witnessed some of that for a season or two in the East. Everything here is poor stuff compared to that."

"_You_ are fond of danger, then?"

*(Gwendolen speculated that men of the coldest manners were often the most adventurous, feeling the strength of her own insight as she weighed the possibility.)*

"One must have something or other. But one gets accustomed to it."

"I begin to think I am very fortunate, because everything is new to me: it is only that I cannot get enough of it. I am unused to anything except being dull, which is precisely what I would like to leave off, just as you have left off shooting."

*(It occurred to Gwendolen that a man of cold, distinguished demeanor might prove a dull companion; yet, on the other hand, she considered that most people were dull, that she had rarely observed husbands acting as companions—and ultimately, she resolved not to accept Grandcourt.)*

"Why are you dull?"

"This is a dreadful neighborhood. There is nothing to be done here. That is why I practiced my archery."

*(Reflecting that the life of an unmarried woman, unable to move freely and lacking command over anything, must inevitably grow dull regardless of comparison as time passes.)*

"You have made yourself queen of it. I imagine you will carry the first prize."

"I don't know that. I have great rivals. Did you not notice how well Miss Arrowpoint shot?"

*(Gwendolen thought that men were known to choose someone other than the woman they most admired, recalling several instances from novels.)*

"Miss Arrowpoint. No—that is, yes."

"Shall we go now to hear the scoring? Everyone is moving to the other end—shall we join them? I think my uncle is looking toward me. He perhaps wants me."

Finding relief in shifting the situation, Gwendolen ended the tête-à-tête. It was not that the private conversation had been disagreeable; rather, while it lasted, she could not shake the unusual flush on her cheeks or the sense of surprise that made her feel less mistress of herself than usual. This Mr. Grandcourt, who seemed to value his own importance far more than hers—a trait few can tolerate—must not assume he was of great significance to her, nor that because others speculated on him as a desirable match, she placed herself entirely at his disposal. How Grandcourt had filled the silences would become clearer later.

"You have just missed the gold arrow, Gwendolen," said Mr. Gascoigne. "Miss Juliet Fenn scores eight points above you."

"I am very glad to hear it. I should have felt that I was making myself too disagreeable—taking the best of everything," Gwendolen replied quite easily.

It was impossible to feel jealousy toward Juliet Fenn, a girl as mediocre as a midday market in every respect save her archery and plainness, in which latter quality she was notable, much like her father: underhanging jaw and a receding brow resembling that of the more intelligent fish species. (Considering the immense weight placed on such physical accidents in female offspring, surely marriageable men—or, as the new England calls them, "intending bridegrooms"—should examine themselves dispassionately in the mirror, since their natural instinct to select a mate prettier than themselves is not guaranteed to shield them from the effects of their own ugliness.)

Now, a lively movement swept through the mingling groups, carrying the conversation along with it. Everyone spoke to everyone else in turn. Gwendolen, choosing to survey her surroundings, observed that Grandcourt was having Klesmer presented to him by someone unknown to her—a middle-aged man with a dark, full face and fat hands, who appeared on the easiest terms with both parties. This stranger soon led the way to join the Arrowpoints, acquaintances already established between him, Grandcourt, and the family. Gwendolen cared little to identify this stranger, but she wished to observe Grandcourt's manner toward others besides herself. It was precisely the same, except that he did not look much at Miss Arrowpoint, but rather at Klesmer, who was speaking with animation—stretching out his long fingers horizontally, pointing downward with his forefinger, folding his arms, and tossing his mane, addressing himself alternately to one and the other, including Grandcourt, who listened with an impassive face and narrowed eyes, his left forefinger tucked in his waistcoat pocket and

"O gentlemen, the time of life is short; To spend that shortness basely were too long..."

On the second day following the archery contest, Mr. Henleigh Mallinger Grandcourt sat at his breakfast table alongside Mr. Lush. The atmosphere surrounding them was undeniably pleasant: the summer breeze drifted through the open casements, allowing the dogs to wander freely onto the ancient green turf of the lawn; beyond lay the park, painted in soft purples and stretching toward a dense woodland border. Inside, the room possessed a stillness born of its sober, antique elegance—a quiet dignity that felt conscious and well-bred, standing in stark contrast to the restless clutter of vulgar furnishings.

Whether the gentlemen themselves were congenial was far less clear. Grandcourt had pulled his chair back to face the garden, propping his left leg atop another seat and resting his right elbow on the table while he smoked a large cigar. His companion, Mr. Lush, continued to eat. A half-dozen dogs of various breeds moved lazily between them, shifting their allegiance vacillatingly from one master to the other. These were dogs of immense privilege, capable of playing at hunger, content merely to be offered delicacies they refused to consume. All, save for Fetch, a magnificent liver-colored water spaniel. She sat with her forepaws firmly planted, her expressive brown gaze fixed upward upon Grandcourt with unwavering constancy.

In his lap, Grandcourt cradled a tiny Maltese dog adorned with a miniature silver collar and bell. Whenever his hand was free from the cigar or the coffee cup, it rested possessively on this small bundle of animal warmth. One might assume Fetch was jealous, wounded by her master's lack of acknowledgment. Eventually, her patience snapped; she gently laid her large, silky paw upon his leg. Grandcourt regarded her with an impassive face for half a minute before laying down his cigar to lift the indifferent Fluff close to his chin, bestowing affectionate pats all the while maintaining a grave stare at Fetch. Poor creature, she whimpered intermittently, seemingly struggling to suppress her visible displeasure, until she finally rested her head beside the pleading paw, gazing up with pitiful entreaty. Thus, at least, a lover of dogs might interpret the scene, and Grandcourt, reputed to adore animals, certainly acted upon such an interpretation. Yet, when the amusing anguish erupted into a howling bark, Grandcourt shoved Fetch aside without a word. He deposited Fluff carelessly onto the table, where the puppy's black nose dominated a salt cellar, and turned his attention back to his cigar, annoyed to find it needed relighting due to Fetch's interference.

Fetch, having commenced her wailing, discovered, like many of her sex, that stopping was difficult. Indeed, the second howl was louder, and the third matched it perfectly. "Turn out that brute, will you?" Grandcourt commanded Lush, neither raising his voice nor looking at him, as if expecting the slightest gesture to command immediate obedience. Lush promptly rose, lifted the surprisingly heavy spaniel despite his dislike for stooping, and carried her outside, disappearing for a couple of minutes before returning. He then lit a fresh cigar, positioned himself at an angle where he could observe Grandcourt's face without turning fully, and soon asked, "Shall you ride or drive to Quetcham today?"

"I am not going to Quetcham."

"You did not go yesterday either."

Grandcourt smoked in silence for a moment before replying, "I suppose you sent my card and inquiries."

"I went myself at four o'clock and informed them you would arrive shortly. They would naturally assume some accident prevented you from keeping your appointment, especially if you intend to go today."

Silence followed for several minutes. Then Grandcourt asked, "What men are invited here with their wives?"

Lush produced a notebook. "The Captain and Mrs. Torrington arrive next week. There are also Mr. Hollis and Lady Flora, along with the Cushats and the Gogoffs."

"A rather ragged collection," Grandcourt observed after a pause. "Why did you invite the Gogoffs? When writing invitations in my name, be kind enough to provide a list beforehand, rather than summoning a giantess upon me without my knowledge. She ruins the aesthetic of the room."

"You invited the Gogoffs yourself when you met them in Paris."

"What does my meeting them in Paris have to do with this? I instructed you to give me a list."

Grandcourt possessed two remarkably distinct voices. Up to this point, we had heard him speak in a superficial, interrupted drawl suggesting primarily languor and ennui. However, this latest remark emerged in subdued, inward, yet distinct tones—a sound Mr. Lush had long recognized as the manifestation of a peremptory will.

"Are there any other couples you wish to invite?"

"Yes; select some respectable people with a daughter or two. And include one of your damned musicians. But no comic fellows."

"I wonder if Klesmer would consent to join us once he leaves Quetcham. Nothing but first-class music will satisfy Miss Arrowpoint."

Lush spoke casually, yet he seized the opening to fix an observant gaze upon Grandcourt, who, for the first time, turned his eyes toward his companion. He waited silently, exhaling two long, luxurious puffs, before saying, perhaps in a tone even lower than before, but edged with perceptible contempt, "What in the name of nonsense have I to do with Miss Arrowpoint and her music?"

"Well, something," Lush replied jocosely. "You need not trouble yourself greatly, perhaps. But certain formalities must be observed before a man can marry a million."

"Very likely. But I am not going to marry a million."

"That is a pity—to squander such an opportunity and dismantle your own plans."

"_Your_ plans, I presume you mean."

"You have debts, you know, and matters may prove inconvenient eventually. The heirship is not absolutely certain."

Grandcourt offered no reply, and Lush pressed on. "Truly, it is a splendid opportunity. The parents demand nothing less than perfection, and the daughter's appearance and demeanor require no deductions whatsoever, as if she possessed not a single penny. She lacks conventional beauty, yet possesses the bearing to carry any rank. Furthermore, she is unlikely to reject the prospects you can offer."

"Perhaps not."

"The parents would allow you to do whatever you wished with them."

"But I should not wish to do anything with them."

Here, Lush paused briefly before speaking again, adopting a deep, admonishing tone. "Good heavens, Grandcourt! After your experiences, will you permit a mere whim to interfere with your comfortable settlement in life?"

"Spare your oratory. I know precisely what I intend to do."

"What?" Lush extinguished his cigar and shoved his hands into his side pockets, appearing exasperated yet determined to maintain his composure. "I am going to marry the other girl."

"Have you fallen in love?" The question carried a sharp sneer.

"I am going to marry her."

"You have already made her an offer, then?"

"No."

"She is a young lady with a will of her own, I imagine. Extremely suited to making a scene. She knows exactly what she desires."

"She does not like you," Grandcourt stated, wearing the faintest ghost of a smile.

"Quite true," Lush added, his sneer becoming more marked. "Nevertheless, if you and she are devoted to one another, that shall suffice."

Grandcourt ignored this comment entirely, sipping his coffee, rising, and strolling out onto the lawn, followed closely by the pack of dogs. Lush watched him depart for a moment, then resumed his cigar, lighting it slowly. He consulted his beard with inspecting eyes and fingers, finally stroking it with the air of someone who had reached a conclusion, murmuring in a subdued voice, "Check, old boy!"

Mr. Lush, a man of considerable ability, had known Grandcourt for fifteen years without failing to learn which measures were futile, though what strategies might succeed often remained obscure. At the start of his career, Lush held a fellowship and nearly entered the clergy for a college living; however, lacking enthusiasm for that path, he accepted the role of traveling companion first to a marquis, and subsequently to young Grandcourt, who, having lost his father early, found Lush so convenient that he permitted him to serve as prime minister in all his personal affairs. Fifteen years of habit had rendered Grandcourt increasingly dependent on Lush's dexterity, while Lush grew equally reliant on the lazy luxury afforded by his transactions on Grandcourt's behalf, which demanded no significant interruption.

One cannot claim that this prolonged association intensified Grandcourt's inherent disrespect for his companion, for such disdain had been absolute from the outset. Rather, it confirmed Grandcourt's belief that he might strike Lush whenever he chose—though he never actually chose to kick any animal, for the act of kicking is a compromising posture, and a gentleman's dogs should be kicked on his behalf. Instead, Grandcourt merely uttered words that might expose him to retaliation if his confidant possessed an independent spirit. But what son of a vicar, who has starved his wife and daughters of calico to send his male offspring to Oxford, can retain an independent spirit when bent on dining with refined discrimination, riding superior horses, and living generally amidst the most luxuriant honey-blossomed clover—all without labor?

Mr. Lush had once passed as a scholar and retained a sense of scholarship when not attempting to recall much of it; yet the bachelor arts and

"Philistia, be thou glad of me!"

Having decided to marry Miss Glanvil, Grandcourt displayed a remarkable talent for aligning his actions with his objectives. For the next two weeks, scarcely a day passed without him arranging a meeting or demonstrating, through emphatic attentions, that she occupied his thoughts. With his cousin, Mrs. Torrington, managing the household affairs, Mrs. Davilow and Gwendolen were invited to a grand gathering at Diplow. There, amidst many witnesses, the host lavished distinction upon the dowerless beauty while showing no concern for the heiress. The social world—including Mr. Gascoigne and all prominent families within visiting distance of Pennicote—grew increasingly confident in the matter. This growing assurance prompted the rector to resolve to perform his duty toward his niece, ensuring that any financial settlements were adequate. Indeed, the wonder shared by Mrs. Davilow and the rector was that an offer, for which so many suitable occasions had arisen, had not yet been made; Grandcourt himself harbored a portion of this surprise. Having told Lush of his resolution, he had expected the affair to conclude swiftly. To his own astonishment, he had repeatedly promised himself in the mornings that he would grant Gwendolen the opportunity to accept him that very day, only to find in the evenings that the necessary formalities remained uncompleted. This delay only hardened his determination for the future. He had never admitted to himself that Gwendolen might refuse him, yet—as heaven help us all!—we are often unable to act upon our certainties. Our aversion to a contrary outcome is so potent it rises like a spectral illusion between us and our truth. We are rationally sure that a blind worm cannot bite us mortally, yet the thought of being bitten is so intolerable, and the creature's gaze so threatening, that we refuse to handle it.

He requested permission to bring a magnificent horse for Gwendolen to ride. Mrs. Davilow was to accompany her in the carriage, and they were to travel to Diplow for lunch, with Grandcourt escorting them. It was a fine mid-harvest season, neither too hot nor too cool for a noonday ride of five miles to be delightful. Poppies glowed along the field borders; a gentle breeze moved softly through the uncut corn like a social spirit, casting clouds' shadows across the soft gray downs. Here stood the sheaves; there, horses strained their muscles under the final loads from vast stretches of stubble. Yet everywhere, the green pasture provided a broad backdrop for the cornfields, while cattle rested beneath wide branches. The road wound through countryside where dairy farms appeared much as they had in the days of our forefathers—a place where peace and permanence seemed to find a home, distant from the frantic changes signaled by the rushing railway trains in the distance.

However, the spirit of peace failed to penetrate poor Mrs. Davilow's mind, overcoming her habitual unease. Watching Gwendolen and Grandcourt canter ahead, then slow to a conversational walk until the carriage caught up, created a gratifying sight. Yet, it primarily fueled the conflict of hopes and fears regarding her daughter's fate. Here was an irresistible opportunity for a suitor to speak and end all uncertainties. Mrs. Davilow trembled, hoping fervently that Gwendolen's decision would be favorable. Certainly, if Rex's love had been repugnant to her, Mr. Grandcourt offered a stark contrast. That he had made a novel impression on her seemed evident in her marked restraint from satire, nay, her total silence regarding his character—a silence Mrs. Davilow dared not break. "Is he a man she would be happy with?" was the inevitable question in the mother's mind. "Well, perhaps as happy as she would be with anyone else—or as most other women are"—was the answer she used to quiet herself, for she could not imagine Gwendolen finding satisfaction in what we traditionally call "mean circumstances."

Grandcourt's thoughts ran in the same direction: he sought to end the uncertainty of his unsaid words. Any further hesitation was baseless, merely a quality in the air acting as an irritant to his desires.

Gwendolen enjoyed the ride, but her pleasure did not burst forth in the girlish, unpremeditated chatter and laughter she had shown that morning with Rex. She spoke a little, even laughed, but with the lightness of a far-off echo. For her, too, there was a peculiar quality in the air. She was certain it was not any subjugation of her will by Mr. Grandcourt, nor the splendid prospects he offered. Gwendolen desired everyone, including that dignified gentleman, to understand that she intended to do exactly as she liked, and that they should not calculate on her pleasing them. If she chose this husband, she wanted him to know she would not renounce her freedom, or, in her favorite formula, "not do as other women did."

Grandcourt's remarks that morning were, as usual, of that brief sort that never fails to command respect when the speaker holds importance in his circle. By stopping so soon, they hinted at a suppressed, formidable ability to say more, while possessing the merit of allowing others to speak longer.

"How do you like Criterion's paces?" he asked after they entered the park and slowed from a canter to a walk.

"He is delightful to ride. I should like to have a leap with him, if it wouldn't frighten mamma. There was a good wide channel we passed five minutes ago. I'd like to gallop back and take it."

"Pray do. We can take it together."

"No, thank you. Mamma is so timid—if she saw me, it might make her ill."

"Let me go and explain. Criterion would take it without fail."

"No—indeed—you are very kind—but it would alarm her too much. I dare take any leap when she isn't by; I do it and don't tell her."

"We can let the carriage pass and then set off."

"No, no, pray don't think of it any more: I spoke quite randomly," Gwendolen said, beginning to feel a new objection to carrying out her own proposal.

"But Mrs. Davilow knows I shall take care of you."

"Yes, but she would think of you as having to take care of my broken neck."

A considerable pause followed before Grandcourt looked toward her and said, "I should like to have the right always to take care of you."

Gwendolen did not turn her eyes to him. It seemed a long while before she first blushed, then turned pale, yet to Grandcourt's rapid judgment, she answered soon enough, with the lightest flute-tone and a careless movement of her head. "Oh, I am not sure that I want to be taken care of: if I chose to risk breaking my neck, I should like to be at liberty to do it."

She checked her horse as she spoke, turning in her saddle to look toward the approaching carriage. Her eyes swept across Grandcourt, but there was no language in them to correct the carelessness of her reply. At that very moment, she realized she was risking something—not her neck, but the possibility of finally halting Grandcourt's advances—and she did not feel content with that possibility.

"Damn her!" Grandcourt thought as he too checked his horse. He was not a wordy thinker, and this explosive phrase represented mixed impressions that eloquent interpreters might expand into sentences full of irritation that he was being mystified, coupled with a determination that this girl should not make a fool of him. Did she want him to throw himself at her feet and declare he was dying for her? Not by that gate could she enter the privileges he could offer. Or did she expect him to write his proposals? Equally a delusion. He would not make his offer in any way that placed him definitively in the position of rejection. As for her accepting him, she had effectively done so by accepting his marked attentions; anything that broke them off would be understood to her disadvantage. Was she merely coqueting?

However, the carriage arrived, and no further tête-à-tête could occur before reaching the house, where abundant company awaited. Clad in riding dress, hat aside, and bearing the reputation of being chosen by Mr. Grandcourt, Gwendolen became naturally a center of observation. Since the objectionable Mr. Lush was absent, this stimulus of admiring attention heightened her spirits and dispersed, for the time, the uneasy consciousness of divided impulses that threatened her with repentance. Whether Grandcourt was offended was impossible to judge: his manners remained unchanged, but Gwendolen's acumen had not penetrated deep enough to discern that his unchanged demeanor offered no clue, leaving her nonetheless afraid of him.

She had visited Diplow before only for dinner. Since certain views from the windows and gardens were worth seeing, Lady Flora Hollis proposed after luncheon, once some guests had departed and the sun was sloping toward four o'clock, that the remaining party explore the grounds. Frequent opportunities arose for Grandcourt to retain Gwendolen apart and speak unheard. But no! He spoke to no one else, and what he said was no more eager or intimate than during their first interview. He looked at her not less than usual; and with some of her defiant spirit returning, she looked full at him in return, not caring—rather preferring—that his eyes held no expression.

Finally, it seemed he entertained some scheme. After nearly completing the tour of the grounds, the whole party stopped by the pool to watch Fetch's accomplishment of bringing a water lily to the bank like Cowper's spaniel Beau. Disappointed in his

"I will not clothe myself in wreckage—wear jewels sawn from the crushed bones of drowned women; feel the chilly, vaporous hands of angry ghosts clutching my necklace; trick my maiden bosom with the inheritance of orphans. Let your dead love marry its dead."

By the next morning, Gwendolen looked lovely and vigorous, like a tall lily freshly opened. A reaction of youthful energy surged through her; yesterday's self-distrust seemed no more than a fleeting shiver upon the surface of a full stream. The upcoming archery match at Cardell Chase was a delightful prospect in itself. She pictured herself moving beneath the beeches like a wood-nymph, surrounded by appreciative company. This imagined scene added a charming allure to Grandcourt's anticipated advances. Today, she foresaw him making slow, conversational moves toward a declaration, and she prepared to await and encourage it, following the rational conclusion she had previously laid out for her uncle.

Descending to breakfast, after everyone had departed except Mrs. Davilow, Gwendolen found letters on her plate. She read one with a gathering smile, then handed it to her mother. Upon receiving it back, Mrs. Davilow also smiled, finding renewed cheer in her daughter's evident good spirits.

"You don't feel inclined to go a thousand miles away?"

"Not exactly so far."

"It was a sad omission not to have written again before now. Can't you write now, before we set out this morning?"

"It isn't so pressing. Tomorrow will suffice. You see, they are leaving town today. I must write to Dover; they'll be there until Monday."

"Shall I write for you, dear, if it bothers you?"

Gwendolen paused, sipped her coffee, and then answered brusquely, "Oh, no, let it be. I will write tomorrow." Feeling a sudden pang of guilt, she looked up and said with playful tenderness, "Dear, old, beautiful mamma!"

"Old, child, truly."

"Please don't, mamma! I meant 'old' for 'darling.' You aren't twenty-five years older than I am. When you talk like that, my life seems to shrivel up before me."

"One can find great happiness in twenty-five years, my dear."

"I must lose no time in beginning," Gwendolen said merrily. "The sooner I secure my palaces and coaches, the better."

"And a good husband who adores you, Gwen," Mrs. Davilow encouraged.

Gwendolen pouted saucily and said nothing.

A slight drawback to her joy was that the rector was detained by magistrate's business and likely wouldn't reach Cardell Chase that day. She cared little that Mrs. Gascoigne and Anna chose not to go without him, but her uncle's presence would have made the execution of their decision seem inevitable. Decision itself had become formidable. Having come close to accepting Grandcourt, Gwendolen felt this unexpected abundance of options rounding itself into something too definitive. When we begin wishing greatly for ourselves, whatever we obtain soon transforms into mere limitation and exclusion. Yet, there remained the comforting notion that marriage would serve as a gateway to greater freedom.

The meeting place was a grassy spot known as Green Arbor, where a cluster of hanging branches formed a sheltering amphitheater. Here, the coachload of servants with provisions prepared the picnic meal. The warden of the Chase was tasked with guiding the roaming archers, ensuring they stayed within the prescribed distance from this center and prevented them from wandering beyond the fixed limits—a curve passing through landmarks like the Double Oak, the Whispering Stones, and the High Cross. The plan called for a brief stroll before lunch, saving the main expedition for the exquisite light of the afternoon. The muster was swift, sparing anyone dull moments of waiting. As groups scattered through the dappled light and shadow cast by neighboring beeches and rarer oaks, one might imagine a painter delighted by the scene. This form of roaming archery was far more picturesque than the stationary game, though success at variable targets was less predictable, distributing hits unevenly among the volunteers. Perhaps for this reason, combined with the dual distraction of being preoccupied and unwilling to reveal it, Gwendolen did not excel in these initial trials, unless it was for the lively grace with which she accepted her comparative failures. She wore white and green, as she had on the day she was introduced to Grandcourt—an event that marked an epoch for her. He was constantly by her side now, yet it would have been difficult to judge from mere appearance that their relationship had altered since their first conversation. Still, other signs led most observers to conclude they were either already engaged or on the verge of it. Gwendolen believed this herself.

As the groups returned toward Green Arbor in divergent clusters, chatting rather than aiming, words were exchanged that seemed the true beginning of that ending—the start of her acceptance. Grandcourt remarked, "Do you know how long it has been since I first saw you in this dress?"

"The archery meeting was on the 25th, and this is the 13th," Gwendolen laughed. "I'm not good at calculations, but I'd venture to say it's nearly three weeks."

A brief pause, then he said, "That is a great loss of time."

"That your knowing me has caused?" she retorted playfully. "Pray, don't be uncomplimentary; I dislike it."

Another pause. "It is because of the gain that I feel the loss."

Here, Gwendolen allowed a silence to stretch. She was thinking, *He is truly ingenious. He never speaks stupidly.* Her unusual quietude seemed the strongest possible affirmative answer, prompting him to continue: "The gain of knowing you makes me feel the time I lose in uncertainty. Do *you* like uncertainty?"

"I think I do, rather," Gwendolen said suddenly, beaming at him with a playful smile. "There is more in it."

Grandcourt met her laughing eyes with a slow, steady gaze that seemed like abstract vision, then asked, "Do you mean more torment for me?"

Something so strange occurred to Gwendolen in that moment that she was shaken out of her usual self-consciousness. Blushing and averting her eyes, she replied, "No, that would make me sorry."

Grandcourt might have pressed this answer, which her shift in demeanor rendered decisively favorable, but he was not so overcome as to ignore that they were now descending into Green Arbor, visible to everyone, at an ill-chosen point where the path grew inconveniently steep. This offered a literal reason for him to offer his hand; she took it, and they descended in silence, much observed by those already on the level—including Mrs. Arrowpoint, who stood with Mrs. Davilow. That lady had decided Grandcourt's merits were insufficient to induce Catherine to accept him, given Catherine's high standards, evidenced by her refusal of Lord Slogan. Thus, she regarded the tenant of Diplow with dispassionate eyes.

"Mr. Grandcourt is not equal as a man to his uncle, Sir Hugo Mallinger—too languid. Granted, Mr. Grandcourt is younger, but I shouldn't be surprised if Sir Hugo outlives him despite the age gap. It is ill-calculating regarding successions," Mrs. Arrowpoint concluded, speaking somewhat too loudly.

"It is indeed," Mrs. Davilow agreed with quiet cheerfulness. She was so satisfied with the current state of affairs that her habitual melancholy regarding life's general unsatisfactoriness was entirely in abeyance.

I need not detail the finest food consumed in that green refectory nor dwell on the tales of forest scenery spreading beyond the hollow's edge. I am bound instead to recount a story of life at a stage when the blissful beauty of earth and sky entered consciousness only through narrow, oblique channels, while the mind was busy with a small social drama, almost as little aware of wider relations as if it were a puppet show. Understand that the food and champagne were of the best; the talk and laughter belonged to the highest society, where no one made invidious displays, and the advantages of the world were taken with the high-bred depreciation born of familiarity. Some gentlemen strolled and smoked cigars during the interval before four o'clock, the time to resume roaming. Strangely, Grandcourt was among them, unlike Mr. Lush, who seemed to enjoy the day generously by making himself particularly useful, ordering things for everyone. By this activity, he became more of a blot on the scene for Gwendolen, though he kept amiably aloof, never even looking at her openly.

When the group prepared to depart, it appeared the bows were under the charge of Lord Brackenshaw's valet. Mr. Lush sought to spare ladies the trouble of retrieving theirs from the carriage. He did not intend to fetch Gwendolen's, but fearing he might, she hurried to get it herself. The valet, seeing her approach, met her with the bow and simultaneously handed her a letter. She asked no questions, instantly recognizing the address was in a lady's delicate handwriting—the kind formerly esteemed feminine before the modern uncial style. Moving away with her bow, she saw Mr. Lush approaching to fetch others. To avoid meeting him, she turned aside, walking with her back toward the carriage stand, and opened the letter. It read:

"If Miss Harleth is in doubt whether she should accept Mr. Grandcourt, let her break from her party after they have passed the Whispering Stones and return to that spot. She will then hear something to decide her; but she can only hear it by keeping this letter

"Festina lente"—let speed be tempered with deliberation.

We have witnessed Gwendolen Harleth spending her time abroad swept up in a fresh fervor for gambling, deluding herself into the role of an Empress of Luck. From these chaotic experiences, she emerged with a vague conviction that in this bewildered world, the actions of individuals mattered little; the sole purpose seemed to be amusement. Furthermore, we observed how certain figures, shrouded in mystery as "Grapnell & Co.," sought to reign within the kingdom of chance and pursue their own diversions regardless of cost. Their machinations resulted in a painful alteration of her family's financial standing. Consequently, she returned home, carrying against her will a necklace she had pawned—which someone else subsequently redeemed.

As she journeyed back to England, Grandcourt was approaching to locate her. However, he approached in his characteristic fashion: not with the urgency of an express train racing directly from Diplow to Leubronn, where her whereabouts were believed to be, but with such deliberate slowness that he allowed himself to be detained by Russian acquaintances at Baden-Baden. He made various appointments with them, only to break them later when his desire to reach Leubronn became paramount.

Grandcourt's passions were of an intermittent, flickering nature; they rarely burned with intense flame. Yet, vast amounts of life proceed without such strong emotion. Countless cravats are meticulously tied, countless dinners attended, and countless speeches delivered proposing the health of august dignitaries—all devoid of the zest born of deep desire. A man can maintain a formidable appearance in high society, feigning classical erudition, scientific reserve, political conviction, and the full sentimentality of the English gentleman, all at a minimal expenditure of vital energy. Moreover, he can remain obstinate or persistent at a low level, occasionally displaying sudden impulses that possess a deceptive aura of demonic strength simply because they appear inexplicable. Often, their true origin lies merely in a lack of regulated channels for the soul to move; without the good and sufficient ducts of habit, human nature easily turns to mere ooze and mud, yielding nothing but a spurt or a puddle under pressure.

Grandcourt was not entirely displeased by Gwendolen's flight from the splendid opportunity he had presented. The act possessed a certain piquancy for him. He preferred to attribute it to resentment regarding his somewhat casual treatment of Cardell Chase—an oversight that, upon reflection, appeared rather cool. To have brought her so close to a tender admission, only to see her walk headlong away from further opportunities of securing her consent, was precisely the kind of provocation that would rouse a spirited girl. And if she were worthy of his mastery, she ought to possess such spirit. Undoubtedly, she expected him to follow, just as he intended to. Yet, for a full week, he took no steps toward departure and did not even inquire where Miss Harleth had gone.

Mr. Lush felt a triumph mixed with profound distrust. Grandcourt had uttered not a word to him regarding her absence and maintained a neutrality akin to that of an alligator; one could never predict what might churn slowly within his mind. Nevertheless, delaying a decision created space for the dissipation of Grandcourt's energy. The guests at Diplow felt a growing curiosity surpassing that of their host. How could nothing more be heard of Miss Harleth? Could it be credible that she had refused Mr. Grandcourt? Lady Flora Hollis, a lively middle-aged woman rich in curiosity, suddenly felt compelled to embark on a round of calls with Mrs. Torrington, visiting the rectory, Offendene, and Quetcham. Through this tour, she not only doubled her efforts but also gleaned from the Arrowpoints that Miss Harleth had departed for Leubronn to join some old friends, the Baron and Baroness von Langen. The immediate agitation and disappointment of Mrs. Davilow and the Gascoignes had crystallized into a collective wish that Gwendolen's disappearance should not be interpreted as eccentric or requiring secrecy.

Indeed, the rector's mind harbored the possibility that the marriage was merely postponed, for Mrs. Davilow had hesitated to reveal the bitter determination with which Gwendolen had spoken. Despite his practical abilities, some of his experience had hardened into maxims and quotations. As Amaryllis fled, she desired her hiding place be known; and in this age of steam, the proverb that love finds a way "over the mountain and over the wave" requires no hyperbole. Gwendolen, he reasoned, was an Amaryllis of excellent sense but coquettish daring; the question remained whether she had dared too much.

Lady Flora, returning laden with news of Miss Harleth, saw no reason why she should not attempt to electrify Mr. Grandcourt by mentioning it during dinner, subtly hinting that rumors had spread of him being a disappointed suitor. Grandcourt listened with quietude but keen attention. The following day, he instructed Lush to arrange a plausible pretext for dispersing the party at Diplow within another week, declaring his intention to yacht to the Baltic or elsewhere. It was impossible to remain at Diplow as if under house arrest with a group of people he had never desired. Lush required no clearer signal that Grandcourt was heading to Leubronn, though he might approach the destination like a creeping billiard ball, sticking to the path. Mr. Lush intended to render himself indispensable so he might accompany his patron, succeeding in his endeavor. Gwendolen's repulsion for him was a fact that only amused his master, making him all the more willing to keep Lush perpetually at hand.

Thus, it came to pass that Grandcourt arrived at the *Czarina* five days after Gwendolen had left Leubronn, finding there his uncle, Sir Hugo Mallinger, along with his family, including Daniel Deronda. It is not necessarily a pleasure for either the reigning power or the heir presumptive when their separate affairs—a touch of gout in one, a touch of willfulness in the other—conspire to bring them to the same spot. Sir Hugo was a man of easy temper, tolerant of differences and defects. However, a differing viewpoint regarding the settlement of family estates fretted him considerably more than disputes over church discipline or voting rights; faults were less forgivable when committed by someone whose very existence was inconvenient.

In no scenario could Grandcourt have been a nephew to Sir Hugo's liking. Yet, as the presumptive heir to the Mallinger estates, he represented the sign and embodiment of the baronet's chief grievance: the lack of a son to inherit the lands, in no portion of which had Sir Hugo held more than a life-interest. An ill-advised settlement by his father, Sir Francis, had left Diplow, with its modest acreage, under the same restrictive conditions as the ancient and extensive inheritance of the two Toppings. Diplow, where Sir Hugo had hunted and lived through many seasons in his youth, and where his wife and daughters ought to have retired after his death, was bound by these terms. This grievance grew sharper with the passing years. Lady Mallinger, after bearing three daughters in quick succession, had remained childless for eight years, now past forty, without producing another girl. Meanwhile, Sir Hugo, nearly twenty years older, found himself at an age when, despite the fashionable delay of everything from dinners to marriages, a man's hopefulness begins to show signs of weariness unless restored by a second childhood.

Having begun to despair of a son, the confirmation of Grandcourt's interest in the estates made his presence increasingly unwelcome. On the other hand, it prompted Sir Hugo to ensure their relationship remained as friendly as possible. This led him to dwell on a plan that had matured alongside his disappointment: to secure Diplow as a future residence for Lady Mallinger and her daughters, thereby keeping this pleasant fragment of family inheritance for his own potential offspring. His knowledge of his nephew's disposition encouraged the belief that Grandcourt might consent to a transaction granting him a substantial sum of ready money in exchange for his prospective interest in the domain and its associated land. Should an unhoped-for son eventually be born, the money would have been wasted, and Grandcourt would have been compensated for relinquishing interests that proved worthless; yet Sir Hugo dismissed this risk as negligible. Of late, he had managed his fortune wisely through mining operations and lease sales, preparing himself for such an outlay.

Here lay an objective that made him cautious of quarreling with Grandcourt. Years prior, while improving the Abbey and needing Grandcourt's concurrence to fell an obstructive mass of timber on the demesne, Sir Hugo had congratulated himself on discovering no active spite in his nephew's peculiar mind. Nothing had since occurred to deepen their hatred beyond what was compatible with perfect politeness or strict mutual accommodation.

From Grandcourt's perspective, his uncle was a superfluity and a bore; he felt that the list of general grievances would improve once Sir Hugo was expunged. However, informed by Lush—a useful intermediary—of the baronet's inclinations regarding Diplow, Grandcourt was gratified to have the option of accepting money. Even if he deemed it unlikely he would choose it, his sense of power would be flattered by his ability to refuse what Sir Hugo desired. These hinted transactions influenced his motives for requesting a year's tenancy of Diplow, which had annoyed Sir Hugo to grant, especially since the excellent hunting nearby might deter Grandcourt from parting with his chance of future possession. A man possessing two locations, one offering inferior hunting, naturally desires a third where the game is better. Additionally, Lush had suggested to Sir Hugo the probability that Grandcourt would woo and win Miss Arrow

Men possess histories as vast and complex as the solar system, comprising both a visible trajectory and an invisible core. An astronomer maps the former with rigorous logic, tracing every arc across the dark; yet a true chronicler of human life must navigate the hidden corridors of emotion and thought that precede every deed, leading inevitably to those moments of profound suffering that transform pain into action—the anguished cries of Promethean figures whose torment burns brighter than the heavens they defy.

For Daniel Deronda, destiny had carved a singular path. One specific afternoon, bathed in July sunlight and the scent of fading pink roses, had become the defining epoch of his youth. Imagine the scene: a thirteen-year-old boy lying prone on the cool grass beneath the shadow of a Gothic cloister, his curly head resting on his arms atop a book. Beside him, seated on a camp-stool, was his tutor, similarly engrossed in reading. In Daniel's hands lay Sismondi's *History of the Italian Republics*. The boy possessed a voracious appetite for history, driven by a desire to comprehend how time had unfolded since the Flood and how humanity persisted through its quieter eras.

Suddenly, lowering his arm, he gazed at his tutor with wide-eyed curiosity. "Mr. Fraser," he asked in a tone of innocent wonder, "how was it that popes and cardinals always had so many nephews?"

His tutor, an able young Scot serving as Sir Hugo Mallinger's secretary, reluctantly shifted his gaze from his treatise on political economy. With the crisp, emphatic cadence characteristic of Scottish speech, he replied, "Their own children were called nephews."

"Why?" Daniel pressed.

"It was simply a matter of propriety," the tutor explained, his patience fraying slightly at the interruption. "Since priests cannot marry, their offspring were deemed illegitimate."

Mr. Fraser thrust out his lower lip, returning his focus to his book, while Daniel, as if struck by a sudden sting, sprang upright, facing away from his mentor.

He had always addressed Sir Hugo Mallinger as his uncle. When he had once dared to inquire about his parents, the baronet had offered only a vague reassurance: "You lost your father and mother when you were quite a little one; that is why I take care of you." In the hazy twilight of that early memory, Daniel retained only a dim sensation of being enveloped in soft, scented drapery, kissed frequently, until his fingers brushed something hard and sharp, causing him to weep. Beyond that, his world consisted entirely of the domestic sphere he inhabited. At the time, he cherished Sir Hugo so deeply that the mystery of his origins mattered little; life was a delightful gift from an indulgent, cheerful guardian—a man in the prime of his life whom Daniel regarded as flawless.

Sir Hugo's residence was a picturesque architectural extension of an ancient abbey, steeped in romance and history. In contrast, Diplo, the family seat in another county, was a modest estate acquired through marriage. The Mallinger lineage, however, traced its roots to Henry VIII's reign, descending from Hugues le Malingre, a companion of William the Conqueror. The family gallery displayed two generations of ancestors: stern men in armor with pointed beards, pale ladies confined by hoops and ruffs, grave figures in black velvet, and frightened mothers clutching their children. Through the refined portraits of Kneller, Reynolds, and Romney, the line culminated in Sir Hugo and his younger brother, Henleigh. Henleigh had married Miss Grandcourt, adopting her surname and merging their ancient heraldry, creating a powerful union embodied by Henleigh Mallinger Grandcourt—a figure who would soon become more significant to the narrative than either Sir Hugo or his nephew.

Sir Thomas Lawrence had captured Sir Hugo's youthful vigor in a portrait, though he had inadvertently elongated the baronet's nose, which was actually shorter than the family standard. Fortunately, this distinctive feature reappeared in his brother Henleigh and, most strikingly, in his nephew, Mallinger Grandcourt. Yet, in Daniel Deronda, the ancestral faces reflected nowhere. Nevertheless, he possessed a beauty surpassing all his forebears. At thirteen, he could have served as the ideal model for a painter seeking to depict the essence of noble boyhood; to behold his face was to feel convinced that humanity had achieved greatness in the past and held the potential for even greater heights in the future. Such childlike countenances hold a consecrating power, making us recoil from the world's coarseness and sorrow, fearing they might stain such purity.

But amidst the rose petals on the grass, Daniel was encountering his first lesson in grief. A new idea had invaded his mind, altering the landscape of his previously carefree existence. Just as a sailor's peace is shattered by the sudden threat of a storm, Daniel sat motionless, his face betraying a rapid internal transformation. The deep blush that initially colored his cheeks faded, replaced by an indefinable intensity of concentration. Unlike other boys, his mind blended childish innocence with surprising insight. Having devoured Shakespeare and history, he understood the plight of bastards—men forced to strive heroically to match their legitimate peers. Yet, he had never applied this knowledge to his own life, which had been too smooth to warrant such questions—until now.

With the speed of lightning, a terrifying possibility seized him: the secret of his birth lay here, and the man he called uncle was, in truth, his father. Some children discover poverty early on, realizing their parents cannot afford everything; Daniel felt the arrival of a new, ominous guest carrying veiled, dreadful truths. The fervor he poured into his imaginary worlds suddenly turned inward, illuminating his own history. The beloved uncle transformed in his mind into a father hiding secrets, committing a wrong. What had become of his mother, whom he must have been separated from? These were mysteries he dared not probe. To speak of them felt like catching fireflies in his imagination. Those who recall their own passionate childhoods understand this terror of voicing shame regarding one's parents.

These surging images overwhelmed him, leaving no room for the rationalization that he might be grasping at shadows. The clash between overwhelming emotion and the fear of exposure finally broke forth in slow, heavy tears.

"Daniel, do you see that you are sitting on the bent pages of your book?" Mr. Fraser's voice cut through the silence.

Daniel shifted the book without turning around, held it briefly before him, then rose and walked into the open grounds to dry his tears unnoticed. Once the initial shock subsided, he remembered that he lacked certainty. He had been constructing narratives about his life as he once invented tales of Pericles or Columbus to fill historical gaps. Yet, stubborn fragments of reality remained, like broken pieces of a bridge, clearly indicating where the arches should lie. Simultaneously, a moral impulse arose, viewing his speculations as a form of religious doubt—an impious prying into matters he was not meant to know. Possessing a delicate sensibility, he struggled to reconcile these conflicting currents. Ultimately, a secret impression had settled upon him, granting him a new perspective on every element of his existence. The realization that others likely harbored undisclosed judgments fostered a premature reserve, intensifying his inner turmoil. Now, words that would have previously gone unheard triggered fresh clusters of feeling around every trivial event linked to his suspicions.

A month later, another incident etched itself deeply into his soul. Daniel possessed a rare, resonant voice and a natural musical talent, often accompanying his singing on the piano. After performing "Sweet Echo" for a gathering of gentlemen sheltered from the rain, Sir Hugo summoned him.

"Come here, Dan!" the baronet called.

The boy approached with unusual hesitation. Dressed in an embroidered holland blouse that highlighted the richness of his complexion, his expression carried a quiet gravity that only enhanced his beauty. Everyone admired him.

"What do you say to becoming a great singer?" Sir Hugo asked, his tone shifting from jovial to serious. "Would you like to be adored by the world and sweep the stage, like Mario or Tamberlik?"

Daniel flushed instantly. After a brief pause, he answered with angry resolve, "No; I should hate it!"

"Well, well, well!" Sir Hugo remarked, attempting to soothe him with feigned surprise. But Daniel turned sharply, retreated to his chamber, and collapsed onto the broad windowsill, his favorite refuge. From there, he watched the rain subside, revealing gleams of light through breaking clouds that illuminated the park's ancient oaks and the green glades piercing the woods. This scenery had always been his home, a backdrop of dignified ease that he had accepted as natural. His ardent nature had embraced it wholly. Though he knew much of what it meant to be a gentleman by inheritance, losing himself in the grandeur of figures like Robert Bruce, he had never imagined being excluded from such a fate or destined for a role vastly different from his uncle's.

While it is possible for some to embrace poverty as a bride, preferring humble surroundings and universal access over exclusive privilege, Daniel's tastes aligned perfectly with his upbringing. Daily routines brought him delight, not ennui. Now, however, he was stung by the notion that his uncle—or perhaps his father—envisioned a career for him utterly alien to his own desires, a path unsuitable for the sons of English gentlemen. Although he had accompanied Sir Hugo to the opera, hearing great tenors, the image of a singer commanding a hall now repelled him. He viewed it as dressing up to perform for an audience that would regard him merely as a fascinating toy. For Daniel, Sir Hugo's suggestion was undeniable proof that his birth disqualified him from the gentlemanly class.

"This is true the poet sings,
That a sorrow's crown of sorrow
Is remembering happier things."
—TENNYSON: *In Memoriam*.

On a fine evening near the end of July, Daniel Deronda was rowing alone on the Thames. It had been nearly a year since he returned to England, ostensibly to finish his education and secure his place in society. Though he had begun reading law to satisfy Sir Hugo's wishes and ward off idleness, the endeavor had only deepened his internal indecision. Now, living with the Mallingers in London, his old passion for boating had surged anew. The river offered a seclusion unavailable anywhere else in the city. He owned a boat at Putney, and whenever Sir Hugo did not require his services, it became his chief holiday to row until past sunset, returning only when the stars appeared.

Deronda was not in a sentimental mood, nor was he lost in idle reverie. Rather, he inhabited a contemplative state common among certain young men of his generation: a questioning of whether it was worthwhile to engage in the "battle of the world"—a struggle fought by those who possess a small fraction of capital accumulated by others' labor. Sir Hugo found Deronda's hesitation puzzling. How could a youth who stood in such splendid contrast to the sickly and whining types be paralyzed by spectral illusions? Especially since Deronda rejected authorship, a profession often viewed as converting foolish thinking into profit.

Rowing in a dark-blue shirt and skull-cap, with his curls closely clipped and his mouth framed by abundant, soft waves of beard, Deronda bore only disguised traces of the seraphic boy who once trailed clouds of glory. Yet, even someone who had not seen him since childhood might have recognized him through a slow familiarity, perhaps triggered by the peculiar intensity of his gaze—a look Gwendolen once called "dreadful," though it possessed a remarkably mild scrutiny. His voice, occasionally heard in subdued fragments of song, was merely a high baritone. To judge by his lithe, powerful frame and the firm gravity of his face, an observer would quickly deduce he lacked the rare, ravishing tenor that nature sacrifices so reluctantly.

Look at his hands: they were neither small and dimpled nor possessing tapering fingers suited only for a deprecating touch. They were long, flexible, and firmly grasping—hands reminiscent of those Titian painted to combine refinement with force. There was a striking resemblance between these hands and the face that wore them: the uniform pale-brown skin, the perpendicular brow, and the calmly penetrating eyes. He was no longer seraphic; he was thoroughly terrestrial and manly, yet of a type capable of recognizing human dignity even in the poorest relations. Such figures appear occasionally among average conditions, like a workman whistling over a measurement and lifting his eyes to answer a query about the road. Often, the profound meanings of faces, as well as written words, reside chiefly in the immediate impressions they evoke. For Deronda, rowing on the Thames in ordinary attire beneath Kew Bridge, passing with no intention of seeking adventure, these impressions were paramount.

Indeed, Deronda strongly objected to the notion, which others had forced upon him, that his appearance naturally drew attention. Complimentary hints regarding this trait resonated angrily within him, stemming from mixed experiences hinted at previously. For years, his reflection in the glass had associated him with thoughts of someone he resembled deeply—a figure whose character and fate he constantly wondered about but never dared inquire.

Between six and seven o'clock near Kew Bridge, the river was no longer a solitude. People sauntered on the towpath, and boats plied the water. Deronda had been rowing hard to clear this area when he noticed a large barge approaching. Guiding his boat aside, he rested his oar within a couple of yards of the riverbank. Throughout, he continued the low-toned chant haunting his throat since leaving the bridge: the gondolier's song from Verdi's *Otello*, setting Dante's immortal words, *"Nessun maggior dolore / Che ricordarsi del tempo felice / Nella miseria"* ("No greater sorrow / Than to recall the happy times / In misery"). As he paused, the soft, melodic wail of *"nella miseria"* drifted clearly over the water's edge.

Three or four bystanders stopped to watch the barge pass the bridge, and undoubtedly included the young gentleman in the boat. However, the low vocal sounds likely reached only one ear with significance greater than the ambient noise. Deronda, waiting for the barge, turned his head toward the riverside and spotted a figure a few yards away that seemed an embodiment of the misery he was unconsciously voicing. It was a girl, hardly more than eighteen, of a low, slim build, with a most delicate face. Her dark curls were pushed back behind her ears beneath a large black hat, and a long woolen cloak draped her shoulders. Her hands hung clasped before her, and her eyes were fixed on the river with an immovable, statue-like despair.

This arresting sight caused Deronda to stop singing. Apparently, his voice had penetrated her inner world without her noting its origin, for when it abruptly ceased, she shifted her stance slightly. Looking around with a frightened glance, she met Deronda's face. The exchange lasted only a couple of moments, yet it felt like an eternity for two strangers locked in mutual gaze. Her look resembled that of a fawn or other gentle animal before it flees: no blush, no specific alarm, only a timidity that could not prevent a prolonged stare before she turned away. It seemed Deronda perceived she was only half-conscious of her surroundings—was she hungry, or bewildered by some other cause? A surge of interest and compassion welled up in him, but instantly she turned and walked to a nearby bench under a tree.

He felt he had no right to linger.
