Poorly dressed, melancholy women were common sights; it was the delicate beauty, the picturesque lines, and the vivid coloring of this particular image that made it exceptional—and thus made it more imperative that he not intrude. He began to row away, soon distancing himself upriver, yet no other thoughts were potent enough to banish the pale image of the unhappy girl. He repeatedly speculated on the romance hidden behind her loneliness and desolation, then smiled at his own bias that interesting faces must harbor interesting adventures. Finally, he justified his feeling that sorrow was more tragic when it befell delicate, childlike beauty.

"I should not have forgotten the look of misery if she had been ugly and vulgar," he told himself. Yet, the attractiveness of the image ensured its persistence. To him, she was clear as an onyx cameo: the brown-black drapery, the white face with minute features and dark, long-lashed eyes. His mind swept over the girl-tragedies occurring worldwide, hidden and unheeded, as if they were plays in a coppice where helpless creatures dragged wounded wings and stained the shadowed moss with the crimson mark of their own deaths.

Recently, Deronda's solitary excursions had been dominated by uncertainties regarding his own path. These doubts, given ample leisure, expanded to encompass vast connections with life and history. Consequently, the new image of helpless sorrow blended seamlessly with his reasons for shrinking from the world's routine—a system that made men apologize for wrongdoing and treated opinions as mere professional tools. Why should he pull strongly on any thread of the hopelessly entangled scheme of things? He used his oars sparingly, content to drift with the tide and be carried back by it. It was his habit to indulge in a solemn passivity that arose with lengthening shadows and mellow light, when thinking and desiring melted imperceptibly together, transforming argument into passionate vision.

By the time he returned with the tide past Richmond Bridge, the sun was nearing setting. The approach of his favorite hour, with its deepening stillness and darkening masses of trees and buildings caught between the double glow of sky and river, disposed him to linger as if listening to an unfinished musical strain. Seeking a perfectly solitary spot to moor his boat, he chose a bend opposite Kew Gardens, offering a broad stretch of water reflecting the sky's glory while keeping him in shadow. Lying on his back with his head propped on cushions, he could observe everything without being seen. For a long while, he did not turn his eyes from the view directly ahead.

He was forgetting everything else in a half-speculative, involuntary identification with the landscape, pondering how far he might shift his center of gravity until his own personality existed outside him as surely as the scenery. Suddenly, the sensation of movement on the opposite bank, bordered by willow bushes, drew his glance. In the first moment, he felt a darting presentiment concerning the moving figure. Soon, he could see the small face illuminated by strange, dying sunlight. Fearing to frighten her with sudden motion, he watched her with motionless attention.

She looked around, seemingly gathering security from the apparent solitude, hid her hat among the willows, and immediately removed her woolen cloak. Shortly thereafter, she sat down and deliberately dipped the cloak into the water, holding it there briefly before pulling it out with evident effort, rising as she did so. Deronda realized she intended to wrap the wet cloak around herself like a drowning shroud. There was no time to hesitate. He rose, seized his oar, and paddled across; fortunately, her position lay slightly below him.

The poor creature, terrified by this sign of discovery from the opposite bank, sank down on the brink again, clutching her cloak half-out of the water. She crouched, covering her face as if hoping she had not been seen, believing the boatman was merely approaching by accident. But soon he was

Life is a fickle parent; sometimes she adorns herself in jewels and fine feathers, striding up marble staircases with her chin high, never glancing down at the servants trailing behind her. Other times, she dresses in rags, wanders through shadowy alleyways, inhales the smoke of cheap spirits, and joins in the screaming chaos of the destitute. Yet today, she appeared as a thrifty matron—neat, capable, and brimming with cheerful morning thoughts and clever schemes to find abundance within scarcity.

Mrs. Meyrick's home was quiet. The front parlor overlooked the river, while the rear faced lush gardens, allowing the window to remain open even as Mrs. Meyrick read aloud to her daughters, refreshing the air in their modest double room where a single lamp and two candles burned. The candles sat on a separate table for Kate, who was sketching illustrations for a publisher; the lamp served not only the reader but also Amy and Mab, who were embroidering satin cushions destined "for the great world."

From the outside, the house looked narrow and shabby, the bright light filtering through the holland blind highlighting the heavy, old-fashioned window frame. However, it is comforting to know that countless such cramped, fog-shrouded slices of London have historically—and still do—serve as sanctuaries for a culture remarkably free from vulgarity. In poverty, ostentation becomes irrelevant; the grand spectacles of society merely provoke petty rivalries or futile attempts at possession. The Meyrick household belonged to this rare breed. They clung to this specific row-house because its interior was anchored by objects permanently placed, holding memories of the mother's marriage for her, and serving as an unquestioned, star-like constancy for the children.

Mrs. Meyrick endured many hardships so she might preserve certain engravings her husband deeply loved. Consequently, the narrow walls displayed a condensed world history in scenes and portraits the children had memorized since infancy. Their chairs and tables were old companions preferred over anything new. Despite lacking furniture a broker would covet—save for the prints and the piano—these two small parlors offered space and tools for a life of broad horizons, elegantly curated to embrace the highest realms of music, painting, and poetry.

Even during their leanest days, before Kate began earning wages, they likely managed without a servant to tend fires or sweep floors. Yet, they remained fastidious. They refused to believe that the manners of fashionable ladies were as coarse, self-centered, and laced with slang as depicted in contemporary literary caricatures. The Meyricks possessed peculiar quirks, streaks of eccentricity inherited from both parents, their minds resembling medieval castles with unexpected recesses, hidden passages, and sudden vistas.

Yet, mother and daughters were bound by a triple knot: family love, a shared reverence for noble deeds and masterful works, and a habit of diligent industry. When Hans once offered to spend his money to make their lives more luxurious, they unanimously declined. Both parties were spared the regret of seeing his artistic ambitions crushed by the allure of secure income—a sacrifice that would eventually force him to abandon his fellowship. They could all laugh at his Gavarni-style caricatures and forgive his pursuit of a natural calling, unobstructed by their own selflessness and independence. A grand treat of opera-going in the gallery awaited whenever Hans visited, but otherwise, they continued their established rhythm.

Seeing the group as they gathered that evening, one could scarcely wish for any alteration to their existence. They were all diminutive, perfectly scaled to their miniature rooms. Mrs. Meyrick was a lively little woman, half-French, half-Scotch, possessing an articulate speech that seemed to illuminate her listeners' understanding. Though nearing fifty, her rippling hair, tucked beneath a Quaker-style net cap, was mostly gray, contrasting with her bright brown eyebrows and eyes. Her black dress, buttoned up like a priest's cassock, suited her neat, barely-five-foot figure.

Her daughters mirrored her, save for Mab, who inherited Hans's light hair and fair complexion, along with a bossy, irregular brow and other distinctive traits reminiscent of him. Everything about them was compact: from the tight coils of their hair pinned *à la Chinoise* to their gray skirts, which defiantly rejected the era's fashion demanding that four feminine forms fill every inch of the front parlor. If they were wax figures, they could easily fit into a fashionable lady's traveling trunk. Their faces seemed ready to speak, as if their minds had been shelled like horse-chestnuts, revealing a bright core within.

The only large entity in the room was Hafiz, the Persian cat, comfortably perched on the brown leather back of a chair, occasionally blinking his large eyes to ensure the smaller creatures caused no mischief.

Mrs. Meyrick had just concluded reading Erckmann-Chatrian's *Histoire d'un Conscrit*. Mab, having dropped her embroidery to stretch forward and fixate on the reader, suddenly declared, "I think that is the finest story in the world!"

"Of course, Mab!" replied Amy. "It is the last you've heard. Whatever pleases you is the best in its turn."

"It hardly counts as a story," corrected Kate. "It is a fragment of history brought close to us with a powerful telescope. We can see the soldiers' faces... no, more than that—we can hear everything. We can almost hear their hearts beating."

"I don't care what you call it," Mab interrupted, tossing her thimble aside. "Call it a chapter in Revelation. It makes me want to do something good, something grand. It fills me with sorrow for everyone. It reminds me of Schiller—I want to take the world in my arms and kiss it. Instead, I must kiss you, little mother!" She flung her arms around her mother's neck.

"When you're in that mood, Mab, your work suffers," Amy noted dryly. "Finishing your cushion without staining it would count as doing something good."

"Oh—oh—oh!" groaned Mab, stooping to retrieve her scattered supplies. "I wish I had three wounded conscripts to nurse!"

"You'd spill their beef tea while talking," Amy teased.

"Poor Mab! Don't be too hard on her," the mother interjected gently. "Hand me the embroidery, child. Continue with your enthusiasm, and I'll resume the pink and white poppies."

"Well, Ma, I think you're more caustic than Amy," Kate observed, tilting her head to inspect her drawing.

"Oh—oh—oh!" cried Mab again, rising and stretching her arms dramatically. "Something wonderful must happen! I feel like the Deluge! The waters of the great deep are breaking loose, and the windows of heaven are opening! I must sit down and play the scales!"

As Mab began opening the piano while the others laughed at her dramatic climax, a carriage halted before the house, followed immediately by a sharp rap on the knocker.

"Dear me!" exclaimed Mrs. Meyrick, jumping up. "It is past ten, and Phoebe is already asleep." She hurried out, leaving the parlor door ajar.

"Mr. Deronda!" she called out.

The girls heard their mother's exclamation from inside. Mab clasped her hands, whispering loudly, "There now! Something *is* going to happen." Kate and Amy abandoned their tasks in sheer amazement. However, Deronda's reply was so low they couldn't catch his words, and Mrs. Meyrick promptly shut the parlor door.

"I know I am placing a tremendous trust in your kindness," Deronda continued after delivering a brief summary of events. "But you can imagine how helpless I feel with a young creature like this on my hands. I cannot leave her among strangers, and given her nervous state, I dread bringing her into a house full of servants. I have relied on your mercy. Please, do not consider my actions unjustified."

"On the contrary," Mrs. Meyrick responded warmly. "You have honored me by trusting me. I understand your predicament. Please bring her in. I will go and prepare the girls."

While Deronda returned to the carriage, Mrs. Meyrick re-entered the parlor and announced, "Here is someone to replace your wounded conscripts, Mab: a poor girl attempting to drown herself in despair. Mr. Deronda found her just in time to save her. He brought her along in his boat, unsure what else to do safely, so he entrusted her to us. She appears to be a Jewess, but highly refined—he says she knows Italian and music."

The three girls, curious and expectant, moved forward, standing together in a silent unity of compassion. Mab looked slightly awestruck, as if this fulfillment of her wish were supernatural. Meanwhile, Deronda approached the carriage door where a pale face now peered out with alert observation.

"I have brought you to some of the kindest people in the world," he told the girl. "These are daughters like yourself. It is a happy home. May I take you to meet them?"

She stepped out obediently, slipping her hand into his and momentarily forgetting her hat. As Deronda led her into the warm glow of the parlor, where the four small women awaited, she became a vision capable of stirring even the coldest hearts. Initially dazed by the sudden brightness, she hadn't fully focused her gaze before Deronda guided her hand into her mother's.

He felt a surge of joy that the Meyricks were so petite; her dark-curling head was the tallest among them. The wandering girl need not fear these gentle faces so close to her own. Now, she

To claim that Daniel Deronda was merely a romantic figure would be to misunderstand the very core of his nature. Beneath his composed, slightly restrained demeanor burned a fire of intense feeling, allowing him to discover profound poetry and drama within the mundane currents of daily existence. Perhaps such beauty is as abundant today as it ever was, obscured only by those indifferent souls who, regardless of the era, dismiss wonder as a foolish error. Poetry does not require isolation; it thrives alongside microscopes and rattles inside train cars, vanishing only when the human spirit fails to perceive it. Why should the vast machinery of the universe, from the furthest stars down to the nurturing embrace of a mother, fail to inspire awe and tenderness in a heart devoid of fellowship? For Deronda, the chance encounter with Mirah stirred emotions comparable to the legendary quests of Orestes or Rinaldo. He spent half the night awake, reliving the moments since he first spotted her by the riverbank with a vividness that only deep emotion can grant.

When he attempted to read to quiet this inner turmoil, the printed words dissolved into a transparent lattice through which he still saw and heard every detail of the recent hours. He perceived not just what happened, but the infinite possibilities of what might have been and what could yet come, fueled by the warm, trembling energy of hope and fear. A specific resonance in his own life caused Mirah's desperate search for her mother to grip his imagination with unusual force. His initial instinct was pure sympathy: if missing persons existed in London, there were methods to locate them, akin to scientific inquiry, requiring only the right tools to be activated. Yet, the complex shadows of his own family history soon colored his concern for Mirah. The desire to uncover his own mother was perpetually shadowed by dread; projecting this onto Mirah, he feared that finding the mother and brother from whom she had been torn away as a child might prove to be a catastrophe. In the boat, she had described them as "good," but that assessment likely stemmed from her innocent ignorance and longing. Twelve years were a lifetime capable of altering character drastically.

Despite his natural inclination to champion the oppressed, Deronda had never felt a practical draw toward the Jewish community. The facts he knew of them—whether they appeared in fine clothes or hid in alleyways—were mostly repellent. He assumed that educated Jews had abandoned their faith to assimilate into their host nations. While scorn directed at a specific individual might evoke his deepest empathy, the blanket hatred cast upon an entire race could sometimes justify the punishment of a single guilty member. Even those unfamiliar with theology, like the Caribs, might logically associate theft with certain moral frameworks based on observation. Deronda could not avoid hearing the ugly stories surrounding Jewish customs and trades. Though he protested against severing the past from the present, he rarely ventured beyond the general notion that Jews possessed the accumulated virtues and vices of a long-suffering people. Now, however, Mirah's urgent longing forced him to scrutinize the details, conjuring disturbing images of what it might mean to meet this middle-aged woman and her son.

He acknowledged the exquisite refinement and charm of Mirah herself, which suggested nobility in her kin, but he decided to wait for more information. Perhaps Mrs. Meyrick could offer clues directly from Mirah. Her voice, her accent, her very appearance—all wrapped in a purity that seemed sacred—made him recoil even further from associating her with anything base or corrupt. Yet, these comforting words are often mere smoke used to obscure harsh realities; true thought operates differently. Deronda's mind raced through grim scenarios: being led by an official guide into a squalid street, entering a dark doorway to see a shrewd, unkempt woman haggling over a starving girl's last dress; or finding himself in a slicker district, breathing the air around a young Jew eager to please gentlemen and unashamed of shady dealings. He excused himself from indulging in such insults, noting that his mind did not naturally equate prophets with advertisements, but currently, he was driven by terror. If Mirah's parents had been Christian, his fears would simply be broader; instead, his dread was rooted in the specific stigma attached to unknown parentage, a connection that felt tragically reasonable in both his own case and hers.

So, what was to be done for Mirah? She required shelter and protection above all else. Every chivalrous impulse within him demanded that he secure the interest of others immediately, believing that sharing her burden was the highest fulfillment of his duty. He felt no right to provide for her completely, despite his ability to do so; the depth of her impact on him made him wish she understood herself as independent of him. Vague, fantastical visions of the future lingered, leaving behind an anxiety sharper than any logical reason. He feared that those observing his actions closely must know the full history of his relationship with Mirah from the start. He had vowed never to let the truth become a disgrace to another person, especially since many truths are not of our own making. He lived in constant terror of breaking this promise, falling into the cynical trap of explaining the world solely through one's own behavior.

At one point, he resolved to confess the entire adventure to Sir Hugo and Lady Mallinger at breakfast the following morning. However, the possibility that his next visit to Mrs. Meyrick might yield new revelations checked this impulse. Ultimately, he fell asleep deciding to wait until he had spoken with Mrs. Meyrick before taking any further steps.

It is rarely disputed that even within this fragile and fallen world, we occasionally encounter individuals whose very bearing and demeanor, coupled with the entirety of their lives, bear such an unmistakable seal of virtue that our judgment of them becomes an immediate intuition rather than the product of prolonged scrutiny. —Alexander Knox, quoted in Southey's *Life of Wesley*.

Mirah claimed she had slept peacefully that night. When she descended in Mab's black dress, her dark hair curling in fresh tendrils as it dried from its abundant bath, she appeared as one beginning to find solace after the long sorrow and vigilance that had pale her cheeks and cast blue shadows beneath her eyes. It was Mab who brought her breakfast and escorted her downstairs, displaying a certain pride in the effect of a pair of tiny felt slippers she had hurried out to purchase, for there were no shoes in the house small enough for Mirah. Her borrowed dress ended at her ankles, revealing the modest garments that molded themselves to her feet, appearing as exquisite as the sheaths of budding flowers. The farthing buckles were jewels indeed.

"Oh, if you please, Mamma?" cried Mab, clasping her hands and bowing toward Mirah's feet as they entered the parlor. "Look at the slippers; how beautifully they fit! I declare she resembles Queen Budoor—'two delicate feet, the work of the protecting and all-recompensing Creator, support her; and I wonder how they can sustain what is above them.'"

Mirah gazed down at her own feet with a childlike wonder and then smiled at Mrs. Meyrick, who was thinking silently, *One could scarcely imagine this creature capable of an evil thought. Yet, wisdom dictates caution.* She returned Mirah's smile and replied, "I fear those feet have had to carry their burden a little too often recently. But today she shall rest and be my companion."

"And she will tell you so many things, and I shall not hear them," grumbled Mab, feeling herself thrust into the first volume of a delightful romance, forced to skip chapters due to her duties with pupils.

Kate had already departed to sketch along the river, and Amy was away on business errands. This arrangement suited the mother perfectly; she desired solitude with this stranger, whose narrative must inevitably be fraught with sorrow, yet one that needed to be spoken.

The small front parlor resembled a temple that morning. Sunlight bathed the river, and a soft breeze drifted through the open window. The walls bore witness to a glorious, silent cloud of art: the Virgin soaring amidst her cherubic entourage; Grand Melancholia with her solemn universe; the Prophets and Sibyls; the School of Athens; the Last Supper; mystical groups where distant ages converged into a single moment; the grave heads of Holbein and Rembrandt; the Tragic Muse; children of the previous century lost in thought or play; Italian poets—all present through the medium of black and white. The neat mother, having weathered her trials and emerged with a face still cheerful, sorted colored wools for her embroidery. Hafiz purred on the windowsill, the mantel-clock ticked without haste, and the occasional rumble of wheels seemed to lie outside the heavier, central silence. Mrs. Meyrick felt this quiet might be the finest invitation for her companion to speak, choosing not to disturb it with comment. Mirah sat opposite in her usual posture, hands clasped on her lap, ankles crossed, her eyes initially drifting slowly over the surroundings before settling with a placid reverence upon Mrs. Meyrick. Finally, she began to speak softly.

"I remember my mother's face better than anything else; yet I was not seven when I was taken away, and I am nineteen now."

"I can understand that," said Mrs. Meyrick. "Some earliest impressions endure the longest."

"Oh, yes, it was the very beginning. I think my life commenced with waking up and loving my mother's face; it was so near to me, her arms encircling mine, and she sang to me. One hymn she sang so frequently, so endlessly: and then she taught me to sing it with her; it was the first song I ever knew. They were always Hebrew hymns she sang; and because I never understood the meaning of the words, they seemed filled with nothing but our love and happiness. When I lay in my little bed, with whiteness above me, she would bend over me, between me and the light, and sing in a sweet, low voice. I can dream myself back into that time when I am awake, and it often returns to me in my sleep—my hand was very small; I would raise it to her face, and she would kiss it. Sometimes in my dreams I begin to tremble, thinking that we are both dead; but then I wake, and my hand lies here, and for a moment I hardly recognize myself. But if I could see my mother again, I would know her instantly."

"You must expect some alteration after twelve years," said Mrs. Meyrick, gently. "Observe my grey hair; ten years ago it was bright brown. Days and months pass over us like restless little birds, leaving the marks of their feet backward and forward; especially when they are birds with heavy hearts—then they tread heavily."

"Ah, I am certain her heart has been heavy for lack of me. But to feel her joy if we could meet again, and for me to make her know my love and give her deep comfort after all her mourning! If that were possible, I would mind nothing; I would be glad simply to have survived my tribulation. I did despair. The world seemed miserable and wicked; no one helped me so that I could bear their looks and words; I felt my mother was dead, and death was the only path to her. But then, in the final moment—yesterday, when I longed for the water to close over me—and I thought death was the best image of mercy—goodness came to me, living. I felt trust in the living. And—it is strange—but I began to hope that she was living too. And now, with you—here—this morning, peace and hope have flooded into me. I desire nothing; I can wait; because I hope and believe and am grateful—oh, so grateful! You have not thought evil of me; you have not despised me."

Mirah spoke with low-toned fervor, remaining as still as a painting throughout.

"Many others would have felt as we do, my dear," said Mrs. Meyrick, feeling a mist rise in her eyes as she looked at her embroidery.

"But I did not meet them—they did not come to me."

"How was it that you were taken from your mother?"

"Ah, I am taking a long time to reach that point. It is dreadful to speak of, yet I must tell you—I must tell you everything. My father—he was the one who took me away. I thought we were merely embarking on a short journey; I was delighted. There was a box containing all my little treasures. But we boarded a ship and drifted farther and farther from the land. Then I became ill; I thought it would never end—it was the first misery, and it seemed endless. But eventually, we landed. I knew nothing then, and believed what my father said. He comforted me, assuring me I would return to my mother. But it was America we had reached, and it was many long years before we returned to Europe. At first, I often asked my father when we were going back; I tried to learn writing quickly because I wanted to write to my mother. But one day, when he found me attempting to compose a letter, he lifted me onto his knee and told me that my mother and brother were dead—that was why we did not return. I remember my brother faintly; he carried me once, but he was not always at home. I believed my father when he said they were dead. I visualized them beneath the earth when he mentioned it, with their eyes forever closed. I never suspected it might not be true; and I used to cry every night in my bed for a long while. Then, when she visited me so often in my sleep, I thought she must be living nearby, though I could not always see her, and that comforted me. I was never afraid in the dark because of that; and very often during the day I would shut my eyes, bury my face, and try to see her and hear her singing. Eventually, I came to do this even without closing my eyes."

Mirah paused, her face radiant with a sweet contentment, as if immersed in a happy vision, gazing out toward the river.

"Still, I hope your father was not unkind to you," said Mrs. Meyrick after a moment, anxious to bring her back to the present.

"No; he doted on me and took pains to instruct me. He was an actor; and I discovered later that the 'Coburg' I used to hear him mention at home was a theater. But he had more involvement with the theater than acting. He had not always been an actor; he had been a teacher and knew many languages. His acting was not particularly good, I think, but he managed the stage, wrote, and translated plays. An Italian lady, a singer, lived with us for a long time. Both of them taught me, and I also had a master who made me memorize and recite texts. I worked quite hard, though I was so young; and I was not nine when I first stepped onto the stage. I could easily learn things, and I was not afraid. But then, and ever since, I hated our way of life. My father possessed money, and we

They say that knowledge is power, but who has ever truly weighed the crushing weight of ignorance? While knowledge patiently constructs over centuries, ignorance can dismantle a lifetime's work in a single hour. Knowledge refines the senses and multiplies needs, transforming life into a vibrant tapestry of new creations; ignorance arrives drunk on the seventh day, wielding a match and a barrel of oil, declaring "Let there not be," and shriveling the colorful world into a suffocating blackness. Knowledge operates with a conscience, restrained by scruples regarding what *must* be and what *may* be. Ignorance, however, is a blind giant; if left unchecked, he would treat the pillars supporting humanity's greatest achievements as toys, plunging all places of joy into the darkness of a buried Babylon.

When examining the trajectory of a single life, one sees clearly how ignorance of the true bonds between events, coupled with a delusion of control, precipitates the soul toward ruin. Like faulty eyesight that mistakes distant horizons for steps away, this blindness drives the misguided forward into destruction.

It was half-past ten in the morning when Gwendolen Harleth arrived at the station bound for Offendene, having traveled from Leubronn. No carriage awaited her; in a telegram sent from Dover, she had requested a later train, driven by an impatience to escape the London delay. She had not pictured the solitude of arriving unannounced at a remote halt, situated not for convenience but as an equidistant point from everywhere. Deposed as a *femme sole* amidst her towering trunks, she waited in the Railway Inn's waiting room for a vehicle. The scene was dreary: dirty walls, a dusty decanter of flat water, and large, glaring texts urging repentance. These elements seemed woven into the fabric of her family's troubles. Hurried to the outer door, she gazed at the lane and fields, yet even the sunlight appeared melancholic. Autumn leaves shivered, and the wind ruffled the feathers of a cock and two hens, likely parentless and bewildered.

A railway official observed her with an innocent yet condescending gaze, his lack of recognition suggesting he deemed her insignificant. Soon, a filthy old barouche arrived, being prepared by an elderly laborer. These contemptible details often form the bedrock of a life's narrative, accumulating until they shape a mood into a theory or a motive. Philosophy itself cannot escape such determinants. Dropped alone at an ugly, irrelevant spot, burdened by a sense of no income, one is prone to discouraging speculation on the origins of existence. How much heavier these trifles fell on a young lady of fastidious taste, draped in an Indian shawl, surrounded by twenty cubic feet of luggage, and suddenly acutely aware of poverty? They crushed Gwendolen's resistance.

"What is the good of enduring hardship, ugliness, and humiliation?" she asked herself. This was the beginning of returning home, a preview of her future. During the slow, jolting drive, with one trunk squeezing the driver and another lashed to the seat before her, her ruling vision shifted. From Leubronn, she had expected the family to return abroad, assuming a small income remained. She feared a dull exile, watching her sisters grow up in tediousness while her mother grew more melancholy. She refused to submit. Weariness and disgust replaced her uneasy dreams; the self-delight of admiring her reflection in the mirror had vanished, replaced by the futility of being charming, clever, or resolute. Men were hateful. Yet, in these final hours, the nature of that hatred changed. It was one thing to hate stolen goods; it was another to hate them because their theft prevented one from using them. Gwendolen began to rage against Grandcourt specifically—for being the obstacle that barred her from marrying him, the architect of her current misery.

As the vehicle crested the hill toward the house, a figure appeared under the portico. A rush of less selfish emotion swept through Gwendolen. Seeing her mother's beautiful, sorrowful face, she threw her arms around her neck, momentarily aligning her own pain with her mother's. Behind them stood the four "superfluous" girls, each possessing a private world of no consequence to others, yet all sensing that Gwendolen's presence signaled a reprieve from misfortune. Even her brisk dismissal—"Now go away, girls"—carried the comfort of authority that weakness craves.

Miss Merry, usually a picture of meek depression, busied herself with the trunks and the coachman's pay, expecting no greeting. Mrs. Davilow and Gwendolen ascended the stairs to the black-and-yellow bedroom.

"Never mind, mamma dear," Gwendolen whispered, pressing a handkerchief to her mother's weeping cheeks. "I don't mind. I will do something. I will be something. Things will come right. It seemed worse because I was away. Be glad I am here."

Every word rang true. A surge of compassionate tenderness ignited her capacity for resolve. Vague projects from her journey crystallized instantly. She suddenly perceived how she could be "something." Her mother, forgetting their dire straits, looked at her with adoration. "Bless you, my good, good darling! I can be happy, if you can!"

But later, the tide receded. The old slippery rocks and weedy places returned. As misfortune ceased to be a mere announcement and became a tyrannical inhabitant, courage shrank. Initially, the comforts of the large house—the roomy silence, the ready luxuries of her toilette, the private tray of favorite foods—soothed her. She had insisted, "Keep them all away from us today. Let you and me be alone."

Descending into the drawing-room, fresh as a newly dipped swan, Gwendolen leaned against the cushions beside her mother, prepared to hear everything. "What have you thought of doing, exactly, mamma?" she asked deliberately.

"Oh, my dear, the next step is to move from this house," Mrs. Davilow replied softly. "Mr. Haynes is delighted to have it now. Lord Brackenshaw's agent, Bazley, will arrange everything."

"I cannot help thinking Lord Brackenshaw would let you stay here rent-free," Gwendolen interjected, her talents unused to business but keen on discerning admiration.

"My dear child, Lord Brackenshaw is in Scotland and knows nothing of us. Neither your uncle nor I would wish to trouble him. Besides, what could we do here without servants or money to heat it? The sooner we leave, the better. We have nothing to carry but our clothes."

"I suppose you mean to go abroad, then?" Gwendolen asked, accepting this familiar scenario.

"Oh, no, dear. How could we travel? You never learned about income and expenses," Mrs. Davilow smiled sadly, taking Gwendolen's hand. "That makes it so much harder for you, my pet."

"But where are we to go?" Gwendolen pressed, a new current of fear coursing through her.

"It is decided. We shall take a little furniture from the rectory." Mrs. Davilow hesitated, dreading the shock she must inflict. "We are to go to Sawyer's Cottage."

At first, Gwendolen remained silent, paling with justified anger. Then, with haughtiness, she declared, "That is impossible. Something else ought to have been thought of. My uncle ought not to allow that. I will not submit to it."

"My sweet child, what else could be done? Your uncle is suffering; he has a family to raise. Do you understand? We have nothing. We shall have absolutely nothing except what he and my sister provide. They have tried wisely and actively, and we must try to earn something. I and the girls are going to work a table-cloth border for the Ladies' Charity at Winchester and a communion cloth for Pennicote Church."

Mrs. Davilow spoke timidly, hoping to convey the gravity of their position to a daughter who, alas, must submit for now, though the mother secretly hoped for a better background.

"But surely somewhere else than Sawyer's Cottage might have been found," Gwendolen persisted, haunted by the image of the house where an exciseman had lived.

"No, indeed, dear. Houses are scarce. We are thankful for anything so private. It is not so very bad. Two little parlors and four bedrooms. You shall sit alone whenever you like."

The sympathy Gwendolen had felt for her mother evaporated completely. She ignored the deprecatory words. "I cannot conceive that all your property is gone at once, mamma. How can you be sure in so short a time? It is not a week since you wrote."

"The first news came much earlier, dear. But I would not spoil your pleasure until it was necessary."

"Oh, how vexatious!" Gwendolen colored with fresh anger. "If I had known, I could have brought home the money I won. For wanting to know, I stayed and lost it. I had nearly two hundred pounds; it would have sustained us until I could execute some plan." She paused, then added impetuously, "Everything has gone against me. People have come near me only to blight me."

Among the "people" included Deronda. Had he not interfered, she might have returned to the gaming tables with a few napoleons and recovered her losses.

"We must resign ourselves to the will of Providence, my child," Mrs. Davilow started, startled by the admission of gambling but afraid to press further, suspecting

We often weave fanciful dreams of radical change, yet our actual lives are spun in a loom where busy passion drives the shuttle back and forth, weaving the familiar patterns of our actions.

Gwendolen's note, arriving "just too soon and just too late," reached Klesmer precisely as he was departing Quetcham. Driven by a desire to honor her plea for kindness, he chose to spend the night in Winchester, despite the inconvenience it caused himself. There were specific reasons preventing him from staying at Quetcham.

That magnificent mansion, built with extravagant expense, had become stiflingly hot for him. Its owners, much like certain grand politicians, were shocked by an uprising against the established order that ordinary people, seeing it clearly in hindsight, recognized had been brewing openly beneath their very noses.

As usual, the house was filled with guests. Among them was one whom Miss Arrowpoint anticipated as a new suitor: a political man of good standing who confidently awaited a peerage and believed, on public grounds, that he required a substantial fortune to uphold the title properly. Heiresses vary greatly; those interested in them beforehand are prepared to find them too pale or too flushed, tall and lanky, or short and stout, volatile and whimsical, or dreamy and bland. Yet, in every instance, it is assumed that the woman will view herself merely as an accessory to her wealth and marry wherever society dictates her fortune should lead. However, nature, accommodating itself poorly to our favored customs by creating "only-child" daughters, occasionally endows such misplaced daughters with a clear mind and a strong will. The Arrowpoints had already felt anxiety regarding these gifts in their Catherine. She refused to accept the social duty dictating a marriage to a needy nobleman or a commoner climbing the ladder toward nobility. They worried about her persistent rejection of suitable offers. As for the possibility that she was in love with Klesmer, they were entirely unconcerned—a very common form of blindness. Generally, mortals are astonishingly surprised by effects they have actively created, yet baffled by the absence of effects they have neglected to pursue. Parents marvel at their sons' ignorance despite investing immense time and money to ensure it; spouses are stunned by the loss of affection they failed to nurture; and everyone is prone to wondering why their neighbors do not admire them. Thus, the truth appears highly improbable. Truth differs vastly from the lazy combinations born of our desires. The Arrowpoints' moment of astonishment had finally arrived.

When a passion exists between an heiress and a proud, independent-minded man, reaching an understanding is difficult. However, obstacles usually dissolve unless the proud man constantly constructs an *alibi*. Brief reunions following calculated absences reveal much; yet, frequent companionship, marked by shared tastes and admirable qualities on both sides, is far more potent—especially when one acts as a teacher and the other delights in their own receptivity, which in turn brings joy to the instructor. This scenario is legendary in history, possessing no less charm today than it held in the days of Abelard.

Yet, this comparison never crossed the minds of the Arrowpoints when they first invited Klesmer to Quetcham. Having a first-rate musician in one's household is a privilege of wealth; Catherine's talent demanded every advantage, and she specifically wanted to utilize her quiet country time for rigorous study. Klesmer was not yet a Liszt, understood to be adored by ladies across Europe (with the exception of Lapland); even with that reputation, it did not imply he would propose to an heiress. No honorable musician would dare. Still less could it be conceived that Catherine would provide him the slightest pretext for such boldness. The large check Mr. Arrowpoint intended to draw in Klesmer's name seemed to render him as safe an inmate as a footman. Where marriage is deemed inconceivable, a girl's sentiments are considered secure.

Klesmer was indeed a man of honor, but marriages rarely commence with formal proposals, and furthermore, Catherine's limits of the conceivable did not align perfectly with her mother's.

Outsiders might have judged Klesmer's position as dangerously precarious if Miss Arrowpoint had been an acknowledged beauty, failing to realize that the most powerful beauty is that which reveals itself only after sympathy, not before. There is a charm in the eyes and lips that accompanies every phrase demonstrating delicate perception or fine judgment, every modest word or smile indicating a heart awake to others; no gesture of clothing or movement is more satisfying than that which restores the confidence that one is in the presence of someone who notices nothing lost. What profound meaning accumulates in frowns and laughter never witnessed in the wrong places; what radiant adorableness fills a human frame where a mind flashes with comprehension and hands execute with finesse! The more obvious beauty, also adorable sometimes—one may say it without blasphemy—often begins as an apology for folly and ends, like other apologies, becoming tiresome through repetition. Therefore, Klesmer's passionate attachment to Miss Arrowpoint, though he was very susceptible to such beauty, was no more a paradox than any other triumph of manifold sympathy over monotonous attraction. We object less to being accused of enslaving excesses of passion than to our deficiencies in wider passions; but if the truth were known, our reputed intensity is often the dullness of not knowing what else to do with ourselves. Tannhäuser, one suspects, was a knight of ill-equipped imagination, hardly possessing broader discourse than a heavy Guardsman; Merlin had certainly seen his best days and was merely repeating himself when falling into that hopeless captivity; and we know Ulysses felt such a manifest ennui under similar circumstances that Calypso herself facilitated his departure. Indeed, there is a report that he later left Penelope; but since she was habitually absorbed in woolen work, and it was likely from her that Telemachus acquired his petty, litigious disposition, always anxious about property and daily meat consumption, no valid inference can be drawn from this dubious scandal regarding the relationship between companionship and constancy.

Klesmer was as versatile and fascinating as a young Ulysses upon sufficient acquaintance—one whom nature seemed to have created generously first, then endowed with music as a dominant power, utilizing all abundant rest. Like Mendelssohn, he found expression not only in the highest finish of execution but in a fervor of creative work and theoretical belief that pierced the entire future of a life with the delight of congruent devotion. His foibles of arrogance and vanity did not exceed those found in the best English families, and Catherine Arrowpoint possessed no corresponding restlessness to clash with his; notwithstanding her innate kindness, she was perhaps too coolly firm and self-sustaining. Yet, she belonged to those satisfactory creatures whose interaction holds the charm of discovery; whose integrity of faculty and expression inspires a desire to know what they will say on all subjects or how they will perform any undertaking, thereby ending in raising not only continuous expectation but a continuous sense of fulfillment—the systole and diastole of blissful companionship. In such cases, the outward presentation easily becomes what the image is to the worshipper. It was not long before the two realized each was interesting to the other, though the extent of this interest remained doubtful. Klesmer did not conceive that Miss Arrowpoint viewed him as a potential lover, and she was not accustomed to thinking of herself as likely to stir more than a friendly regard, nor to fear expressing more to any man who was not enamored of her fortune. Each was content to endure some unshared sense of denial for the sake of loving the other's society a little too well; under these conditions, there had been no need to restrict Klesmer's visits for the past year, whether in the country or the city. He knew very well that if Miss Arrowpoint had been poor, he would have made ardent love to her instead of sending storms through the piano or folding his arms to pour out hyperbolic tirades about impersonal topics like the North Pole. Nor was she less aware that if it had been possible for Klesmer to wish for her hand, she would have found overwhelming reasons to grant it. Here lay the security of full cups, which are as secure from overflowing as half-empty ones, provided no disturbance occurs. Naturally, silent feelings had not remained static, any more than the stealthily moving clock hand. During this current visit to Quetcham, Klesmer had begun to think he would not return, while Catherine was more sensitive to his frequent brusqueness, which she resented as a needless attempt to assert his superiority in every sense except the conventional.

Meanwhile entered the expectant peer, Mr. Bult, an esteemed party man who, though neutral in private life, held strong opinions regarding the districts of the Niger, was equally at home in Brazil, spoke decisively of affairs in the South Seas, studied his parliamentary and itinerant speeches, and possessed the general solidity and suffusing pinkness of a healthy Briton on the central plateau of life. Aware of a tacit understanding that he was an undeniable match for an heiress, Catherine had nothing against him save that he was thoroughly tiresome. Mr. Bult was amiably confident and had no notion that his insensitivity to counterpoint could ever be counted against him. He barely regarded Klesmer as a serious human being entitled to a vote, and he did not mind Miss Arrowpoint's addiction to music any more than her probable expenses in antique lace. Consequently, he was somewhat amazed by an after-dinner outburst from Klesmer regarding the lack of idealism in English politics, which reduced all mutual relations between distant races to the simple necessity of a market. To Klesmer, the Crusades had at least the excuse of a banner of sentiment around which generous feelings could rally; of course, the scoundrels rallied too, but what then

Like the division of the Promised Land among the heirs of Art, every claimant must fight hard to win his share; the promise itself arrives like a prophecy—a title held without possession. Carrying a map of an estate you do not yet own in your pocket is merely a precarious form of tenure. To imagine casting one's shoe over Eden offers no guarantee that one will ever set the sole of one's foot upon a single acre of it.

The most stubborn beliefs humans hold about themselves are those supported by no evidence other than a constant, spontaneous throbbing of self-satisfaction. It is akin to a hidden seed of madness: a confident delusion that one can move the world without understanding where one stands or what leverage one possesses.

"Please go to church, Mamma," Gwendolen said the following morning. "I prefer seeing Herr Klesmer alone." (He had replied to her note stating he would meet her at eleven.)

"That seems hardly proper, I think," Mrs. Davilow said, her voice tinged with anxiety.

"Our affairs are too serious for us to concern ourselves with such nonsensical rules," Gwendolen retorted, her tone dripping with contempt. "They are insulting as well as ridiculous."

"You wouldn't mind Isabel sitting with you? She could read quietly in a corner."

"No; she couldn't. She'd bite her nails and stare. It would be too irritating. Trust my judgment, Mamma; I must be alone. Take everyone else to church."

As usual, Gwendolen got her way. Only Miss Merry and two of the younger girls remained behind, staying inside to give the house the appearance of habitation by sitting at the dining-room windows.

It was a delicious Sunday morning. The melancholy, fading sunlight of autumn rested upon the half-mown grass and filtered gently through the windows in slanting bands of brightness. These beams illuminated the old furniture, the glass panel reflecting it, the tapestried chairs adorned with faded floral wreaths, the dark, enigmatic paintings, and the superannuated organ at which Gwendolen had delighted in playing Saint Cecilia upon her first joyful arrival. Through the open doors of the antechamber, a crowd of pallid, dusty knick-knacks was visible—the very spot where she had once achieved the role of Hermione in her Greek dress. This memory was vividly active in her mind now; surely Herr Klesmer had been struck by admiration for her pose and expression then? Whatever he had actually said, or whatever she imagined he had thought, was currently the object of her keenest scrutiny. Perhaps she had never in her life felt so inwardly dependent, so acutely aware of needing another person's opinion. A new fluttering stirred within her spirit, introducing an element of deliberation into her self-assessment that had previously been a blissful, intuitive gift. Yet, the recurring burden of her inner monologue remained: *Herr Klesmer had seen very little of me.* Any unfavorable conclusion he reached would necessarily rest on too narrow a foundation. She truly felt clever enough for anything.

To pass the time, she gathered her volumes and musical scores, placing them atop the piano, and set herself to classifying them. Catching the reflection of her movements in the glass panel, she was diverted to contemplating her own image. Dressed entirely in black, devoid of ornament, with the warm whiteness of her skin highlighted between her light-brown crown of hair and her square-cut bodice, she might have tempted an artist to attempt the Roman trick of a statue carved in black, white, and tawny marble. Watching her reflection advance slowly, she thought, *"I am beautiful"*—not with exultation, but with grave decision. Beauty, after all, was the condition upon which she most desperately craved external validation. If anyone objected to the turn of her nose or the shape of her neck and chin, she lacked the sense that she could immediately demonstrate her power of attainment in these branches of feminine perfection.

There was little time left to fill before the sound of wheels, the loud ringing of a bell, and the opening of doors assured her that she was not, by any accident, to be disappointed. This slight increase in her internal flutter served only to heighten her dread. Despite her self-confidence, she feared Klesmer as part of that unmanageable world independent of her wishes—something vitriolic that would not cease burning simply because one smiled or frowned at it. Poor creature! She was facing a higher crisis in her woman's fate than in her last experience with Grandcourt. Then, the question had been whether she should marry a particular man. Now, the deepest fold of her questioning was whether she needed to marry at all—whether she could not achieve substantial independence and gratified ambition without bondage.

Herr Klesmer made his most deferential bow in the wide doorway of the antechamber, displaying the deference of the finest gray kerseymere trousers and perfect gloves (for the "masters of those who know" are, happily, altogether human). Gwendolen met him with unusual gravity, extending her hand. "It is most kind of you to come, Herr Klesmer," she said. "I hope you have not thought me presumptuous."

"I took your wish as a command that did me honor," Klesmer replied with matching gravity. He was indeed setting aside his own affairs to give his utmost attention to whatever Gwendolen had to say; yet his temperament was still in a state of excitation from the events of the previous day, likely lending his expressions a sharper, more biting edge than usual.

For once, Gwendolen was under such a strain of feeling that she forgot formalities. Standing near the piano, she waited, and Klesmer took his position near the opposite end, his back to the light and his terribly omniscient eyes fixed upon her. No affectation would serve, and she began without delay.

"I wish to consult you, Herr Klesmer. We have lost all our fortune; we have nothing. I must earn my own bread, and I desire to provide for my mother so as to spare her any hardship. The only way I can think of—and I should like it better than anything else—is to become an actress—to go on the stage. But, of course, I would aspire to a high position. Therefore, I thought—if you believed I could"—here Gwendolen grew slightly nervous—"it would be preferable for me to be a singer. I wish to study singing as well."

Klesmer placed his hat upon the piano and folded his arms as if concentrating intensely.

"I know," Gwendolen resumed, flushing from pale to pink and back again—"I know that my method of singing is very defective; but I have been ill-taught. I could be better taught; I could study. And you will understand my wish: to sing and act, like Grisi, represents a much higher station. Naturally, I wish to attain the highest rank possible. I can rely on your judgment. I am sure you will tell me the truth."

Gwendolen possessed a strange conviction that now, in making this serious appeal, the truth would prove favorable.

Yet Klesmer remained silent. He quickly removed his gloves, tossed them into his hat, rested his hands on his hips, and walked to the other end of the room. He was filled with compassion for this girl and wanted to guard his speech carefully. When he turned back, he looked at her with a mild, inquiring frown and spoke with gentle but rapid utterance: "You have never seen anything, I think, of artists and their lives?—I mean musicians, actors, artists of that kind?"

"Oh, no," Gwendolen replied, unperturbed by a reference to this obvious fact in the history of a young lady who had hitherto been well-provided for.

"You are—in pardon me," Klesmer said again, pausing near the piano—"coming to a conclusion on such a matter as this without considering everything. Are you perhaps twenty?"

"I am twenty-one," Gwendolen said, a slight fear rising within her. "Do you think I am too old?"

Klesmer pursed his lower lip and shook his long fingers upward in a manner totally enigmatic.

"Many persons begin later than others," Gwendolen remarked, betrayed by her habitual consciousness of possessing valuable information to bestow.

Klesmer paid her no heed, instead speaking with even greater studied gentleness: "You have probably not considered an artistic career until now. You did not entertain the notion, the longing—what shall I say?—you did not wish to be an actress, or anything of that sort, until this present trouble arose?"

"Not exactly: but I was fond of acting. I have acted; you saw me, if you remember—you saw me here in charades, and as Hermione," Gwendolen said, genuinely fearing that Klesmer had forgotten.

"Yes, yes," he answered quickly, "I remember—I remember perfectly," and once more walked to the other end of the room. It was difficult for him to refrain from this peculiar movement whenever he was engaged in any argument, audible or silent.

Gwendolen felt that she was being weighed. The delay was unpleasant, but she did not yet conceive that the scales could tip against her. It seemed only graceful to say, "I shall be very much obliged to you for taking the trouble to give me your advice, whatever it may be."

"Miss Harleth," Klesmer said, turning toward her and speaking with a slight increase of accent, "I will veil nothing from you in this matter. I should reckon myself guilty if I put a false visage on things—making them appear too black or

"I question things but do not find One that will answer to my mind: And all the world appears unkind." — WORDSWORTH.

Gwendolen breathed a sigh of relief that her audience with Klesmer was concluded before she faced her uncle and aunt. She had resolved that whatever lay ahead consisted solely of unpleasantness, and she felt a grim determination to remain impassively calm in the face of any humiliation they might propose.

The confrontation did not occur until Monday. Gwentolen accompanied her mother to the rectory. On their way, they had stopped at Sawyer's Cottage, inspecting every inch of the cramped quarters bathed in the harsh, unfiltered light of noon. Blinds and curtains were nonexistent; the furnishings intended to soften the space had not yet arrived, gathered piecemeal from the rectory's stores.

"How *shall* you endure it, mamma?" Gwendolen asked as they walked back, her voice tight. During the tour of the bare walls, the scrubbed floors, the tiny garden choked with cabbage stalks, and the yew arbor thick with dust and cobwebs, she had kept her mouth shut. Now, the reality settled on her. "You and the four girls squeezed into that closet of a room, staring at green and yellow wallpaper that presses right against your eyes? And without me?"

"It will be some comfort to you, dear, that you do not have to share it."

"If it weren't for the need of money, I'd sooner be there than go to serve as a governess."

"Do not set your heart against it beforehand, Gwendolen. If you secure a post at the palace, every luxury will surround you. You know how deeply you have always cherished that. You won't find it nearly as difficult as climbing those steep, narrow stairs, listening to crockery clatter through the house, and overhearing the girls' conversations."

"It feels like a nightmare," Gwendolen exclaimed impulsively. "I cannot believe Uncle will allow you to go to such a place. He should have taken other measures."

"Don't be unreasonable, dear child. What else could he have done?"

"That was for him to figure out. It seems to me a remarkably strange world if people in our position must sink so abruptly," Gwendolen remarked, her mental landscape shifting instantly to accommodate only futures that suited her own desires.

It was her temperament that shaped her words under this crushing weight of adversity. She might have spoken more wisely regarding the hardships of others, though expressing virtue was never her ambition; she aimed for cleverness. This distinction serves to excuse her words, which were often sharper than her intentions warranted.

Despite the acute pain of her own situation, Gwendolen felt a pang of guilt when her uncle and aunt greeted her with a warmth they had rarely shown before. She was struck by the dignified cheerfulness with which they discussed the necessary cuts in their lifestyle and the education of the boys. Mr. Gascoigne's inherent worth, slightly clouded by worldly distractions—much as a woman's poetic beauty is obscured by the demands of fashion—shone brightly under this sudden reduction of fortune. Prompt and methodical, he had not only ordered the carriage withdrawn but also planned to mend his worn suits, skip meat at breakfast, abandon periodicals, remove Edwy from school to tutor the younger boys himself, and organize the entire household on the starkest possible budget. For healthy minds, economy holds its own pleasures; the rector's spirit had permeated the home. Mrs. Gascoigne and Anna, who always modeled themselves after their father, genuinely missed nothing they truly valued. In sincere sorrow, they agreed that the most tragic aspect of their losses was the fate awaiting Mrs. Davilow and her children.

For the first time, Anna could merge her resentment over Rex into her sympathy for Gwendolen. Mrs. Gascoigne hoped that trouble might prove beneficial for her niece, carefully avoiding any additional bitterness that might compound the lesson. Both women had been busy planning how to procure blinds and curtains from the household stores, yet with delicate tact, they pushed these practicalities aside to discuss Gwendolen's upcoming journey and the comfort her return brought to her mother.

There was nothing for Gwendolen to justify extending her discontent toward those immediately around her. Instead, she felt shaken into a state of hyper-alertness, as if summoned to a drill everyone else was obediently attending, when her uncle began, in a voice of firm kindness, to outline the efforts he had made to secure her a suitable position. Mr. Gascoigne had not forgotten Grandcourt, but the vagueness of any potential advances from that quarter was insufficient to distract a man of his sound judgment from acting decisively for his niece's present needs.

"I felt there was no time to lose, Gwendolen," he said. "A position in a respectable family, offering proper consideration, does not appear at a moment's notice. However long we wait, we could hardly find one where you would be better situated than at Bishop Mompert's. I am known to both him and Mrs. Mompert, which is an asset for you. Our correspondence has been favorable, but I am not surprised that Mrs. Mompert wishes to meet you before finalizing an agreement. She intends to arrange for you to meet her at Wanchester on her way to London. I daresay you will find the interview somewhat trying, my dear; but you will have time to prepare your mind."

"Do you know *why* she wants to see me, uncle?" Gwendolen asked, her mind rapidly cataloging the various, disagreeable reasons a hypothetical Mrs. Mompert with three daughters might possess for interviewing a prospective governess.

The rector smiled gently. "Do not be alarmed, my dear. She simply wishes to form a clearer impression of you than my report allows. A mother is naturally meticulous about choosing a companion for her daughters. I have mentioned that you are very young. However, she personally supervises her daughters' education closely, which makes her less concerned about age. She is a woman of taste and strict principle, and she objects to having a French person in the house. I am certain she will deem your manners and accomplishments adequate; furthermore, regarding the religious and moral tone of your education, she—and indeed the Bishop himself—will preside."

Gwendolen dared not reply. The suppression of her profound dislike for the entire prospect sent a deep flush racing over her face and neck, fading as quickly as it arrived. Anna, filled with tender fear, slipped her small hand into her cousin's. Mr. Gascoigne, a kindly man, could not fail to perceive the trial this sudden reversal posed for a girl like Gwendolen. Determined to offer a cheerful perspective, he continued in an easy, conversational tone, as if addressing no objections:

"I hold this position in such high regard that I would have been tempted to try to secure it for Anna, had she been likely to meet Mrs. Mompert's requirements. It is truly a home, offering a continuation of education in the highest sense; 'governess' is a misnomer. The Bishop's views lean decidedly Low Church compared to mine—he is a close friend of Lord Grampian's. Yet, though privately strict, he is not narrow in public matters. Indeed, he has aroused as little dislike in his diocese as any bishop currently on the bench. He has always remained friendly to me, although prior to his promotion, when he served as an incumbent in this diocese, we had a minor disagreement concerning the Bible Society."

The rector's words were laden with meanings that satisfied his own conscience, leading him to assume similar effects would resonate in his niece's mind. "Continuation of education"—"Bishop's views"—"Privately strict"—"Bible Society"—to Gwendolen, already recoiling from the opening prospect, these phrases felt like the rising heat of a burning glass, not the comforting links of logical persuasion they formed for her good uncle. Desperate, she began searching for an alternative.

"There was another situation, I think, Mamma mentioned?" she said, forcing a display of self-control.

"Yes," the rector replied in a slightly apologetic tone. "But that is in a school. I would derive less satisfaction from you taking that role. It would involve much harder work, as you are aware, and offer fewer benefits in other respects. Moreover, you do not have an equal chance of securing it."

"Oh, dear no," interjected Mrs. Gascoigne. "It would be much harder for you, my dear—and much less appropriate. You might not even have a private bedroom." Memories of school flooded Gwendolen's mind, suggesting other indignities that forced her to admit this alternative offered no relief. Turning back to her uncle, she feigned acceptance.

"When is Mrs. Mompert likely to send for me?"

"That is somewhat uncertain, but she has promised not to entertain any other proposals until she has seen you. She has entered deeply into your plight. It will likely be within the next fortnight. But I must depart now; I am going to lease part of my glebe property."

The rector concluded with cheerful assurance, leaving the room convinced that Gwendolen would adapt to her circumstances with the sense of a sensible girl. Having spoken appropriately, he naturally assumed the results would follow suit; accustomed, as a head of household and parish authority, to "speaking to" refractory individuals with the expectation of moral correction, he believed his words carried sufficient weight.

"What a stay Henry is to us all!" Mrs. Gascoigne remarked once her husband had left.

"He indeed is," Mrs. Davilow responded warmly. "I think cheerfulness is a fortune in

To map the contours of a mind stripped bare by a fastidious egoism—one where every direct desire has dulled, shrinking from motive into a wavering hope of motivation—is a difficult task. It is a psyche composed entirely of shifting moods, where erratic impulses sprout like weeds among the general decay. Such a state often befalls a life lived too freely, untouched by the shaping pressure of duty. *Nam deteriores omnes sumus licentiæ*; or, in plainer terms, "Do as you please" is a terrible signpost. Rulers announce their wills and shift markets with minimal speech. Similarly, when Grandcourt learned Gwendolen had fled Leubronn, he casually dismissed that fashionable retreat as a "beastly hole," worse than Baden. To Mr. Lush, this remark was a definitive signal: his patron intended immediately to return to Diplow.

Of course, action rarely matches intent instantly. Grandcourt indeed loitered the following day, offering no clear orders. Perhaps he sensed Lush's anticipation. He delayed over his grooming, descending finally with a faded aura of perfect distinction that made fresh complexions and flushed hands appear crude and vulgar. He drifted through the terraces, gambling rooms, and reading areas, cultivating an indifference to everyone and everything. Yet, when encountering Lady Mallinger, he exerted effort—raising his hat, pausing, and feigning interest in her suggestion regarding the local waters. "Yes," he replied, "I heard someone say how providential it was that springs always exist near gambling houses."

"Oh, that was a joke," said the innocent Lady Mallinger, misled by his languid gravity, "a parody of the old tale about towns and rivers."

"Ah, perhaps," Grandcourt murmured, his face unchanged.

Lady Mallinger deemed this worthy of reporting to Sir Hugo, who chuckled. "Oh, my dear, he isn't a fool. Do not imagine he cannot see a jest. He plays his cards as well as most of us."

"He has never struck me as particularly sensible," Lady Mallinger offered, defending herself. She harbored a secret aversion to Grandcourt; to her, he was merely a living monument to her own failure as a wife—the fact that she had failed to produce a son for Sir Hugo. She constantly wondered if her husband regretted his choice, fearing that if he weren't exceptionally kind, he might treat her roughly, as men naturally dislike being disappointed.

Deronda, too, received a nod from Grandcourt, one he did not appreciate yet returned with flawless politeness. No amount of philosophical debate could erase the deep-seated belief that his birth had been marred by his father's error. Since Grandcourt's fortune might otherwise have belonged to him, Deronda was fiercely resolved not to display any irritation born of that grievance. He recognized the easy slide into petty, irrational resentment that others felt when thwarted; determined not to descend into that ugly abyss, he clung to kinder affections within himself. Pride aided him—a pride in refusing to acknowledge disadvantages that vulgar minds exaggerate, such as the shabby trappings of poverty. He would not allow a man like Grandcourt to suspect he was envied.

However, one cannot guard against interpretation. Grandcourt believed Deronda, the unfortunate cousin by the father's side, inwardly winced at their shared predicament. Therefore, Deronda's presence became more tolerable to him. The imagined envy of others—that they feel their own lack—is the standard retinue of egoism; and Grandcourt's pet dogs were not the only creatures he delighted in making jealous. Consequently, he was civil enough to converse with Deronda on the terrace about the hunt at Diplow, even suggesting, "You'd better come over for a run or two when the season starts."

Lush, pleased by the delay, amused himself gossiping with Sir Hugo and answering inquiries about Grandcourt's affairs, specifically regarding his willingness to sell his interest in Diplow. On a bright autumn day, there was nothing more pleasant than discussing a tyrannical patron behind his back. Sir Hugo eagerly listened to these "traits of manners," though he kept such whispers strictly from ears that might take them too seriously. Whatever secrets he knew of his nephew, he never shared them with Deronda, who viewed Grandcourt as a pale-blooded mortal and preferred not to learn how the red corpuscles had been washed from him.

Lush's policy was to please everyone when he had no reason not to. The baronet always treated him well, regarding him as one of those versatile figures who, while not quite gentlemen themselves, serve the company of gentlemen with comfortable utility—like second-best garments worn without anxiety.

"Well, keep me posted," Sir Hugo said. "If this marriage looks likely, or if money becomes tight, let me know. My plan would suit him better than burdening Ryelands."

"That's true," Lush agreed. "But it mustn't be forced upon him. Just place it in his path so the scent tickles him. Grandcourt isn't a man to be led solely by self-interest, especially if he senses you're doing it for your own gain. I'm devoted to him, of course. I've sacrificed everything for fifteen years to stay by his side. He wouldn't easily replace me. He's a peculiar character, Henleigh Grandcourt, and he's grown on me lately. Still, I'm steady. I've been his guardian since he was twenty. He was fascinating then, and could be now if he chose. I'm attached to him; it would be far worse for him if I weren't at his elbow."

Sir Hugo offered no explicit sympathy, nor did Lush expect his confession of motives to be analyzed precisely. How can a man avoid becoming the subject of his own conversation? One must dress oneself decently in words as in cloth. His listener, a Member of Parliament, allowed for the necessities of verbal attire, and the dialogue proceeded without friction.

Yet, Lush's hope for indefinite postponement ended abruptly the next morning. Grandcourt greeted him with, "Are you making all the arrangements for our departure via the Paris train?"

"I didn't realize you planned to leave," Lush said, unsurprised.

"You should have known," Grandcourt snapped, staring at the burnt stub of his cigar, speaking in that low, disgusted tone reserved for peremptory commands. "See to everything. Ensure no brute shares our carriage. Leave my P.P.C. at the Mallingers'.

They arrived in Paris the following day. Lush was gratified when instructed to proceed directly to Diplow to oversee preparations, leaving Grandcourt and the valet behind. Days passed before Lush received the telegram summoning the carriage to Wanchester station. During this interval, he worked diligently, executing orders regarding the stud and household while gathering intelligence on Gwendolen and the situation at Offendene. He calculated the probable impact of the family's misfortune on Grandcourt's stubborn nature. If Gwendolen's poverty removed the fear of rejection, it might prompt Grandcourt to act despite his reservations; conversely, the certainty of acceptance might render him as fickle as a moth.

Having observed his patron closely for years, Lush knew Grandcourt better than anyone, yet knowing him meant doubting his actions in any specific instance. Could he suddenly display magnanimity, like a tragic hero awakening to virtue only to relapse? Would he decline the heiress to marry the penniless girl? Lush held general certainties—for instance, that Grandcourt possessed no capacity for genuine generosity—but these were useless when trying to predict the internal stimuli driving him along a network of possibilities. Lush was uncertain about the outcome, yet desperately desired the confidence that they would *never* marry. He would have welcomed a union with an heiress or even Mrs. Glasher; the latter would ensure gratitude, a debt Lush felt entitled to collect. He did not mind social exclusion for the bride, nor did he object to no marriage at all. But he felt justified in obstructing a match that promised only trouble for her husband and potential ruin for his old companion, whom he wished to protect. He sought what he perceived as good, unaware he was wishing ill upon the inconvenient Gwendolen.

Accustomed to being seen as agreeable, Lush was not prepared to be insulted by more than one person. Armed with this fragile strategy, he awaited Grandcourt's arrival, wondering how the campaign would commence.

Grandcourt spent the first day with the stables, ordering a groom to saddle Criterion and demonstrate its gait. This signaled his purpose. Lush reconsidered whether to volunteer information about Gwendolen's dire circumstances while still confident no compromise had occurred. The next morning, he resolved to drop the news if Grandcourt seemed in a conversational mood. But Grandcourt offered no talk. Even asked about his own comfort, he maintained an unwinking silence. After reading his letters, he issued various orders, then gestured for Lush to leave. Before exiting, Grandcourt turned slightly and emitted a broken, languid "Oh."

"What is it?" Lush asked, noting he did not carry his dusty pudding with excessive reverence.

"Shut the door. I can't speak in the corridor."

Lush complied, sat down, and waited. Finally, Grandcourt asked, "Is Miss Harleth at Offendene?" He assumed Lush had investigated and took pleasure in believing Lush did not want *him* to ask.

"Well, I hardly know," Lush replied carelessly. "The family is completely ruined. Both the Harleths and the Gascoignes have lost everything due to some rascally banking scheme. The mother has not a sou; they've huddled into a laborer's cottage

He brings white asses laden with the freight of Tyrian vessels, purple, gold, and balm, to bribe my will: I'll bid them chase him forth, nor let him breathe the taint of his surmise on my secure resolve. Ay, 'tis secure: and therefore let him come to spread his freight. For firmness hath its appetite and craves the stronger lure, more strongly to resist; would know the touch of gold to fling it off; scent wine to feel its lip the soberer; behold soft byssus, ivory, and plumes to say, "They're fair, but I will none of them," and flout enticement in the very face.

Mr. Gascoigne one day came to Offendene with what he felt to be the satisfactory news that Mrs. Mompert had fixed Tuesday in the following week for her interview with Gwendolen at Wanchester. He said nothing of his having incidentally heard that Mr. Grandcourt had returned to Diplow; knowing no more than she did that Leubronn had been the goal of her admirer's journeying, and feeling that it would be unkind uselessly to revive the memory of a brilliant prospect under the present reverses. In his secret soul he thought of his niece's unintelligible caprice with regret, but he vindicated her to himself by considering that Grandcourt had been the first to behave oddly, in suddenly walking away when there had been the best opportunity for crowning his marked attentions. The rector's practical judgment told him that his chief duty to his niece now was to encourage her resolutely to face the change in her lot, since there was no manifest promise of any event that would avert it.

"You will find an interest in varied experience, my dear, and I have no doubt you will be a more valuable woman for having sustained such a part as you are called to."

"I cannot pretend to believe that I shall like it," said Gwendolen, for the first time showing her uncle some petulance. "But I am quite aware that I am obliged to bear it." She remembered having submitted to his admonition on a different occasion when she was expected to like a very different prospect.

"And your good sense will teach you to behave suitably under it," said Mr. Gascoigne, with a shade more gravity. "I feel sure that Mrs. Mompert will be pleased with you. You will know how to conduct yourself to a woman who holds in all senses the relation of a superior to you. This trouble has come on you young, but that makes it in some respects easier, and there is a benefit in all chastisement if we adjust our minds to it."

This was precisely what Gwendolen was unable to do; and after her uncle was gone, the bitter tears, which had rarely come during the late trouble, rose and fell slowly as she sat alone. Her heart denied that the trouble was easier because she was young. When was she to have any happiness, if it did not come while she was young? Not that her visions of possible happiness for herself were as unmixed with necessary evil as they used to be—not that she could still imagine herself plucking the fruits of life without suspicion of their core.
