But this general disenchantment with the world—nay, with herself, since it appeared that she was not made for easy pre-eminence—only intensified her sense of forlornness; it was a visibly sterile distance enclosing the dreary path at her feet, in which she had no courage to tread. She was in that first crisis of passionate youthful rebellion against what is not fitly called pain, but rather the absence of joy—that first rage of disappointment in life's morning, which we whom the years have subdued are apt to remember but dimly as part of our own experience, and so to be intolerant of its self-enclosed unreasonableness and impiety. What passion seems more absurd, when we have got outside it and looked at calamity as a collective risk, than this amazed anguish that *I* and not *Thou*, *He* or *She*, should be just the smitten one? Yet perhaps some who have afterward made themselves a willing fence before the breast of another, and have carried their own heart-wound in heroic silence—some who have made their deeds great, nevertheless began with this angry amazement at their own smart, and on the mere denial of their fantastic desires raged as if under the sting of wasps which reduced the universe for them to an unjust infliction of pain. This was nearly poor Gwendolen's condition. What though such a reverse as hers had often happened to other girls? The one point she had been all her life learning to care for was that it had happened to *her*: it was what *she* felt under Klesmer's demonstration that she was not remarkable enough to command fortune by force of will and merit; it was what *she* would feel under the rigors of Mrs. Mompert's constant expectation, under the dull demand that she should be cheerful with three Miss Momperts, under the necessity of showing herself entirely submissive, and keeping her thoughts to herself. To be a queen disthroned is not so hard as some other down-stepping: imagine one who had been made to believe in his own divinity finding all homage withdrawn, and himself unable to perform a miracle that would recall the homage and restore his own confidence. Something akin to this illusion and this helplessness had befallen the poor spoiled child, with the lovely lips and eyes and the majestic figure—which seemed now to have no magic in them.

She rose from the low ottoman where she had been sitting purposeless, and walked up and down the drawing-room, resting her elbow on one palm while she leaned down her cheek on the other, and a slow tear fell. She thought, "I have always, ever since I was little, felt that mamma was not a happy woman; and now I dare say I shall be more unhappy than she has been." Her mind dwelt for a few moments on the picture of herself losing her youth and ceasing to enjoy—not minding whether she did this or that: but such picturing inevitably brought back the image of her mother. "Poor mamma! it will be still worse for her now. I can get a little money for her—that is all I shall care about now." And then with an entirely new movement of her imagination, she saw her mother getting quite old and white, and herself no longer young but faded, and their two faces meeting still with memory and love, and she knowing what was in her mother's mind—"Poor Gwen too is sad and faded now"—and then, for the first time, she sobbed, not in anger, but with a sort of tender misery.

Her face was toward the door, and she saw her mother enter. She barely saw that; for her eyes were large with tears, and she pressed her handkerchief against them hurriedly. Before she took it away she felt her mother's arms round her, and this sensation, which seemed a prolongation of her inward vision, overcame her will to be reticent; she sobbed anew in spite of herself, as they pressed their cheeks together. Mrs. Davilow had brought something in her hand which had already caused her an agitating anxiety, and she dared not speak until her darling had become calmer. But Gwendolen, with whom weeping had always been a painful manifestation to be resisted, if possible, again pressed her handkerchief against her eyes, and, with a deep breath, drew her head backward and looked at her mother, who was pale and tremulous.

"It was nothing, mamma," said Gwendolen, thinking that her mother had been moved in this way simply by finding her in distress. "It is all over now."

But Mrs. Davilow had withdrawn her arms, and Gwendolen perceived a letter in her hand.

"What is that letter?—worse news still?" she asked, with a touch of bitterness.

"I don't know what you will think it, dear," said Mrs. Davilow, keeping the letter in her hand. "You will hardly guess where it comes from."

"Don't ask me to guess anything," said Gwendolen, rather impatiently, as if a bruise were being pressed. "It is addressed to you, dear." Gwendolen gave the slightest perceptible toss of the head.

"It comes from Diplow," said Mrs. Davilow, giving her the letter. She knew Grandcourt's indistinct handwriting, and her mother was not surprised to see her blush deeply; but watching her as she read, and wondering much what was the purport of the letter, she saw the color die out. Gwendolen's lips even were pale as she turned the open note toward her mother. The words were few and formal: *Mr. Grandcourt presents his compliments to Miss Harleth, and begs to know whether he may be permitted to call at Offendene to-morrow after two and to see her alone. Mr. Grandcourt has just returned from Leubronn, where he had hoped to find Miss Harleth.*

Mrs. Davilow read, and then looked at her daughter inquiringly, leaving the note in her hand. Gwendolen let it fall to the floor, and turned away.

"It must be answered, darling," said Mrs. Davilow, timidly. "The man waits."

Gwendolen sank on the settee, clasped her hands, and looked straight before her, not at her mother. She had the expression of one who had been startled by a sound and was listening to know what would come of it. The sudden change of the situation was bewildering. A few

Desire sets the sails, and circumstance merely provides the wind to catch them. While Grandcourt rode his magnificent black stallion, Yarico, with his groom trailing behind on Criterion, enjoying the pleasant journey from Diplow to Offendene, Gwendolen sat before her mirror. Her mother was busy gathering the long, light-brown tresses she had been meticulously brushing.

"Only gather it up easily and make a coil, mamma," Gwendolen instructed.

"Gwen, let me bring you some earrings," Mrs. Davilow offered, as they both gazed into the glass.

It was impossible for either of them not to notice that Gwendolen's eyes shone with a brightness absent lately, and that a shadow seemed lifted from her face, restoring its lines to a placid youthfulness. The mother drew an encouraging inference, her voice brightening.

"Do you want your earrings?"

"No, mamma. I won't wear any ornaments. I'll put on my black silk. Black is the only attire suitable when one intends to refuse an offer," Gwendolen replied, offering one of her characteristic smiles before rising to shed her dressing gown.

"Suppose the offer isn't made after all?" Mrs. Davilow ventured, with a sly glint in her eye.

"Then that will simply be because I refused it beforehand," Gwendolen countered proudly. "It amounts to the same thing."

There was a small, defiant toss of her head as she spoke. When she descended the stairs in her long black robes, she possessed that firm poise of head and elastic grace that had recently been missing, like vitality returning to a parched plant. Her mother watched her, thinking, *She is quite herself again. It must be the joy of his coming. Could her mind truly be made up against him?*

Gwendolen would have been furious if that thought had been spoken aloud, especially since, over the last twenty hours with scant sleep, she had been torn between alternating images and arguments regarding her potential marriage to Grandcourt. The resolution she had formed earlier had slipped from her grasp; the constant ebb and flow of counterbalancing thoughts, born of conflicting desires, had left her in a state where no conclusion appeared fixed. She intended to refuse Grandcourt. From the moment she received his letter, she had decided to reject him. Yet, this very resolve forced her to confront the unwelcome realities head-on, stripping away some of her awe. Her imagination, fueled by uncertainty, filled in gaps in her knowledge, reshaping what she knew into various possibilities. By staring at a doubtful object with a constructive mind, one can conjure twenty different forms.

Her vague hesitations from the previous day at the Whispering Stones now counted for nothing, submerged beneath a final, overwhelming repulsion. *If it hadn't been for that day at Cardell Chase,* she told herself, *there would be no barrier to marrying Grandcourt.* On that day and those following, she had not reasoned or weighed options; she had acted on an impulsive force against which all questioning was drowned out like a whisper against a torrent. That impulse stemmed not only from maidenly pride and jealousy, nor solely from the shock of witnessing another woman's misery, but from a profound dread of wrongdoing. Though vague and distant from her daily life, this fear was potent. Whatever aligned with being a "lady" posed no scruple for her, but from the dim realm of the disgraceful, the wrong, the guilty, she recoiled with a mixture of pride and terror. Apart from shame alone, her instinct would have placed any deliberate harm inflicted on another squarely within the category of guilt.

But now—did she truly know the extent of the situation concerning Mrs. Glasher and her children? She had given a sort of promise, saying, *"I will not interfere with your wishes."* Would another woman marrying Grandcourt actually be the decisive obstacle to those wishes, or cause real injury to the mother and son? Might it not be just as well, or even better, for Grandcourt to marry? After all, what couldn't a woman achieve once married, if she knew how to assert herself? Here, however, lay purely constructive imagination. Gwendolen possessed as accurate a concept of marriage—the mutual influences, demands, and duties of man and woman—as she did of magnetic currents or storm laws.

*"Mamma managed badly,"* was her summary of her mother's experience; she assured herself she would manage quite differently. The trials of matrimony were the last topic Mrs. Davilow wished to explore deeply with this daughter.

*I wonder what mamma and my uncle would say if they knew about Mrs. Glasher!* Gwendolen mused internally, debating silently. Not that she imagined revealing it, even if she weren't bound by silence. *I wonder what anyone would say; or what they'd say to Mr. Grandcourt marrying someone else and fathering other children!* Considering what "anyone" would think liberated her from the difficulty of judging where everything remained obscure to her when emotion had ceased to be decisive. She merely collected her memories, which convinced her that society viewed illegitimate children as more deserving of shunning and deprivation of advantage than illegitimate fathers. The verdict of "the public" seemed to absolve her of significant concern for Mrs. Glasher and her offspring.

Yet, there was another dimension to their impact on her. Public opinion could not erase a feeling best described initially as indignation and loathing—to be expected to unite herself with an exhausted life, heavy with backward secrets that must have been acutely felt by the woman involved, yet alien to her own existence. True, the question of love on her part had played almost no role in her assessment of Grandcourt. The desirability of marriage had always seemed rooted in feelings other than love; to be enamored was the province of the man initiating the advances. Gwendolen found no fault with Grandcourt's manner of being enamored until she glimpsed his past, which she resented as a deliberate affront. His advances to *her* had been calculated, and she felt a retrospective disgust toward them. Perhaps other men's lives were similarly constructed—filled with secrets that reduced the innocent assumptions of the women they sought to marry to a farce, performed while they laughed in their sleeves.

These feelings of disgust and indignation had sunk deep. Although subsequent troubling experiences in recent weeks had dulled them from passionate fire to mere memory, it was chiefly their lingering resonance that kept her steadfast in her understanding: she would not accept Grandcourt. She had never intended to form a new determination; she had only been contemplating what might be thought or said. If anything could have swayed her, it would have been the prospect of making life easy for "poor mamma"—a genuine temptation. But no! She was going to refuse him.

Meanwhile, the anticipation of refusing him invigorated her. She held the white reins once more; a fresh current flowed through her frame, reviving her from the defeated consciousness left by her interview with Klesmer. She was no longer going to seek validation of her capabilities; she was going to exercise her power. Was this what caused her heart to beat anxiously when she heard the horse's hooves on the gravel? When Miss Merry opened the door to announce that Grandcourt was in the drawing-room?

The hours of preparation and the triumph of the situation seemed futile; she might as well have encountered Grandcourt suddenly amidst her despair. Walking into the drawing-room required concentrating every ounce of energy into self-control, allowing her to appear gravely gracious. She extended her hand to him and answered his inquiry about her health in a voice as low and languid as his own.

A moment later, seated on two wreath-painted chairs—Gwendolen upright with lowered eyelids, Grandcourt sitting two yards away, leaning an arm over the back of his chair and studying her while holding his hat in his left hand—any observer would have concluded they were in a suspended moment of romantic tension. Indeed, the courting had begun. She already felt herself being wooed by this silent man seated at an agreeable distance, surrounded by a subtle atmosphere reminiscent of attar of roses, with an attention bent entirely on her. He, too, considered himself to be wooing. He was not a man to believe his presence carried no weight, and he was precisely the type to derive the utmost spice from a girl he had not yet deemed fully calculable.

"I was disappointed not to find you at Leubronn," he began, his usual broken drawl tinged with a shade of amorous laziness. "The place was intolerable without you. A mere kennel of a location. Don't you agree?"

"I cannot judge what it would be without myself," Gwendolen replied, turning her eyes onto him with a recovering sense of mischief. "*With* myself, I like it well enough to have stayed longer, if I could. But I was obliged to return home due to family troubles."

"It was very cruel of you to go to Leubronn," Grandcourt remarked, ignoring the troubles—a detail Gwendolen, for reasons she barely understood, wished to clarify immediately. "You must have known it would ruin everything. You knew you were the heart and soul of everything that transpired. Are you quite reckless about me?"

It would be impossible to say "yes" in a tone taken seriously; equally impossible to say "no." What else could she say? In her difficulty, she lowered her eyelids again and blushed across her face and neck. Grandcourt observed her in a new phase, interpreting it as a display of inclination. Determined to have her show it more decisively, he pressed on.

"Perhaps there is some deeper interest? Some attraction—an

"It is easier to know man in general than to know a particular man." — La Rochefoucauld.

An hour after Grandcourt departed, the significant news of Gwendolen's engagement rippled through the rectory. Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne, accompanied by Anna, spent the evening at Offendene.

"My dear, allow me to congratulate you on securing such a profound attachment," the rector remarked warmly. "You appear quite grave, and I find no surprise in that; a lifelong union is a solemn undertaking. Yet, judging by Mr. Grandcourt's recent conduct and words, I believe we may already discern some silver lining emerging from our adversity. It has afforded you a rare opportunity to observe your future husband's delicate generosity."

Mr. Gascoigne subtly alluded to Grandcourt's manner of hinting that he would provide for Mrs. Davilow—a detail Gwendolen had recalled citing to her mother with precise accuracy during their earlier conversations.

"But I have no doubt that Mr. Grandcourt would have behaved with equal magnanimity had you not fled to Germany," Mrs. Gascoigne interjected, feeling compelled to fulfill her social duty on this occasion. "Had you remained engaged to him, as you surely might have been months ago, his conduct would undoubtedly have been impeccable. Now, however, there is no room for caprice; indeed, I trust you harbor no inclinations toward it. A woman owes a great debt of gratitude to a man who persists in offering himself despite obstacles. No doubt you feel this properly?"

"I am not entirely certain that I do, Aunt," Gwendolen replied with a saucy gravity. "I do not know precisely what feelings are deemed 'proper' when one becomes engaged."

The rector patted her shoulder, smiling as if at a piece of innocent mischief, while his wife interpreted his demeanor as a signal that she need not be displeased. As for Anna, she kissed Gwendolen and whispered, "I truly hope you will be happy," before sinking into the background, struggling vainly to hold back tears. In recent days, she had nurtured a small romance regarding Rex—imagining that if he still longed for Gwendolen, her heart might eventually soften under the weight of sorrow until they could marry. That fantasy had transformed into a prayer that she, Anna, might rejoice as a faithful sister, finding purpose only in serving Gwendolen as long as Rex lacked wealth. But now, she sought grace to celebrate something else entirely.

Miss Merry and the four young girls—Alice with her high shoulders, Bertha and Fanny the whisperers, and Isabel the attentive listener—were all present for this family occasion. Everything seemed to pivot appropriately around the honor and glory of Gwendolen, rendering real life as captivating as *Sir Charles Grandison*. The evening unfolded primarily through the rector's decisive remarks, responding to conjectures from the two elder ladies. According to him, the situation was not one where mentioning financial settlements was his duty; everything must, and doubtless would safely, be left to Mr. Grandcourt.

"I should like to know exactly what sort of places Ryelands and Gadsmere are," Mrs. Davilow asked curiously.

"Gadsmere, I believe, is a secondary residence," Mr. Gascoigne noted. "However, Ryelands is known to be one of our finest seats. The park is extensive, the woods of considerable value. The house was constructed by Inigo Jones, and the ceilings are painted in the Italian style. The estate is reportedly worth twelve thousand a year, and there are two livings, including a rectory, in the gift of the Grandcourts. There may be some burdens on the land, still, Mr. Grandcourt was an only child."

"It would be most remarkable," Mrs. Gascoigne exclaimed, "if he were to become Lord Stannery in addition to everything else. Only think: there is the Grandcourt estate, the Mallinger estate, *and* the baronetcy, *and* the peerage"—she counted off the items on her fingers, pausing on the fourth while adding, "though they say there will be no land accompanying the peerage." It seemed a pity there was nothing for the fifth finger.

"The peerage," the rector observed judiciously, "must be regarded as a remote possibility. There are two cousins between the current peer and Mr. Grandcourt. It is certainly a sobering reflection how death and other causes sometimes concentrate inheritances upon a single individual. However, such an excess ought to be deprecated. To be Sir Mallinger Grandcourt Mallinger—with corresponding properties—is a valuable talent sufficient for any man entrusted with it. Let us hope it will be well utilized."

"And what a position for the wife, Gwendolen!" Mrs. Gascoigne declared. "A great responsibility indeed. But you must lose no time in writing to Mrs. Mompert, Henry. It is fortunate you have an engagement to offer as an excuse; otherwise, she might feel deeply offended. She is rather a formidable woman."

*I am rid of that horror,* Gwendolen thought internally, to whom the name of Mompert had become a sort of mystical jargon. She remained remarkably silent throughout the evening, and that night she could scarcely sleep in her small white bed. It was a rarity in her robust youth to be wakeful; perhaps an even greater anomaly for her to be cautious enough to hide her restlessness from her mother. Her state of mind was entirely novel: she, who had always felt secure in herself and capable of managing others, had just taken a decisive step she had previously believed she would never take—or perhaps, was bound not to take. She could not retreat now; she liked much of what lay ahead, and there was nothing left to enjoy if she returned. Yet, her resolution was haunted by the shadow of that prior resolve, which had initially moved her entire being with undoubting certainty.

While lying on her pillow with wide-open eyes, "gazing on darkness which the blind do see," she was appalled by the realization that she was proceeding with what she had once fled from with repugnance. It was new to her that a question of right or wrong in her conduct should stir such terror; she had known no compunction that atoning caresses and gifts could not soothe. But here came a moment when a new consciousness was awakened. She seemed poised to adopt deliberately, as a guiding principle for the rest of her life, what she had rashly proclaimed in her bitterness when her discovery drove her away to Leubronn: that it mattered not what she did; she merely needed to amuse herself as best she could. That lawlessness, that abandonment of all concern for justification, suddenly terrified her. It arrived with the shadowy array of possible calamity trailing behind it—calamity that had ceased to be a mere abstract name for her. All the infiltrated influences of neglected religious teaching, alongside deeper impressions of something awful and inexorable enveloping her, seemed to converge in the vague concept of an avenging power.

The brilliant position she had longed for, the imagined freedom she envisioned creating through marriage, the deliverance from the dull insignificance of her girlhood—all stood immediately before her. Yet, they approached her like tainted food, carrying the flavor of sacrilege, which she must snatch with trembling fear. In the darkness and loneliness of her little bed, her more resistant self could not withstand the first onslaught of dread following her irrevocable decision. That unhappy-faced woman and her children—Grandcourt and his relations with her—repeated themselves in her imagination like the clinging memory of a disgrace, gradually erasing all other thought, leaving only the awareness that she had dragged those scenes into her life. Her prolonged wakefulness seemed a delirium; a faint, faint light penetrated beside the window curtain; the chill intensified. She could bear it no longer and cried, "Mamma!"

"Yes, dear," Mrs. Davilow responded instantly, her voice awake.

"Let me come to you."

Soon she fell asleep on her mother's shoulder, sleeping until late. Dreaming of a brightly lit ballroom, she opened her eyes to find her mother standing by the bedside holding a small packet.

"I am sorry to wake you, darling, but I thought it better to give this to you immediately. The groom has brought *Criterion*; he has come on another horse and says he is to stay here."

Gwendolen sat up in bed and opened the packet. Inside was a delicate enameled casket containing a magnificent diamond ring, along with a letter enclosing a folded piece of colored paper bearing these words:

*"Pray wear this ring when I come at twelve in sign of our betrothal. I enclose a check drawn in the name of Mr. Gascoigne for immediate expenses. Of course Mrs. Davilow will remain at Offendene, at least for some time. I hope, when I come, you will have granted me an early day, when you may begin to command me at a shorter distance.—Yours devotedly, H. M. GRANDCOURT."*

The check amounted to five hundred pounds. Gwendolen turned it toward her mother along with the letter.

"How very kind and delicate!" Mrs. Davilow said with deep emotion. "But I really prefer not to be dependent on a son-in-law. Myself and the girls could manage very well."

"Mamma, if you say that again, I will not marry him," Gwendolen snapped angrily.

"My dear child, I trust you are not marrying solely for my sake," Mrs. Davilow pleaded, trying to deflect the blow.

Gwendolen tossed her head on the pillow

"Surely whoever speaks to me in the right voice, him or her I shall follow. As the water follows the moon, silently, with fluid steps anywhere around the globe." —WALT WHITMAN.

"My cousins are at Diplow," Grandcourt announced, "shall you go there? Tomorrow? The carriage will come for Mrs. Davilow. You may dictate what arrangements you desire for the rooms. We must ensure everything is in proper order while we are away at Ryelands, and tomorrow is the only day available."

Grandcourt sat sideways on a sofa in the drawing-room at Offendene, one arm propped casually on the backrest, the other tucked between his crossed knees. He adopted the posture of a man deeply engrossed in observing the person beside him. Gwendolen, who had previously harbored a distaste for needlework, had recently embraced it with feigned enthusiasm since her engagement. Currently, she held a piece of white embroidery that, upon closer inspection, revealed numerous faulty stitches. Over the past eight or nine days, their time had been predominantly spent riding, yet they always reserved moments for this more demanding form of companionship, which Gwendolen had surprisingly found tolerable. She was entirely content with Grandcourt. His responses to her spirited inquiries about his travels and experiences were delivered in a slow, deliberate manner that suited her perfectly. From the outset, she had noted his uncanny ability to say exactly the right thing. She constantly sensed not only that he possessed none of the foolishness found in others but that, through some subtle alchemy, he projected the impression that all folly belonged to everyone else, save for those who acted as he did. A man who appears capable of commanding the best possesses a sovereign power to diminish all else. Furthermore, Grandcourt's conduct as a lover barely exceeded the bounds of affectionate homage; it was as inconspicuous as the scent of drifting roses, serving primarily to gratify his own vanity. On one occasion, he had kissed not her cheek but the skin beneath her ear. Startled, Gwendolen had risen with palpable agitation, prompting him to stand and ask, "I beg your pardon—did I offend you?" "Oh, it was nothing," Gwendolen replied, somewhat frightened of her own reaction, "only I cannot bear to be kissed under my ear." She sat down again with a playful laugh, yet her heart hammered with a vague terror. She was no longer free to mock him as she had mocked poor Rex. Her agitation seemed flattering to him, and he was content not to push further.

Today, a light rain prevented riding, but as compensation, a package arrived from London. Mrs. Davilow had just departed the room, presenting the exquisite items (arranged by Grandcourt's orders) scattered across the tables for admiration. Gwendolen was currently basking in the scenery of her life. Letting her hands drop onto her lap, she said with a prettily perverse air, "Why is tomorrow the only day?"

"Because the following day marks the first hunt," Grandcourt replied.

"And after that?"

"After that, I must depart for a couple of days—it is tedious—but I shall leave one day and return the next." Noticing a shift in her expression, Grandcourt withdrew his hand from beneath his knees and placed it over hers. "You object to my leaving?"

"It is useless to object," Gwendolen said coldly. She fought desperately against the urge to confess her suspicion regarding his destination, to speak freely without restraint.

"Yes, it is useful," Grandcourt said, enveloping her hand. "I will postpone my departure. I shall travel at night, ensuring I am away for only one day." He believed he understood the cause of what he internally termed this fleeting temper; she was particularly captivating to him at this moment.

"Then do not postpone it, but travel at night," Gwendolen insisted, feeling a surge of control and finding a small vent for her irritation in his peremptory tone.

"So, you will go to Diplow tomorrow?"

"Oh, yes, if you wish it," Gwendolen agreed in a high, careless register. Her preoccupation with other emotions had blinded her to the fact that her hand was being held.

"How you treat us poor devils of men!" Grandcourt murmured, lowering his voice. "We always end up with the worst of it."

"_Are_ you?" Gwendolen asked, her tone inquisitive, gazing at him with unusual naivety. She yearned to accept this banal jest as profound truth about her lover; if so, she too would be justified. If she knew everything, Mrs. Glasher would appear far more blameworthy than Grandcourt. "_Are_ you always receiving the worst?"

"Yes. Are you as kind to me as I am to you?" Grandcourt asked, fixing her with his narrow gaze.

Gwendolen felt struck dumb. Convinced of the immense favor she had received, her sense of command faltered and dissolved into the realization that, regardless of how she looked around, she could not retreat. It was as if she had agreed to board a chariot where another held the reins, and it was not in her nature to leap out before the world's eyes. She had not consented in ignorance; all she could offer now was a confession that she had not been unaware. Her right to demand explanations had vanished. All that remained was to adjust herself so that the sharp thorns of the penance imposed by conscience would not wound her. With a mental shudder, she resolutely shifted her mindset. After a brief pause during which she had not averted her eyes, she broke into a smile and said, "If I were as kind to you as you are to me, it would ruin your generosity: it would cease to be as magnificent as it is now."

"Then I am not to expect a single kiss," Grandcourt stated, pleased to pay a high price for this new style of courtship, which introduced marriage through the finest contrasts.

"Not one?" Gwendolen teased, nodding defiantly.

He raised her small left hand to his lips before releasing it with respectful deference. To call him merely "not disgusting" would be faint praise; he was almost charming. At this moment, she felt unlikely to ever love another man more deeply than this one. His reserve granted her an inexplicable, delightful awareness.

"Apropos," she said, picking up her embroidery again, "are there anyone else at Diplow besides Captain and Mrs. Torrington? Or do you leave them alone together? I suppose he talks of cigars, and she replies with her chignon."

"She has a sister with her," Grandcourt said, offering a shadow of a smile, "and there are two other men—one of whom, I believe, you know."

"Oh, then I hold a poor opinion of him," Gwendolen said, shaking her head.

"You met him at Leubronn—Young Deronda, a youth associated with the Mallingers."

Gwendolen felt as though her heart performed a sudden somersault, and her fingers, striving to maintain a firm grip on her work, grew icy.

"I never spoke to him," she said, fearing any detectable change in her demeanor. "Is he not disagreeable?"

"No, not particularly," Grandcourt replied in his most languid manner. "He thinks a little too highly of himself. I thought he had been introduced to you."

"No. Someone mentioned his name the evening before I left. That was all. Who is he?"

"A sort of ward of Sir Hugo Mallinger's. Nothing of any consequence."

"Oh, poor creature! How very unpleasant for him!" Gwendonen said, speaking lightly without intending sarcasm. "I wonder if the rain has stopped!" she added, rising to look out the window.

Fortunately, the next day was dry, and Gwendolen rode to Diplow on *Criterion*, as she had done on the previous occasion when returning with her mother in the carriage. She always felt bolder in her riding attire, bolstered by the pleasant belief that she looked impeccable—a comforting assurance in any encounter that seemed daunting. Her anger toward Deronda had transformed into a superstitious dread, perhaps due to the coercion he had exerted over her thoughts, lest his initial interference in her life presaged some future influence. Superstitions are often forged from such material: an intense self-feeling that makes the evening star shine with menace, or a beggar's blessing encourage us. Such beliefs carry consequences that frequently validate their hopes or fears.

Before luncheon, Gwendolen toured the rooms with Mrs. Torrington and Mrs. Davilow. She assumed that if she encountered Deronda, a mere bow would suffice. She intended to acknowledge him as little as possible.

Yet, ultimately, she found herself under an internal compulsion stronger than her pride. From the moment they entered the room together, she seemed to do nothing but observe him; everything else became an automatic performance of a habitual role.

When Deronda took his seat at lunch, Grandcourt remarked, "Deronda, Miss Harleth informed me you were not introduced to her at Leubronn?"

"Miss Harleth hardly remembers me, I imagine," Deronda said, looking at her with simple sincerity as they bowed. "She was intensely occupied when I saw her."

Did he suppose she had not suspected him as the man who had redeemed her necklace?

"On the contrary. I remember you very well," Gwendolen said, feeling nervous but mastering herself, returning his gaze with renewed scrutiny. "You did not approve of my playing at roulette."

"How did you arrive at that conclusion?" Deronda asked seriously.

There is no confession, no priest to absolve, yet souls are condemned to sit among the ashes of their wasted years.

Picture a sprawling, haphazard estate: the finest portions constructed of grey stone and topped with red tiles, featuring a round tower jutting from a corner, its conical roof cloaked in mellow shadow and crowned by a weather-vane that stands out starkly against summer skies or winter snows. The grounds are shaded by ancient trees; on one side, a magnificent oak flourishes alongside ragged Scots firs on a broken bank where roots hang bare, overlooking a distant rookery. On the other, a pool hangs heavy with overhanging bushes, teeming with screaming waterfowl. Surrounding it all lies a vast meadow, effectively a park, bordered by an old plantation and guarded by stone lodges resembling small prisons. Beyond the gates, the countryside, once purely pastoral and lovely, is now choked with black coal mines, inhabited mostly by laborers wearing candles in their hats—their faces twisted into a diabolical hue that makes them objects of suspicion to the children of Gadsmere. These are Mrs. Glasher's four beautiful children, who have lived there for three years.

Now, in November, with flower beds empty, trees stripped of leaves, and the pool shivering in the gloom, the place seems somberly appropriate to the black roads and black mounds that cast the district into mourning. Yet, when the children play on the gravel with the dogs for company, the mood shifts. Under her current circumstances, Mrs. Glasher finds Gadsmere preferable to any other home. Its total isolation, enforced by the unappealing nature of the surrounding land, suits her perfectly. When she drives her two ponies pulling a wagonette full of children, she meets no gentry in carriages, only businessmen in gigs. At church, there are no eyes she needs to dodge; the curate and his wife are either ignorant of her history or deliberately ignore it. To them, she is merely a widow lady, the tenant of Gadsmere. The name of Grandcourt holds little weight in that district compared to the local lessees of the collieries, Fletcher and Gawcome.

It has been nearly ten years since the scandalous elopement of an Irish officer's beautiful wife with young Grandcourt, followed by a duel where the bullets missed their marks entirely. Those who remember the affair now wonder what became of Mrs. Glasher, whose beauty and brilliance once made her a conspicuous figure abroad, living openly with the young man.

To Grandcourt, severing ties with her seemed natural and desirable. As for her, public opinion assumed that a woman who abandoned her child along with her husband had sunk to a lower station. Grandcourt, naturally prone to pursuing women, was now focused on securing a suitable marriage with the daughter of a noble house. Consequently, Mrs. Glasher faded from conversation, much like the forgotten victim of a manslaughter trial a decade prior. She was a lost vessel; no one sent expeditions to find her. Meanwhile, Grandcourt sailed into harbor, his colors flying, registered as seaworthy as ever.

In truth, Grandcourt had never truly disentangled himself. His passion for her had been the strongest and most enduring of his life. Though now as dead as the music of a cracked flute, it left behind a dull disposition. Upon her husband's death three years prior, this lingering sentiment had sparked a wavering idea in him to marry her, fulfilling promises made during his first fervent days. Back then, Grandcourt would have gladly paid for a divorce to free himself, but the husband refused—not wishing to remarry himself, and certainly not wanting his domestic habits scrutinized in court.

The dynamic had shifted drastically for Mrs. Glasher. Initially, she was carefree regarding marriage; escaping a cruel husband and finding bliss with a fascinating lover—who was young, handsome, wealthy, and worldly—was enough. She was an impassioned, vivacious woman, starved for adoration after five years of marital brutality. The sheer sense of release quieted her anxieties. An ambiguous status mattered little; she envied no dull, neglected wife. The only blemish on her new paradise was the memory of leaving her three-year-old son, who died two years later. Even his first cry of "mamma" sounded different from the subsequent children.

But time had wrought further changes. The prospect of Grandcourt marrying her had become her overriding desire. The ambiguity she once accepted now weighed heavily on her through her remaining children, whom she loved with a devotion charged with the passion of atonement. She possessed no repentance elsewhere. If Grandcourt wed her, the children would fare better: their mother would occupy a dignified position, shielding them from worldly disadvantage. Their son could become his grandfather's heir. This yearning gave supreme importance to Grandcourt's feelings toward her. Her love had transformed into an anxious demand that he grant her the permanent claim of a wife. She expected no other joy in marriage than the satisfaction of her maternal pride. For this result, she was prepared to endure anything quietly. She possessed the acumen to nurture Grandcourt's flickering intentions negatively, avoiding passionate appeals or scenes. Like everyone else seeking something from him, she feared his unpredictable nature and his tendency to harden under pleading—a trait revealed slowly in the demeanor of a youthful lover with a fine face and soft manners. However, this reticence had cost her dearly, breeding bitterness. There is no uglier side to quailing than that forced upon the helpless. The withheld sting gathered venom.

She was utterly dependent on Grandcourt. While he had been generous with expenses, he insisted everything remain voluntary. With marriage as her goal, she demanded nothing less. He had stated he would never settle anything via will. Thus, she often worried that even without becoming his wife, he might die without a legitimate son, leaving her son as the heir to the bulk of his estates. No child of her age could promise greater physical resemblance to the father. Yet, the idea of her becoming his wife was not so improbable that even Lush dismissed it; he believed betting on it was as sound as any other wager concerning his familiar companion. Indeed, when Lush inferred that Grandcourt intended to woo Miss Arrowpoint at Diplow, he encouraged the scheme, viewing it as a tacit abandonment of the marriage with Mrs. Glasher, which had long hovered on the periphery of Grandcourt's caprice. Both prospects were ultimately negated by Gwendolen's arrival. Naturally, Mrs. Glasher eagerly joined Lush's plan to hinder this new threat by erecting a mental barrier in the mind of the girl being courted. She embraced it with passion and purpose, allowing some of her stored-up venom to vent through this strategy.

Following Gwendolen's departure, Lush reported that the danger had passed. But no letter arrived to confirm its return as a certainty. Since then, Mrs. Glasher wrote to Grandcourt as usual, noting his unusually delayed response. She inferred he might be arriving at Gadsmere soon. Hope, born of desperate wishing, fueled her belief that the frustration of his recent courtship might lead him back to the old path of intention.

Grandcourt visited Gadsmere for two serious reasons: to announce his impending marriage personally, thereby resolving the immediate difficulty, and to retrieve the diamonds he had entrusted to Lydia years ago. Lydia suited diamonds; they enhanced her appearance, making them seem worth the price paid. These were not massive gems but small pearls and beads for ears, neck, and hair, yet worth thousands. Grandcourt needed them for his future wife. Previously, when he requested their return for safety, Lydia had firmly refused, stating they were secure. Finally, she had challenged him: "If you ever marry another woman, I will give them up to her. Are you going to marry another woman?" At the time, Grandcourt lacked the motive to persist, possessing a rare grace in his restraint toward her. One might argue that the facts of their relationship and her dependent position served as a standing banquet for his delight in domination. Yet, there was more: the surviving, albeit metamorphosed, effect of the power she once held over him. This residual influence, causing occasional fits of dull longing for the solicitations that once zestfully filled life, repeatedly inclined him to choose a familiar past over the effort required for novelty. But now, novelty seized him, urging him to maximize it.

Mrs. Glasher sat in her pleasant morning room, surrounded by her children. A square window overlooked a slope of gravel and grass leading to a brook entering the pool. The room was cluttered with toys, books, and gardening clothes atop a low black cabinet and an old oak table. Above, a painting of a smiling maternal lady in pastel watched over the scene. All the children were present. The three girls, seated near the window, were miniature portraits of their mother—dark-eyed, delicate brunettes with rich cheek blooms, their features refined as if they were tiny women; the eldest was barely nine. The boy sat on the carpet, bent over the animals of a Noah's Ark, commanding them in a threatening voice and occasionally licking the spotted ones to test the paint. Josephine received her French lesson while the others sat demurely with dolls, like Madonnas.

Mrs. Glasher's toilette was meticulously prepared. Each day she reminded herself that Grandcourt might arrive. Despite her emaciation, her head retained an indelible beauty in its fine profile, crisp curls, and marked eyebrows, rising impressively above her bronze silk and velvet, adorned with the gold necklace Grandcourt had first clasped around her neck years ago. She derived no pleasure from dressing; her reflection in the glass provoked only the thought, "How changed!" Yet, whatever goodness remained

*"A wild dedication of yourselves / To unpath'd waters, undream'd shores."* —Shakespeare.

On the day Gwendolen Harleth became Mrs. Grandcourt, the morning air was crisp and clear, the low sun casting a slight frost that curled the edges of the leaves. The bridal procession was a spectacle in itself; half the village of Pennicote lined the path to the church to witness it. An old friend of the rector officiated, while the rector himself acted as the bride's father—a role that added a certain dignified gravity to the parade. Yet, amidst the general rejoicing, two faces betrayed a distinct sadness: Mrs. Davilow's and Anna's. The mother's delicate eyelids were flushed pink, as though she had wept throughout the night, and it was easy to understand her grief at parting with a daughter who was the pride of her life. It was harder to comprehend why Anna, adorned in her bridesmaid's finery, appeared so troubled. Everyone else, including the radiant bride, reflected the brilliance of the occasion.

Gwendolen was universally acknowledged as possessing the figure and carriage of a "lady of title." While some might have wished her face were rosier, the groom's own pale complexion—remarkably similar to that of the miller's wife—made the match seem perfectly balanced in the public eye. They must be deeply in love, the villagers reasoned, hoping he would never hold it against her that she had once worked as a governess or that her mother lived modestly at Sawyer's Cottage. Even the miller's fourteen-year-old daughter couldn't conceive that high-born gentlemen treated their wives poorly, until her mother whispered, "Oh, child, men are men, whether gentle or simple. I've heard Squire Pelton carry his dogs and a long whip into his wife's room to frighten her."

"That's unlucky talk for a wedding, Mrs. Girdle," the tailor interjected. "A quarrel may end with the whip, but it starts with the tongue, and women usually get the worst of that."

"The Lord gave them tongues to use, I suppose," Mrs. Girdle replied. "He didn't intend for you to have it all your own way."

"From the gentleman who handles the grooming at Offendene," the tailor noted, "Mr. Grandcourt seems to have a wonderful little tongue. Everything must be done silently, without his ordering."

"Then he's the more whip, I suspect," Mrs. Girdle murmured. "She's got plenty of tongue herself. Look, there they come!"

"What wonderful long corners she has to her eyes!" the tailor observed. "They make you feel comical when she looks at you."

In truth, Gwendolen displayed more elasticity in her bearing and more luster in her long brown gaze than ever before. She possessed the brilliancy of intense excitement, a state that can sometimes arise even from pain. Yet, today, it was not pain she felt. She had driven herself into a condition akin to standing at a gambling table while Deronda watched her; she was beginning to lose. There was a perverse enjoyment in it. Whatever unease a growing conscience might have stirred was dismissed as a minor ailment, drowned out by the gratification of her ambitious vanity and craving for luxury.

She could not honestly claim regret for accepting Grandcourt, nor could she admit that vague fears clouded the glowing reality of the moment. She ignored the possibility that she was doing wrong, that a punishment awaited her, or that the woman she had promised to protect was suffering in bitter reproach. She pushed aside the image of Deronda despising her for this union, just as he had despised her for gambling. Above all, she silenced the terrifying thought that the bond uniting her to her lover was now being strangled and thrown over her neck. This chaotic mixture of dim facts, deep impressions, and half-real fantasies had disturbed her during the weeks of engagement. Was that turmoil nullified this morning? No; it was surmounted with an exultant defiance. Standing before many eyes, she felt herself playing a game of life, daring everything to win glory—or, if she lost, to lose with *éclat* and a profound sense of importance. For the first time, the prospect of a losing destiny did not terrify her; instead, she believed she was stepping into a realm of greater power, armed with the official authority of marriage.

The intoxicating fog of youthful egoism, previously shattered by trouble and shame, had returned, fed by a renewed strength of her old desires. She bore no resemblance to the tearful, trembling bride others imagined. Poor Gwendolen, deemed too worldly and forward by some, walked among illusions with an erect head and elastic step, though beneath it lay a subconscious realization that she was slightly drunk on the moment.

"Why, thank God you bear it so well, my darling!" Mrs. Davilow exclaimed, helping Gwendolen remove her white veil and slip into her traveling dress. The mother's trembling had been severe, urging Gwendolen to view the morning as a triumph.

"You might have said that if I were going to Mrs. Mompert's, you dear, sad, incorrigible mamma!" Gwendolen laughed tenderly, pressing her hands to her mother's cheeks before pulling back to spread her arms wide. "Here I am—Mrs. Grandcourt! What else would you have me?"

"Hush, my child, for heaven's sake!" Mrs. Davilow whispered, overcome. "How can I help feeling it when I part from you? But I can bear anything gladly if you are happy."

"Not gladly, mamma, no!" Gwendolen shook her head, smiling brightly. "You would bear it willingly, but always sorrowfully. Sorrowing is your sauce; you cannot take anything without it."

Clasping her mother's shoulders, she rained kisses upon her cheeks. "And you shall sorrow over my having everything at my command! Splendid houses, horses, diamonds—I shall have diamonds! Going to court, becoming Lady Certainly and Lady Perhaps, grand everywhere, and always loving you better than anyone else in the world."

"My sweet child! But I shall not be jealous if you love your husband better; he expects to be first."

Gwendolen thrust out her lips and chin in a pretty grimace. "Rather a ridiculous expectation. However, I don't mean to treat him ill, unless he deserves it."

The two embraced tightly, and Gwendolen could not stop a rising sob as she said, "I wish you were going with me, mamma." The slight moisture on her long eyelashes only enhanced her charm as she handed her arm to Grandcourt to lead her to the carriage. The rector paused to offer a final blessing—"God bless you; we shall see you again soon"—before turning to Mrs. Davilow with a mix of cheerfulness and solemnity. "Let us be thankful, Fanny. She is in a position well suited to her, far beyond what I dared hope. Few women have been chosen more entirely for their own sake. You should feel yourself a happy mother."

***

After a fifty-mile railway journey, the new couple arrived at the station near Ryelands. The sky had grown overcast, and twilight descended as they entered the park gates. Yet, Gwendolen, peering from the carriage window, drank in the grandeur of the scene: the winding drive bordered by evergreens and massive gray trees, opening into vast grassy plains dotted with dark clumps, finally revealing the white house framed by a hanging wood and a sweeping terrace.

During the ride, Gwendolen had been at her liveliest, chatting incessantly and ignoring the shift in their dynamic from the previous day. Grandcourt remained quietly ecstatic, allowing her to seize his hand with both of hers, her energy bubbling like a kitten refusing to be petted. She was genuinely feverish with excitement, and the changing light and scenery caused her heart to palpitate anew. Was it merely the novelty, or the fulfillment of her girlish dream to become "somebody"? Walking through corridors of impossible height, under ceilings where her own painted springs shed flowers and zephyrs blew trumpets, served by lackeys who revered her insolence? Was it the closeness of this dream realized, or a dim, insistent premonition mixing triumph with dread? In her nature, exultation inevitably carried an infusion of fear, ready to curdle.

As they approached the gates, she fell silent despite herself. When Grandcourt declared, "Here we are at home!" and kissed her on the lips for the first time, she barely registered it, offering only a passive acceptance amidst the overwhelming display. Wasn't her frantic life of the last three months merely a show, with her consciousness as a wondering spectator? Following the willful excitement of the wedding day, a numbness had settled over her.

Yet, the hall shone with brilliant light, warmth, matting, carpets, full-length portraits, and Olympian statues, attended by diligent servants. There were not many, only a few transferred from Diplow plus the permanent staff, and her new maid was being guided by the housekeeper. Led by Grandcourt down a subtly scented corridor, she entered an ante-room where an open doorway spilled rich light and color.

"These are our dens," Grandcourt said. "You will like to be quiet here until dinner. We shall dine early." He pressed her hand to his lips and withdrew, more in love than he had anticipated.

Gwendolen yielded her hat and mantle, throwing herself into a chair beside the glowing hearth. She saw her reflection repeated in

Throughout history, it has been a favored belief that a powerful love possesses the nature of an isolated fate, to which the mind's opinions and habitual resolutions are entirely strangers. Consider Daphnis's frenzy, where it served him little to be convinced of Heraclitus's doctrines; or Tristan's potion-born passion, who, though as profound as Duns Scotus, would have had his reasoning shattered by that cup; or Romeo, seized suddenly by Juliet, where any objections he might have held against Ptolemy made little difference to his discourse beneath the balcony. Yet, not all love is thus, even when potent. Nay, this passion holds as vast a scope as any for allying itself with every operation of the soul; acknowledging effects from the imagined light of unproven firmaments and setting its scale to the grander orbits of what has been and shall be.

On his return to town, Deronda could assure Sir Hugo that he had implanted in Grandcourt's mind a clear understanding: that Deronda could secure fifty thousand pounds by relinquishing a prospect that was likely distant and not absolutely certain. Beyond this, however, he received no further sign of Grandcourt's true disposition regarding the matter than that the younger man remained inclined to maintain friendly communications.

"And what did you think of the future bride upon closer inspection?" asked Sir Hugo.

"I thought better of her than I did in Leubronn. Roulette was a poor setting for her; it brought out something of the demon. At Diplow, she seemed much more womanly and attractive—less hard and self-possessed. I perceived a quite different expression in her mouth and eyes."

"Don't flirt with her too much, Dan," said Sir Hugo, intending to be playfully agreeable. "If you make Grandcourt savage when they arrive at the Abbey at Christmas, it will interfere with my affairs."

"I can remain in town, sir."

"No, no. Lady Mallinger and the children cannot do without you at Christmas. Only, do not make mischief—unless you can stage a duel and manage to shoot Grandcourt, which might be worth a little inconvenience."

"I don't believe you have ever seen me flirt," said Deronda, not amused.

"Oh, haven't I, though?" replied Sir Hugo, provocatively. "You are always gazing tenderly at women and speaking to them in a Jesuitical manner. You are a dangerous young fellow—a kind of Lovelace who will cause the Clarissas to chase you rather than you chasing them."

Why be exasperated by a tasteless jest? Exasperation often precedes reflection on utility. Few friendly remarks are more irritating than the information that we constantly appear to be doing what we never intend to do. Sir Hugo's definition of flirting was, it was hoped, peculiar; for his own part, Deronda was certain he had never flirted. Still, he was relieved that the baronet knew nothing of the repurchase of Gwendolen's necklace to fuel his appetite for such banter.

He resolved to be on his guard in the future, particularly regarding his upcoming visit to Mrs. Meyrick's, where he intended to pay his first call since returning from Leubronn. For Mirah was certainly a creature in whom it was difficult not to display a tender interest, both in look and speech.

Mrs. Meyrick had sent Deronda a report of Mirah's well-being within her household. "We are growing fonder of her every day," she had written. "At breakfast, we all look toward the door expecting to see her enter; we watch her and listen to her as if she were a native from a new land. I have heard not a single word from her lips that casts doubt upon her. She is perfectly contented and full of gratitude. My daughters are learning from her, and they hope to secure her other pupils, for she is eager not to eat the bread of idleness but to work, like my girls. Mab says our life has become like a fairy tale, and her only fear is that Mirah will transform into a nightingale again and fly away from us. Her voice is simply perfect: not loud and strong, but searching and melting, like the thoughts of what has been. That is how old people like myself perceive a beautiful voice."

Yet Mrs. Meyrick omitted details that would have required her to admit that Amy and Mab, who had accompanied Mirah to the synagogue, found the Jewish faith less reconcilable with their desires in Mirah's case than in that of Scott's Rebecca. They maintained silence out of delicacy toward Mirah, for whom religion was too tender a subject to be touched lightly. However, eventually, Amy, a practical reformer, could not restrain a question.

"Excuse me, Mirah, but *does* it truly seem right to you that women should sit behind rails in a separate gallery?"

"Yes, I never thought of anything else," Mirah replied with mild surprise.

"And you prefer seeing the men with their hats on?" asked Mab, cautiously proposing the smallest point of difference.

"Oh, yes. I like what I have always seen there, because it evokes in me the same feelings—the feelings I would not exchange for anything else in the world."

After this, any criticism, whether of doctrine or practice, would have seemed to these generous little souls an inhospitable cruelty. Mirah's religion was woven into the very fiber of her affections and had never presented itself to her as a set of propositions.

"She says herself she is a very bad Jewess and does not fully know her people's religion," Amy remarked when Mirah retired to bed. "Perhaps it will gradually fade from her, and she will pass into Christianity like the rest of the world, if she grows to love us deeply and never finds her mother. It is so strange to be of the Jewish religion now."

"Oh, oh, oh!" cried Mab. "I wish I were not such a hideous Christian. How can an ugly Christian, who is always dropping her work, convert a beautiful Jewess who has no faults?"

"It may be wicked of me," said shrewd Kate, "but I cannot help wishing that her mother may not be found. There might be something unpleasant."

"I don't think so, my dear," said Mrs. Meyrick. "I believe Mirah is fashioned after her mother's pattern. What a joy it would be for her to have such a daughter brought back! But a mother's feelings are not worth reckoning, I suppose"—she shot a mischievous glance at her own daughters—"and a dead mother is worth more than a living one?"

"Well, and so she may be, little mother," said Kate; "but we would rather hold you cheaper and have you alive."

Not only the Meyricks, whose varied knowledge had been acquired through the irregular foraging to which clever girls are often reduced, but Deronda himself, despite his extensive masculine education, had been awakened by Mirah's appearance to the realization that he knew almost nothing about modern Judaism or inner Jewish history. The Chosen People have commonly been treated as a people chosen for someone else's sake; their thinking regarded as something (whatever it may be) that ought to have been entirely different. Deronda, like his neighbors, had viewed Judaism as a sort of eccentric, fossilized form that an accomplished man might dispense with studying and leave to specialists. But Mirah, with her terrified flight from one parent and her yearning for the other, flashed upon him the hitherto neglected reality that Judaism was something still throbbing in human lives, still offering them the only conceivable garment for the world. During the idle excursion he immediately undertook with Sir Hugo, he began to seek out the exteriors of synagogues and titles of books concerning the Jews.

This awakening of a new interest—this transition from the assumption that we hold correct opinions on subjects we neglect, to a sudden care for them and a recognition that our previous views were ignorance—is an effective remedy for ennui, which unfortunately cannot be prescribed by a physician. Deronda carried this change with him, enduring his weeks of lounging all the better. It was during this journey that he first entered a Jewish synagogue at Frankfort, where his party rested on a Friday. While exploring the Juden-gasse, which he had seen long before, he recalled its picturesque old houses; what his eyes chiefly dwelt upon now were the human types there. His thoughts, busily connecting them with past phases of their race, stirred that fiber of historic sympathy which had helped determine certain traits in him worth noting for those interested in his future.

True, when a young man possesses a fine person, no eccentricity of manners, a gentleman's education, and a present income, it is not customary to feel a prying curiosity about his way of thinking or peculiar tastes. He may very well settle into life as an agreeable, clever young fellow without passing a special examination on such matters. Later, when he becomes somewhat slovenly and portly, his peculiarities are more distinctly discerned, and it is considered a mercy if they are not highly objectionable. But anyone wishing to understand the effect of later events on Deronda should know a little more of what he was at twenty-five than was evident in ordinary intercourse.

It happened that the vividness of his impressions often made him more enigmatic to his friends, contributing to an apparent indefiniteness in his sentiments. His early-wakened sensibility and reflectiveness had developed into a many-sided sympathy threatening to hinder any persistent course of action: as soon as he adopted any antagonism, even merely in thought, he seemed to himself like the Sabine warriors in the memorable story—with nothing to meet his spear but flesh of his own flesh and objects he loved. His imagination had trained itself to the habit

"No man," a Rabbi once declared as an undeniable example, "shall turn the bones of his father and mother into spoons"—certainly, his listeners felt a moral recoil against such economic reductionism. Yet, the market for spoons has never expanded sufficiently for anyone to ask, "Why not?" and argue that human progress demands such a utilitarian application of material. The only barrier is sentiment, a force that coerces only those who believe feelings constitute the greater portion of the world's wealth.

Deronda, meanwhile, abandoned the fashionable exercise of riding in Rotten Row. Instead, he frequently wandered through those districts of London most densely populated by common Jews. He visited synagogues during services, peered into shop windows, and studied faces—a pursuit offering little promise of specific revelation. Why did he not seek out an influential Rabbi or a prominent member of the Jewish community to inquire about the odds of finding a mother named Cohen, a son named Ezra, and a lost daughter named Mirah? He considered doing so after Christmas. The truth was, despite his appreciation for the poetry inherent in everyday things, Deronda, whenever a keen personal interest was stirred, could not, any more than the rest of humanity, consistently escape the crushing weight of the hard, unyielding Reality. This reality never consults our tastes and admits no selection. We know that enthusiasm finds comfort among abstract ideas; it tolerates the scent of garlic in medieval times and sees no shabbiness in the ceremonial trappings of classic processions. However, it grows squeamish when ideals press upon it as something warmly incarnate, and struggles to face them without fainting.

Lying dreamily in a boat, imagining oneself seeking a beautiful maiden's relatives in Cordoba amidst Jews in the era of Ibn-Gebirol, one bears all physical inconveniences without shock. Similarly, if the grim scenery of St. Mary Axe and Whitechapel were imaginatively transplanted to the banks of the Rhine at the end of the eleventh century—where the cries of the Crusaders sounded like the bay of bloodhounds, and the cowering figure of the reviled Jew stood erect, heroic, and flashing with sublime constancy in the face of torture and death before devilish missionaries with swords and torches—what significance would the dingy shops and unlovely faces hold against the thrill of contemplative emotion? Yet, the fervor of sympathy we feel for a grandiose martyrdom pales in comparison to the enthusiasm that remains unslaked where there is no danger, no challenge—only the impartial midday sun falling upon commonplace, perhaps half-repulsive, objects which are truly the beloved ideas made flesh. Here lies the true poetic energy: in the power of imagination that pierces or exalts solid fact, rather than floating among cloud-like fantasies. To glory in a prophetic vision of knowledge covering the earth is an easier exercise for the believing mind than to witness its beginning in newspaper placards staring from a bridge beyond the cornfields. Indeed, it might well happen to most of us, the delicate few, that we stand in the thick of the Battle of Armageddon without realizing anything more than the annoyance of a puff of explosive smoke and the struggle of bodies on the ground immediately around us.

It was contrary to Deronda's nature to despise the weak, fastidious sympathy that shrinks from the broad life of mankind; but now, with Mirah standing before him as a living reality, whose fate he was compelled to care for, he viewed every common Jew and Jewess through the lens of comparison with her. He harbored a presentiment of the collision between her concept of an unknown mother and brother and the discovered fact—a foreboding sharpened by a suppressed awareness that a similar possibility of collision might lie hidden within his own destiny. He would not have looked with greater complacency at wealthy Jews who outdid the lords of the Philistines in their sports; but since there was no likelihood of Mirah's friends being found among that class, their habits did not immediately concern him. In this mood, he wandered without expecting a result more fruitful than a preparation of his own mind, perhaps for future theorizing as well as practice—much as if Mirah were related to Welsh miners, and he had gone to study their ways, hoping simultaneously to gather details regarding the history of strikes.

He did not truly wish to find any particular individual. When, as was his habit, he glanced at the sign above a shop door, he was perfectly content that it did not read "Ezra Cohen." I confess, he particularly desired that Ezra Cohen should not keep a shop. Wishes are often deemed ominous; according to this belief, the order of the world is arranged such that if one holds an impious objection to a squint, one's offspring is more likely to be born with one; conversely, if one desires a squint, one will not receive it. This despondent view of probability is entirely rejected by the hopeful, who take their wishes as sufficient security for fulfillment. Who is absolutely neutral? One morning, Deronda happened to turn into a quiet side street to escape the noise and obstructions of Holborn, and he felt the scales tip toward the despondent side.

He was growing weary of the streets and had paused to hail a hansom cab when his attention was caught by some exquisite old clasps in chased silver displayed in the window to his right. His initial thought was that Lady Mallinger, who possessed a strictly Protestant taste for such Catholic relics, might appreciate having these missal-clasps transformed into a bracelet. Then his eyes traveled over the other contents of the window, revealing that this was a pawnbroker's establishment where lead is given for jewelry, lace, and all equivocal objects introduced as *bric-à-brac*. A placard in the corner announced: *Watches and Jewelry exchanged and repaired*. However, his scrutiny had been noticed from within. A figure appeared at the door, looking around at him with a tone of cordial encouragement, "Good day, sir." That instant was sufficient for Deronda to recognize the face, unmistakably Jewish, belonging to a young man of about thirty. Wincing from the shopkeeper's persuasive tactics that surely followed, he returned the greeting and immediately crossed to the other side of the street, beckoning the cabman to pull up there. From that vantage point, he read the name above the shop window: **Ezra Cohen**.

There might be a hundred Ezra Cohens lettered above shop windows, but Deronda had not encountered them before. Probably the young man, interested in a potential customer, was Ezra himself; he was of the expected age to be Mirah's brother, having grown up while she remained a child. Yet, Deronda's first effort as he drove home was to convince himself that there was no warrantable presumption that this Ezra was Mirah's brother. Next, he reasoned that even if, despite sound logic, he turned out to be that brother, and inquiries revealed the mother to be dead, it was not his duty to disclose the discovery to Mirah. This conclusion was disturbed by his recently acquired knowledge that Mirah would possess a religious desire to know of her mother's death and to learn whether her brother lived. How far was he justified in determining another life by his own notions? Was it not his secret grievance against the way others had ordered his own life that he lacked open daylight on all its relations, preventing him, like other men, from following the full guidance of primary duties?

Immediate relief from this internal debate came from the reflection that he had made no real discovery yet, and that by investigating the facts more closely, he would certify that no demand existed for any decision whatsoever. He intended to return to the shop as soon as convenient to purchase the clasps for Lady Mallinger. However, he was hindered for several days by Sir Hugo, who, preparing to deliver an after-dinner speech on a burning topic, required Deronda to research the legal aspects of the question, alongside wasting daily hours in arguments that invariably ended in a stalemate. On many issues, they held opposing views, yet Sir Hugo cared little for this. When Deronda presented his point effectively, Sir Hugo replied with a mixture of satisfaction and regret:

"Confound it, Dan! Why don't you make an opportunity of saying these things in public? You're wrong, you know. You won't succeed. You've got the massive sentiment—the heavy artillery of the country against you. But it's all the better ground for a young man to display himself on. When I was your age, I should have seized it. And it would be quite as well for you to be in opposition to me here and there. It would throw you more into relief. If you would seize an occasion of this sort to make an impression, you might be in Parliament in no time. And you know that would gratify me."

"I am sorry not to do what would gratify you, sir," Deronda replied. "But I cannot persuade myself to look at politics as a profession."

"Why not? If a man is not born into public life by his position in the country, there is no other way but to embrace it through his own efforts. The business of the country must be conducted—Her Majesty's Government carried on, as the old Duke said. And it never could be, my boy, if everyone regarded politics as prophecy, demanding an inspired vocation. If you are to get into Parliament, it won't do to sit still waiting for a call either from heaven or constituents."

"I don't want to make a living out of opinions," Deronda stated firmly. "Especially out of borrowed opinions. Not that I mean to blame other men. I dare say many better fellows than I don't mind getting on to a platform to praise themselves, and giving their word

*"He is named Israel. Upon him the witch's spell hath changed him into a dog. * * * * * Yet every Friday evening, in the twilight hour, suddenly the magic wanes, and the dog anew becomes a human creature."* —HEINE: *The Princess Sabbath*.

When Deronda arrived at five o'clock, the shop was already closed. The door was unlatched for him by a Christian servant, who led him into the room situated behind the counter. To his surprise, he found the interior surprisingly charming. The house was old and extended somewhat at the rear; likely, the large chamber he now occupied would have been gloomy under the harsh glare of daylight, but now it glowed with a warm, agreeable radiance. A magnificent antique brass lamp, holding seven oil-lights, hung suspended above a table draped in snow-white cloth. The ceiling and walls bore the soft, smoky patina of age, creating shadows that rendered the human figures within a luminous, Venetian-like hue.

The grandmother was dressed in shades of yellowish-brown, adorned with a heavy gold chain serving as a necklace. Under this light, her yellow complexion, marked by dark eyebrows and framed by a roll of gray hair, possessed a striking, picturesque beauty. Beside her, young Mrs. Cohen wore red and black, encircling her neck with a thick string of large artificial pearls. The infant slept peacefully in a cradle beneath a scarlet coverlet. Adelaide Rebekah was attired in braided amber fabric, while Jacob Alexander wore black velveteen paired with scarlet stockings.

As the four pairs of dark eyes gleamed with welcome upon Deronda's arrival, he felt a sudden flush of shame regarding the haughty disdain these cheerful-looking individuals had previously inspired in him during the day. The reception was nothing short of cordial. Both mother and grandmother seemed to derive an additional sense of dignity from hosting a guest within the privacy of their home, displaying a warmth that felt genuinely hospitable.

Deronda glanced around with mild wonder at the antiquated furnishings. The oak bureau and the tall side-table appeared to be acquired through necessity rather than aesthetic choice. On the side-table stood a large dish of blue and yellow ceramic ware, flanked by two aged silver vessels. Before them rested a massive volume bound in darkened vellum with a deeply ribbed spine. At the far end of the room, an open door revealed a glimpse of another illuminated chamber. While absorbing these details in passing glances, Deronda focused his attention on Jacob's earnest inquiries regarding a knife. Jacob had gone to considerable trouble to procure one featuring a hook and a white handle, presenting it eagerly. "Is this the sort you require, Jacob?" he asked.

Jacob subjected the item to a rigorous inspection, opening the hooks and blades, before producing the cork-screw knife intended for trade. "Why prefer a hook over a cork-screw?" Deronda asked. "'Cause I can grab things with a hook," Jacob replied seriously. "A cork-screw fits nowhere but corks. But it suits you better; you can pull corks."

"Do you agree to the exchange, then?" Deronda asked, noting the grandmother watching with evident delight. "What else do you carry in your pockets?" Jacob pressed, his demeanor grave.

"Hush, hush, Jacob, dear," the grandmother chided gently. Mindful of propriety, Deronda responded, "I believe I ought not to disclose that. Our purpose concerns only the knives."

Jacob scanned Deronda's face intently for a moment, seemingly satisfied with his assessment, before nodding solemnly. "I'll swap," he declared, handing over the cork-screw knife. Deronda accepted it with equal gravity, slipping it into his pocket.

Immediately, the youngest son of Shem darted into the adjacent room, his voice rising in rapid chatter, only to return moments later. Spotting his father, the child snatched a small velveteen hat from a chair and donned it before approaching. Mr. Cohen retained his own hat, ignoring the visitor initially, but stood still as the two children rushed forward to clasp his knees. He placed his hands upon each child in turn, uttering a Hebrew benediction. His wife, having recently lifted the sleeping baby from the cradle, brought the infant forward, holding it beneath her husband's outstretched hands to receive the blessing even in slumber.

For a fleeting instant, Deronda perceived that this pawnbroker, proud of his calling, was not entirely devoid of spiritual depth.

"Well, sir, you seem to have found a warm welcome in my household," Cohen said, removing his hat and resuming his usual composure. "And you were punctual. There is nothing quite like a bit of pressure here," he added, tapping his side pocket as he settled onto a seat. "It benefits us all in rotation. I've felt it when I needed to arrange payments. I started fitting various boxes. It's invigorating for the mind. Now then! Let us see, let us see."

"That is the ring I mentioned," Deronda said, lifting it from his finger. "I believe it cost a hundred pounds. It should serve as adequate security for fifty, I trust. I expect to redeem it within a month or so."

Cohen's glistening eyes narrowed slightly as they met the innocent gaze of this unsophisticated young gentleman, who apparently assumed that redemption was a source of satisfaction for pawnbrokers. Cohen took the ring, inspected it briefly, and returned it with an air of indifference. "Good, good. We shall discuss it after our meal. Perhaps you will join us, if you have no objection. My wife and I shall feel honored, and so will Mother; won't you, Mother?"

The invitation was enthusiastically echoed by both women, and Deronda gladly accepted. Everyone turned and gathered around the table. Aside from a single dish concealed beneath a napkin, no food was visible yet. Mrs. Cohen had positioned a china basin near her husband so he might wash his hands. However, after placing his hat back on his head, Cohen paused and called out loudly, "Mordecai!"

Could this be part of a religious rite? Deronda wondered, unsure what ancient customs might be expected. He heard a "Yes" from the next room and turned toward the open doorway. To his astonishment, he beheld the enigmatic Jew he had encountered earlier that morning in the bookshop. Their eyes met, and Mordecai appeared just as surprised as Deronda; neither offered a sign of recognition. Yet, as Mordecai took his seat at the far end of the table, he merely bowed his head toward the guest in a cold, distant manner, as if the disappointment of the morning lingered as an unpleasant association with this new acquaintance.

Cohen now washed his hands, reciting Hebrew words throughout the process.
