He then removed the napkin from the dish, revealing two long, flat loaves sprinkled with seeds—a remembrance of the manna that sustained the wandering ancestors. Breaking off small pieces, he distributed them to each family member, including Adelaide Rebekah, who stood on a chair, her entire height displayed in her amber gown, her little Jewish nose appearing elongated by the compression of her lips as she strained to maintain a proper posture. Cohen pronounced another Hebrew blessing, after which the men uncovered their heads, everyone seated themselves, and the meal commenced without any particular feature that captured Deronda's immediate interest.

He was barely conscious of the dishes before him, preoccupied with a desire to steer the conversation toward questions that might yield answers. Simultaneously, his thoughts drifted to Mordecai, between whom and himself there existed an exchange of fascinated, half-furtive glances. Mordecai lacked a handsome Sabbath garment; instead of the threadbare, rusty black coat worn the previous morning, he wore a light drab jacket that appeared to have once been a handsome, loose paletot, now shrunk tight by repeated washings. This change in attire accentuated his dark-haired, eager face, which possessed a prophetic intensity reminiscent of Ezekiel, certainly not fashionable in contemporary eyes.

It was notable that the slender tails of the fried fish were reserved for Mordecai, and generally, the portions allotted to him reflected those given to a poorer relative—a survival of prehistoric custom, perhaps, no longer widely acknowledged as superstitious. Mr. Cohen maintained a lively conversation, introducing topics always chosen with tact (for the Jew prides himself on his loyalty): the Queen and the Royal Family, the Emperor and Empress of France. Both the grandmother and wife participated with genuine enthusiasm. Young Mrs. Cohen demonstrated an accurate memory of distinguished birthdays, while the elder assisted her son in recounting events from when the Imperial couple visited the city ten years prior.

"I daresay you know all about it better than we do, sir," Cohen remarked repeatedly, prefacing his detailed accounts. These interesting narratives continued in a spirited trio.

"Our baby is named Eugénie Esther," announced young Mrs. Cohen, speaking vivaciously. "It is wonderful how the Emperor resembles a cousin of mine in the face," added the grandmother. "It struck me like lightning when I first saw him. I couldn't have imagined it."

"Mother and I went to see the Emperor and Empress at the Crystal Palace," Mr. Cohen stated. "I had a difficult task managing Mother; she might have been crushed flat—though she was nearly as robust then as she is now. I said if I had a hundred mothers, I'd never take one of them to see the Emperor and Empress at the Crystal Palace again; and you may think a man cannot afford such risks when he possesses but one mother—not even if he holds a

If the weight of a guilty conscience were perfectly proportional to the magnitude of the crime committed, the pages of human history would read quite differently. We might expect to find the architects of greedy wars and the ruthless predators of the financial markets marching shoulder-to-shoulder with petty thieves, pickpockets, and murderers who stain their hands with small-scale brutality, all united in a single regiment of self-flagellating penitents. Yet, wickedness does dispense its rewards; however, those who win in this diabolical game inevitably become baser, crueller, and more brutish than the average human being—the vast majority of women's offspring—is capable of sustaining. Most carry within them a form of conscience: a fear that serves as the shadow of justice, and a pity that acts as the shadow of love. These shadows prevent the attainment of "serene wickedness," a state of being that is notoriously difficult to maintain in our complex, dual-natured flesh.

On the twenty-ninth of December, Deronda learned that the Grandcourts had arrived at the Abbey, though he had not caught a glimpse of them until he prepared for dinner. A magnificent snowfall had recently occurred, granting the children the rare joy of snowball fights and snow fortifications. During the Christmas holidays, the Mallinger girls were satisfied with no amusement unless it involved "Cousin"—the name they had always bestowed upon Deronda—and required his direction. Following this outdoor exertion, Deronda had played billiards, allowing the hours to pass without his mind dwelling on the impending dinner encounter with Gwendolen. Nevertheless, the prospect held a distinct interest for him. As he retired to his room, slightly weary and flushed from his games, before the half-hour bell rang, he began to speculate on the influence her marriage to Grandcourt would exert upon her. He considered the likelihood of discernible changes in her demeanor compared to his previous encounters with her at Diplow, and even earlier, at Leubronn.

"I suspect there are certain natures that one could watch grow or degenerate daily if one kept close observation," Deronda mused. "Some of us progress faster than others. I am certain she retains strong traces of whatever once impressed her deeply. That incident involving the necklace, and the notion that someone deemed her gambling improper, clearly left a bite in her. However, such susceptibility works both ways: it can drive one toward desperation just as readily as toward improvement. Whatever fascinations Grandcourt holds for her capricious tastes—who can truly believe he possesses the power to summon tender affection through daily companionship? One might be tempted to strike him for the mere spectacle of eliciting emotion from his face and words. I fear she married him out of ambition, perhaps to escape poverty. But why did she initially avoid him? The poverty came later, though. Poor thing! She may have been pressured into it. How can one feel anything but pity for a young creature, brimming with untapped vitality, recklessly ignorant, hanging all her blind hopes on the remains of a human being?"

Naturally, the terms Deronda applied to the bridegroom were less flattering than the excuses and pity used to describe the bride. His view of Grandcourt as a "remnant" stemmed not from specific knowledge, but from the general impression of polite interaction that suggested Grandcourt had exhausted all his natural, healthy interest in the world. Generally speaking, whenever a notable marriage occurs, male acquaintances tend to pity the bride while female acquaintances pity the groom; each feels the other might have done better. This sentiment is especially prevalent when the bride is charming and young; gentlemen present often conclude she lacks genuine attachment to a husband so uninteresting to them, assuming she married on other grounds. Under such circumstances, who pities the husband? Even his female friends often deem his situation retributive, believing he should have chosen differently. Perhaps Deronda deserves exemption here, as he felt no pity for Grandcourt, who had never struck observers as a man likely to suffer more than he inflicted. Conversely, regarding Gwendolen—young, impulsive, eager for pleasure, and fed by flattery that convinces a lovely girl of her divine right to rule—one wonders how quickly life might shift from expectation to a bitter realization of the irreparable. Given what he had witnessed of her, Deronda surely possessed duller feelings than necessary not to look forward with some curiosity to her entry into the dining room. Still, since the honeymoon was already three weeks distant, and Gwendolen had been enthroned not only at Ryelands but also at Diplow, she was likely to have composed her countenance, manifesting or concealing her emotions suitably. She was not a woman inclined to expose her feelings helplessly to the curious gaze.

A diverse party had gathered to welcome the new couple. The old aristocracy was represented by Lord and Lady Pentreath; the established gentry by young Mr. and Mrs. Fitzadam of the Worcestershire branch; politics and the public good, specifically regarding the cider industry, by Mr. Fenn, Member for West Orchards, accompanied by his two daughters; Lady Mallinger's family by her brother, Mr. Raymond, and his wife; and the useful bachelor element by Mr. Sinker, the eminent counsel, and Mr. Vandernoodt, whose acquaintance Sir Hugo had found pleasant enough at Leubronn to warrant his adoption in England.

Everyone had assembled in the drawing-room before the new couple appeared. In the interim, time was spent primarily observing the children—various little Raymonds, nephews and nieces of Lady Mallinger, along with her own three girls, who were invariably permitted to appear at this hour. The scene was genuinely delightful, enhanced by full-length portraits with deep backgrounds set into the cedar paneling, surmounted by a ceiling glowing with the rich colors of coats of arms arranged between the sockets. The room was illuminated nearly as much by the red fire of oak boughs as by pale wax candles, softened by the deep-piled carpet and the high English breeding that subdued all voices. The mixture of ages, ranging from the white-haired Lord and Lady Pentreath to the four-year-old Edgar Raymond, lent a varied charm to the living groups. Lady Mallinger, possessing a fair, matronly roundness and mildly prominent blue eyes, moved about in black velvet, carrying a tiny white dog on her arm as a finishing touch to her attire. The children were scattered among the ladies, while most gentlemen stood somewhat apart, conversing with a moderate vivacity typical of the long minutes preceding dinner. Deronda stood slightly outside the main circle, engaged in a dialogue fixed upon him by Mr. Vandernoodt, a man of the finest Dutch blood imported during the Revolution. For the rest, he was one of those commodious figures in society who possess nothing remarkable themselves but are believed to be acquainted with the best in every department: close-clipped, pale-eyed, nonchalant, serving as an excellent foil to the intense coloring and vivid gravity of Deronda.

They discussed the bride and bridegroom, whose arrival was awaited. Mr. Vandernoodt was an industrious collector of personal details, capable of recounting everything about a great philosopher or physicist save for their theories or discoveries. Now, he implied he had learned numerous facts about Grandcourt since meeting him at Leubronn.

"Men who have seen a great deal of life do not always end by choosing their wives wisely. He has rather an anecdotal history—I fancy he plunged quite deep into pleasures, considering how lazy he is. But, of course, you know all about him."

"No, really," Deronda replied in an indifferent tone. "I know little more of him than that he is Sir Hugo's nephew."

Just then, the door opened, deferring any further satisfaction of Mr. Vandernoodt's communicativeness.

The scene was designed to highlight any figure of distinction entering it, and certainly, when Mr. and Mrs. Grandcourt appeared, no observer could deny their figures possessed distinction. The bridegroom wore neither more nor less perfect ease of costume, nor more nor less impassive composure of face than before his marriage. It was assumed he would accept nothing less than the best in outward equipment, including his wife; and the bride was precisely what one might expect him to choose. "By George, I think she's handsomer, if anything!" exclaimed Mr. Vandernoodt. Deronda agreed but remained silent. Her white silk and diamonds—though it may seem odd, she did wear diamonds around her neck, in her ears, and in her hair—may have contributed to the new imposingness of her beauty. This beauty flashed upon him as more unquestionable, if not entirely more satisfying, than when he had first seen her at the gaming table. Some faces, peculiar in their beauty, resemble original works of art; they are almost always met with skepticism at first. However, upon seeing Gwendolen at Diplow, Deronda had discerned in her more than he had anticipated of that tender, appealing charm known as womanliness. Was there any new change since then? He distrusted his own impressions; yet, as he saw her receive greetings with what appeared to be proud, cold quietude and a superficial smile, it seemed that the same demonic force active when she had taken him in her resolute glance and turned away a losing player from the gaming table was still at work within her. There was no time for further conclusion, nor even for him to offer his greeting before the summons to dinner.

He sat not far from opposite her at the table and could occasionally hear her answers to Sir Hugo, who was at his liveliest in conversation with her. Though he intended to bow, she offered him no opportunity for some time. Finally, Sir Hugo, seemingly imagining they had already spoken,

"Nothing weighs so heavily as a secret; carrying it far is difficult for ladies: and I know even on this point, many men who are women." — La Fontaine.

Meanwhile, Deronda had been hurried along by Mr. Vandernoodt, who desired a brisker pace, a cigar, and a bit of gossip. Since a man cannot easily confess his own secrets to another man, the constraint of his company often breeds a desire to pair off in conversation with someone more ignorant. Mr. Vandernoodt soon seized this opening.

"What a washed-out piece of cambric Grandcourt is!" he observed. "But if he is a favorite of yours, I withdraw the remark."

"Not the least in the world," replied Deronda.

"I thought not. One wonders how he came to have a great passion again; and he must have had—to marry in this way. Though Lush, his old chum, hints that he married this girl out of obstinacy. By George! it was a very accountable obstinacy. A man might make up his mind to marry her without the stimulus of contradiction. But he must have made himself a pretty large drain of money, eh?"

"I know nothing of his affairs."

"What! Not of the other establishment he keeps up?"

"Diplow? Of course. He took that of Sir Hugo. But merely for the year."

"No, no; not Diplow: Gadsmere. Sir Hugo knows, I'll answer for it."

Deronda said nothing. He really began to feel some curiosity, but he foresaw that he should hear what Mr. Vandernoodt had to tell without the condescension of asking.

"Lush would not altogether own to it, of course. He's a confidential agent and go-between of Grandcourt's. But I have it on the best authority. The fact is, there's another lady with four children at Gadsmere. She has had the upper hand of him these ten years and more, and by what I can understand has it still—she left her husband for him, and used to travel with him everywhere. Her husband's dead now; I found a fellow who was in the same regiment with him, and knew this Mrs. Glasher before she took wing. A fiery dark-eyed woman—a noted beauty at that time—he thought she was dead. They say she has Grandcourt under her thumb still, and it's a wonder he didn't marry her, for there's a very fine boy, and I understand Grandcourt can do absolutely as he pleases with the estates. Lush told me as much as that."

"What right had he to marry this girl?" said Deronda, with disgust.

Mr. Vandernoodt, adjusting the end of his cigar, shrugged his shoulders and pursed his lips.

"She can know nothing of it," said Deronda, emphatically. But that positive statement was immediately followed by an inward query—*Could she have known anything of it?*

"It's rather a piquant picture," said Mr. Vandernoodt—"Grandcourt between two fiery women. For depend upon it this light-haired one has plenty of devil in her. I formed that opinion of her at Leubronn. It's a sort of Medea and Creüsa business. Fancy the two meeting! Grandcourt is a new kind of Jason: I wonder what sort of a part he'll make of it. It's a dog's part at best. I think I hear Ristori now, saying, 'Jasone! Jasone!' These fine women generally get hold of a stick."

"Grandcourt can bite, I fancy," said Deronda. "He is no stick."

"No, no; I meant Jason. I can't quite make out Grandcourt. But he's a keen fellow enough—uncommonly well built too. And if he comes into all this property, the estates will bear dividing. This girl, whose friends had come to beggary, I understand, may think herself lucky to get him. I don't want to be hard on a man because he gets involved in an affair of that sort. But he might make himself more agreeable. I was telling him a capital story last night, and he got up and walked away in the middle. I felt inclined to kick him. Do you suppose that is inattention or insolence, now?"

"Oh, a mixture. He generally observes the forms: but he doesn't listen much," said Deronda. Then, after a moment's pause, he went on, "I should think there must be some exaggeration or inaccuracy in what you have heard about this lady at Gadsmere."

"Not a bit, depend upon it; it has all lain snug of late years. People have forgotten all about it. But there the nest is, and the birds are in it. And I know Grandcourt goes there. I have good evidence that he goes there. However, that's nobody's business but his own. The affair has sunk below the surface."

"I wonder you could have learned so much about it," said Deronda, rather drily.

"Oh, there are plenty of people who knew all about it; but such stories get packed away like old letters. They interest me. I like to know the manners of my time—contemporary gossip, not antediluvian. These Dryasdust fellows get a reputation by raking up some small scandal about Semiramis or Nitocris, and then we have a thousand and one poems written upon it by all the warblers big and little. But I don't care a straw about the *faux pas* of the mummies. You do, though. You are one of the historical men—more interested in a lady when she's got a ragged face and skeleton toes peeping out. Does that flatter your imagination?"

"Well, if she had any woes in her love, one has the satisfaction of knowing that she's well out of them."

"Ah, you are thinking of the Medea, I see."

Deronda then chose to point to some giant oaks worth looking at in their bareness. He also felt an interest in this piece of contemporary gossip, but he was satisfied that Mr. Vandernoodt had no more to tell about it.

Since the early days when he tried to construct the hidden story of his own birth, his mind had perhaps never been so active in weaving probabilities about any private affair as it had now begun to be about Gwendolen's marriage. This unavowed relation of Grandcourt's—could she have gained some knowledge of it, which caused her to shrink from the match—a shrinking finally overcome by the urgency of poverty? He could recall almost every word she had said to him, and in certain of these words he seemed to discern that she was conscious of having done some wrong—inflicted some injury. His own acute experience made him alive to the form of injury which might affect the unavowed children and their mother. Was Mrs. Grandcourt, under all her determined show of satisfaction, gnawed by a double, a treble-headed grief—self-reproach, disappointment, jealousy? He dwelt especially on all the slight signs of self-reproach: he was inclined to judge her tenderly, to excuse, to pity. He thought he had found a key now by which to interpret her more clearly: what magnifying of her misery might not a young creature get into who had wedded her fresh hopes to old secrets! He thought he saw clearly enough now why Sir Hugo had never dropped any hint of this affair to him; and immediately the image of this Mrs. Glasher became painfully associated with his own hidden birth. Gwendolen knowing of that woman and her children, marrying Grandcourt, and showing herself contented, would have been among the most repulsive of beings to him; but Gwendolen tasting the bitterness of remorse for having contributed to their injury was brought very near to his fellow-feeling. If it were so, she had got to a common plane of understanding with him on some difficulties of life which a woman is rarely able to judge of with any justice or generosity; for, according to precedent, Gwendolen's view of her position might easily have been no other than that her husband's marriage with her was his entrance on the path of virtue, while Mrs. Glasher represented his forsaken sin. And Deronda had naturally some resentment on behalf of the Hagars and Ishmaels.

Undeniably Deronda's growing solicitude about Gwendolen depended chiefly on her peculiar manner toward him; and I suppose neither man nor woman would be the better for an utter insensibility to such appeals. One sign that his interest in her had changed its footing was that he dismissed any caution against her being a coquette setting snares to involve him in a vulgar flirtation, and determined that he would not again evade any opportunity of talking to her. He had shaken off Mr. Vandernoodt, and got into a solitary corner in the twilight; but half an hour was long enough to think of those possibilities in Gwendolen's position and state of mind; and on forming the determination not to avoid her, he remembered that she was likely to be at tea with the other ladies in the drawing-room. The conjecture was true; for Gwendolen, after resolving not to go down again for the next four hours, began to feel, at the end of one, that in shutting herself up she missed all chances of seeing and hearing, and that her visit would only last two days more. She adjusted herself, put on her little air of self-possession, and going down, made herself resolutely agreeable. Only ladies were assembled, and Lady Pentreath was amusing them with a description of a drawing-room under the Regency, and the figure

"Pardon me, my Lord," Aspern interjected, "but I speak on behalf of Sigismund."

"For him?" Fronsberg replied instantly. "Oh, yes—for him I always keep a pardon safely banked. He will draw upon me sooner or later. What is his need? Has some mad project failed? Are his fine mechanical wings proving unable to fly? Is he in durance, assaulted by the watch, owing a bill for Epernay with not a crust to eat?"

"Oh, none of these, my Lord," Aspern assured him. "He has escaped Circe's herd and seeks to win the love of your fair ward, Cecilia. But he desires to win your consent first."

"You frown," Fronsberg observed sharply.

"Distinguish your words, my Lord," Aspern retorted. "I said I held a *pardon*, not *consent*."

***

Despite Daniel Deronda's pressing reasons to return to town—motives blended from his deep anxiety for Mirah and a burning curiosity regarding the enigmatic Mordecai—he did not reach Sir Hugo before the latter proceeded ahead of his family to prepare for the opening of Parliament on the sixth of February. Deronda took up his quarters in Park Lane, fully expecting to find his chambers occupied by Hans Meyrick. This much he anticipated; however, he discovered other details that defied his expectations.

Many recall Retzsch's drawing depicting Destiny as Mephistopheles playing chess with Man for his soul. In this game, the clever adversary feints with unintended moves, luring the mortal defender away from the true point of attack. The fiend mocks preparation above all else, persuading us against taking out waterproofs when the sky is destined to clear, foreseeing that our resulting prejudice will cloud our judgment of future weather signs. It is a peculiar test of a man's mettle when, after painfully adjusting oneself to what seems a wise provision, one finds all mental precautions slightly astray, and excellent intentions no better than miscalculated dovetails cut from a wrong starting point. When magnanimity prepares to meet misbehavior, it often finds a completely different call upon it. Something akin to this befell Deronda.

His first impression was one of pure pleasure and amusement. His sitting room had been transformed into an *atelier*, strewn with miscellaneous drawings and the contents of two chests brought from Rome. The lower half of the windows were darkened with baize, and the blonde Hans, in his weird youth, presided over the littered space as its genius. His hair was longer than before, his face more whimsically creased, and his high voice rose even higher under the excitement of rapid talk. The friendship between the two had been maintained warmly since their memorable days at Cambridge, sustained not only by correspondence but by little episodes of companionship abroad and in England. The original dynamic of confidence on one side and indulgence on the other had developed naturally, as is wont to occur where such spiritual borrowing and lending begins well.

"I knew you would like to see my casts and antiquities," Hans said after the initial hearty greetings and inquiries, "so I didn't scruple to unload my chests here. But I've found two rooms in Chelsea, not many hundred yards from my mother and sisters, and I shall soon be ready to hang out there once they scrape the walls and install new lights. That is all I'm waiting for. But you see, I don't wait to begin work: you cannot conceive what a great fellow I am going to be. The seed of immortality has sprouted within me."

"Only a fungoid growth, I dare say—a growing disease in the lungs," Deronda remarked, treating Hans in his customary brotherly fashion. He walked toward some drawings propped on the ledge of his bookcases: five rapidly sketched heads, different aspects of the same face. He stood at a convenient distance, offering no immediate comment. Hans remained silent for a minute, picked up his palette, and began touching the picture on his easel.

"What do you think of them?" he asked finally.

"The full face looks too massive; otherwise, the likenesses are good," Deronda said, more coldly than was usual with him.

"No, it is not too massive," Hans decided firmly. "I have noted that. There is always a little surprise when one passes from the profile to the full face. But I shall enlarge her scale for Berenice. I am making a Berenice series—look at the sketches along there—and now, thinking of it, you are just the model I want for the Agrippa."

Hans, still holding pencil and palette, had moved to Deronda's side while saying this, but he added hastily, as if conscious of a mistake, "No, no, I forgot; you don't like sitting for your portrait, confound you! However, I've picked up a capital Titus. There are to be five in the series. The first is Berenice clasping the knees of Gessius Florus, beseeching him to spare her people; I've got that on the easel. Then this one, where she stands on the Xystus with Agrippa, entreating the people not to injure themselves by resistance."

"Agrippa's legs will never do," Deronda objected.

"The legs are good realistically," Hans replied, his face creasing drolly. "Public men are often shaky about the legs—'Their legs, the emblem of their various thought,' as somebody says in the *Rehearsal*."

"But these are as impossible as the legs of Raphael's Alcibiades," Deronda countered.

"Then they are good ideally," Hans insisted. "Agrippa's legs were possibly bad; I idealize that and make them impossibly bad. Art, my Eugenius, must intensify. But never mind the legs now: the third sketch shows Berenice exulting in the prospect of becoming Empress of Rome, when news comes that Vespasian is declared Emperor and her lover Titus his successor."

"You must put a scroll in her mouth, else people will not understand that. You can't tell that in a picture."

"It will make them feel their ignorance then—an excellent aesthetic effect. The fourth depicts Titus sending Berenice away from Rome after she has shared his palace for ten years—both reluctant, both sad—*invitus invitam*, as Suetonius hath it. I've found a model for the Roman brute."

"Shall you make Berenice look fifty? She must have been that age."

"No, no; a few mature touches to show the lapse of time. Dark-eyed beauty wears well; hers particularly. But now, here is the fifth: Berenice seated lonely on the ruins of Jerusalem. That is pure imagination. That is what ought to have been—perhaps was. Now, see how I tell a pathetic negative. Nobody knows what became of her—that is finely indicated by the series coming to a close. There is no sixth picture."

Hans pretended to speak with a gasping sense of sublimity, drew back his head with a frown as if searching for a similar impression on Deronda, and continued, "I break off in the Homeric style. The story is chipped off, so to speak, and passes with a ragged edge into nothing—*le néant*; can anything be more sublime, especially in French? The vulgar would desire to see her corpse and burial—perhaps her will read and her linen distributed. But now, come and look at this on the easel. I have made some way there."

"That beseeching attitude is really good," Deronda said after a moment's contemplation. "You have been very industrious during the Christmas holidays; for I suppose you have taken up the subject since you came to London." Neither of them had yet mentioned Mirah.

"No," Hans said, applying touches to his picture, "I made up my mind to the subject beforehand. I take that lucky chance for an augury that I am going to burst on the world as a great painter. I saw a splendid woman in Trastevere—the grandest women there are half-Jewesses—and she set me hunting for a fine situation of a Jewess in Rome. Like other men of vast learning, I ended by taking what lay on the surface. I'll show you a sketch of the Trasteverina's head when I can lay my hands on it."

"I should think she would be a more suitable model for Berenice," Deronda said, struggling to express his discontent.

"Not a bit of it. The model ought to be the most beautiful Jewess in the world, and I have found her."

"Have you made yourself sure that she would like to figure in that character? I should think no woman would be more abhorrent to her. Does she quite know what you are doing?"

"Certainly. I got her to throw herself precisely into this attitude. Little Mother sat for Gessius Florus, and Mirah clasped her knees." Hans stepped away slightly to inspect the effect of his touches.

"I dare say she knows nothing about Berenice's history," Deronda said, feeling more indignation than he could strictly justify.

"Oh, yes, she does—the ladies' edition. Berenice was a fervid patriot, but was beguiled by love and ambition into attaching herself to the arch-enemy of her people. Whence the Nemesis. Mirah takes it as a tragic parable and cries to think what the penitent Berenice suffered as she wandered back to Jerusalem and sat desolate amidst desolation. That was her own phrase. I couldn

Some believe that the deepest tragedies occur not after a man has seized the divine fire and bound it to his staff to bring it to humanity, but *before* that moment. Before he is thrust by the crushing weight of established systems into a lonely exile of rejected ideas, pinned down by the agonizing grip of poverty and sickness—a desolate place where crowds walk past without seeing him. "Second sight" is a banner flying over contested territory. Yet, it is undeniable that there exist individuals whose deepest yearnings, conceptions, and even reasoned conclusions constantly manifest as vivid images possessing a prophetic power. The act they intend to perform rises before them in perfect form, becoming a compelling archetype; the event they crave or fear grows rapidly from a tiny seed, nourished by countless impressions. These individuals are not necessarily less logical or less sane than the ordinary calculators of commerce; indeed, their natures may possess manifold openings, like the city of Thebes with its hundred gates, allowing a vast and diverse influx of experience that cannot enter through a narrow, guarded door.

Of course, there are pathetic examples of the visionary, just as there is a tiny mammal you might trap inside the finger of a glove. This small relative of the elephant causes no harm, yet what great mental or social category is entirely free of specimens whose insignificance is both ugly and harmful? One hesitates to consider everything encompassed by the label "patriot"; or to imagine the jostling that might occur on Judgment Day among those who claimed authorship, carrying volumes in their hands or on carts. This defense of inevitable kinship serves only to introduce the facts concerning Mordecai, whose figure had gnawed its way into Daniel Deronda's mind as a new question he felt compelled to answer. However, Deronda's interest was merely a vague, expectant suspense. The consumptive-looking Jew, appearing to be a fervent student earning his living through a quiet craft, similar to Spinoza, fit into none of Deronda's expectations.

It was quite different with the impact of their meeting on Mordecai. For many winters, while aware of his fading physical life and expanding spiritual isolation, all his passionate desire had concentrated on finding a young ear into which he could pour his mind as a testament. He sought a soul kindred enough to receive the spiritual fruit of his own brief, painful existence as a mission to be executed. Remarkably, the hopefulness often granted as a benign illusion to consumptive patients was in Mordecai entirely diverted from the prospect of bodily recovery and channeled into this yearning for transmission. The desperate longing, panting upward from overwhelming discouragement, had grown into hope, and that hope into a confident belief. Instead of being dampened by his clear awareness of his hastening decline, this belief intensified into an expectant faith in a prophecy destined to fulfill itself in a short remaining time.

Several years had passed since he first began measuring men with a keen glance, searching for a possibility that gradually sharpened into a distinct concept. Initially, this clarity was achieved primarily through contrast: he sought a man who differed from himself. Analyzing the reasons within his own self for the rebuffs he faced and the obstacles that hindered him, he imagined a person who possessed all the elements necessary for sympathy with him, yet embodied them differently. This man must be a Jew, intellectually cultivated and morally fervent—in every way a nature ready to be filled from Mordecai's—but his face and frame must be beautiful and strong. He must be accustomed to the refinements of social life, his voice flowing with a full and easy current, his circumstances free from sordid need. He must glorify the possibilities of the Jew, rather than sitting in wonder as Mordecai did, bearing the mark of his people amidst the signs of poverty and failing breath.

Sensitive to physical characteristics, Mordecai had, both abroad and in England, examined pictures as well as people. In idle hours, he would sometimes linger in the National Gallery seeking paintings that could feed his hopefulness with grave and noble types of the human form, suitable for men of his own race. Yet, he returned disappointed. Instances are scattered thinly across the galleries of Europe where the fortune or selection of even the greatest masters has gifted art with a face that is simultaneously young, grand, and beautiful. Where melancholy exists, it is not a feeble passivity but enters into a foreseen capacity for heroism. Observant souls may recall his emaciated figure and dark eyes set deep in their sockets as he stood before a painting that touched him to new or habitual meditation. He commonly wore a cloth cap with black fur trim, which no painter would have asked him to remove. Spectators likely viewed him as an oddly dressed Jew, perhaps trying to extract money from the artworks; Mordecai was perfectly aware of the impression he made. Experience had made him acutely sensitive to how a man's poverty and other physical disadvantages cheapen his ideas, unless he is a Peter the Hermit ringing a bell for the rabble.

However, he was too sane and generous to blame his spiritual banishment solely on the excusable prejudices of others. Certain incapacities of his own had contributed to the sentence of exclusion. Hence, his imagination constructed another man—one who would be something more ample than the "second soul" bestowed, according to Cabalist notions, to supplement the insufficient first. This figure would be a blooming human life, ready to incorporate all that was worthy in an existence whose visible, tangible part was burning itself away quickly. His inner need for the conception of this expanded, prolonged self was reflected as an outer necessity. The thoughts of his heart—that ancient phrase best shadowing the truth—seemed to him too precious, too intricately woven into the growth of things to lack a further destiny. As the more beautiful, stronger, and more executive self took shape in his mind, he loved it beforehand with an affection that was half-identifying, half-contemplative and grateful.

Mordecai's mind worked so constantly in images that his coherent chains of thought often resembled the significant dreams attributed to sleepers by awake observers in their most creative moments. Nay, they often mimicked genuine dreams in their manner of breaking off the passage from the known to the unknown. For a long while, he habitually conceived of the Being answering to his need as someone distantly approaching or turning their back toward him, darkly silhouetted against a golden sky. The reason for the golden sky lay in one of Mordecai's habits. He was keenly alive to certain poetic aspects of London; a favorite resort, when strength and leisure permitted, was some of the bridges, particularly around sunrise or sunset. Even when bent over watch-wheels and trinkets, or seated in a small upper room overlooking dingy bricks and cracked windows, his imagination spontaneously placed him on a spot offering a far-stretching view. His thoughts operated in wide spaces, and whenever possible, he tried to experience the influences of a large sky in reality. Leaning on the parapet of Blackfriars Bridge, gazing meditatively, the breadth and calm of the river, with its long vista half-hazy, half-luminous; the grand, dim masses of tall buildings signaling world-commerce; the oncoming boats and barges moving from the distant stillness into sound and color—all entered his mood and blended indistinguishably with his thinking, like a fine symphony that acts as a medium supporting our spiritual wings.

Thus, the figure representing Mordecai's longing was mentally seen darkened by the excess of light in the aerial background. But in the inevitable progression of his imagination toward fuller detail, he ceased to see the figure with its back turned. It began to advance, and a face became discernible. The attributes of youth, beauty, refinement, Jewish birth, and noble gravity coalesced into a form and color that were hardly individual but typical. Gathered from his memories of faces seen among the Jews of Holland and Bohemia, and from paintings that revived those memories, this vision took shape. Reverently, it may be said of this mature spiritual need that it was akin to the boy's or girl's picturing of a future beloved; yet, the stirrings of such young desire are feeble compared with the passionate current of an ideal life straining to embody itself, made intense by resistance to imminent dissolution.

The visionary form became a companion and auditor, occupying a place not only in the waking imagination but in those dreams of lighter slumber wherein it is truest to say, "I sleep, but my heart waketh"—when the disturbing trivia of yesterday is charged with the impassioned purpose of years. Recently, the urgency of irremediable time, measured by the gradual choking of life, had turned Mordecai's trust into an agitated watch for the fulfillment that must be at hand. Was the bell on the verge of tolling, the sentence about to be executed? The Deliverer's footsteps must be near—the one who would rescue Mordecai's spiritual toil from oblivion and grant it an abiding place in the finest heritage of his people.

An insane exaggeration of his own value, even if his ideas were as true and precious as those of Columbus or Newton, might lead many to dismiss this yearning as the sublimer part of a man to declare, "If not I, then another," and to hold cheap the meaning of his own life. But the fuller nature desires to be an agent, to create, and not merely to observe; strong love hungers to bless, not merely to behold blessing. While there is warmth enough in the sun to sustain an energetic life, there will still be men who feel, "I am lord of this moment's change, and will charge it with my soul." With that mingling of inconsequence belonging to us all—and not unhappily,

Momentous events unfolded for Daniel Deronda on the very evening of his visit to the small Chelsea house, concerning the matter of Mirah's public identity. Yet, for the Meyrick family, the true ripple of this occurrence washed ashore two days later.

Around four o'clock, the familiar rhythm of the street was broken by the pause of wheels before the door. A knock accompanied by a ringing bell shattered the quiet domesticity of the neighborhood, where such signals usually belonged to the muffin-man. Inside, the atmosphere was one of cozy industry. The two rooms had been merged to accommodate Kate's latest drawing and a sprawling piece of embroidery that served as the centerpiece of the afternoon's needlework. Mrs. Meyrick bent over one corner, Mab occupied the center, and Amy stretched out at the far end.

Mirah, whose seamanship resembled nothing so much as a tailor-bird's makeshift nest due to her neglected training, acted as the group's reader. Seated on a camp-stool, she also served as the living model for Kate's title-page vignette—a fair public engrossed in the family's tea-table chronicles. She read aloud George Eliot's essay, "The Praise of Chimney-Sweeps," her voice clear and charming as the sisters laughed over the "innocent blackness" of the subject.

Then, the imposing knock returned, pulling their thoughts upward.

"My goodness!" exclaimed Mrs. Meyrick. "Could it be Lady Mallinger? Is there a grand carriage, Amy?"

"No, merely a hansom cab. It must be a gentleman," Kate replied dryly. "Hans claims the Prime Minister himself might ride in a hansom."

"Oh, oh, oh!" cried Mab. "Suppose it is Lord Russell!"

Five bright faces turned in amusement as the old maid-servant brought in a card, leaving the parlor door ajar. Bowing toward Mrs. Meyrick stood a figure entirely unlike the staid Premier: tall and physically commanding, dressed in kid gloves and kerseymere, with a massive face, flamboyant hair, and gold-rimmed spectacles. As Mrs. Meyrick glanced at the card, she recognized **Julius Klesmer**.

Even embarrassment could not have rendered the "little mother" more awkward, yet her sharp instincts instantly grasped the significance. Here was a giant of the world coming to *her* house, seeking *her* daughter, rather than demanding her attendance elsewhere. It was a sign of profound interest.

However, the moment Klesmer stepped inside, the cottage seemed to shrink. To him, the piano appeared a ridiculous toy, and the entire household felt as insignificant as a colony of mice in the Tuileries. His personality radiated vast horizons and a multitude of unseen audiences; indeed, everyone carries their inner theater within them, and those who gaze wider than their neighbors often appear vain. Klesmer was vain, though perhaps no more so than many men of heavy countenance whose self-importance springs forth startlingly, like a spear from a walking stick. His mannerisms were as natural to him as the length of his fingers; the only affectation he could possibly display would be feigned diffidence.

While his grandeur made Mab feel like a doll beside the toy piano, Klesmer surveyed the room with a keen, genuinely kind sensitivity. Having known homes no larger than this on the Bohemian outskirts, and having navigated the romantic struggles of small incomes in his own figurative homeland, he approached Mrs. Meyrick with utmost deference.

"I hope I have not taken too great a liberty," he began. "Being in the neighborhood, I ventured to save time by calling. My friend, Mr. Deronda, mentioned an understanding that I should have the honor of meeting a young lady here—Miss Lapidoth."

Klesmer had spotted Mirah instantly, yet with subtle politeness, he bowed broadly at the three sisters, feigning uncertainty regarding which one was the guest of honor.

"These are my daughters," Mrs. Meyrick said, gesturing toward Mirah. "This is Miss Lapidoth."

"Ah," Klesmer responded, his tone brimming with gratified expectation. He offered a radiant smile and a deep bow to Mirah. Unlike the others, she was not startled; a calm pleasure softened her features. She liked the look in his eyes, sensing that he would treat her with the stern kindness of a great musician and a benevolent man.

"You will not object to beginning our acquaintance with a song?" he asked, eager to bypass formalities.

"I shall be very glad. It is kind of you to listen," Mirah replied, moving toward the piano.

"Accompany yourself?"

"By all means," Klesmer agreed, settling himself where he could watch her clearly. Mrs. Meyrick, hiding her own insecurity, secretly hoped that seeing her would please the critic.

The other three girls' hearts hammered with anxiety, viewing Klesmer's listening frown as terrifying. They dared only glance at him furtively, fearing harsh criticism. They comforted themselves with the memory of Prince Camaralzaman, who had supposedly preferred Mirah's singing above all others. Mirah, however, seemed more at ease than usual, pouring her soul into a fine setting of Leopardi's *Ode to Italy*. She sang of ancient walls and columns, then moved to a mournful melody, building to a climax of devout triumph—an exultant allegro celebrating love and sacrifice.

When she finished, Klesmer paused briefly. "That is old Leo's music."

"Yes, he was my last master in Vienna," Mirah said with a melancholy smile. "So fierce and so good. He prophesied my voice was unfit for the stage. And he was right."

"*Continue*, if you please," Klesmer urged, making unintelligible sounds with his lips and shaking his long fingers.

The three sisters detested his silence, but Mrs. Meyrick grew alarmed. Determined to please, Mirah proceeded to sing several of Prince Radzivill's arrangements of Gretchen's songs from *Faust*. Only when she concluded did Klesmer rise, walk to the edge of the room, and return to the piano. Mirah stood waiting, hands clasped, meekly awaiting judgment.

Suddenly, his brow unknitted, his eyes beamed, and he extended his hand. "Let us shake hands: you are a musician."

Mab nearly wept with joy, and the sisters deemed him adorable. Mrs. Meyrick breathed a sigh of relief. But instantly, the frown returned. His long hand, palm up, reached out only to touch the back of Mirah's hand with a fingertip, his lip protruding slightly.

"Not for great tasks. No high roofs. We are no skylarks. We must be modest."

He paused, allowing the weight of his words to settle. Mab lost her admiration momentarily, thinking, *As if she showed the slightest hint of conceit!* Mirah remained silent, waiting for the verdict. Finally, Klesmer continued, "I would not advise—and I urge you not to pursue singing in any space larger than a private drawing-room. But you will excel there. In London, that is one of the finest careers available. Lessons will follow. Will you join me for a private concert at my house on Wednesday?"

"Oh, I shall be grateful," Mirah said, pressing her hands together. "I would rather earn my bread that way than through anything more public. I will strive to improve. What should I focus on?"

Klesmer emitted a series of guttural noises that sounded like words bitten in half before swallowing them, shaking his fingers rhythmically. Then, clearly, he spoke: "I shall introduce you to Astorga. He is the father of good singing and will guide you." Turning to Mrs. Meyrick, he added, "Mrs. Klesmer will call before Wednesday, with your permission."

"We shall consider that a great kindness," Mrs. Meyrick replied.

"You will sing to her," Klesmer instructed, returning his attention to Mirah. "She is a thorough musician, possessing a soul with more ears than you often find in a professional. Your singing will satisfy her. 'Vor den Wissenden sich stellen,' you know the rest?"

"'Sicher ist's in allen Fälle,'" Mirah recited promptly.

Klesmer nodded approvingly, "Schön!" and extended his hand for a final farewell. He had chosen the most delicate method of praise possible, earning the total esteem of the Meyrick girls.

Yet, as he prepared to leave, he suddenly fixed his gaze on Mab. "That young lady is musical, I see!"

Mab flushed crimson, feeling scorched.

"Yes," Mirah answered on her behalf.

"And she has a touch."

"Oh, please, Mirah—it's a scramble, not a touch!" Mab cried in anguish, terrified that this divine judge might now demand she play the piano. Her heart melted like wax.

But this was a trivial price for the amazement of her joy when Klesmer turned benignly to Mrs. Meyrick. "Will she like to accompany Miss Lapidoth and hear the music on Wednesday?"

"There could hardly be a greater pleasure for her," Mrs. Meyrick assured him. "She will be most grateful."

With a grand bow encompassing the three sisters, Klesmer departed. The scene was comical: the small room dominated by his magnificent inclinations toward the diminutive female figures, who looked like images slightly smaller than life-size, while the grave Holbein portraits on the walls stared

There exists within the human soul a latent faculty capable of manipulating circumstances. What might otherwise obscure or dim one's essence can be transformed into mere contingencies of grandeur, serving to elevate one's innate brilliance. Consider the moon on a silent summer evening, rising behind a dense, towering forest. Its light burns like an unconsumed fire through the green canopy, igniting the foliage until the dark shadows merge into a splendor indistinguishable from the moonlight itself, woven together by a power vast and serene.

Daniel Deronda emerged from his narrow Chelsea residence in a mood that demanded vigorous physical exertion to dispel what he termed the "fumes" of his irritation. Heading toward the city, the sight of the Chelsea Steps and the waiting boats instantly resolved his dilemma: he would abandon the passive confinement of a carriage. Instead, he hailed a wherry and took an oar.

His destination was Ram's bookshop. He had visited yesterday only to arrive too late for Mordecai's afternoon vigil, having been informed that the remarkable resident invariably appeared between five and six. Deronda felt a pressing need to deepen his acquaintance with this enigmatic inhabitant of the Cohen household. This familiarity was a prerequisite for reclaiming his ring; he feared their previous conversations ended too abruptly, leaving Mordecai's interest waning like a retractable drawbridge, threatening to seal off any future ease of communication. As the rhythm of rowing warmed his limbs and focused his mind on his errand and his intentions regarding Mirah, Deronda underwent his customary shift in perspective. He mentally stepped into the shoes of the man he had previously viewed merely as a tool for his own ends. He chided himself for resembling a recruiting sergeant who secures soldiers without caring for the dramas that bring them to him.

*"Suppose I obtain the information I crave from this man,"* Deronda mused, *"and he remains indifferent to revealing more of himself—or why he seems to harbor expectations of me that have gone unfulfilled? Such curiosity would fade. Yet, perhaps they passed each other like two ships crossing paths, each carrying an exile who would have recognized the other had they gazed face-to-face. Though there is little chance of a unique bond between me and this poor fellow, whose journey, I suspect, is nearly over... Still, I wonder if there is much of that profound mutual longing between strangers who exchange vacant glances, or even those who secretly desire one another's absence in a crowd. Regardless, one creates opportunities for connection by adopting the recruiter's strategy."*

As the wherry approached Blackfriars Bridge, where Deronda intended to disembark, the clock struck half-past four. The gray daylight was dissolving gloriously; the western clouds fractured into narrowing bands of purple before a sweeping expanse of saffron clarity. The sky held a monumental calm, but on the river, this light danced as a luminous motion—the alternating flashes of ripples, the sudden glow of a brown sail, the transition of laden barges from shadow to color—all responding actively to the brooding beauty above.

Now sufficiently heated, Deronda set down the oar and draped his Inverness cape over his shoulders. Just as he raised his head to fasten the final button, his eyes caught a familiar face peering over the bridge parapet. Bathed in the western light, the visage appeared with startling distinctness and brilliance—a striking emblem of physical emaciation and spiritual urgency. It was Mordecai. Having watched the westward horizon, Mordecai had spotted the approaching boat. Initially drawn by its movement, his gaze sharpened into a trembling anticipation, a presentiment seizing him until the near figure finally lifted its face toward him—the face of his visions. Immediately, Mordecai raised a white hand, beckoning repeatedly.

Anticipating recognition, Deronda had signaled without delay, and the reply was instant. Mordecai tipped his cap and waved it, feeling in that moment that his internal prophecy was realized. Obstacles, contradictions, all dissolved into a sense of completion flooding his soul with the satisfaction of a longed-for reunion. His exultation mirrored that of an experimenter witnessing the first stirrings of change corresponding to what his feverish concentration had foreseen. The anticipated friend emerged from the golden backdrop and signaled; this was reality; the remainder was destiny.

Within three minutes, Deronda had landed, settled his fare, and joined Mordecai, who stood perfectly still, waiting for him.

"I was delighted to see you standing here," Deronda said. "I intended to proceed to the bookshop to find you again. I was there yesterday—did they mention it?"

"Yes," Mordecai replied with simple gravity. "That was precisely why I came to the bridge."

To Deronda, this straightforward answer was profoundly mysterious. Did the peculiarities of this man truly stem from some form of mental alienation, as Cohen had hinted?

"You knew nothing of my presence in Chelsea?" Deronda asked after a pause.

"No; but I expected you to descend the river. I have been waiting for you these five years." Mordecai's sunken eyes locked onto Deronda's with a look of affectionate dependence, simultaneously pathetic and solemn. Deronda's sensitivity responded fully, despite his inability to accept that this strangely revealed relationship was founded on illusion.

"It will please me greatly if I can be of genuine use to you," Deronda answered earnestly. "Shall we take a cab to wherever you wish? You have likely walked enough with your short breath."

"To the bookshop," Mordecai said, turning back toward the river. "It will soon be time for me to be there. But first, look upstream. See the sky slowly fading. I have always loved this bridge; I stood upon it as a child. It is a meeting place for spiritual messengers. It is true what the Masters said: every order of existence has its angel—that is, the full message of each from afar. Here, I have listened to the messages of earth and sky. When I was stronger, I would linger watching the stars in the deep heavens. But this time, just about sunset, was what I loved best. It sank into me, dwelling with me—fading, slowly fading. It was my own decline. It paused, it waited, until finally it brought me my new life, my new self—one who will live when this breath is spent."

Deronda remained silent, deeply affected. His initial suspicion—that Mordecai might suffer from hallucinations or monomania due to a strained constitution—yielded to a more submissive expectancy. His nature was too expansive, too willing to conceive realms beyond his immediate experience, to settle quickly on the easy diagnosis of "madness" whenever a consciousness displayed such fullness and conviction where his own was empty. Consistent with his disposition, he preferred to meet claims of need rather than resist them. This claim radiated a solemnity from Mordecai that seemed to nullify his outward poverty, elevating him into authority as if he were a supernatural guide shedding a mean disguise to reveal a manifest Power. This impression was reinforced by a resolved quietude in Mordecai's demeanor, born of his conviction of fulfillment. After a moment of silence, Mordecai said, "Let us go now." Once seated in the cab, he added, "We will alight at the end of the street and walk to the shop. You may examine the books; Mr. Ram is departing shortly and will leave us alone."

It appeared this enthusiast was as cautious and attuned to the judgments of others as if he were the antithesis of enthusiasm known as "a man of the world."

As the cab rattled along, Mirah remained present in Deronda's mind amidst this strange experience, though he foresaw that the conversation would be dictated by Mordecai, not himself. He was no longer certain what questions he could pose. Reacting internally to his own mood, he thought, *"I suppose I am in a state of complete superstition, akin to awaiting a destiny that interprets an oracle. Yet, there must be a strong bond between us, for he feels it so intensely. Great heaven! What relation has proven more potent in the world than faith, even when mistaken? Than expectation, even when perpetually disappointed? Will my side of this relation prove disappointing or fulfilling? Well, if it is ever possible for me to fulfill, I will not disappoint."*

Ten minutes later, the two men entered the small, gas-lit bookshop. Possessing an intensity of consciousness as if they were undeclared lovers, they faced one another, each removing their hats instinctively, wishing to see the other fully. Mordecai leaned his back against the small counter, while Deronda stood against the opposite wall, barely four feet away. *If I could capture those two faces,* the narrator reflects, *as Titian captured contrasting types in 'The Tribute Money,' it would suffice. Imagine—the pathetic mark of consumption, paired with a brilliant glance, set upon a sharply defined structure of features reminiscent of a forsaken temple, lending an air of reluctant distance. Now imagine this on a Jewish face, naturally expressive of an eager mind—the face of a man little past thirty, yet bearing the weight of an age prolonged by suffering. His black hair and beard throw out the yellow pallor of his skin; his labored breathing marks his mobile nostrils; his wasted yellow hands are conspicuous on his folded arms. Give to that yearning, consumptive glance the look of a slowly dying mother when her beloved son visits her bedside, and the flickering power of gladness leaps forth as she cries, 'My boy!'—for the sense of spiritual perpetuation in another mirrors that maternal transfer of

CHAPTER XLI. "This, too is probable, according to that saying of Agathon: 'It is a part of probability that many improbable things will happen.'" —ARISTOTLE: *Poetics*.

Imagine the turmoil within a mind like Daniel Deronda's—a mind capable not only of profound feeling but of rigorous, active questioning. On the evening following his intense interview with Mordecai, the adventure that had unfolded would have seemed merely extraordinary to a youth of lesser sensitivity, sufficient to occupy his thoughts but not dominate them. Yet, for Deronda, the encounter had struck a chord so deeply that his intellect immediately recoiled, compelled to scrutinize the foundations of his emotions and determine how far he ought to resist their pull.

He felt a distinct alarm rising within him, born of the realization that he was being partially steered by Mordecai's energetic certainty and, even more so, by the old man's fervent trust. It was Deronda's nature to recoil from the moral blindness of undervaluing what had touched him closely, of failing to recognize in his own contemporary life a crisis that history would later deem momentous and sacred. Had he read of such an event occurring centuries past—in Rome, Greece, Asia Minor, Palestine, or Cairo—to a young man similarly situated, dissatisfied with neutrality and craving a deeper purpose, he would have found it entirely natural that such an incident left an indelible mark. Why, then, should he feel shame for his own agitated heart simply because he dressed for dinner, wore a white tie, and navigated a circle where possessing a conscience was often mocked as the solemn folly of taking oneself too seriously? That was the great bugbear of fashionable society: the belief that a lack of grave emotion equaled wit. Deronda despised this cowardice before modern ignorance.

Yet, he also feared surrendering his course to mere contagion without the consent of reason, or allowing a reverential pity for spiritual struggle to rush him down a dimly lit path. So, he asked himself: What had truly happened? He knew precisely what Sir Hugo would have answered: *"A consumptive Jew, driven by a fanaticism intensified by obstacles and impending death, has fixated on Deronda as the living embodiment of some visionary image born of wedded hope and despair. Despair of his own life, coupled with an irrepressible hope in spreading his fanatical beliefs. The case is perhaps odd, exceptional in form, but not rare in substance. Fanaticism is not uncommon; while perhaps less frequent than bankruptcy, it is abundant in all its guises. While Mordecai waited on the bridge for his visions to unfold, another man believed he held the mathematical key to the universe, surpassing Newton, viewing all physicists as conspirators; another claimed the metaphysical key, differing only by a hair's breadth from existing locks. Scattered everywhere are terrible figures, gifted with varying powers of speech and eyes either glittering or unnervingly dull, searching for the listener. Most possess volumes difficult to print, or impossible to read. This Mordecai possesses a more pathetic aspect, a more passionate and penetrating speech than typical monomaniacs; he is more poetic than a social reformer obsessed with geometric morals or an enthusiast for sewage systems. Still, he belongs to the same class. It is right and kind to indulge him slightly, to offer practical comfort, but what likelihood exists that his notions hold the value he ascribes to them? In such matters, a man of the world knows what to expect beforehand. Regarding Mordecai's conviction that he has found a new executive self, it may be preparing him for the worst disappointment of all—that which presents itself as final."*

Deronda's mind caught every negative whisper, repeating them distinctly to himself. This was not the first time he faced this question of the family resemblance among the heirs of enthusiasm—whether prophets, dreamers, "great benefactors," or devotees of phantom discoveries. From the first believer in his own unmanifested inspiration to the last inventor of a perpetual motion machine, the kinship of human passion and the uniformity of mortal scenery inevitably fill reality with burlesque and parody. Errors and follies have sacrificed countless martyrs. If one reduces the grandest type of man to an abstract statement of qualities, he enters dangerous company: say he is immovably convinced despite ridicule, like Copernicus or Galileo; so too is the contriver of perpetual motion. One cannot fairly judge spirits by such tests alone. To avoid sentencing the innocent to hemlock or exile, one needs the capacity to understand the specific subject-matter in which the immovable man is convinced, and a fellowship with human toil, both near and distant, to prevent us from scanning deep experiences lightly. Should we say, "Let the ages try the spirits"? No, for we are the beginning of the ages, justified only by just judgments formed in separate yet connected human hearts. Even steam engines required this condition; they remained in James Watt's mind until the time was right.

This train of thought was familiar enough to Deronda to save him from contemptuous prejudice against Mordecai, even though the Jew's communication carried a peculiar claim upon him, ushered in by a long-growing agitation. Rationally, this claim might seem dismissible as illusory or preposterous; yet, it was precisely this element that transformed Mordecai's appeal from a request for sympathy into a grasp on Deronda's struggling conscience. Consciences are not all cast in the same mold; they are voices of sensibilities as varied as our memories. Deronda's conscience included sensitivities beyond the common, expanded by his early habit of imaginatively inhabiting the experiences of others.

What claim did this eager soul make? "You must believe my beliefs—be moved by my reasons—hope my hopes—see the vision I point to—behold a glory where I behold it!" To treat such a demand as a direct obligation would be preposterous, bordering on dishonesty. Deronda, reflecting on the agitation of those moments, felt grateful that amidst his compassion, he had preserved himself from the bondage of false concessions. Furthermore, this claim rested on a supposition that might be—and probably was—in discordance with the full fact: the idea that Deronda was of Jewish blood. Was there ever a more hypothetical appeal? Since the age of thirteen, however, Deronda had associated his deepest emotional struggles with a similar pure supposition: that Sir Hugo was his father. By the light of this hypothesis, he had learned to subdue and cherish conflicting feelings. He was well-practiced in finding motives in concepts that might be disproven, and in considering revelations that could alter his understanding of duty. Being in a state of suspense, charged with emotive activity and scruple, was a familiar posture for his conscience.

Now, suppose that this wish-born belief in his Jewish birth, paired with Mordecai's extravagant demand for discipleship, foreshadowed an actual discovery and a genuine spiritual result. Suppose Mordecai's ideas achieved a real conquest over Deronda's conviction? It was conceivable that just as Mordecai needed and believed he had found replenishment in Deronda, so Deronda might receive from Mordecai's mind the complete ideal shape of that personal duty and citizenship which lay in his own thoughts like sculptured fragments, certifying a beauty yearned for but not yet traced. As this possibility arose in his meditation, he realized it would be labeled dreamy, and he began to defend it. If the influence he imagined submitting to came from an honored professor, a seat of learning, or a philosopher accepted as the voice of the age, would thorough receptiveness be ridiculed? Only by those who consider being obliged for an idea a sign of weakness, preferring to hint they have always held the truth in a more correct form than others' explicit statements. After all, what is there but vulgarity in using the fact that Mordecai was a poor Jewish workman, perhaps met on a sanded floor in the parlor of the *Hand and Banner*, as a reason to decide beforehand that no spiritual force within him could affect a white-handed gentleman?

There is a legend of Emperor Domitian, who, hearing of a Jewish family descended from David, summoned them in alarm but quickly released them upon seeing they had the hands of laborers. Conversely, a Rabbi stood confidently at the gates of Rome expecting the Messiah among the destitute. Both Emperor and Rabbi were wrong in trusting outward signs. Poverty and ragged clothes are no guarantee of inspiration, Deronda reasoned to his inward objector, yet they have accompanied it in remarkable cases. To rule out discipleship solely due to these factors would be mere dullness of imagination. A more plausible reason to reject discipleship was the strain of visionary excitement in Mordecai, turning his wishes into overmastering impressions and causing him to read outward facts as fulfillments. Could such a temperament accompany the wise estimation of consequences necessary to guard against fatal error, even for an ennobling motive? It remained to be seen if this rare conjunction existed in Mordecai: perhaps his nature fused a wise estimate of consequences within the fires of passionate belief, thereby determining the very consequences he envisioned. Inspirations of the world have often arrived thus; even strictly measuring science could not progress without that forecasting ardor which feels the agitations of discovery beforehand, sustaining a faith in preconception that surmounts experimental failure. In relation to human motives, passionate belief holds a fuller efficacy. Enthusiasm may possess the validity of proof; when it happens in one soul, it gives the type of what will one day become general. At least, Deronda argued, Mordecai's visionary excitability was hardly a reason to conclude beforehand that he was not worth listening to, save for pity's sake.

Suppose he had introduced himself as one of the strictest reasoners. Did they

"If there exist hierarchies of suffering, the people of Israel occupy the highest rung; if endurance of pain and the fortitude with which it is borne confer nobility, then the Jews belong to the aristocracy of every land; and if a literature is deemed rich because it possesses a handful of classic tragedies, what judgment passes upon a National Tragedy sustained for fifteen centuries, written and enacted by the heroes themselves?" — ZUNZ: *Synagogue Poetry of the Middle Ages*.

Daniel Deronda had recently read this passage by Zunz. As he walked toward the Cohens' home, the words struck him with a sharp contrast. The Cohens bore no visible mark of such distinguished sorrow or spiritual aristocracy. Mr. Ezra Cohen was not draped in the sublime pathos of the martyr; indeed, his appetite for acquiring money seemed blessed with a success that history records as the most irritating trait of Jewish greed throughout the ages of their scattering. This "Jeshurun" of a pawnbroker was no living symbol of the great Jewish tragedy. Yet, Deronda wondered, was there not something profoundly typical in the fact that a life like Mordecai's—a fragile vessel of national consciousness, struggling to breathe—was nestled within the self-congratulatory ignorance of the Cohens' comfortable prosperity?

When Deronda reappeared among them, a radiant gladness illuminated their faces. Mr. Cohen himself took the opportunity to remark that while a diamond ring—or even a little longer credit limit—would have generated more profit, he cared not a sixpence for that when weighed against the sheer pleasure of seeing the women and children delighted by a young gentleman whose first visit had been so agreeable that they had spoken of nothing else since. Young Mrs. Cohen expressed regret that the baby was asleep, followed immediately by delight that Adelaide was not yet in bed, earnestly urging Deronda not to linger in the shop but to proceed directly to the parlors to meet "Mother and the children." He gladly accepted the invitation, having prepared portable gifts: a set of paper figures for Adelaide and an ivory cup-and-ball toy for Jacob.

The grandmother sat with a deck of cards, playing "plates" with the children. A plate had just been tossed and landed intact.

"Stop!" cried Jacob, darting toward Deronda as he entered. "Do not step on my plate. Watch me throw it up again."

Deronda complied, exchanging a knowing smile with the grandmother. The plate soared several times before finally shattering; only then was the visitor permitted to approach and take his seat. He noted that the door from which Mordecai had emerged on his previous visit was now shut tight, though he intended to demonstrate his interest in the Cohens before revealing his deeper concern for their unusual lodger.

It was not until he had Adelaide on his lap, arranging the paper figures in a dance on the table, while Jacob practiced with the cup and ball, that Deronda asked, "Is Mordecai in just now?"

"Where is he, Addy?" asked Cohen, seizing a brief pause in his business to peer over.

"In the workroom," replied his wife, nodding toward the closed door.

"The truth is, sir," said Cohen, "we cannot fathom what has come over him these past days or two. He is always, as I might say, 'a little touched,' you understand"—he tapped his own temple—"not entirely rational in all matters, unlike you and me; yet he has been wonderfully regular and industrious, as a poor creature can be, and takes as much delight in the boy as anyone could. But these last couple of days, he has moved about like a sleepwalker, or sat as still as a wax figure."

"It is the disease, poor dear thing," said the grandmother, tenderly. "I doubt if he can withstand it much longer."

"No; I think it is merely something troubling his mind," corrected Mrs. Cohen the younger. "He has been turning over papers constantly, and when I speak to him, it takes him a long time to hear and respond."

"You may think us somewhat weak ourselves," said Cohen, offering an apology. "But neither my wife nor my mother would part with him even if he became a greater burden. It is not that we do not understand the situation, but it is our principle. Some fools conduct business at a loss without realizing it. I am not one of those."

"Oh, Mordecai carries a blessing within him," declared the grandmother.

"He has something wrong inside him," interjected Jacob, correcting his grandmother's error. "He said he couldn't talk to me, and he wouldn't accept a single piece of bread."

"Far from wondering at your feelings for him," said Deronda, "I already share a similar sentiment. I have recently spoken with him at Ram's bookshop—and in fact, I promised to call here so that we might walk out together."

"That explains it!" exclaimed Cohen, slapping his knee. "He has been expecting you, and it has affected him. I suppose he discusses his learning with you. How very kind of you, sir; for I daresay there is little to be gained from it, otherwise it would not have left him in his present state. But here comes the shop." Cohen hurried out, and Jacob, who had been listening uncomfortably close to Deronda's elbow, addressed him with obliging familiarity: "I'll fetch Mordecai for you, if you wish."

"No, Jacob," said his mother. "Open the door for the gentleman and let him go in himself. Hush! Make no noise."

The skillful Jacob seemed to embrace the role, turning the door handle as silently as possible. Deronda followed behind him, standing on the threshold. The small room was dimly lit by a dying fire and a single candle shaded from the draft. Upon the board beneath the window lay various articles of jewelry; in the corner beyond them, books were piled haphazardly. Mordecai sat on a high chair facing the board, his back to the door, his hands clasped together and resting on the surface, with a watch propped on a stand before him. He appeared in a state of expectant agony, akin to a prisoner awaiting delayed liberation. When he heard Deronda's voice saying, "I have come for you. Are you ready?", he instantly turned without a word, grabbed his fur-lined cap lying nearby, and moved to join Deronda.

Within moments, they were both in the sitting-room. Jacob, noting the shift in his friend's demeanor and expression, seized him by the arm and shouted, "Look at my cup and ball!" launching the ball high near Mordecai's face as a gesture meant to cheer a recovering patient. It was evident that the tension in Mordecai's mind had eased, for he smiled and remarked, "Fine, fine!"

"You have forgotten your greatcoat and comforter," said young Mrs. Cohen. She returned to the workroom to retrieve them.

"He has come back to life, do you see?" whispered Cohen, who had re-entered, speaking in an undertone. "I told you so; I am mostly right." Then, in his normal voice, "Well, sir, we must not detain you further, I suppose; but I trust this is not the last time we shall see you."

"Will you come again?" asked Jacob, stepping forward. "Watch, I can catch the ball! I'll wager I can catch it without stopping if you return."

"He has remarkably dexterous hands," observed Deronda, looking at the grandmother. "From which side of the family does he inherit them?"

The grandmother merely nodded toward her son, who replied promptly, "My side. My wife's family are not skilled in that manner. But bless your soul! Ours possess a sort of cleverness as durable as gutta-percha; you can twist it whichever way you please. There is nothing some elderly gentlemen will not accomplish if you set them to it." Cohen winked down at Jacob's retreating back, though it was doubtful whether this tactful allusion achieved its purpose, for its subject emitted a nasal whinnying laugh and stamped about, singing, "Old gentlemen, old gentlemen," in a chime-like rhythm.

Deronda thought, *I shall never learn anything definitive about these people unless I ask Cohen directly whether he lost a sister named Mirah when she was six.* The moment to confront this question did not yet seem easy for him to face. Nevertheless, his initial sense of repulsion regarding the commonness of these individuals began to soften into kinder feelings. Regardless of their unrefined manners and speech, he was compelled to acknowledge a certain moral refinement in their treatment of the consumptive workman, whose intellectual distinction they perceived chiefly as a harmless, silent rambling.

"The Cohens seem to have a genuine affection for you," said Deronda once he and Mordecai were off the doorstep.

"And I for them," was the immediate reply. "They possess the heart of the Israelite within them, though they are like horses and mules, lacking understanding beyond the narrow path they tread."

"I fear I have caused you some anxiety," said Deronda, "by my delay in keeping my promise. I wished to come yesterday, but found it impossible."

"Yes—yes, I trusted you. But it is true I felt uneasy, for the spirit of my youth has been stirred within me, and this body is not strong enough to bear the beating of its wings. I am as a man bound and imprisoned for many years: behold him brought to speech with his fellow and his limbs set free: he weeps, he totters, the joy within him threatens to break and overturn the tabernacle of flesh."

"You

For a few moments, an unusual silence settled over Mordecai's consciousness. He looked up at Deronda, not with confusion or surprise, but with a gaze heavy with peaceful contentment. Deronda rose and pulled his chair closer, ensuring no need existed to raise his voice. To Mordecai, this gesture felt like the gentle easing of a pillow beneath a weary head. Speaking in a low, intimate murmur—as if articulating thoughts meant only for himself—he began.

"In the teachings of the Cabbala, souls are reborn repeatedly into new vessels until they achieve perfection and purification. When a soul is freed from a worn-out body, it joins the fellow-soul that requires it, so that together they may be perfected and fulfill their earthly mission. Only then do they depart the mortal realm, leaving space for fresh souls drawn from the eternal reservoir.
