It is the lingering imperfection of those already born into mortality that delays the arrival of new souls and postpones the Messianic era. Thus, the mind gives shape to what is hidden, casting a shadow of the known, speaking truth even in parable. When my wandering soul is finally liberated from this tired body, it will unite with yours, and our shared work will be completed."

Mordecai's pause hung in the air, an appeal Deronda could not ignore. Struggling to find words that were truly honest, he spoke anyway, though for Mordecai's ear, every syllable carried an unspoken depth. "Whatever I can do, in clear conscience, to make your life meaningful, I shall do."

"I know," Mordecai replied, his tone radiating a quiet certainty that rendered further reassurance unnecessary. "I heard you. You see everything; you stand beside me on the mountain of vision, witnessing the paths of fulfillment that others refuse to acknowledge."

After a brief silence, Mordecai continued, his voice meditative. "You will carry on where my life was severed. I feel myself transported back to that fateful day when my life broke. The bright morning sun shone on the quay at Trieste; the garments of men from every nation glittered like jewels; boats pushed off into the water. The Greek vessel bound for Beirut was scheduled to depart within the hour. I was traveling with a merchant as his clerk and companion. I told myself I would behold the lands and peoples of the East, and speak with a fuller vision. I breathed then as you do now, without effort; I possessed the light step and enduring strength of youth. I could fast, I could sleep on hard ground. I had married poverty, and I loved my bride—for to me, poverty was freedom. My heart exulted as if it belonged to Moses ben Maimon, strengthened by the vigor of sixty years, knowing the vast work ahead.

"It was the first time I had traveled south; the soul within me felt its ancient sun. Standing on the quay, where the very ground seemed to emit light and the shadows wore an azure glory as if spirits were made visible, I felt immersed in a glorious life, so overwhelming that my own small, counted existence melted away, unnoticed. A great sob rose within me, like the rush of waters too powerful to resist—a bliss too strong to bear. So I waited for my companion, and I did not see him until he said, 'Ezra, I have been to the post; here is your letter.'"

"Ezra!" Deronda exclaimed, unable to hold back his shock.

"Ezra," Mordecai repeated, affirmative and absorbed in memory. "I was expecting a letter, for I wrote constantly to my mother. That sound of my name was like the touch of a wand, recalling me to the body from which I had been released, allowing me to mingle once more with the ocean of human existence, free from the pressure of individual isolation. I opened the letter; and the name came again as a cry that would have disturbed me even in the bosom of heaven, making me yearn to return to that sorrow. 'Ezra, my son!'"

Mordecai paused again, his imagination arrested by the grip of that distant moment. Deronda's mind hung suspended, breathless, as a strange possibility suddenly crystallized. Mordecai's eyes were cast downward in abstracted contemplation. Moments later, he resumed.

"She was a mother worthy of the saying, 'Her children arise and call her blessed.' In her, I understood the meaning of the Master who, perceiving his mother's footsteps, rose and declared, 'The Majesty of the Eternal draws near!' That letter was her cry from the depths of anguish and desolation—the scream of a mother robbed of her little ones. I was her eldest. Death had taken four babies one after another. Then came, late, my little sister, who was, more than all the rest, the desire of my mother's eyes. The letter was a piercing cry to me: 'Ezra, my son, I am robbed of her. He has taken her away and left disgrace behind. They will never come again.'"

Here, Mordecai lifted his eyes suddenly, laid his hand on Deronda's arm, and said, "Mine was the lot of Israel. Because of the father's sin, my soul must go into exile. Because of the father's sin, the work was broken, and the day of fulfillment delayed. She who bore me was desolate, disgraced, destitute. I turned back. On the instant I turned, the spirit of my mother and the spirit of her fathers, who held worthy Jewish hearts, moved within me and drew me forward. God, in whom the universe dwells, became within me the strength of obedience. I turned and traveled with hardship—to save the scant money she would need. I left the sunshine and journeyed into freezing cold. In the final stages, I spent a night exposed to snow and ice. That was the beginning of this slow death."

Mordecai let his eyes wander again and withdrew his hand. Deronda resolutely suppressed the questions rising in his throat. While Mordecai remained in this state of emotion, no other confession could be sought than what came spontaneously; indeed, Deronda felt a kindred emotion that made him dread his own speech as too momentous.

"But I worked. We were destitute—everything had been seized. And she was ill: the clutch of anguish was too strong for her, working alongside some lurking disease. At times she could not stand against the pounding of her heart, and the images in her brain became chambers of terror where she beheld my sister being raised in evil. In the dead of night, I heard her crying for her child. Then I rose, and we stretched forth our arms together and prayed. We poured our souls out in desire that Mirah might be delivered from evil."

"Mirah?" Deronda repeated, wishing to ensure his ears had not betrayed him to a forecasting imagination. "Did you say Mirah?"

"That was my little sister's name."

"After we had prayed for her, my mother would rest awhile. It lasted hardly four years, and in the minute before she died, we were praying the same prayer—I aloud, she silently. Her soul went out upon its wings."

"Have you never since heard of your sister?" Deronda asked, as quietly as he could manage.

"Never. Never have I heard whether she was delivered according to our prayer. I know not, I know not. Who shall say where the pathways lie? The poisonous will of the wicked is strong. It poisoned my life—it is slowly stifling this breath. Death delivered my mother, and I felt it a blessedness to be alone in the winters of suffering. But what are the winters now?—they are far off." Here Mordecai rested his hand on Deronda's arm again, looking at him with that joy of the hectic patient that pierces the observer to sadness. "There is nothing to wail in the withering of my body. The work will be done the better. Once I said the work of this beginning was mine, I am born to do it. Well, I shall do it. I shall live in you. I shall live in you."

His grasp had become convulsive in its force. Deronda, agitated as he had never been before—the certainty that this was Mirah's brother suffusing his own strange connection to Mordecai with a new solemnity and tenderness—felt his strong young heart beating faster and his lips growing pale. He shrank from speech. He feared that, in Mordecai's present state of exaltation (an already alarming strain on his feeble frame), to utter a word of revelation about Mirah would be disastrous. He feared to offer an answer below that high pitch of expectation, which resembled a flash from a dying fire, causing watchers to fear seeing it extinguish all the faster. His dominant impulse was to act as he had once before: he laid his firm, gentle hand on the hand that gripped him.

Mordecai's hand, as if possessing a soul of its own—for he was not distinctly willing to release it—relaxed its grasp and turned upward under Deronda's. As the two palms met and pressed together, Mordecai regained some sense of his surroundings and said, "Let us go now. I cannot talk any longer."

Indeed, they parted at Cohen's door without exchanging another word—only with another pressing of hands. Deronda felt a weight upon him that was half joy, half anxiety. The joy of finding in Mirah's brother a nature even more worthy of that relation to her carried the weight of solemnity and sadness; the reunion of brother and sister was, in reality, the first stage of a supreme parting—like a farewell kiss that resembles a greeting, that

Hear the seed sprout in the spring,
And for music to their dance,
Hear the hedgerows wake from trance,
Sap that trembles into buds,
Sending little rhythmic floods
Of fairy sound in fairy ears.

Thus all beauty that appears
Has birth as sound to finer sense
And lighter-clad intelligence.

But Gwendolen? She was thinking of Deronda much more than he was thinking of her. Often she wondered what his ideas were "about things," and how his life was truly occupied. Yet, a lapdog would be necessarily at a loss trying to frame the motives and adventures of doghood at large; and it was just as far from Gwendolen's conception that Deronda's life could be determined by the historical destiny of the Jews as it was for him to rise into the air on a brazen horse and vanish from her horizon as a twinkling star.

With all the sense of inferiority forced upon her, it was inevitable that she imagined a larger place for herself in his thoughts than she actually possessed. Only old and wise persons fail to see their own anxiety or elation reflected in other minds; and Gwendolen, with her youth and inward solitude, may be excused for dwelling on signs of special interest shown by the one person who had impressed her with a feeling of submission, and for mistaking the color and proportion of those signs in Deronda's mind.

Meanwhile, what would he tell her to do? *"He said I must get more interest in others, and more knowledge, and that I must care about the best things—but how am I to begin?"* She wondered what books he would recommend for her private study, recalling the famous writers she had either avoided or found utterly unreadable, with a half-smiling wish that she could mischievously ask Deronda if they weren't simply "medicine for the mind." Then she repented of her sauciness. When safely out of sight, she carried upstairs a miscellaneous selection—Descartes, Bacon, Locke, Butler, Burke, Guizot. As a clever young lady of education, she knew these authors were ornaments of mankind, feeling sure Deronda had read them, and hoping that by dipping into them all in succession, with her rapid understanding, she might reach a viewpoint nearer to his level.

But it was astonishing how little time she found for such vast mental excursions. Constantly she had to be on the scene as Mrs. Grandcourt, feeling herself watched by the exacting eyes of a husband who had found a motive to exercise his tenacity: making his marriage serve all the ends he chose, and with greater completeness the more he discerned any opposing will in her. Whatever rebellion might rage within her, she could not have made up her mind to failure in her performance. No feeling had yet reconciled her for a moment to any act, word, or look that would be a confession to the world. What she most dreaded in herself was any violent impulse that would make an involuntary confession. It was the will to remain silent in every other direction that had thrown such impetuosity into her confidences toward Deronda, to whom her thoughts continually turned as a help against herself.

Her riding, hunting, visiting, and receiving visitors were all performed in a spirit of achievement that served instead of zest and young gladness. Consequently, throughout Diplow during those weeks of the new year, Mrs. Grandcourt was regarded as wearing her honors with triumph.

"She disguises it under an air of taking everything as a matter of course," said Mrs. Arrowpoint. "A stranger might suppose that she had condescended rather than risen. I always noticed that doubleness in her."

To her mother above all, Gwendolen was bent on acting complete satisfaction. Poor Mrs. Davilow was so far deceived that she took the unexpected distance at which she was kept—as a result of Grandcourt's handsome provision for her—as a comparative indifference in her daughter, now that marriage had created new interests. Being fetched to lunch and then to dinner along with the Gascoignes, driven back soon after breakfast the next morning, and receiving brief calls where her husband waited for her outside on horseback or in the carriage, constituted all the intercourse allowed to her mother.

The truth was, the second time Gwendolen proposed inviting her mother with Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne, Grandcourt had first remained silent, then drawled, *"We can't be having those people always. Gascoigne talks too much. Country clergy are always bores—with their confounded fuss about everything."* That speech was full of foreboding for Gwendolen. To have her mother classed under "those people" confirmed her previous dread of bringing her too near. Still, she could not give the true reasons; she could not say to her mother, *"Mr. Grandcourt wants to recognize you as little as possible; and besides, it is better you should not see much of my married life, else you might find out that I am miserable."*

So she waived every allusion to the subject as lightly as she could. When Mrs. Davilow again hinted at the possibility of having a house close to Ryelands, Gwendolen replied, *"It would not be so nice for you as being near the rectory here, mamma. We shall perhaps be very little at Ryelands. You would miss my aunt and uncle."*

All the while, this contemptuous veto of her husband regarding any intimacy with her family, making her proudly shrink from giving them the aspect of troublesome pensioners, was rousing more inward inclination toward them. She had never felt so kindly toward her uncle, so disposed to look back on his cheerful, complacent activity and spirit of kind management—even when mistaken—as more of a comfort than the neutral loftiness that was every day chilling her. Here, perhaps, she was unconsciously finding some of that mental enlargement which was hard to get from her occasional dashes into difficult authors, who instead of blending with her daily agitations required her to dismiss them.

It was a delightful surprise one day when Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne were at Offendene to see Gwendolen ride up without her husband—accompanied only by the groom. All, including the four girls and Miss Merry, seated in the dining-room at lunch, could see the welcome approach. Even the elder ones were not without something of Isabel's romantic sense that the beautiful sister on the splendid chestnut—which held its head as if proud to bear her—was a sort of Harriet Byron or Miss Wardour reappearing out of her "happiness ever after."

Her uncle went to the door to give her his hand, and she sprang from her horse with an air of alacrity that encouraged that notion of guaranteed happiness. For Gwendolen was particularly bent today on setting her mother's heart at rest, and her unusual sense of freedom in being able to make this visit alone enabled her to bear up under the pressure of painful facts urging themselves anew. The seven family kisses were not so tiresome as they used to be.

*"Mr. Grandcourt is gone out, so I determined to fill up the time by coming to you, mamma,"* said Gwendolen, as she laid down her hat and seated herself next to her mother. Then, looking at her with a playfully monitory air, *"That is a punishment to you for not wearing better lace on your head. You didn't think I should come and detect you—you dreadfully careless-about-yourself, mamma!"* She gave a caressing touch to the dear head.

*"Scold me, dear,"* said Mrs. Davilow, her delicate, worn face flushing with delight. *"But I wish there was something you could eat after your ride—instead of these scraps. Let Jocosa make you a cup of chocolate in your old way. You used to like that."*

Miss Merry immediately rose to go out, though Gwendolen said, *"Oh, no, a piece of bread, or one of those hard biscuits. I can't think about eating. I am come to say good-bye."*

*"What! Going to Ryelands again?"* said Mr. Gascoigne.

*"No, we are going to town,"* said Gwendolen, beginning to break up a piece of bread but putting no morsel into her mouth.

*"It is rather early to go to town,"* said Mrs. Gascoigne, *"and Mr. Grandcourt not in Parliament."*

*"Oh, there is only one more day's hunting to be had, and Henleigh has some business in town with lawyers, I think,"* said Gwendolen. *"I am very glad. I shall like to go to town."*

*"You will see your house in Grosvenor Square,"* said Mrs. Davilow. She and the girls were devouring with their eyes every movement of their goddess, soon to vanish.

*"Yes,"* said Gwendolen, in a tone of assent to the interest of that expectation. *"And there is so much to be seen and done in town."*

*"I wish, my dear Gwendolen,"* said Mr. Gascoigne, in a kind of cordial advice, *"that you would use your influence with Mr. Grandcourt to induce him to enter Parliament. A man of his position should make his weight felt in politics. The best judges are confident that the ministry will have to appeal to the country on this question of further Reform, and Mr. Grandcourt should be ready for the opportunity. I am not quite sure that his opinions and mine accord entirely; I have not heard him express himself very fully. But I don't look at the matter from that point of view. I am

Behold the lady's carriage halt its progress,
With powdered lackeys and a charming bay;
She sweeps the matting, treads the crimson stair,
Her sole arduous function: "To be there."
Like Sirius rising o'er the silent sea,
She hides her heart in lustre, loftily.

So the Grandcourts arrived in Grosvenor Square, timely enough to receive a card for the musical gathering at Lady Mallinger's. There were matters of business that had compelled Sir Hugo to anticipate the arrival of his ill-favored nephew. It was merely the third evening since their return, yet Gwendolen made a rather distracted acquaintance with her new ceilings and furnishings, her mind preoccupied with the certainty that she would soon speak to Deronda again, and that she would finally meet the Miss Lapidoth who had endured so much, and was deemed "capable of submitting to anything in the form of duty."

Gwendolen had recalled nearly every word Deronda had spoken concerning Mirah, particularly that phrase which she repeated to herself with bitter intensity. She possessed an ill-defined consciousness that her own submission was something vastly different. Had anyone pointed it out to her, she would have been forced to admit that what she submitted to could not possibly take the shape of duty. Rather, it was submission to a yoke imposed upon her by an action she was ashamed of, worn with a strength of selfish motives that left no weight for duty to bear.

The drawing-rooms in Park Lane, adorned in white, gold, and pale crimson, were agreeably furnished and not yet crowded with guests. Before Mr. and Mrs. Grandcourt entered, more than half an hour of instrumental music had been succeeded by an interval of mingled movement and chatter. Klesmer was present with his wife, and in his generous regard for Mirah, he proposed accompanying her in a rendition of Leo's *"O patria mia,"* a piece he had previously recommended as more distinctive of her character than better-known selections. He was already seated at the piano, and Mirah stood there conspicuously when Gwendolen, magnificent in her pale green velvet and poisoned diamonds, was ushered to a seat of honor, well in view of them.

With her long sight and self-command, she possessed the rare ability to distinguish persons and objects instantly upon entering a full room. As she turned her gaze toward Mirah, she did not neglect to exchange a bow with Klesmer as she passed. The smile seemed to flash back like lightning to that morning when it had been her ambition to stand where the "little Jewess" now stood—to survey a grand audience from the higher rank of her talent—instead of finding herself one of the ordinary crowd in silk and gems, whose utmost performance was limited to admiration or criticism.

"He thinks I am in the right road now," murmured the lurking resentment within her.

Gwendolen had not caught sight of Deronda during her entrance. Seated now, engaging in chat with Sir Hugo, she glanced around with careful ease, bowing a recognition here and there, yet fearful lest an anxious-looking exploration in search of Deronda be observed by her husband and subsequently rebuked as something "damnably vulgar." Yet, all traveling, even that of a slow, gradual scan of a room, carries a liability to undesired encounters. Among the eyes that met Gwendolen's, forcing her into a slight bow, were those of the "amateur too fond of Meyerbeer," Mr. Lush, whom Sir Hugo continued to find useful as a half-caste among gentlemen. He stood near her husband, though Sir Hugo turned a shoulder toward him, pretending to listen to Lord Pentreath.

How was it that, at this precise moment, for the first time, a disagreeable sensation darted through Gwendolen—the idea that this man knew all about her husband's life? He had been banished from her sight, according to her will, and she had been satisfied; he had sunk entirely into the background of her thoughts, screened away by the agitating figures that kept up an inward drama in which Lush had no place. Suddenly, he reappeared at her husband's elbow, and there sprang up in her, like an instantaneously fabricated memory in a dream, the sense of his connection with the secrets that made her wretched.

Conscious of the effort required to turn her head away, she tried to continue her wandering survey as if she had seen nothing of more consequence than the picture on the wall, until she discovered Deronda. But he was not looking toward her. She withdrew her eyes without securing any recognition, consoling herself with the assurance that he must have seen her arrive. In truth, he was not standing far from the door with Hans Meyrick, whom he had carefully included in Lady Mallinger's guest list. Both were slightly more anxious than comfortable lest Mirah should not be heard to advantage. Deronda even felt himself on the brink of betraying emotion; Mirah's presence now linked with crowding images of what had gone before and what was to come after—all centering in the brother he was soon to reveal to her. He had escaped as soon as he could from the side of Lady Pentreath, who had spoken in her violoncello voice: "Well, your Jewess is pretty—there's no denying that. But where is her Jewish impudence? She looks as demure as a nun. I suppose she learned that on the stage."

Deronda was beginning to feel on Mirah's behalf something akin to what he had once felt for himself in his seraphic boyhood, when Sir Hugo asked him if he would like to become a great singer. An indignant dislike arose in him at the suggestion of her being remarked upon in such a free and easy manner, as if she were an imported commodity disdainfully purchased by the fashionable public. He winced the more because he knew Mordecai would feel that the term "Jewess" was taken as a sort of stamp, like the lettering on Chinese silk.

In this susceptible mood, he saw the Grandcourts enter and was immediately addressed by Hans regarding "that Vandyke duchess of a beauty." Deronda felt a transient renewal of his first repulsion from Gwendolen, as if her beauty and her failings were to blame for the undervaluing of Mirah as a woman—a feeling somewhat like class animosity, which affection for what is not fully recognized by others, whether in persons or poetry, rarely allows us to escape. To Hans, who admired Gwendolen with his habitual hyperbole, Deronda answered with a sarcasm that was not quite good-natured: "I thought you could admire no style of woman but your Berenice."

"That is the style I worship—not admire," said Hans. "Other styles of women I might make myself wicked for, but for Berenice I could make myself—well, pretty good, which is something much more difficult."

"Hush," said Deronda, under the pretext that the singing was about to begin. He was not so delighted with the answer as might have been expected, and was relieved by Hans's movement to a more advanced spot.

Deronda had never before heard Mirah sing *"O patria mia."* He knew well Leopardi's fine Ode to Italy (where Italy sat like a disconsolate mother in chains, hiding her face on her knees and weeping), and the few selected words were filled for him with the grandeur of the whole, seeming to breathe an inspiration through the music. Mirah singing this made Mordecai more than ever one presence with her. Certain words not included in the song nevertheless rang within Deronda as harmonies from the invisible: *"Non ti difende Nessun de' tuoi! L'armi, qua l'armi: io solo Combatterò, procomberò sol io"*—"Do none of thy children defend thee? Arms! bring me arms! alone I will fight, alone I will fall." These seemed the very voice of that heroic passion which is falsely said to devote itself in vain when it achieves the godlike end of manifesting unselfish love. That passion was present to Deronda now as the vivid image of a man dying helplessly, far removed from the possibility of battle.

Mirah was equal to his wishes. While the general applause sounded, Klesmer gave a more valued testimony, audible only to her: "Good, good—the crescendo better than before." But her chief anxiety was to know that she had satisfied Mr. Deronda; any failure on her part this evening would have pained her as an especial injury to him. Of course, all her prospects were due to what he had done for her, yet this occasion of singing in the house that was his home brought a peculiar demand. She looked toward him in the distance, and he saw that she did; but he remained where he was, watching the streams of emulous admirers closing round her, until presently they parted to make way for Gwendolen, who was taken up to be introduced by Mrs. Klesmer.

Easier now regarding "the little Jewess," Daniel relented toward poor Gwendolen in her splendor, and his memory returned, with some penitence for his momentary hardness, over all the signs and confessions that she too needed a rescue—one much more difficult than that of the wanderer by the river—a rescue for which he felt himself helpless. The silent question, "But is it not cowardly to make that a reason for turning away?" became the form in which he framed his resolve to approach her on the first opportunity and show his regard for her past confidence, despite Sir Hugo's unwelcome hints.

Klesmer, having risen as Gwendolen approached and being included by her in the opening conversation with Mirah,

"If anyone should press me to give a reason why I loved him, I feel it could be expressed in no other way than by answering, 'Because it was he, because it was I.' There exists, beyond what I can articulate, some inexplicable power that drew us together." — *Montaigne, On Friendship*

The moment had arrived to prepare Mordecai for the revelation of his restored sister and the impending change of residence, a necessity before Mirah's reunion with her brother. Mrs. Meyrick, to whom Daniel Deronda had entrusted every detail save Mordecai's unique bond with himself, had energetically assisted him in securing a suitable lodging in Brompton. It was located mere minutes' walk from her own home, ensuring that the siblings would remain within easy reach of her maternal care. Her delightful blend of Scottish fervor and Gaelic vivacity had successfully guarded the secret from the younger girls and from Hans; any accidental breach to them risked reaching Mirah prematurely, thereby spoiling the delicate opening of the endeavor designed to secure her independence—a form of dependence, however arduous and dignified, that society often labels "independence."

Both Mrs. Meyrick and Deronda possessed deeper, unspoken reasons for insisting that Mirah prove capable of maintaining herself. Perhaps the "little mother" was aided in her secrecy by a certain hesitation regarding the remarkable brother described to her. Certainly, if she felt any joy or anticipatory admiration, it stemmed solely from her faith in Deronda's judgment. The reality of consumption was a sorrowful fact that appealed to her tenderness, yet how could she truly celebrate an enthusiasm she could only view as Jewish pertinacity? Furthermore, she feared the introduction of a man whose conversation lacked the modern encouragement she valued, resembling instead the rigid discourse of Scott's Covenanters. Her mind was far from purely prosaic; she retained a share of Mab's delight in the romance of Mirah's story and her new domestic arrangement. Yet, the romantic or unusual elements of real life require adaptation. We stay awake at night to read of Sakya-Mouni, St. Francis, or Oliver Cromwell; but whether we would welcome someone exactly like them calling on us the next morning, or revealing themselves as a new relative, is a vastly different matter.

Moreover, Mrs. Meyrick had secretly hoped that the intensity of Mirah's feelings regarding Judaism would gradually subside, merging into the deepening current of loving exchange with her new friends. In truth, her favorite continuation of the romance had not involved the discovery of Jewish relations, but rather something more favorable to the hopes she harbored for Hans. Now, however, here stood a brother who would plunge Mirah's mind once again into the deepest dye of Jewish sentiment. She could not help telling Deronda,

"I am as glad as you are that the pawnbroker is not her brother. There are Ezras and Ezras in the world; indeed, it is comforting to think that not all Jews are like those shopkeepers who simply won't let you leave their premises. Besides, what he said to you about his mother and sister makes me bless him. I am sure he is good. But I have never liked anything fanatical. I suppose I heard too much preaching in my youth and lost my palate for it."

"I do not believe you will find that Mordecai imposes any preaching," Deronda replied. "He is not what I would call fanatical. I reserve the term 'fanatic' for a man whose enthusiasm is narrow and blinded, lacking a sense of proportion, becoming unjust and unsympathetic to those outside his own circle. Mordecai is an enthusiast; I use that word for the highest order of minds—those who care supremely for grand and universal benefits to humanity. He is not a strictly orthodox Jew and is full of allowances for others; his conformity in many matters is itself an allowance for the condition of other Jews. The people he lives with adore him, yet they cannot comprehend his ideas."

"Oh, well, I can live up to the level of the pawnbroker's mother and like him for what I perceive as good in him; for what I do not see, I will take your word. By your definition, one might argue that worshipping common-sense is fanatical; my poor husband used to say the world would be a poor place if it contained nothing but common-sense. However, Mirah's brother will have excellent bedding—I have seen to that—and I shall paste up this extra window to prevent drafts." (The conversation took place in the destined lodging.) "It is comforting to know that the householders are no strangers to me—no hypocritical harpies. Once the children know, we shall be able to make the rooms much prettier."

"The next stage of the affair is to tell everything to Mordecai and arrange his move, which may prove more difficult," Deronda noted.

"And will you tell Mirah before I say anything to the children?" Mrs. Meyrick asked. Deronda hesitated, and she continued in a tone of persuasive deliberation, "No, I think not. Let me tell Hans and the girls the evening before, and they will be gone the next morning?"

"Yes, that is best. But please do justice to my account of Mordecai—or Ezra, as I suppose Mirah will wish to call him. Do not assist their imaginations by referring to Habakkuk Mucklewrath," Deronda smiled, noting that Mrs. Meyrick herself had previously compared the Covenanters to the situation.

"Trust me, trust me," said the little mother. "I shall have to persuade them so hard to be glad that I shall convert myself. When I am frightened, I find it beneficial to have someone to be angry with for not being brave; it warms the blood."

Deronda might have been more argumentative regarding the view to be taken of Mirah's brother had he not been anxiously preoccupied with a more immediate and important task, one he wished to accomplish without wounding the Cohens. Mordecai, through a memorable response, had made it clear that he would be acutely sensitive to any inadvertence concerning their feelings. In the interim, he had met Mordecai at the *Hand and Banner*, but after careful reflection, he wrote to invite him to his own home the following evening. He requested to sit with Mordecai in his workroom for an hour, provided the Cohens did not consider it an intrusion. He phrased the invitation so that if there were any objection, Mordecai would accompany him elsewhere. Deronda hoped this approach would create a modest expectation, serving as a preparatory measure.

He was received with customary friendliness, accompanied by additional costumes worn by the women and children, and by a slight air of wonder among the elders—even Cohen himself could not allow it to pass the bounds of silence. The guest's dealings with Mordecai remained a mystery, one that Cohen was rather proud to believe lay outside the sphere of light enclosing his own understanding. However, when Deronda remarked, "I suppose Mordecai is at home and expecting me," Jacob, who had absorbed the family remarks, climbed onto his knee and asked, "What do you want to talk to Mordecai about?"

"Something very interesting to him," Deronda replied, pinching the boy's ear, "but that you cannot understand."

"Can you say this?" Jacob demanded, immediately reciting a string of rote-learned Hebrew verses with a wonderful mixture of throaty and nasal tones, nodding his small head at his listener with a sense of offering formidable evidence that might alter their mutual standing.

"No, really," Deronda answered gravely, "I cannot say anything like that."

"I thought not," Jacob declared, performing a dance of triumph with his small scarlet legs. He produced various objects from the deep pockets of his knickerbockers and returned them, signaling his resources, before running to the door of the workroom. He opened it wide, leaned his back against it, and announced, "Mordecai, here's the young swell"—a copy of his father's phrase, which he deemed fitting to cap his recitation of Hebrew.

He was recalled with hushed warnings by his mother and grandmother. Deronda entered, closing the door behind him, to find that a piece of carpet had been laid down, a chair placed, and the fire and lights tended to—all signs of the Cohens' respect. As Mordecai rose to greet him, Deronda was struck by the air of solemn expectation on his face, a demeanor that would have seemed entirely natural if his letter had declared a revelation regarding the lost sister. Neither spoke until Deronda, with his usual tenderness, drew the vacant chair from the opposite side of the hearth and seated himself near Mordecai.

"You are coming to tell me something that my soul longs for," Mordecai said in a tone of fervid certainty.

"It is true I have something very weighty to tell you—something I trust you will rejoice in," Deronda replied, guarding against the possibility that Mordecai had prepared himself for something entirely different.

"It is all revealed—it is made clear to you," Mordecai said more eagerly, leaning forward with clasped hands. "You are even as my brother that sucked the breasts of my mother—the heritage is yours—there is no doubt to divide us."

"I have learned nothing new about myself," Deronda stated. The disappointment was inevitable; it was better not to strain the feeling in a mistaken hope.

Mordecai sank back in his chair, momentarily unable to care what was actually coming. All day his mind had been in a state of tension toward one fulfillment. The reaction was sickening, and he closed his eyes.

"Except," Deronda continued gently after

One might almost envy Daniel Deronda the simple delight of procuring new clothes for Mordecai. As he imagined the effect of fine gray flannel shirts and a dressing gown resembling a Franciscan's brown frock upon Mordecai's lean frame, he felt a surge of creative pleasure, as if he were sketching a portrait. Much of this satisfaction stemmed from seeing his friend's brother through Mirah's eyes, shielding her from any jarring impressions by presenting a dignified image. Yet, even after preparing everything, a shadow of doubt crept in. Was he perhaps misreading her nature? Did he mistake the superficial for the profound? Could Mirah not appreciate the striking contrast between her brother's newfound appearance and his history of poverty, just as Daniel himself had once admired such distinctions?

However, practical concerns intervened. There were the Meyrick family to consider, specifically their potential prejudice against this "too Judaic" brother. Daniel realized he was driven to hide anything that might trigger immediate dislike in minds untouched by the sacred vision of true kinship—the ability to bathe every object in the solemn glow of loving reverence. This impulse would have been stronger had he listened to the conversation around Mrs. Meyrick's hearth that evening, after Mirah retired.

Hans, now settled in his Chelsea lodgings, had stayed late. Mrs. Meyrick, stoking the fire into a blaze, called out, "Now, Kate, extinguish your candle. Let us all gather cozy by the fire. Hans, dearest, cease laughing at those poems for the ninety-ninth time and join us. I have something wonderful to announce."

"As if I didn't know that, ma," Kate retorted, watching the girls step forward to warm their feet on the fender. Hans pushed his chair close, straddling it with his fists and chin resting on the backrest. "I've noticed it in the corner of your eye for ages, along with your feigned errands."

"Well, since you're so perceptive," Mrs. Meyrick declared clearly, "perhaps you know that Mirah's brother has been found!"

"Oh, confound it!" Hans exclaimed instantly.

"Hans, that is wicked," Mab protested. "What if we had lost *you*?"

"I cannot help feeling rather sorry," Kate admitted. "And where is her mother?"

"She is dead," Hans replied flatly.

"I hope the brother isn't a bad man," Amy ventured timidly.

"Nor a fellow covered in smiles and jewelry—a 'Crystal Palace Assyrian' with a hat on," Hans sneered, in the worst possible mood. "Are there such unfeeling children?" Mrs. Meyrick challenged, emboldened by the need for opposition. "Do you suppose Mirah cares the slightest bit about her brother's joy in this reunion?"

"You know, ma, Mirah hardly remembers her brother," Kate explained.

"People lost for twelve years should never return," Hans insisted. "They are always in the way."

"Hans!" Mrs. Meyrick reproached sharply. "If you had been lost for *twenty* years, I would have thought—"

"I said twelve years," Hans interrupted. "Anywhere near twelve years is the exact duration lost relatives should keep out of sight."

"But it is nice to find people; there is something to tell," Mab said, clasping her knees. "Did Prince Camaralzaman find him?"

Mrs. Meyrick proceeded to recount everything she knew in her neat, narrative style, uninterrupted. "Mr. Deronda holds the highest admiration for him," she concluded. "He seems to look right up to him. And he insists that Mirah is precisely the sister needed to understand this brother."

"Deronda is becoming perfectly preposterous about those Jews," Hans spat with disgust, rising and slamming his chair back. "He wants to do everything possible to encourage Mirah in her prejudices."

"Oh, for shame, Hans! To speak of Mr. Deronda that way," Mab chided. A subtle, unreadable tension flickered across Mrs. Meyrick's face.

"And now we shall never be all together," Hans continued, pacing with his hands deep in the pockets of his brown velveteen coat. "But we must invite this Prophet Elijah to tea, and Mirah will spend her days sitting amidst the ruins of Jerusalem. She'll be spoiled like an artist—mind that—and grow as narrow-minded as a nun. Everything will be ruined—our home and everything. I shall take to drinking."

"Oh, really, Hans," Kate snapped impatiently. "I truly think men are the most contemptible creatures in existence. Every single one demands everything go their way, or they become unbearable."

"Oh, oh, oh, it's dreadful!" cried Mab. "I feel as though ancient Nineveh has returned."

"I'd like to know what good comes of attending university and knowing everything if you remain so childish, Hans," Amy said. "You ought to accept the kindness Providence sends you. *We* shall have to endure him."

"I hope you will all enjoy the new *Lamentations of Jeremiah*—'to be continued in our next'—that's all," Hans said, grabbing his wide-awake hat. "There is no point in being one thing more than another if you must suffer the company of men with fixed ideas, staring blankly at you and expecting all your comments to serve merely as footnotes to their texts. If you're trapped under a petrifying wall, you might as well be an old boot. I don't feel like an old boot."

Abruptly, he leaned down to kiss his mother's brow in a hurried, desperate gesture, then offered a condescending nod to the girls on his way to the door. "Good night, little mother."

"Suppose Mirah knew how you are behaving," Kate whispered.

His reply was the sharp slam of the front door. Turning back to his mother, he added, "I *would* like to see Mirah when Mr. Deronda tells her. I know she will look so beautiful."

On second thought, however, Daniel Deronda had chosen to write a letter. Mrs. Meyrick received it the following morning, requesting that she reveal the news rather than waiting for Daniel's arrival. He omitted his true reason—that he wished to avoid reliving a narrative where he appeared central and benevolent—and instead suggested that he stay with Mordecai while Mrs. Meyrick brought Mirah for a visit. This arrangement would create a brief interval before the move Daniel anticipated Mirah would soon propose.

Secretly, Daniel felt a mixture of wonder and anxiety. How far would Mordecai, after years of solitary immersion in abstract ideas exacerbated by declining physical strength, allow himself to feel tenderness for his sister beyond mere religious duty? His affection for the Cohen family, particularly little Jacob, remained active and persistent; these had been part of his daily life for years. Yet, Deronda noted that Mordecai asked no new questions about Mirah, maintaining an unusual silence regarding all subjects and seemingly submitting passively to the impending changes in his personal life.

Mordecai obeyed willingly when dressed in the new clothes, but afterward, offering a faint smile to Daniel, he said, "I must keep my old garments nearby as a reminder."

When they sat waiting for Mirah, Mordecai spoke no word. He kept his eyelids lowered, yet his face and hands betrayed a restless agitation. Indeed, Mordecai was experiencing a peculiar nervous disturbance familiar only to those whose minds, long flowing strongly in one direction, are suddenly forced into a new or reopened channel. People susceptible to deep currents, whose strength has been absorbed by dormant biases, often dread interviews that imperiously revive the past with the same terror they feel facing a threatening illness. Joy may be present, but joy, too, can be terrifying.

Daniel caught the infection of this excitement. Upon hearing the doorbell, he rose to greet Mirah beforehand, unsure of exactly why he felt the urge. He was startled to find her wearing the hat and cloak in which he had first encountered her—the memorable cloak that had once been soaked and used as a winding sheet. She had descended stairs clad in this attire. When Mrs. Meyrick asked in a questioning tone, "You wish to go in that dress, dear?" Mirah replied, "My brother is poor, and I want to look as much like him as I can, else he may feel distant from me"—presuming she would meet him in his workman's garb.

Daniel could offer no comment, feeling a secret shame for his own fastidious preparations. They shook hands silently; Mirah looked pale and awe-struck. As Daniel held the door open for her, Mordecai had risen, his eyes turning eagerly toward the entrance. Mirah took only two or three steps before stopping. They stared at one another, motionless. It was less their physical presence that registered than a spiritual connection; they were meeting first in memory, a bond deeper than mere touch.

Mirah broke the silence first, standing where she was. "Ezra," she said, using the exact same tone she employed when describing her mother's call to him.

With a sudden movement, Mordecai stepped forward and placed his hand on her shoulders. He was a head taller, looking down at her tenderly. "That was our mother's voice," he said softly. "Do you remember her calling me?"

"Yes," Mirah answered, "and how you responded—'Mother!'—and I knew you loved her."

Mirah threw her arms around her brother's neck, clasping her small hands behind it, drawing his face down to kiss it with childlike abundance. Her hat fell backward, revealing all

It is a difficult and poorly rewarded endeavor to arrange every event according to the rules of our own safety, as Machiavelli wisely noted regarding Cesare Borgia. He supposedly calculated every contingency following his father's death, guarding against every misfortune except one: he never considered that his own demise might swiftly follow his father's.

Grandcourt's significance in this kingdom was of a grandly passive nature, rooted solely in his inherited lands. Political shifts and social reforms brushed against him only through the lens of his rental income. One need not consult his biography to learn about Schleswig-Holstein, Bismarck's policies, trade unions, household suffrage, or recent financial panics. He skimmed the leading newspapers on these subjects, yet his views lacked breadth. He lumped together all Germans, all merchants, and all voters prone to using inferior soap under the single label of "brutes." Beyond casting sidelong glances from beneath his lashes at anyone mentioning these issues and maintaining a silence designed to unsettle timid thinkers, he took no action.

However, within his own sphere of influence, Grandcourt displayed qualities reminiscent of the most triumphant continental diplomacy.

Not a movement of Gwendolen relative to Deronda escaped his notice. He would deny being jealous, for jealousy implies a doubt in one's own power to prevent what one has resolved against. It pained him not that his wife preferred another man's company to his own, but that she failed to recognize her situation as clearly as one recognizes a locked handcuff—that her inclinations were powerless to contradict his will. While his entry into marriage may have been marked by vacillating whimsy, his interpretation of the marital bond was rigid. He had not regretted his union; it had injected purpose into his life, offering new objectives for his will. He had not regretted his choice. His tastes were fastidious, and Gwendolen satisfied them. He desired a wife elevated by his rank, commanding admiration through her bearing and beauty, possessing perfectly shaped nails, and lacking overly large or red earlobes. Furthermore, she must not be a simpleton incapable of spirited replies. These standards may seem excessive to modern romantics who struggle to fall in love due to a lack of specific criteria, yet few match his satisfaction in securing a wife willing to fly into a rage if provoked, and who had been persuaded to marry him by motives other than passionate attachment. For those who value command over love, there is no reason for the mindset to shift precisely at the moment of matrimony.

Grandcourt did not believe he had chosen the wrong wife. Having assumed the role of husband, he refused to be deceived or appear pitiable. This was his mental state—not jealousy, though his behavior often mirrored it as closely as yellow resembles yellow, a hue caused by vastly different origins.

He arrived in London earlier than usual to attend to legal matters regarding his will, mortgage transfers, and the arrangement with his uncle concerning the succession to Diplow, a deal secured by the lure of immediate cash. Another benefit of his city visit was presenting himself alongside the beautiful bride he had selected despite societal expectations. Though Grandcourt claimed he cared not a whit for anyone's admiration, this apathy, like desire itself, required an audience. One must look stonily at smiling faces; the smiles must exist, and the observers must be present. Those who complain of humanity's contemptibility often forget this fundamental truth. In town for the first time with his wife, Grandcourt expanded his abstention from cursing into a series of splendid receptions, conspicuous drives, and distinguished appearances. He wished for her to be sought after; he enjoyed watching "fellow" gentlemen eagerly converse with her and escort her within his view. He even tolerated a certain haughty coquetry on her part. Yet, what he detested were her interactions with Deronda.

Following the musical gathering at Lady Mallinger's, where Grandcourt watched the conversation on the settee with the intensity of a hawk, it was characteristic of him to invite Deronda along with the Mallingers, carefully avoiding any hint that Deronda's presence mattered to him. He refrained from commenting directly on Gwendolen's behavior that night, fearing his disgust would be too overt for his pride. A few days later, however, he remarked casually, regardless of relevance:

"Nothing makes a woman more awkward than staring after people and displaying tempers in public. A woman must possess good manners. Otherwise, appearing with her is unbearable."

Gwendolen applied this comment to herself, alarmed by the prospect of being called awkward. Like her, she preferred her distaste to extend even to her admirers. The looming threat of his rebuke intensified her anxiety regarding any encounter with Deronda. The novelty and excitement of her city life resembled the frantic pace of foreign travel; despite inner despondency, there was a program to fulfill, providing gratification to her multifaceted self. Yet, whenever the rare opportunity arose to speak with Deronda, the experience had a diffusing effect on her consciousness, magnifying their exchanges and inflating the importance she imagined they held in his mind. How could Deronda avoid this? He certainly did not shun her; rather, he subtly signaled through delicate indirect means that her trust in him was not indiscreet, as it had not diminished his respect. Moreover, he enjoyed her proximity. She was more than a puzzle; she was a lovely woman, and he harbored a concern for her mind and fate. Though futile, this solicitude burdened him as a responsibility, perhaps heightened by the fact that, when he dared envision his own future, it lay distant from this splendid, sorrowful creature. Because he had once impulsively arrested her attention—as if seizing her arm to warn her from stepping into danger—she had turned to him with a persistent, pleading need.

There was one instance where Grandcourt stirred a feeling in Gwendolen that he wished to suppress without appearing indifferent: the matter of Mirah. Gwendolen's inclination to pursue singing lessons lingered as a form of obedience to Deronda's advice, but day succeeded day in a void of perceived leisure, typical of lives devoid of structured work. The constant pressure of Grandcourt's presence and surveillance flattened every effort to the level of the boredom his manner exuded. His negative mind was as pervasive as fog, clinging to all objects and ruining all connection.

One morning during breakfast, driven by a recurring urge to revive her old spirit, Gwendolen dabbled prettily with her prawns, refusing to eat them.

"I intend to become accomplished while we are in town," she said, "and take singing lessons."

"Why?" Grandcourt asked, languidly.

"Why?" Gwendolen echoed, feigning sauciness. "Because I cannot eat *pâté de foie gras* to induce sleep, I cannot smoke, and I cannot visit the club to make me wish to leave—I crave a variety of ennui. When are you free amidst your lawyers and guests for me to receive lessons from that little Jewess, whose singing is currently all the rage?"

"Whenever you like," Grandcourt replied, pushing his plate aside and leaning back. He fixed her with his most reptilian gaze while playing with the ears of the tiny spaniel on his lap—a dog Gwendolen disliked for its fawning nature.

Then, in a lazy tone, he added, "I fail to see why a lady should sing. Amateurs make fools of themselves. A lady cannot risk exposing herself in company. Nor does one wish to hear squalling in private."

"I appreciate frankness; it seems to me a husband's greatest charm," Gwendolen said, lifting her chin slightly as she averted her eyes. She lifted a prawn, preferring its boiled innocence to the lizard's stare. "But," she continued, swallowing her humiliation, "I assume you do not object to Miss Lapidoth singing at our party on the fourth? I intended to engage her. Lady Brackenshaw had her, you know, as did the Raymonds, who are meticulous about their music. And Mr. Deronda, being a musician and an excellent judge, states there is no singing of better taste for a drawing room. I consider his opinion authoritative."

She aimed to throw a small stone at her husband with this remark.

"It is highly indecent of Deronda to praise that girl," Grandcourt said, his tone indifferent.

"Indecent!" Gwendolen exclaimed, flushing and fixing him with startled wonder, unable to process the potential falsity of the accusation—"to praise."

"Yes; especially given her patronage by Lady Mallinger. He should keep silent about her. Men can discern his relationship to her."

"Men who judge others by their own standards," Gwendolen retorted, turning pale after her flush, instantly struck by terror at her own words.

"Of course. And a woman should accept their judgment; otherwise, she risks running her head into the wrong place," Grandcourt said, acutely aware of pinching her with his words. "Do you take Deronda for a saint?"

"Oh, dear no!" Gwendolen cried, summoning her nearly miraculous power of self-control in a high, hard tone. "Only a little less of a monster."

She rose, pushed her chair away without haste, and exited the room with the cautious demeanor of someone fearful of revealing they had consumed too much wine. Locking herself in her dressing room, she sat for some time, pale and quiet, resembling her departure from the breakfast room. Even after reading the venomous letter, she had experienced fewer cruel sensations than now; emotion had reached an acute point indistinguishable from physical sensation. Unlike what she believed, Deronda appeared to her not as a trusted friend, but as a hideous apparition, affecting her independently of its origin

In the depths of his spirit, a higher justice prevailed, transforming gratitude into a triumph over lingering resentment. This capacity for magnanimity marked the regal nature within him—a soul endowed with a broader life and a fuller potential for joy. Unlike those sharp-witted individuals who revel in dissecting virtue until it dissolves into insignificance, dismissing kindness as unworthy of thanks, Deronda possessed a different strength. These cynics, often masquerading as humble devotees of love and trust, ultimately beg from a world that hoards its own humanity.

As he parted ways with Gwendolen, Deronda carefully withheld the words, *"I shall not see you again for a long time; I am leaving."* He feared that Sir Hugo would interpret such a statement as significant to her, whereas the truth was far more personal. He was departing under circumstances of profound gravity to himself. When he finally set out to keep his promise of visiting her, he carried within him a solemn emotion that stirred the deepest currents of his existence.

Sir Hugo had summoned him to his chambers with a terse note: *"Come immediately. Something has happened."* Upon entering the baronet's study, however, Deronda found not the distress he had anticipated, but a grave affection. Sir Hugo extended his hand, and Deronda grasped it, speaking in a tone that blended restored confidence with inquiry: "Is there nothing here to cause you grief, sir?"

There was an unusual weight in Sir Hugo's gaze and a subdued tremor in his voice as he replied, "No, Dan, no. Sit down. I have something to say." Deronda complied, though a heavy presentiment weighed upon him. It was exceedingly rare for the baronet to display such intense seriousness.

"It does not grieve me, my boy," Sir Hugo continued gently. "At least, not in a way that should trouble you deeply. But I confess, I scarcely expected this specific event to occur. There have been reasons why I never prepared you for it, and why I kept silent regarding your parentage. Yet, I strove in every manner to ensure that this secret remained an injury to none."

Deronda sat in stunned silence, unable to articulate that he had never truly felt it as an injury. Even if that sentiment were true, his throat tightened against the thought. More than anyone else could possibly comprehend hung suspended in this fragile moment, waiting for the veil of secrecy to tear apart. Sir Hugo observed the pallor draining from Daniel's usually noble face, noting how his lips were pressed together in visible pain.

"I acted in obedience to your mother's wishes," Sir Hugo explained, his anxiety deepening as if fearing further wounds. "Her desire was for secrecy. But now, she desires to remove it. She wishes to see you. I will place this letter in your hands; you may read it later. It contains only her instructions on what you must do and where you may find her."

He handed over a letter written on foreign stationery. Deronda tucked it safely into his breast pocket, relieved not to be forced to confront its contents instantly. The emotional turmoil on Daniel's face had begun to unsettle the baronet's composure as well. Finding it difficult to proceed, Sir Hugo fell silent, allowing the tension to build.

Then, a question rose in Deronda's heart—the most arduous in the world to voice. Yet, he could not endure the delay. This was a sacramental instant; if he allowed it to pass, he might lose the unique spiritual alignment required to speak these words and receive their answer. For several moments, their gazes dropped, and it seemed as though unspoken thoughts drifted through the air between them. Finally, looking up at Sir Hugo, Deronda spoke with a trembling reverence, dreading that his phrasing might inadvertently suggest a reproach for years of stifled affection: "Is my father still alive?"

The answer came immediately, low and emphatic: "No."

A complex storm of emotions swept through the room, making it impossible to disentangle pure joy from acute sorrow. A new light had illuminated the past for Sir Hugo as well during this intimate exchange. After a silence that left Deronda feeling as though his old beliefs had dissolved before he could embrace a new faith, the baronet confessed, "Perhaps I was wrong, Dan, to undertake what I did. Perhaps I enjoyed having you entirely to myself a little too much. But if you have suffered any pain that I could have alleviated, I ask your forgiveness."

"The forgiveness has long been granted," Deronda responded softly. "My greatest pain has always stemmed from someone else—someone I never knew, whom I am now destined to know. It has never prevented me from feeling a profound affection for you, a bond that has constituted a vast portion of the life I remember."

Driven by a single, powerful impulse, the two men clasped hands for a brief, poignant moment.

"If some mortal, born too soon, / Were laid away in some great trance—the ages / Coming and going all the while—till dawned / His true time's advent; and could then record / The words they spoke who kept watch by his bed, / Then I might tell more of the breath so light / Upon my eyelids, and the fingers warm / Among my hair. Youth is confused; yet never / So dull was I but, when that spirit passed, / I turned to him, scarce consciously, as turns / A water-snake when fairies cross his sleep."
—*Browning: Paracelsus*.

These lines prefaced the letter Sir Hugo placed into Daniel Deronda's hands.

My good friend and yours, Sir Hugo Mallinger, will have informed you that I wish to see you. My health is shaken, and I desire there should be no time lost before I deliver to you what I have long withheld. Let nothing hinder you from being at the *Albergo dell'Italia* in Genoa by the fourteenth of this month. Wait for me there. I am uncertain when I shall be able to make the journey from Spezia, where I shall be staying. That will depend on several things. Wait for me—the Princess Halm-Eberstein. Bring with you the diamond ring that Sir Hugo gave you. I shall like to see it again.

—Your unknown mother,

The letter's colorless wording offered Deronda no clue to what awaited him. Yet, he could not refuse to accept Sir Hugo's reticence, which seemed to imply a pledge not to anticipate his mother's disclosures. Furthermore, the realization that his lifelong conjectures regarding his parentage had been mistaken checked any further speculation. Deronda could not stop his imagination from soaring quickly over the landscape of possibilities, but he refused to entertain any single scenario as more probable than another. To do so would be to nurse a specific desire or aversion into dominance, rather than simply preparing himself with resolve to face the truth, however it might reveal itself.

In this state of mind, he could not explain to anyone the reason for his impending absence, especially not to Mordecai, whom the news would affect as powerfully as himself, albeit differently. If he were to declare, "I am going to learn the truth about my birth," Mordecai's hope would gather a momentum that might prove painfully dangerous. To avoid fueling such suppositions, Deronda framed his journey as undertaken solely by Sir Hugo's wish. He threw as much indifference as he could muster into his announcement, stating he was uncertain of its duration but expecting it to be very short.

"I will ask to have the child Jacob stay with me," Mordecai said, comforting himself with this arrangement after casting his first mournful glances.

"I will drive round and ask Mrs. Cohen to let him come," Mirah replied.

"The grandmother will deny you nothing," Deronda assured them.

"I'm glad you were a little wrong as well as I," he added, smiling warmly at Mordecai. "You thought that old Mrs. Cohen would not bear to see Mirah."

"I undervalued her heart," Mordecai responded. "She is capable of rejoicing that another's plant blooms though her own be withered."

"Oh, they are dear good people; I feel as if we all belonged to each other," Mirah said, a tinge of merriment softening her smile.

"What should you have felt if that Ezra had been your brother?" Deronda asked mischievously, slightly provoked that she had taken kindly to people who had caused him such prospective annoyance on her account.

Mirah looked at him with a slight surprise for a moment before saying, "He is not a bad man—I think he would never forsake anyone." As she spoke, she blushed deeply. Glancing timidly at Mordecai, she turned away to attend to some occupation. Her father was in her mind, a subject on which she and her brother shared a painful, mutual consciousness.

"If he should come and find us!" was a thought that sometimes made the daylight of the street seem as shadowy as a haunted forest to Mirah, where every turn screened an imaginary apparition. Deronda sensed her involuntary allusion and understood the flush. How could he be slow to recognize feelings that now seemed nearer to his own than ever? For the words in his mother's letter implied that his filial relationship was not to be freed from painful conditions; indeed, singularly enough, the letter that had brought his mother closer as a living reality had simultaneously cast her into greater remoteness within his affections.

The tender yearning for a being whose life might have been worsened by lacking his care and love, the image of a mother who had not received all her due—whether of reverence or compassion—had long been secretly present in his observations of the women he encountered. Yet, it now seemed that this picturing of his mother fitted the facts no better than his former conceptions about Sir Hugo. He wondered how it was that when his mother's very handwriting arrived with words holding her actual feeling, his affections had suddenly shrunk into a state of comparative neutrality toward her. A veiled figure with enigmatic speech had pushed aside the image that, despite uncertainty, his clinging thought had gradually molded into the object of his tenderness and dutiful longing.

When he finally set off for Genoa, the interest truly uppermost in his mind had hardly so much to do with his mother as with Mordecai and Mirah.

"God bless you, Dan!" Sir Hugo had said when they shook hands. "Whatever else changes for you, it can't change my being the oldest friend you have known, and the one who has all along felt the most for you. I couldn't have loved you better if you'd been my own—only I should have been better pleased with thinking of you always as the future master of the Abbey instead of my fine nephew; and then you would have seen it necessary for you to take a political line. However—things must be as they may."

It was a defensive movement on the baronet's part to mingle purposeless remarks with the expression of serious feeling.

Upon arriving at the *Italia* in Genoa, no Princess Halm-Eberstein was present. However, on the second day, a letter arrived stating that her arrival might occur within a week or might be deferred by a fortnight or more. She was under circumstances making it impossible to fix her journey precisely and entreated him to wait as patiently as he could.

With this indefinite prospect of suspense hanging over matters of supreme importance, Deronda undertook the difficult task of seeking amusement on philosophical grounds, hoping to quiet his excited feelings and lend patience strength for the weary road ahead. His previous visit to the superb city had been cursory, leaving much to discover beyond the prescribed rounds of sightseeing. He spent the cooler hours wandering observantly through the streets, the quay, and the environs, often taking a boat to enjoy the magnificent view of the city and harbor from the sea.

All sights, all subjects—even the anticipated meeting with his mother—found a central union in Mordecai and Mirah, and the ideas immediately associated with them. Among the thoughts that most filled his mind while his boat drifted within view of the grand harbor was that of the multitudinous Spanish Jews centuries ago, driven destitute from their homes, allowed to land from crowded ships only for a brief rest on this grand quay of Genoa. They overspread it with a pall of famine and plague—dying mothers clutching dying children, fathers and sons gazing at each other's haggardness, like groups from a hundred Hunger Towers turned out beneath the midday sun.

Inevitably, dreamy constructions of a possible ancestry for himself wove themselves together with these historic memories, which had begun to hold a new interest since his discovery of Mirah. Now, under the influence of Mordecai, they became irresistibly dominant. He would have sealed his mind against such constructions if it had been possible, and he had never yet fully admitted to himself that he wished the facts to verify Mordecai's conviction. He inwardly repeated that he had no choice in the matter and that wishing was folly; nay, on the question of parentage, wishing seemed part of that meanness which disowns kinship—it was a disowning by anticipation. What he had to do was simply to accept the fact. And he had really no strong presumption to go upon now that he was assured of his mistake regarding Sir Hugo. There had been a resolved concealment that rendered all inference untrustworthy, and the very name he bore might be false. If Mordecai was wrong—if he, the so-called Daniel Deronda, was held by ties entirely aloof from the course his friend's pathetic hope had outlined—he would not say "I wish"; but he could not help feeling on which side the sacrifice lay.

Across these two importunate thoughts, which he resisted as much as one can resist anything in that unstrung condition belonging to suspense, came continually an anxiety he made no effort to banish. Instead, he dwelt on it with a mournfulness that often seems the best atonement we can offer to one whose need we have been unable to meet. The anxiety was for Gwendolen.

In the wonderful mixtures of human nature, there exists a feeling distinct from that exclusive passionate love of which some men and women (by no means all) are capable, yet which is neither friendship nor mere benevolent regard, whether admiring or compassionate. A man, for instance, hardly represents this shade of feeling toward a woman more nearly than in

Errina sat holding the spindle, her thick coils of raven hair framing a face of deepest agate. With a sorrowful startle, she gazed at the surging visions of her fate: to spin the byssus in weary, insect-like toil while gods and men around her forged legends worthy of song.

When Daniel Deronda stepped into the antechamber of his mother's apartment in the *Italia*, a familiar tremor of boyish anxiety seized him. Two servants stared at him with marked surprise; the doctor his mother had summoned was a striking young man whose very presence seemed to adorn the severe lines of his evening wear. Yet, Deronda paid them no mind until the inner door swung open, revealing a figure waiting at the far end of the vast room.

She was draped in black lace that hung loosely from the summit of her whitening hair down to a long train trailing behind her tall form. Her arms, bare to the elbow save for rich bracelets, were folded before her, and the poised tilt of her head rendered her visage more handsome than reality warranted. Deronda felt no hesitation before stepping forward, taking the hand she offered and pressing it to his lips. She retained his grip, studying him intently, while his own overwhelming awareness was of her piercing gaze and a face so fluid it seemed capable of transforming into someone entirely different in the next instant. Even as she examined him, a subtle interplay of brow and nostril formed a silent language. Paralyzed, unable to guess what emotional display she required, Deronda flushed like a schoolgirl, yet marveled at his own lack of profound reaction. He had rehearsed countless idealized reunions with his mother in his mind, imagining them more vivid than this stark reality. He could not even predict the tongue she would use. Suddenly, she released his hand, placing both palms upon his shoulders. In doing so, her face flashed with a radiant admiration that smoothed every wrinkle, restoring a youthful glow.

"You are a beautiful creature!" she murmured in a low, melodic voice, her syllables possessing a foreign yet pleasing cadence. "I knew you would be." She kissed him on each cheek, and he returned the gesture. It felt less like a reunion between kin and more like a greeting between sovereigns.

After a brief pause, as the lines of age crept back into her features, her tone hardened. "I am your mother. But you can hold no love for me."

"I have thought of you more than of any other being in the world," Deronda replied, his voice trembling with nervous intensity.

"I am not what you imagined," she stated decisively, pulling her hands away to fold them once more, inviting him to scrutinize her. He had often pictured her face mirroring his own; now he saw traces of that resemblance amidst startling differences. She was a remarkable entity. What caused him such a painful sense of detachment? Her worn beauty possessed a strangeness, as if she were not quite a human mother, but a Melusine bound to a realm independent of their own.

"I used to think you might be suffering," Deronda said, driven by a desperate need not to wound her. "I wished I could comfort you."

"I *am* suffering. But with a suffering you cannot alleviate," the Princess said, her voice sterner than before. She moved to a sofa where cushions had been meticulously arranged. "Sit down." She indicated a seat nearby. Noticing the distress clouding Deronda's countenance, she softened. "I am not suffering at this moment. I am at ease. I am able to speak."

Deronda sat, waiting for her to continue. It felt as though he stood before a mysterious Fate rather than the long-awaited mother. He watched her with wonder, separated from her by a spiritual distance she had erected.

"No," she began. "I did not summon you to comfort me. I could not foresee what you would feel toward me. I possess no foolish notion that you can love me simply because I am your mother, especially since you have never seen or heard of me in your entire life. However, I believed I had chosen something superior for you than mere companionship. I did not realize I was depriving you of anything valuable."

"You cannot expect me to believe your affection would have been worthless," Deronda countered, noting her pause as if she awaited a response.

"I do not intend to speak ill of myself," the Princess declared with proud impetuosity. "Yet, I had little affection to offer. I craved none. I had been suffocated by it. I sought to live out the life inherent within me, unhampered by other lives. You wonder what I was? I was no princess then." Rising abruptly, she stood as she had before; Deronda rose instantly, feeling breathless.

"No princess in this tame existence I lead now. I was a great singer, and an actor as well. All others were poor comparisons. Men followed me from nation to nation. I lived a thousand lives in one. I desired no child."

A passionate defense colored her tone. She had discarded all social precedents, seeking justification only in the most intense words available to describe her experience. She seemed to hurl her final defenses against potential reproaches in her son's mind. Deronda stood clutching his coat collar as if clinging to a lifeline, his blood churning with a commotion akin to witnessing a sacred ritual of crime. What else could she reveal? Continuing with the same ferocity, a pale illumination in her face, she spoke:

"I refused to marry. I was forced into wedlock with your father—not by my will, but by my father's commands. Moreover, it was my finest strategy for securing freedom. I could rule my husband, but not my father. I had a right to liberty. I had a right to flee a bondage I loathed."

Seating herself again, she performed a subtle shift in her eyes and closed lips, signaling a suppressed continuation of speech. Deronda remained standing. After a moment, she looked up, her plea less defiant now.

"And the bondage I despised for myself, I intended to spare you. What greater gift could a loving mother bestow? I liberated you from the bondage of being born a Jew."

"Then I *am* a Jew?" Deronda exploded with a deep, resonant energy that made his mother recoil slightly against her cushions. "My father was a Jew, and you are a Jewess?"

"Yes, your father was my cousin," the mother replied, observing him with a shifting expression, as if dreading a revelation she might regret.

"I am glad of it," Deronda said impetuously, his voice veiled in passion. He could not have anticipated the vehemence of his declaration, nor dreamed it would oppose his mother so sharply. He was shaken by a mixed anger, unchecked by reason, directed at this mother who seemed to have borne him reluctantly, made herself a stranger, and perhaps now revealed herself unwillingly. This suspicion illuminated her previous words.

The mother, too, was shaken by a differently blended anger, her composure failing physically. Her eyes widened in pallid excitement as she cried out violently, "Why do you claim gladness? You are an English gentleman. I secured that status for you."

"You did not know what you secured for me. How could you choose my birthright for me?" Deronda retorted, sinking sideways into his chair, leaning his arm over the back, averting his gaze. An intolerant fire burned within him, foreign yet undeniable. Struggling to master himself, fearing he might utter something irreversible in this epochal moment, he fell silent.

A pause ensued before his mother resumed, her voice firmer, its finely varied tones resisting defeat. "I chose for you what I would have chosen for myself. How could I know you possessed the spirit of my father? How could I know you would love what I detested—if you truly love to be a Jew?" The bitterness in her last words suggested a hatred arising between mother and son.

However, Deronda reclaimed his full composure. Recalling the realities of her life, whose prime years had faded, and seeing the signs of suffering etched into her frame as she struggled to recount a past shared by neither of them, his habitual shame regarding the acceptance of fate aided him. As he looked at her silently following her final words, a penetrating calm returned to his face, yet it exerted a strangely agitating influence. Her eyes fixed upon him with fascination, devoid of maternal delight.

"Forgive me if I speak hastily," he said with diffident gravity. "Why have you decided now to disclose to me what you ensured I remain ignorant of? Why, given your apparent anger at my gladness?"

"Oh—the reasons of our actions!" the Princess remarked with a ring of sarcastic scorn. "When you reach my age, it will not appear so simple a query: 'Why did you do this?' People discuss motives in neat packages. Every woman is presumed to share a standard set of motivations, or else she is a monster. I am not a monster, yet I have not felt exactly as other women feel—or say they feel, terrified of appearing unlike them. When you reproach me inwardly for sending you away, you imply I should claim to have felt about you as other mothers claim to feel about their children. I did *not* feel that. I rejoiced in being freed from you. Nevertheless, I acted rightly for you, providing your father's fortune. Do I seem to revoke everything now? Well, there are reasons. I harbor many feelings I cannot comprehend. A fatal illness has grown within me for a year; I shall likely not survive another. I will deny no action I have taken. I will not feign love

"The same firmness that serves to resist love also renders it violent and enduring; and weak persons, who are perpetually agitated by passions, are seldom truly filled with them." — La Rochefoucauld.

The next morning, among the pile of correspondence awaiting Daniel Deronda, lay a letter from Hans Meyrick. It spanned four quarto pages, penned in the small, exquisite handwriting characteristic of the Meyrick family.

"My Dear Deronda," the letter began, "in return for your sketch of Italian movements and your broader views on worldly affairs, I must report that here at home, the most prudent opinion regarding the effects of current events is simply that 'time will show.'
