Regarding the causes of past effects, it is becoming clear that the recent fraudulent telegrams explain last year's cattle plague—a refutation of false philosophy and a justification for compensating the farmers. My own theory that a murrain will soon strike the commercial class, revealing itself later in the sudden sale of rejected paintings, has been dismissed as an unsound use of analogy. Yet, there are minds unwilling to deprive even the neglected painter of his solace. To my sensibility, there is great beauty in the notion that a bad judge might pay a high price for my *Berenice* series, thereby punishing the city men for my ill-deserved luck.

"Meanwhile, I console myself for your absence by discovering advantages within it. I shine like Hesperus when Hyperion has departed; I sit with our Hebrew prophet and study his head during the hours he formerly spent with you, earning credit with him as a learned young Gentile who would have been a Jew if circumstances allowed. We agree on the general principle that whatever is best is therefore Jewish. Though I have never considered myself a severe reasoner, I see the logic: if whatever is best is A, and B happens to be best, then B must be A, regardless of prior expectations. On this principle, I could understand a pamphlet proving all good art is Protestant. However, our prophet is an uncommonly interesting sitter—a superior model to Rembrandt's Rabbi—and I depart from him with fresh discoveries daily. Constantly, I marvel that despite his fiery passion for his race and traditions, he is not a rigid Jew, spitting at the word 'Christian' and rejoicing in the prospect of the Gentile mouth watering in vain for a slice of roasted Leviathan, while Israel sends up plates for more *ad libitum*. (You perceive my studies prepared me for this expectation of the orthodox Jew.) I confess I have always viewed your account of Mordecai as somewhat apologetic, merely part of your tendency to adopt an antediluvian perspective to avoid injustice to the megatherium. But now, hearing him in his proper person, I find him a philosophical, allegorical, mystical believer nonetheless, possessing a sharp dialectic point capable of silencing any argumentative rattler of peas in a bladder. This mixture may be a Jewish prerogative. Indeed, his mind is so broad that my own correct opinions lie comfortably within it; how they align with the vast remainder is his affair, not mine. I leave the foundation to him, never having seen a basis that isn't a world-supporting elephant, however powerful or expensive to maintain. My means cannot support a private elephant, so I retreat into mystery, a cheaper and more lasting gas supplied by the decomposition of elephants. If an opinion pleases me, I treat it civilly, without suspicious inquiry. I hold a friendly feeling toward Mordecai's notion that a whole Christian is three-quarters a Jew, and that since Alexandria, the most comprehensive minds have been Jewish. I intend to point out to Mirah that, aside from Arabic and other life-incidents, there is little difference between me and Maimonides.

"But recently, I've realized that your shallow lover cannot help making declarations. If Mirah's ways were less distracting, and her presence less of a heaven to watch, I would long ago have thrown myself at her feet, requesting her to state directly whether she wished me to blow my brains out. I possess a knack of hoping, equivalent to an estate in reversion, provided one resists the temptation to convert it into certainty, which may ruin everything. My Hope wanders among orchard blossoms, feels warm snow falling on it through sunshine, and doubts nothing. But catching sight of Certainty in the distance, it sees an ugly, Janus-faced deity winking dubiously on the near side, and turns away quickly. You, with your supreme reasonableness, self-nullification, and preparation for the worst, know nothing of Hope, that immortal, delicious maiden forever courted and forever propitious, whom fools call deceitful as if Hope carried the cup of disappointment. No, it is her deadly enemy, Certainty, whom she escapes only by transformation. (Do you observe my new vein of allegory?) Seriously, however, I must allege that truth prevails, prejudice melts before it, diversity accompanied by merit becomes fascinating, and no virtuous aspiration is frustrated—all doctrines of the schools implying that the Jewess I prefer will prefer me. Any blockhead cites generalities, but the mind-master discerns the particular cases they represent.

"I am less convinced that my society compensates Mordecai for your absence, though another substitute occasionally arrives in the form of Jacob Cohen. It is worthwhile to catch our prophet's expression when he has that remarkable young Israel on his knee, pouring forth Semitic inspiration with a sublime look of melancholy patience and devoutness. Sometimes Jacob thinks Hebrew would be more edifying if he stopped his ears with his palms and imitated the venerable sounds heard through that muffled medium. When Mordecai gently draws down the little fists and holds them fast, Jacob's features assume extraordinary activity, as if walking through a menagerie, succeeding best with the owl and the peccary. You have surely seen this. He treats me with familiar ease, generally looking at me as a second-hand Christian commodity likely to depreciate, remarking on my disadvantages with a frankness implying thoughts of future purchase. It is pleasant, however, to see the change in him if Mirah enters. He turns childlike instantly—his age usually striking one as resembling Israelitish garments in the desert, perhaps near forty, yet with an air of recent production. With Mirah, he reminds me of dogs brought up by women, remaining manageable only by them. Still, the dog is fond of Mordecai, bringing sugar-plums to share, filling his own mouth to an embarrassing extent while watching how Mordecai handles a smaller supply. Judging from this modern Jacob at six years old, I am astonished that his race hasn't bought us all up long ago, pocketing our feebler generations as stock and scrip, mere slave property. There is one Jewess I wouldn't mind being a slave to. But I wish I didn't imagine Mirah grows slightly sadder, trying constantly to hide it. It is natural, of course, while she watches the slow death of this brother, whom she worships with looks of loving devoutness that make me wish to be in his place.

"For the rest, we are a little merrier than usual. Rex Gascoigne—you recall the head you admired among my sketches, a fellow with a good upper lip studying law—now has rooms in town nearby and has had a neat sister (also with a good upper lip) staying with him for two weeks. I introduced them to my mother and the girls, who learned from Miss Gascoigne that she is a cousin to your Vandyke Duchess!!! I added exclamation marks to denote the surprise this information initially produced on my feeble understanding. Upon reflection, I found no ground for surprise unless I had previously believed nobody could be anyone's cousin without my knowledge. This kind of surprise, I take it, depends on a lively spine coupled with a constant nullity of brain. There was a fellow I met in Rome who effervesced with surprise at the simplest information. Tell him you loved easy boots; he would always reply, 'No! Are you?' with the same energetic wonder: the very fellow Pastoral Browne prophesied about—"A wretch so empty that if e'er there be / In nature found the least vacuity / 'Twill be in him." I have accounted for it all—he possessed a lively spine.

"However, this cousinship with the Duchess emerged by chance one day Mirah was visiting, and they discussed the Mallingers. *Apropos*; I am becoming so important that I have rival invitations. Gascoigne invites me to his father's rectory in August to see the countryside. But I think self-interest dictates I go to Topping Abbey, for Sir Hugo has invited me and proposed—God bless him for his rashness!—that I paint a picture of his three daughters sitting on a bank, in the Gainsborough style. He visited my studio recently, recommending I focus on portraiture. Of course, I know what that means: 'My good fellow, your attempts at the historic and poetic are simply pitiable. Your brush is that of a successful portrait-painter—it has a little truth and great facility in falsehood—your idealism will never suffice for gods and heroes, but it may fetch a high price as flattery. Fate, my friend, has made you the hind wheel—*rota posterior curras, et in axe secundo*—run behind because you can't help it.' What great effort it costs our friends to offer such candid opinions! I have even known a man call specifically to tell me I had irretrievably exposed my lack of judgment in treating my subject, adding that if I had asked, he would have lent his own judgment. Such was my ingratitude and readiness for composition that even while he spoke, I inwardly sk

"My desolation does begin to make A better life." —Shakespeare: *Antony and Cleopatra.*

Before Daniel Deronda was summoned to a second audience with his mother, a full day had elapsed during which she had merely dispatched a message stating she was not yet well enough to receive him. However, on the third morning, a note arrived commanding his immediate presence: "I leave to-day. Come and see me at once."

He was ushered into the same chamber as before, yet the atmosphere had shifted dramatically. The room was now heavily darkened by drawn blinds and thick curtains, casting the space in a gloomy twilight. The Princess was initially absent, but she soon appeared, draped in a loose wrap of soft, dusky-orange silk. Black lace floated loosely around her head, and her arms were bare beneath the wide sleeves of her gown. In the somber light, her face seemed even more striking; her eyes appeared larger, and the lines etched upon her visage carried a newfound vigor. One might have imagined her a sorceress, stretching forth a powerful hand to brew elixirs of youth for others, yet scorning to mix them for herself, having already drunk her fill of that fleeting season.

She placed her arms around her son's shoulders instantly, kissing him on both cheeks, before settling onto her cushions with an air of assured firmness and dignity—a stark contrast to the erratic volatility of their first meeting. She gestured for Deronda to sit beside her. He complied, asking softly, "You are quite relieved now, I trust?"

"Yes, I am at ease again," she replied, her tone possessing the matter-of-fact authority of a queen rather than the warmth of a mother. "Is there anything further you wish to ask?"

"May I inquire about the house in Genoa where you once lived with my grandfather?" Deronda asked.

"No," she answered, making a dismissive gesture with her arm. "It has been demolished—not to be found. As for our family history and where my father dwelt at various times, you will discover all of that among the papers in the chest; it is better served there than by my tongue. My father was a physician; my mother was a Morteira. I used to listen to these stories without truly hearing them. You will find them all recorded. I was born amidst them against my will, but I banished them the moment I could."

Deronda attempted to mask the pain swelling in his chest. "Is there anything else I should desire to know from you that would offer you personal satisfaction in telling me?"

"I believe I have disclosed everything that could reasonably be demanded of me," the Princess said, her expression turning cold and meditative. It seemed she had exhausted her emotional reserves during their previous encounter. In truth, she had reasoned to herself, *"I have done it all. I have confessed every secret. I will not relive this agony. I will spare myself further agitation."* She was meticulously enacting this plan of withdrawal. Yet, for Deronda's sensitive nature, the moment was cruel; it transformed his lifelong filial longing into a pilgrim's journey to a shrine stripped of its sacred symbols. It felt as though all the womanhood missing from her was concentrated entirely within him. With a tremor in his voice, he asked, "Then are we to part, and I never be anything to you?"

"It is better so," the Princess replied, her voice suddenly softer, mellower. "Even if it were possible for you to assume the role of my son, there could be nothing but hard duty involved. You would not love me. Do not deny it," she interrupted sharply, raising her hand. "I know the truth. You dislike what I have done. You are angry with me. You feel I have robbed you of something. You stand on your grandfather's side, and you will harbor a condemnation of me in your heart forever."

Deronda felt himself under a silent banishment. Rising from his seat, he preferred to stand if obedience required him to withhold any tenderness. But his mother looked up at him, her gaze filled with a fresh, intense admiration. "You are wrong to be angry with me," she said. "You are the better for what I did."

After a brief pause, she added abruptly, "And now, tell me what you intend to do?"

"Do you mean immediately?" Deronda clarified. "Or regarding the course of my future life?"

"I mean in the future. How does it affect you that I have revealed your birth?"

"A very great difference," Deronda stated emphatically. "I can hardly conceive of anything that would alter my existence more profoundly."

"What will you do, then?" the Princess pressed, her tone sharpening. "Will you mold yourself exactly like your grandfather—become what he wished, turn yourself into a Jew as he did?"

"That is impossible," Deronda insisted, his voice gaining tenacity. "The effects of my upbringing can never be erased. The Christian sympathies in which my mind was nurtured can never die out of me. However, I consider it my duty—and it is the impulse of my feelings—to identify myself, as far as possible, with my hereditary people. If I can discern any work to be done for them to which I can devote my soul and hand, I shall choose to do it."

His mother fixed her eyes upon him, speculating with wonder, examining his face as if trying to decipher a complex language through close scrutiny. He met her gaze firmly, sustained by a resolute opposition that expressed his most authentic self. She leaned forward slightly, emphasizing her next words decisively. "You are in love with a Jewess."

Deronda blushed. "My reasons would be independent of any such fact."

"I know better. I have observed men throughout my life," the Princess declared peremptorily. "Tell me the truth. She is a Jewess who will accept no one but a Jew. There are a few such," she added with a hint of scorn.

Deronda faced the difficulty everyone encounters when speaking to those whose minds are rigidly fixed on their own interpretations, refusing enlightenment. Beyond this, the issue at hand stirred a repugnance in him to either deny or affirm. He remained silent. Presently, she spoke again. "You love her as your father loved me, and she draws you after her as I drew him."

These words struck a chord in Deronda's filial imagination, and a tenderness in his glance was interpreted by his mother as an assent. Her passion rose as she continued, "But I was leading him the other way. And now your grandfather is getting his revenge."

"Mother," Deronda remonstrated gently, "let us not view it in that manner. I admit that some benefit may arise from the education you chose for me. I prefer to cherish that benefit with gratitude rather than dwell with resentment on the perceived injury. I believe it would have been right for me to grow up conscious of being a Jew, yet it must always have been a good to possess such broad instruction and sympathy. Now, you have restored me my inheritance—circumstances have brought a fuller restitution than you could have engineered alone. You have been spared from robbing my people of my service and me of my duty. Can you not bring your whole soul to consent to this?"

Deronda paused in his plea; his mother listened intently, as if the rhythm of his voice captivated her ear, yet she shook her head slowly. He began again, with even greater urgency. "You have told me you sought what you deemed best for me. Open your heart to relenting and love toward my grandfather, who sought what he held best for you."

"Not for me, no," she replied, shaking her head with absolute denial and crossing her arms tightly. "I tell you, he never thought of his daughter except as an instrument. Because I had needs outside his purpose, I was to be framed and tortured. If that is the law of the world, I will not claim to love it. If my actions were wrong—if it is God demanding that I surrender what I withheld—who is punishing me for deceiving my father and failing to warn him that I would contradict his trust? Well, I have told everything. I have done what I could. And *your* soul consents. That is sufficient. I have, after all, been the instrument my father desired. 'I desire a grandson who shall have a true Jewish heart. Every Jew should rear his family as if he hoped that a Deliverer might spring from it.'"

As she uttered these final sentences, the Princess narrowed her eyes, nodded her head up and down, and spoke slowly with a strange, resonant chest-voice, as if reciting words she did not willingly endorse.

"Were those my grandfather's words?" Deronda asked.

"Yes, yes; and you will find them written," the Princess confirmed. "I wanted to thwart him," she burst out suddenly, reigniting the passion she had displayed earlier. Then, more slowly, she added, "You would have me love what I have hated since the time I was so high"—here she held her left hand a yard above the floor—"That can never be. But what does it matter? His yoke has been on me, whether I loved it or not. You are the grandson he wanted. You speak as men do—as if you felt yourself wise. What does it all mean?"

Her tone was abrupt and scornful. In his pained state, under the solemn urgency of the moment, Deronda struggled to hold fast to a memory of their relationship, lest his words become cruel. Speaking in a deep, entreaty-filled tone, he said, "Mother, do not say that I

"The unwilling brain Feigns often what it would not; and we trust Imagination with such phantasies As the tongue dares not fashion into words; Which have no words, their horror makes them dim To the mind's eye." —SHELLEY.

Madonna Pia, whose husband, feeling himself injured by her, took her to his castle amid the swampy flats of the Maremma and got rid of her there, makes a pathetic figure in Dante's Purgatory, among the sinners who repented at the last and desire to be remembered compassionately by their fellow-countrymen. We know little about the grounds of mutual discontent between the Siennese couple, but we may infer with some confidence that the husband had never been a very delightful companion, and that on the flats of the Maremma his disagreeable manners had a background which threw them out remarkably; whence in his desire to punish his wife to the utmost, the nature of things was so far against him that in relieving himself of her he could not avoid making the relief mutual. And thus, without any hardness to the poor Tuscan lady, who had her deliverance long ago, one may feel warranted in thinking of her with a less sympathetic interest than of the better-known Gwendolen, who, instead of being delivered from her errors on earth and cleansed from their effect in purgatory, is at the very height of her entanglement in those fatal meshes which are woven within more closely than without, and often make the inward torture disproportionate to what is discernible as outward cause.

In taking his wife with him on a yachting expedition, Grandcourt had no intention to get rid of her; on the contrary, he wanted to feel more securely that she was his to do as he liked with, and to make her feel it also. Moreover, he was himself very fond of yachting: its dreamy, do-nothing absolutism, unmolested by social demands, suited his disposition, and he did not in the least regard it as an equivalent for the dreariness of the Maremma. He had his reasons for carrying Gwendolen out of reach, but they were not reasons that can seem black in the mere statement. He suspected a growing spirit of opposition in her, and his feeling about the sentimental inclination she betrayed for Deronda was what in another man he would have called jealousy. In himself it seemed merely a resolution to put an end to such foolery as must have been going on in that prearranged visit of Deronda's which he had divined and interrupted.

And Grandcourt might have pleaded that he was perfectly justified in taking care that his wife should fulfill the obligations she had accepted. Her marriage was a contract where all the ostensible advantages were on her side, and it was only of those advantages that her husband should use his power to hinder her from any injurious self-committal or unsuitable behavior. He knew quite well that she had not married him—had not overcome her repugnance to certain facts—out of love to him personally; he had won her by the rank and luxuries he had to give her, and these she had got: he had fulfilled his side of the contract.

And Gwendolen, we know, was thoroughly aware of the situation. She could not excuse herself by saying that there had been a tacit part of the contract on her side—namely, that she meant to rule and have her own way. With all her early indulgence in the disposition to dominate, she was not one of the narrow-brained women who through life regard all their own selfish demands as rights, and every claim upon themselves as an injury. She had a root of conscience in her, and the process of purgatory had begun for her on the green earth: she knew that she had been wrong.

But now enter into the soul of this young creature as she found herself, with the blue Mediterranean dividing her from the world, on the tiny plank-island of a yacht, the domain of the husband to whom she felt that she had sold herself, and had been paid the strict price—nay, paid more than she had dared to ask in the handsome maintenance of her mother:—the husband to whom she had sold her truthfulness and sense of justice, so that he held them throttled into silence, collared and dragged behind him to witness what he would, without remonstrance.

What had she to complain of? The yacht was of the prettiest; the cabin fitted up to perfection, smelling of cedar, soft-cushioned, hung with silk, expanded with mirrors; the crew such as suited an elegant toy, one of them having even ringlets, as well as a bronze complexion and fine teeth; and Mr. Lush was not there, for he had taken his way back to England as soon as he had seen all and everything on board. Moreover, Gwendolen herself liked the sea: it did not make her ill; and to observe the rigging of the vessel and forecast the necessary adjustments was a sort of amusement that might have gratified her activity and enjoyment of imaginary rule; the weather was fine, and they were coasting southward, where even the rain-furrowed, heat-cracked clay becomes gem-like with purple shadows, and where one may float between blue and blue in an open-eyed dream that the world has done with sorrow.

But what can still that hunger of the heart which sickens the eye for beauty, and makes sweet-scented ease an oppression? What sort of Moslem paradise would quiet the terrible fury of moral repulsion and cowed resistance which, like an eating pain intensifying into torture, concentrates the mind in that poisonous misery? While Gwendolen, throned on her cushions at evening, and beholding the glory of sea and sky softening as if with boundless love around her, was hoping that Grandcourt in his march up and down was not going to pause near her, not going to look at her or speak to her, some woman, under a smoky sky, obliged to consider the price of eggs in arranging her dinner, was listening for the music of a footstep that would remove all risk from her foretaste of joy; some couple, bending cheek by cheek, over a bit of work done by the one and delighted in by the other, were reckoning the earnings that would make them rich enough for a holiday among the furze and heather.

Had Grandcourt the least conception of what was going on in the breast of his wife? He conceived that she did not love him; but was that necessary? She was under his power, and he was not accustomed to soothe himself, as some cheerfully-disposed persons are, with the conviction that he was very generally and justly beloved. But what lay quite away from his conception was, that she could have any special repulsion for him personally. How could she? He himself knew what personal repulsion was—nobody better; his mind was much furnished with a sense of what brutes his fellow-creatures were, both masculine and feminine; what odious familiarities they had, what smirks, what modes of flourishing their handkerchiefs, what costume, what lavender water, what bulging eyes, and what foolish notions of making themselves agreeable by remarks which were not wanted. In this critical view of mankind there was an affinity between him and Gwendolen before their marriage, and we know that she had been attractingly wrought upon by the refined negations he presented to her. Hence he understood her repulsion for Lush. But how was he to understand or conceive her present repulsion for Henleigh Grandcourt? Some men bring themselves to believe, and not merely maintain, the non-existence of an external world; a few others believe themselves objects of repulsion to a woman without being told so in plain language. But Grandcourt did not belong to this eccentric body of thinkers. He had all his life had reason to take a flattering view of his own attractiveness, and to place himself in fine antithesis to the men who, he saw at once, must be revolting to a woman of taste. He had no idea of moral repulsion, and could not have believed, if he had been told it, that there may be a resentment and disgust which will gradually make beauty more detestable than ugliness, through exasperation at that outward virtue in which hateful things can flaunt themselves or find a supercilious advantage.

How, then, could Grandcourt divine what was going on in Gwendolen's breast?

For their behavior to each other scandalized no observer—not even the foreign maid, warranted against sea-sickness; nor Grandcourt's own experienced valet: still less the picturesque crew, who regarded them as a model couple in high life. Their companionship consisted chiefly in a well-bred silence. Grandcourt had no humorous observations at which Gwendolen could refuse to smile, no chit-chat to make small occasions of dispute. He was perfectly polite in arranging an additional garment over her when needful, and in handing her any object that he perceived her to need, and she could not fall into the vulgarity of accepting or rejecting such politeness rudely.

Grandcourt put up his telescope and said, "There's a plantation of sugar-canes at the foot of that rock; should you like to look?"

Gwendolen said, "Yes, please," remembering that she must try and interest herself in sugar-canes as something outside her personal affairs. Then Grandcourt would walk up and down and smoke for a long while, pausing occasionally to point out a sail on the horizon, and at last would seat himself and look at Gwendolen with his narrow immovable gaze, as if she were part of the complete yacht; while she, conscious of being looked at, was exerting her ingenuity not to meet his eyes. At dinner

*"Ritorna a tua scienza / Che vuol, quanto la cosa è più perfetta / Più senta il bene, e così la doglienza."* — Dante.

When Deronda encountered Gwendolen and Grandcourt upon the staircase, his mind was already deeply preoccupied. He had just concluded a second, harrowing interview with his mother. Only two hours prior to meeting them, he had learned that the Princess Halm-Eberstein had departed the hotel. Logically, he should have immediately begun his journey to Mainz to deliver the letter from Joseph Kalonymos and secure the family chest. Yet, a complex web of unresolved emotions anchored him to Genoa. A heavy weight of retrospective feeling held him passive long after his farewells.

In his solitude, Deronda relived the recent scenes with a renewed, piercing intensity, as if they existed merely as a prelude to their full impact within his soul. He allowed himself to weep, with a compassion perhaps sharper than most women's, over a life so intimately connected to his own yet so tragically distant. He perceived the world shifting beneath him, altered by the certainty of bonds that disrupted his balance of hopes and fears, granting him a profound sense of fellowship. It felt as though, hidden under the night's cover, he had mistakenly joined a band of wanderers, only to discover at dawn that his true kin's tents lay miles away. He possessed a trembling, imaginative awareness of a close bond with a grandfather whose strong impulses and loving thoughts might now be stirring within his own dormant heart. Throughout this passionate meditation, Mordecai and Mirah remained ever-present, silent companions clasping hands with him in shared understanding.

Deronda was forged of such quick, responsive fiber, draped in a mantle of self-controlled reserve that had absorbed much of his youthful strength through early hardship. When the persistent ringing of a bell signaled the hour, he considered consulting the railway guide (*Bradshaw*) to make the necessary arrangements for the next train to Mainz. He thought of it, but took no action. His desires drifted between Mainz and its treasures, and London and the people there who formed the deepest attachments of his life. Yet, other wishes tethered him to Genoa—the force that compels one to linger during an interview carrying the shadow of a final farewell or overwhelming sorrow.

He did not consciously decide, "I shall stay tonight because it is Friday, and I wish to attend the synagogue service where they surely gathered; moreover, I might encounter the Grandcourts again." Instead, he simply ceased packing and rang for his bill, sitting motionless while his mind wandered to the synagogue. There, he envisioned faces scarcely different from those of his grandfather's era, hearing the Spanish-Hebrew liturgy endure through centuries of wandering generations like a plant with drifting seeds, binding distant lands to the exile's home. Simultaneously, his thoughts turned to Gwendolen, filled with anxious recollection of the past and a half-formed conviction that it would be cruel to depart immediately without attempting, despite Grandcourt's likely displeasure, to demonstrate his continued sympathy following their abrupt separation.

In this state of suspended animation, he delayed leaving, eating dinner without tasting its flavor, then rising abruptly to seek the synagogue. Passing the porter, he inquired if Mr. and Mrs. Grandcourt remained in the hotel and requested their room number. The porter supplied the number but added that they had gone out boating. This piece of information exerted a powerful influence on Deronda, dividing his focus between the memories awakened by the sparse *talithim* and the solemn, dark faces of worshippers who approached their prayers with an ease that seemed like Hebrew culture dyed Italian. These reflections led him to consider that his grandfather, based on the Princess's descriptions, must have been nearly as exceptional a Jew as Mordecai himself. Were not men of ardent zeal and boundless hope inherently exceptional? They were the visionaries Mordecai spoke of—the creators and nourishers of the world, shaping the passive lives that, without them, would wither into the narrow instinct of insects, blind to anything beyond their antennae.

A mournful impatience mingled with his concern for Gwendolen, fueled by his current freedom from immediate anxieties, urging him to leave the synagogue and take his customary evening walk along the quay. The spot was usually his favorite, and tonight it held extra allure due to the possibility of intercepting the Grandcourts returning from their boating trip. Resolving to approach them deliberately and disregard any reason Grandcourt might have for excluding him, Deronda stepped out.

The sun had dipped behind a bank of clouds, casting only a faint yellow farewell upon the waves, which churned under an active breeze. Sauntering slowly near the strand, Deronda observed groups gathering their gaze on a sailing-boat moving swiftly toward shore, propelled by two men. Amidst the clamor of voices in various tongues, Deronda decided against asking questions directly. Instead, he pushed forward to stand as an unobstructed witness. Telescopes were raised, and loud speculations erupted: one claimed the boat carried someone who had drowned; another insisted it was the *milord*; a Frenchman, lacking a glass, suggested the *milord* had likely taken his wife out to drown her—a notion an English skipper immediately dismissed as nonsense, comparing it to ore that had failed a mining process, before decisively stating the reclining figure was a woman.

For Deronda, terrified by these fluctuating fears, the rhythmic stroke of the oars was punctuated by rapid visions of possible and impossible outcomes, hinting at a tragedy with a darker half concealed—if the woman seemingly rescued from the water was indeed Mrs. Grandcourt. Soon, however, doubt vanished. The boat was drawn to land, and Deronda saw Gwendolen half-rising on her hands, aided only by her own effort, encased in a heavy layer of tarpaulins and pea-jackets. She appeared pale as one of the sheeted dead, shivering violently, her wet hair streaming down her face. Her eyes held a wild, amazed consciousness, as if she had awakened in a world awaiting judgment, surrounded by figures ready to seize her.

The first rower leaped ashore, drenched and running off. The sailors crowding the boat blocked Deronda's path, allowing him only to watch as Gwendolen cast frightened glances, shrinking in terror as she was gently but firmly extracted and guided away by the strong, bronzed arms of the rough men. Her wet garments clung to her limbs, hindering her weak movements. Suddenly, her wandering eyes landed on Deronda, standing before her. Instantly, as if she had been waiting for him, she attempted to stretch out her arms, restrained by her supporters, whispering in a muffled voice, "It is come, it is come! He is dead!"

"Hush, hush!" Deronda commanded, his tone authoritative. Turning to the assistants, he declared, "I am a connection of this lady's husband. If you will get her onto the *Italia* as quickly as possible, I will handle everything else."

He remained briefly to learn from the remaining boatman that her husband had sunk irretrievably and that his boat floated empty nearby. The crew recounted how they had heard a cry, arrived in time to see the lady leap in after her husband, and managed to rescue her before severe injury occurred. Deronda then hurried to the hotel to ensure the finest medical aid was secured. Once satisfied, he telegraphed the news to Sir Hugo, requesting his immediate arrival, and contacted Mr. Gascoigne at the rectory, knowing this was the quickest way to inform Gwendolen's mother. As he acted, certain words Gwendolen had spoken in the past returned to him with the force of inspiration: in moments of agitated confession, she had mentioned her mother's presence as a potential help, had she only been able to obtain it.

"The pang, the curse with which they died, Had never passed away: I could not draw my eyes from theirs, Nor lift them up to pray." —COLERIDGE.

That night, Deronda did not remove his clothes. Gwendolen, after demanding to see him once more before agreeing to undress, had fallen into a profound silence. With a whispering, repressed urgency, she had only begged him to promise that he would come to her when summoned in the morning. Yet, a foreboding gnawed at Deronda's heart. The possibility that her state might shift, the danger of a sudden fever, and the suspicion that the recent catastrophe was manifesting in her psyche through erratic words weighed heavily upon him. He instructed her attendant that he remained on standby for any alarming change, ensuring everyone understood he was in constant contact with her family in England and felt bound to guard her interests—a role he assumed easily, being well-known to Grandcourt's sole remaining valet from the voyage.

When exhaustion from the day's tumultuous emotions finally claimed Deronda, he slept fitfully, disturbed only by morning dreams that wove yesterday's events into a tangled web. These dreams culminated in an image born of his pressing anxiety, waking him abruptly.

Yet, it was morning, and there had been no summons. This silence brought cheer as he dressed, leading him to believe it was too early to inquire further. Later, he learned she had endured a sleepless night but showed no violent signs of agitation, eventually succumbing to sleep. He marveled at the strength dwelling within this creature, so acutely sensitive to dread; he felt an irresistible conviction that even under the shock of severe trauma, she was mastering herself with a determined secrecy. Conversely, he believed his own sensibilities had been dulled by the ordeal of meeting his mother. To him, his current actions felt merely like fulfilling obligations, his passionate sympathy temporarily suspended. Having lived recently in experiences far removed from Gwendolen's fate, his present cares for her felt like a revisit to familiar scenes, lacking the full revival of inner response they once commanded.

In the meantime, Deronda sought a formal, legal statement from the fishermen who had rescued Gwendolen. Little detail emerged. The boat Grandcourt had departed in was found drifting with its sail loose and was towed ashore. The fishermen surmised he had been knocked overboard by the flapping sail while maneuvering, unable to swim. However, though nearby, their attention was first seized by a cry resembling a man in distress. As they rowed urgently, they heard a shriek from the lady and saw her plunge into the water.

Upon returning to the hotel, Deronda was informed that Gwendolen had risen and desired to see him. He was led to a room darkened by blinds and curtains. Seated within, she wore a white shawl wrapped around her, gazing toward the open door with uneasy anticipation. Her long hair was gathered and coiled meticulously, and the blue star-shaped earrings retained their place. As she rose impulsively to her full height, encased in the white shawl, her face and neck equally pale save for a purple line beneath her eyes, her lips slightly parted with the distinct expression of the accused and helpless, she appeared as the tragic ghost of the Gwendolen Harleth Deronda had previously witnessed turning away from her gambling losses with firm lips and proud composure. The sight pierced him with pity, reviving the echoes of their shared history.

"I beseech you to rest—not to stand," Deronda said as he approached. She obeyed, sinking back into her chair.

"Will you sit down near me?" she requested. "I wish to speak very softly."

She occupied a large armchair, and he drew a small one beside her. The gesture touched her profoundly. Turning her pale face fully toward his, positioned very close, she whispered in the lowest audible tone, "You know I am a guilty woman?"

Deronda himself grew pale as he replied, "I know nothing." He dared not speak further.

"He is dead," she stated with the same subdued certainty.

"Yes," Deronda murmured, his voice heavy with mournful suspension, reluctant to articulate the words.

"His face will not be seen above the water again," Gwendolen continued, her tone not louder but charged with suppressed eagerness, her hands clenched tightly.

"No."

"Not by anyone else—only by me. A dead face... I shall never be able to escape it."

With an inward voice of desperate self-repression, she spoke these final words, averting her gaze from Deronda toward something distant on the floor. Was she viewing the entire event—including her own deeds—through an exaggerating lens of excitement and horror? Was she in a state of delirium colored by a sense of concealment and the necessity of hiding? Such thoughts flashed through Deronda as a faint hope. Yet, imagine the conflict of feelings that kept him silent. She was bent on confession, and he dreaded hearing it. Against his better judgment, he shrank from the task imposed upon him; he wished, yet chided the wish as cowardly, that she could bury her secrets within her own breast. He was not a priest. He feared the weight of this woman's soul cast upon his own with imploring dependence.

Then, hurriedly, she looked at him again. "You will not say that I ought to tell the world? You will not say that I ought to be disgraced? I could not do it. I could not bear it. I cannot have my mother know. Not if I were dead. I could not have her know. I must tell you; but you will not say that anyone else should know."

"In my ignorance," Deronda said mournfully, "I can say nothing except that I desire to help you."

"I told you from the beginning—as soon as I could—that I was afraid of myself." There was a pitiful pleading in the low murmur to which Deronda leaned his ear. Her afflicted face tormented him. "I felt a hatred within me that worked constantly like an evil spirit, contriving things. Every means I could devise to free myself came to mind; and it worsened—all things deteriorated. That is why I asked you to come to me in town. I thought then I would tell you the worst about myself. I tried. But I could not tell everything. And *he* came in."

She paused, a shudder passing through her body, before continuing.

"I will tell you everything now. Do you think a woman who cried, prayed, and struggled to be saved from herself could be a murderer?"

"Great God!" Deronda exclaimed, his voice deep and shaken. "Do not torture me needlessly. You have not murdered him. You threw yourself into the water with the impulse to save him. Tell me the rest afterward. This death was an accident you could not have prevented."

"Don't be impatient with me." The tremor and the childlike beseeching in these words compelled Deronda to turn his head and look at her face. The poor, quivering lips continued. "You said—you used to say—you felt more for those who had done something wicked and were miserable; you said they might improve—they might be scourged into something better. If you had not spoken thus, everything would have been worse. I *did* remember all you said. It came to me always. It came to me at the very last—that was the reason why I—But now, if you cannot endure listening while I tell you everything, if you turn away and forsake me, what shall I do? Am I worse than when you found me and sought to make me better? All the wrong I committed existed then—and more—and more—if you had not come and been patient with me. And now—will you forsake me?"

Her hands, so tightly clenched moments prior, were now helplessly relaxed and trembling on the arm of her chair. Her quivering lips remained parted as she ceased speaking. Deronda could not answer; he was forced to look away. Taking one of her hands, he clasped it as if preparing to walk together like two children; it was the only manner in which he could respond, "I will not forsake you." Throughout, he felt as though signing his name to a blank page that might be filled with terror. Their posture, his averted face bearing an expression of suffering he was solemnly resolved to endure, might have revealed half the truth to a stranger who had suddenly entered.

That grasp was an entirely novel experience for Gwendolen. Never before had any man offered a sign of tenderness that her very being required, and she interpreted its powerful effect as a promise of inexhaustible patience and constancy. The surge of renewed strength enabled her to continue, resuming her fitful, wandering confession where the repetition of experience seemed to erase the sense of time or order. She began again in fragments:

"All sorts of schemes in my mind—but all so difficult. And I fought against them—I was terrified at them—I saw his dead face"—here her voice sank to a whisper close to Deronda's ear—"so long ago I saw it and wished him dead. And yet it terrified me. I was like two creatures. I could not speak—I wanted to kill—it was as strong as thirst—and then immediately—I felt beforehand I had done something dreadful, unalterable—that would make me like an evil spirit. And it came—it came."

Silent for a moment or two, as if her memory had become lost in a web where every thread pulled the others, she resumed.

"It had all been in my mind when I first spoke to you—at the Abbey. I had done something then. I could not tell

"The unripe grape, the ripe, and the dried. All things are changes, not into nothing, but into that which is not at present." — Marcus Aurelius.

Deeds are the pulse of Time, his beating life; and whether righteous or unrighteous, once performed, they must throb in after-pangs until Time itself is laid in darkness, and the universe quivers and breathes upon no mirror more.

In the evening, she summoned him again. It was nearly the hour when she had been brought in from the sea the previous day. The room was dimmed by raised blinds and open windows, allowing a subdued light to filter in. She sat gazing fixedly at the sea, her cheek resting on her hand. Though she appeared less shattered than when he had last left her, a profound melancholy clouded her features, shifting into an anxious timidity as Deronda drew near. She did not offer her hand, merely saying, "How long ago it is!" Then, softly, "Will you sit near me again a little while?"

He settled beside her as before. Noticing that she turned toward him with an indefinable expression suggesting a desperate need to speak, he waited patiently. Yet, she only looked back toward the window in silence, repeating the gesture without uttering a word. Fear held her tongue. Seeking to ease her hesitation, Deronda averted his gaze. Soon, however, he heard her cry out imploringly, "You will not say that anyone else should know?"

"Most decidedly not," Deronda replied firmly. "There is no action that ought to be taken in consequence. There is no injury that could be righted in that manner. There is no retribution that any mortal could apportion justly."

She remained utterly still during the pause, seeming to hold her breath, before whispering, "But if I had not had that murderous will—that moment—if I had thrown the rope on the instant—perhaps it would have hindered death?"

"No—I think not," Deronda answered slowly. "If it were true that he could swim, he must have been seized with cramp. With your quickest, utmost effort, it seems impossible that you could have done anything to save him. That momentary murderous will cannot, I think, have altered the course of events. Its effect is confined to the motives within your own breast. Within ourselves, our evil will is momentous; sooner or later, it works its way outward. It may manifest in the vitiating conditions that breed evil acts, but equally in the self-abhorrence that stings us into better striving."

"I am saved from robbing others," she said, her voice trembling. "There are others—they will have everything—they will have what they ought to have. I knew that some time before I left town. You do not suspect me of wrong desires regarding those things?"

She spoke hesitantly. "I had not thought of them," Deronda admitted, "for I was thinking too much of the other matters."

"Perhaps you do not quite know the beginning of it all," Gwendolen said slowly, as if struggling against her own reluctance. "There was someone else he ought to have married. I knew it, and I told her I would not hinder it. I went away—that was when you first saw me. But then we became poor all at once, and I was terribly miserable. I was tempted. I thought, 'I shall do as I like and make everything right.' I persuaded myself. And it was all different. It was all dreadful. Then came hatred and wicked thoughts. That is how it all started. I told you I was afraid of myself. And I did what you told me; I tried to make my fear a safeguard. I thought of what would happen if I... I felt what would come—the dread of the morning, wishing it would remain forever night, yet even in the darkness always seeing something, always seeing death. If you did not know how miserable I was, you might understand—but now, it has all been useless. I can care for nothing but saving the rest from knowing—especially poor Mamma, who has never been happy."

Silence stretched between them before she spoke again, her voice breaking with a repressed sob: "You cannot bear to look at me anymore. You think I am too wicked. You do not believe that I can become any better—worth anything—worthy enough. I shall always be too wicked to..." Her voice failed her, leaving her helpless.

Deronda's heart was pierced. Turning his eyes to her poor, beseeching face, he said, "I believe that you may become worthier than you have ever yet been—worthy to lead a life that may be a blessing. No evil dooms us hopelessly except the evil we love, and desire to continue in, and make no effort to escape from. You *have* made efforts—you will go on making them."

"But you were the beginning of them. You must not forsake me," Gwendolen pleaded, leaning forward with clasped hands on the arm of her chair. Her face bore the piteous traces of a lifetime of experience compressed into these twenty-four hours—a new, terrible existence lying on the other side of the deed that fulfilled a criminal desire. "I will bear any penance. I will lead any life you tell me. But you must not forsake me. You must be near. If you had been near me—if I could have confided everything to you, I should have been different. You will not forsake me?"

"It could never be my impulse to forsake you," Deronda said promptly, using a voice that, like his eyes, inadvertently made his ready sympathy feel more personal and special than it truly was. In that moment, however, he was not entirely free from a foreboding of such a self-committing effect. His strong compassion for this stricken creature could not prevent rushing images of future difficulties from crowding his mind. He continued to meet her appealing gaze, yet with the painful awareness that to her ears, his words carried a promise that one day might seem unfulfilled; he was making an indefinite pledge to an indefinite hope. Immediate and distant anxieties swarmed his thoughts, and under their weight, after a brief silence, he said, "I expect Sir Hugo Mallinger to arrive by tomorrow night at the latest; and I am not without hope that Mrs. Davilow may shortly follow him. Her presence will be the greatest comfort to you—it will give you a motive to save her from unnecessary pain?"

"Yes, yes—I will try. And you will not go away?"

"Not until after Sir Hugo has come."

"But we shall all go to England?"

"As soon as possible," Deronda replied, unwilling to enter into particulars.

Gwendolen looked toward the window again, her expression resembling a gradual awakening to new thoughts. The twilight was perceptibly deepening, yet Deronda observed a movement in her eyes and hands, akin to the return of perception in one who has been stunned.

"You will always be with Sir Hugo now!" she said suddenly, fixing her gaze on him. "You will always live at the Abbey—or else at Diplow?"

"I am quite uncertain where I shall live," Deronda said, blushing.

Warned by his changed countenance that she had spoken too rashly, she fell silent. After a moment, she began again, looking away, "It is impossible to think how my life will go on. I think now it would be better for me to be poor and obliged to work."

"New promptings will come as the days pass. When you are among your friends again, you will discern new duties," Deronda advised. "Make it a task now to get as well and calm—as much like yourself as you can, before—" He hesitated.

"Before my mother comes," Gwendolen finished.

"Ah! I must be changed. I have not looked at myself. Should you have known me," she added, turning toward him, "if you had met me now?—should you have recognized me as the one you saw at Leubronn?"

"Yes, I should have known you," Deronda said mournfully. "The external change is not great. I should have seen at once that it was you, and that you had endured some great sorrow."

"Don't wish now that you had never seen me; don't wish that," Gwendolen implored, tears gathering in her eyes.

"I should despise myself for wishing it," Deronda said. "How could I know what I was wishing? We must find our duties in what comes to us, not in what we imagine might have been. If I took to foolish wishing of that sort, I should wish—not that I had never seen you, but that I had been able to save you from this."

"You have saved me from worse," Gwendolen sobbed. "I should have been worse if it had not been for you. If you had not been good, I should have been more wicked than I am."

"It will be better for me to go now," Deronda said, worn in spirit by the perpetual strain of the scene. "Remember what we said of your task—to get well and calm before other friends come."

He rose as he spoke, and she gave him her hand submissively. But when he had left her, she sank to her knees, overcome by hysterical crying. The distance between them felt insurmountable. She was a banished soul, beholding a possible life she had sinfully alienated herself from. She was found thus, crushed upon the floor. Such grief seemed natural indeed for a lady whose husband had been drowned in her presence.

"Much ado there was, God wot; He wold love and she wold not." —Nicholas Breton.

Time, we know, is a deceptive ruler. Its extension is a flawed metric; the vast distance the sun travels across the sky tells us nothing of how deeply life has shifted beneath our feet, just as measuring a field's acreage reveals nothing of the wildflowers bursting forth within it. A man might march south, stumble over a forgotten bone, and spend years unraveling the secrets of anatomy; or turn east, discovering a linguistic key that unlocks the history of entire races. He might lead an expedition that carves new paths across continents, endure physical ruin, and write a heroic epic of resolve. Yet, when he returns after only a few months, he finds his neighbors still grumbling about the same parish grievances, sees the same elderly gentleman pacing the pavement, muttering to himself, shaking his head at the same butcher's boy, and staring longingly at the same shop window displays.

If the sharpest intellect moves with the speed of a greyhound, the slowest must crawl like a limpet, appearing stuck until one realizes it is inching forward. Human experience swings wildly between these poles: from the revolutionary surge of change that births a new inner and outer existence, to the quiet, recurring rhythm of the familiar, marked only by hunger and the turning of the heavens.

Something of this stark contrast defined the year that transformed the brilliant, self-assured Gwendolen Harleth of the Archery Meeting into a crushed penitent, driven to confess her unworthiness—a confession she would have preferred to avoid entirely. Meanwhile, her family at Pennicote Rectory underwent far subtler shifts: merely adjustments to habits, a recalibration of hopes to match a reduced income, fewer social calls, and fading compliments. The rectory remained as charming as ever. The red and pink peonies on the lawn and the rows of hollyhocks along the hedges bloomed with equal vigor this year as last. The rector maintained his cheerful faith in the goodwill of his patrons, resolved to earn it through diligent duty, regardless of whether anyone noticed. He wrote two ecclesiastical articles annually, unsigned and attributed to anonymous scholars, save for the select few patrons who received private copies. They knew the author intimately but rarely bothered to read the content. The rector took no bitter pleasure in this obscurity; instead, he annotated his own copies to make them engaging loans, pleased that the Archdeacon and other authorities found no fault with his arguments. What peaceful authorship! Living amidst the fields and downs, breathing air untouched by the suffocating critique of the city, avoiding the gaunt leanness of Dante's inferno, and finding nourishment in complacency rather than the arduous labor of composing a *Divina Commedia*.

Then there was the father's renewed delight in his favorite son, a joy that outweighed the loss of eighteen hundred pounds a year. Whatever hidden transformation Rex had undergone following the disappointment of his first love appeared secondary to the clear evidence of serious ambition sparked by the family's recent misfortune. Indeed, Mr. Gascoigne tended to view the previous year's crisis as merely an evaporation of excess moisture, a final step in the baking process required to harden the human dough.

Rex had recently returned for a summer visit, bringing Anna home. While he displayed nearly his old vivacity among his siblings, he diligently maintained the habits of the eager student, rising early and retiring early to pursue a rigorous course of study.

"You do not regret choosing the law as a profession, Rex?" his father asked.

"There is no profession I would prefer," Rex replied. "I wish to end my days as a first-rate judge and help draft a legal code. I reverse the famous saying. 'Give me something to do with making the laws,' I'd say, 'and let others compose the songs.'"

"You'll have to stuff yourself full of immense amounts of rubbish, I suppose—that's the drawback," the rector noted.

"I don't see that legal rubbish is worse than any other. It isn't as bad as the trivial literature that clogs people's minds. It doesn't dull the senses. Our wittiest men have often been lawyers. An orderly method of viewing the world as cases and evidence seems preferable to a chaotic wash of disconnected fragments. From a higher perspective, the foundations and evolution of law offer the most fascinating insights into philosophy and history. Of course, there will be much that is tedious, drudgery-inducing, and frustrating. But the greatest prizes in life cannot be won easily—I've learned that."

"Well, my boy, the best omen for success in any career is believing it to be the finest in the world. Most people feel this way when they enter a task with true willpower." The rector recalled a conversation with Brewitt, the blacksmith, whose apprentice lacked enthusiasm for his trade. "'And yet, sir,' Brewitt said, 'what would a young fellow have if he didn't like blacksmithing?' The rector cherished a paternal pride, allowing it to surface only in measured doses. He had easily accepted Warham's departure for India, but Rex represented a different kind of romance—the later-life discovery of a son who surpasses oneself, inspiring visions of future eminence based on famous precedents.

He spoke decisively to his wife: "Rex will be a distinguished man, Nancy, I am certain of it—as sure as Paley was about his son."

"Was Paley an old bachelor?" Mrs. Gascoigne asked.

"That is hardly relevant, my dear," the rector replied, forgetting the irrelevance instantly. Mrs. Gascoigne felt her remark had been somewhat weak.

This tranquil passage of time at the rectory was mirrored in the household of the Jodsons, a modest white house nestled near the rectory, surrounded by evergreens and known locally as "Jodson's." Mrs. Davilow's delicate face bore only a slight deepening of its gentle melancholy, her hair showing just a few more strands of silver due to the trials of the past year. Her four daughters had blossomed slightly, freed from the shadows of their earlier struggles, while the capable Jocosa maintained her neutral stance toward worldly pleasures, reserved for those not "in a situation."

In the low, narrow drawing room, expanded by two quaint projecting windows with lattices wide open to a July afternoon, the air was thick with the scent of monthly roses, the soft murmur of the garden, and the occasional distant clip-clop of hooves or rumble of wheels that only heightened the ensuing silence. With Rex and Anna joining the usual group of six, the scene felt crowded and lively. Anna, always beloved by her younger cousins, eagerly recounted her new experiences in London and the acquaintances she had made. When she arrived alone previously, countless questions had been directed at her regarding Gwendolen's residence in Grosvenor Square, her words, and the opinions of others about Gwendolen.

Had Anna visited Gwendolen after learning about the yacht? No—an answer that left speculation rampant regarding everything connected to that mysterious vessel, aside from the fact that Gwendolen had written shortly before departing to announce that Mr. Grandcourt and she were yachting in the Mediterranean, and again from Marseilles to declare her certainty of enjoying the trip, promising not to write another letter until she had penned a lengthy diary filled with repetitions. Furthermore, Mr. and Mrs. Grandcourt's movements had been reported in the newspapers, adding to the intrigue. Thus, this new chapter in Gwendolen's elevated life became a striking element of the sisters' romantic narratives, with the book-obsessed Isabel weaving in a dash of piracy to ensure a happy ending.

However, when Rex was present, the girls, adhering to strict instructions, avoided this captivating topic. Instead, today featured animated discussions of the Meyricks and their extraordinary Jewish friends, prompting astonished queries from minds for whom the concept of living Jews, drawn from books, suggested a difference so profound it bordered on zoological—a strange race akin to those described by Pliny that might sleep beneath the shade of their own ears. Bertha could not conceive what Jews believed nowadays, harboring a vague notion that they rejected the Old Testament because it validated the New. Miss Merry thought Mirah and her brother were "unarguable," while the amiable Alice expressed indifference to their beliefs, admitting she simply "couldn't bear them." Mrs. Davilow gently corrected her, noting that prominent Jewish families in London and Paris were perfectly respectable, though acknowledging that common, unconverted Jews were objectionable. Isabel asked whether Mirah spoke differently, or if one could be in her company without realizing she was a Jewess.

Rex, possessing no partisan loyalty to the Israelites despite his academic cramming on their ancient history, amused himself by playfully exaggerating each speaker's prejudice, until Anna interceded, begging them to understand he was only joking. Their laughter was cut short by the arrival of a letter for Mrs. Davilow. A messenger had delivered it with urgent haste from the rectory. It contained a telegram. As Mrs. Davilow read and reread it in silent agitation, all eyes turned to her with growing anxiety, yet no one dared to speak. Finally lifting her gaze, she saw the young faces painted with fear. Remembering they might be imagining horrors worse than reality, she paused, then began again with a sob that was half-relief: "My dears, Mr. Grandcourt—"

She hesitated an instant, then continued, "Mr. Grandcourt is drowned."

Rex sprang up as if struck by a projectile. He could not restrain himself, and Anna's first instinct was to look at him

"I count myself in nothing else so happy / As in a soul remembering my good friends." —Shakespeare.

Sir Hugo Mallinger was not as eager to depart for Genoa as Mr. Gascoigne had been, nor would Daniel Deronda leave until he had spoken with the baronet. Between Grandcourt's death and the recent upheaval in his own life, Deronda felt unable to write down the full truth about the mother who had appeared and vanished like a ghost. It was not until the fifth evening, upon receiving a telegram, that he met Sir Hugo at the station, waiting for his arrival between eight and nine. While gazing toward the platform, anticipating the sight of a kind, familiar face from his earliest memories, a faint smile touched Deronda's lips despite his recent tragedies. He imagined Sir Hugo's delight in finally becoming the undisputed master of his estates, free to leave them to his daughters—or, according to a new theory of inheritance that had recently seized Deronda's imagination, to adopt makeshift female heirs as intermediaries for a future grandson.

We would be ungrateful creatures if we could not share in another's joy, unless that joy conflicted with our strictest moral ideals. How sour our expressions become, how cold our glances, when someone else's happiness does not align with our vision of perfect righteousness! Yet, our own desires rarely conform to such high standards. We must learn to fellowship with imperfection; indeed, it is possible to feel genuine gratitude even when we recognize a mistake that caused harm, provided the mistake was carried out through a lifetime of kindly intentions.

Deronda's heart and judgment strongly opposed Sir Hugo's role in perpetuating a falsehood—there was no softer term for the deception under which he had been raised. Still, the baronet likely lacked clear knowledge of his mother's betrayal. With his breezy approach to life, Sir Hugo had probably deemed it reasonable to ensure his son was born an English gentleman, considering his daughter-in-law's eccentric refusal to separate from her child. Daniel's deep affection for Sir Hugo made him seek excuses rather than condemnation. It is as easy to hold firm principles while offering gentle blame as it is to be disturbed by disorder yet remain patient with the person who perceives it wrongly. Had Sir Hugo, in his bachelor days, viewed children merely as accessories to adult comfort, he had simply shared a common assumption of that era. Deronda, recalling his own painful childhood struggles, remembered clearly that such experiences had never entered Sir Hugo's worldview. Ignorant kindness can sometimes amount to cruelty, but to punish it as if it were deliberate malice would be a greater cruelty itself—the furthest thing from Deronda's generous spirit.

After the intense scrutiny of the last ten days, during which the veil had been lifted on lives unlike his own, Deronda found himself less inclined toward rash indignation, which often masks a desire for punishment. When he saw Sir Hugo's familiar figure step from the train, the lifelong bond of affection washed over him, drowning out any new grounds for blame.

"Well, Dan," Sir Hugo said, gripping Deronda's hand with earnest warmth. He offered no further greeting; the weight of their shared history required no words. Next came instructions for the courier, followed by a proposal to stroll slowly in the mild evening air, with no rush to reach the hotel.

"I traveled comfortably and am in fine shape," Sir Hugo remarked as they walked beneath the stars, still tinged with the day's fading light. "I took my time leaving so I could investigate matters a bit. In fact, I saw your letter to Lady Mallinger before I departed. But now, how is the widow?"

"Calmer," Deronda replied. "She appears to be avoiding the physical illness we might have feared following her plunge and the shock. Her uncle and mother arrived two days ago, and she is being cared for well."

"Do you think there's any chance of an heir?"

Based on what Mr. Gascoigne told me, I doubt it. He spoke as if the question was whether she would retain the estates for her lifetime.

"It won't be much of a wrench to her feelings, I imagine, losing her husband so suddenly?" Sir Hugo asked, glancing around.

"The suddenness was a great blow," Deronda said, carefully sidestepping the deeper implications.

"Wonder if Grandcourt mentioned anything in his will?"

"Do you know its terms, sir?" Deronda countered gently.

"Yes, I do," the baronet replied promptly. "Good heavens, if there's no legitimate heir, he left everything to a boy he had with a Mrs. Glasher—you know nothing of this, I assume? She was practically his wife for many years. There are three older girls, but the boy takes precedence. His name is already Henleigh, and he'll be known as Henleigh Mallinger Grandcourt. The Mallinger title means nothing to him, thankfully, but the young rascal will have plenty to manage during his fourteen-year minority. No need to plug the holes in my fifty thousand pounds spent on Diplow that he wasn't entitled to. Meanwhile, my dear beauty, the young widow, gets a mere two thousand a year and the house at Gadsmere—a lovely form of exile if she chooses to lock herself away there, though I doubt she will. That woman has lived there for years. I'm utterly disgusted with Grandcourt. Though, regarding my own affairs, nothing in his life suited him better than dying."

"In my view, he erred greatly in marrying this woman—not necessarily in leaving the estate to the son," Deronda said dryly.

"I take no issue with giving the land to the lad," Sir Hugo agreed. "But since he married her, he should have provided a substantial allowance, enough for her to maintain the lifestyle her marriage afforded her. Four or five thousand a year and the London house for life—that's what I would have done. Since she started penniless, her friends surely couldn't demand a formal settlement beforehand. Otherwise, relying on a man's will after marriage is foolish. Even sensible men leak folly into their wills; my father did, I recall. If a man harbors spite or tyranny, he tends to hide it in legal documents. It's plain Grandcourt intended his death to extinguish his wife's prospects if she failed to bear him an heir."

"And in the other scenario, everything would have been reversed—illegitimacy leading to extinction?" Deronda asked, with a touch of scorn.

"Precisely. Gadsmere and the two thousand. It's peculiar. One annoyance is that Grandcourt named me executor. But as he was the son of my only brother, I cannot refuse. I'd rather mind it less if I can assist the widow. Lush suggests she wasn't unaware of the family situation behind closed doors and hinted at a strained relationship between the couple. But I believe you know best what Mrs. Grandcourt felt or didn't feel, don't you, Dan?"

Sir Hugo dropped his usual joking tone, speaking with a lowered, genuinely curious voice. Deronda realized evasion would be misunderstood.

"She was certainly unhappy. They were mismatched souls. As for the property division, based on what I've observed, I predict she will accept it contentedly."

"Then she isn't typical of her sex," Sir Hugo shrugged slightly. "Still, she must be extraordinary, given the entanglement between your fate and hers—eh? When that massive telegram arrived, Lady Mallinger's first remark was, 'How strange that it should be Daniel sending it!' But I've had similar moments in my own life. Once, at a foreign hotel, I encountered a woman abandoned by her husband with no money. Upon learning of it and stepping in to help, who should appear but an early flame of mine, foolish enough to marry an Austrian baron with a long mustache and short affection? It was purely a personal matter calling me there—no knight-errantry involved, any more than your trip to Genoa concerns the Grandcourts."

A brief silence fell between them. Sir Hugo had shifted to discussing the Grandcourts as a safer topic, yet both wished to bridge the gap toward complete honesty regarding the events affecting their relationship. Deronda sensed his earlier letter, sent after meeting his mother, had thickened the ice rather than melting it. He waited for Sir Hugo to break the tension.

Just as they neared the harbor, the baronet paused, turning for a final look at the port before speaking in a more serious tone. "And regarding the main reason for your coming to Genoa, Dan? Have you not been deeply distressed by what you've learned? Is there nothing that makes you reconsider your position? Remember, whatever happens to you remains important to me."

"I wish to honor your generosity with total confidence, sir," Deronda replied. "Yet I cannot answer those questions with a simple yes or no. Much of what I've heard about the past has pained me. Meeting and parting with my mother in her suffering state was also painful. But knowing my parentage clears up doubts, and for that, I am grateful. Regarding my position, my gratitude to you for your fatherly care and affection remains unchanged. However, discovering I was born a Jew may profoundly alter my life, and I am hardly ready to explain fully at this moment."

Deronda spoke the final sentence with determination overcoming his hesitation. He felt that the differences between Sir Hugo's nature and his own would eventually reveal themselves more sharply than necessary.

The baronet cast a quick glance at him, then continued walking. After a moment of silence, during which he mentally weighed every word Deronda had used, he said, "I've long expected something remarkable from you, Dan; but for God's sake, avoid any eccentricities! I can tolerate anyone

"But I shall say no more of this at present; for this is a matter to be felt rather than spoken aloud. Those who have never touched it with their own hands may secretly laugh at it in their hearts, yet remain forever unaware of its weight." — *Jeremy Taylor.*

In ancient legend, a Roman Emperor ordered the execution of ten learned Israelites to avenge the betrayal of Joseph by his brothers. Throughout history, there have always been enough souls of similar temperament, whose piety consists solely in punishing those they deem guilty, while failing to recognize the justice owed to gratitude. One cannot convince a stronger emotion that it lacks a stronger reason, nor can one persuade someone devoid of love that there exists valid ground for affection. As language itself reveals, *love* precedes the *lovable*.

When Deronda delivered his letter to the banking house on Schuster Strasse in Mainz and requested to see Joseph Kalonymos, he was shortly ushered into an inner chamber. Seated at a table amidst scattered correspondence was the white-bearded man he had encountered the previous year within the Frankfurt synagogue. He wore the same worn felt hat, and beside him sat a packed portmanteau topped with a wrap and overcoat. Upon Deronda's entrance, Kalonymos rose but did not step forward or offer his hand. Instead, he regarded the young man with small, penetrating eyes that glimmered like black gems against his yellowed complexion and snow-white hair.

"Good!" Kalonymos declared in German. "Now it is you who seek me, young man."

"Yes," Deronda replied, "I seek you with gratitude, as a friend of my grandfather. I owe you a debt for the considerable trouble you have taken on my behalf." He spoke effortlessly in that expansive German tongue, which readily embraces foreign accents into its maternal embrace.

Kalonymos extended his hand warmly. "So, you are no longer angry at having become something more than merely an Englishman?"

"On the contrary," Deronda said sincerely. "I thank you heartily for aiding me in escaping the ignorance of my parentage, and for safeguarding the chest my grandfather entrusted to your care."

"Sit, sit," Kalonymos urged softly, reseating himself and gesturing to a chair nearby. Deliberately removing his hat, he revealed a head thick with white hair, stroking and clutching his beard as he examined Deronda's youthful face with intense scrutiny.

The atmosphere struck Deronda deeply. To stand before a man still bound by zealous friendship to the grandfather whose hopes had yearned for him before birth—a figure who, though dead, seemed to speak through these written memorables containing, as Milton noted, "a potency of life... as active as that soul whose progeny they are"—Deronda felt as though he were touching the electric chain of his own ancestry. He endured Kalonymos's searching gaze with a mixture of delight and awe, akin to the reverence felt during solemn commemorations of ancient deeds that profoundly influence the present day. Such sensitivity might seem incomprehensible to those of duller fiber, whose affections fail to spread across the vast journey of imagination; yet it existed alongside their skepticism, coloring Deronda's face with an expression that appeared entirely satisfactory to the observer.

"In Hebrew," Kalonymos quoted from a fine hymn of the liturgy, "As thy goodness has been great to the former generations, even so may it be to the latter." After a brief pause, he continued, "Young man, I rejoice that I was not yet dispatched on further travels, and that you arrived in time for me to witness the image of my friend as he once was in his youth—no longer alienated from the fellowship of your people, no longer recoiling in proud wrath from the touch of one who claimed you as a Jew. You come with thankfulness to reclaim the kinship and heritage that wicked schemes attempted to steal from you. You arrive with a willing soul to declare, 'I am the grandson of Daniel Charisi.' Is this not so?"

"Assuredly it is," Deronda confirmed.

"But let me assure you," Kalonymos interjected, "that I would never have treated a Jew with incivility simply because he was a Jew. You understand why I hesitated to tell a stranger, 'I know nothing of my mother.'"

"A sin, a sin!" Kalonymos exclaimed, raising a hand and closing his eyes in revulsion. "A theft against our people—just as when our youths and maidens were raised for the Roman Edom. But it is thwarted. I have thwarted it. When Daniel Charisi—may his Rock and his Redeemer protect him!—was a stripling and I a boy scarcely taller than his shoulder, we made a solemn vow to be eternal friends. He said, 'Let us bind ourselves with duty, as if we were sons of the same mother.' That was his constant inclination—to fortify his soul with bonds. It was his maxim: 'Let us bind love with duty; for duty is the love of law; and law is the nature of the Eternal.' Thus, we bound ourselves. Though separated by distance in later years, the bond remained unbroken. When he died, they sought to rob him; but they could not rob him of me. I saved that remnant of him which he treasured for his offspring. And I have restored to him the child they stole from him. I will bring you the chest immediately."

Kalonymos departed briefly and returned accompanied by a clerk bearing the chest. He placed it on the floor, removed a leather covering, and exited again. It was modest in size but weighted down by ornamental brass bracers and handles of gilded iron. The wood was exquisitely carved with Arabic script.

"So!" Kalonymos said upon returning to his seat. "And here is the peculiar key," he added, retrieving it from a small leather pouch. "Keep it safely. I trust you are methodical and cautious." He bestowed upon Deronda the stern, slightly suspicious glance that age often directs when entrusting precious objects to the keeping of youth.

"I shall guard this more carefully than any other possession," Deronda smiled, slipping the key into his breast pocket. "Never before have I held anything serving as a sign of such cherished hope and labor. And I shall never forget that this effort was partly yours. Do you have time to tell me more of my grandfather? Or am I intruding by staying longer?"

"Remain a while. In an hour and eighteen minutes, I depart for Trieste," Kalonymos announced, checking his watch. "Soon my sons will demand my attention.
