Would you allow me to introduce you to them, so they may take the pleasure of hosting my friend's grandson? They reside here in comfort and luxury, whereas I prefer the life of a wanderer."

"I shall be delighted if you recommend me to their acquaintance for a future occasion," Deronda replied. "Pressing duties call me to England—friends who require my presence urgently. I have been absent from them far too long due to unforeseen circumstances. However, learning more of you and your family would be sufficient motivation to return to Mainz."

"Excellent! You will hardly find me, for I am past seventy years, a wanderer carrying my shroud with me. Yet my sons and their descendants dwell here in wealth and unity. Times have changed for us since Charles the Great brought my ancestors from Italy to impart a touch of knowledge to our rugged German neighbors. My generation and ours had to fight for it. Our youth faced dark days, but we prevailed. We accumulate wealth securely, and the learning of all Germany is nourished by Jewish intellects—even if they do not always retain their Jewish hearts. Have you been left entirely ignorant of your people's life, young man?"

"No," Deronda admitted. "Recently, before suspecting my parentage, I was led to study everything pertaining to their history with greater interest than any other subject. It appears I have been preparing myself to understand my grandfather somewhat."

He hoped to steer the conversation quickly back to the core of his desire. Time rarely distinguishes clearly between what age needs to express and what youth needs to know; distance seems to equalize the objects of memory. Although Kalonymos displayed keen activity, misplaced an inkstand, and his imagination would have flown straight to Beirut, he had long united restless travel with meticulous observation. Deronda's final remark achieved its purpose.

"So," Kalonymos observed, "you might have become such a man as he if your education had not intervened. Your features resemble his—yet not entirely, young man. He possessed an iron will in his countenance; it strengthened everyone around him. Even in his youth, he bore a single deep vertical line upon his brow. I see none of that in you. Daniel Charisi used to say, 'Better a wrong will than a wavering; better a steadfast enemy than an uncertain friend; better a false belief than no belief at all.' What he despised most was indifference. He had deeper reasons than I can explain."

"Was his knowledge then not narrow?" Deronda asked, subtly referencing the common excuse for indecision—that it stems from knowing too much.

"Narrow? No," Kalonymos shook his head with a compassionate smile. "From childhood, he absorbed learning as easily as a plant draws up water. But he turned early to medicine and theories concerning life and health. He traveled widely, spending much of his fortune observing and understanding. What he insisted upon was that the strength and wealth of humanity depended on the balance between separation and communication. He was fiercely opposed to our people dissolving among the Gentiles: 'It is no better,' he would say, 'than various grains reverting from their diversity into uniformity.' He blended all kinds of learning

"Within the gentle heart Love shelters him, As birds within the green shade of the grove. Before the gentle heart, in Nature's scheme, Love was not, nor the gentle heart ere Love." —GUIDO GUINICELLI (*Rossetti's Translation*).

There existed another household beside the white house at Pennicote, another bosom besides Rex Gascoigne's, where the tidings of Grandcourt's demise stirred a tumult of profound agitation followed by a desperate effort to suppress it. It was the custom of Hans Meyrick to dispatch or carry the *Times* for his mother's perusal. She was a voracious consumer of current affairs, ranging from sweeping political upheavals to the latest lists of nuptials; the latter, she remarked, afforded her the delightful illusion of completing fashionable romances without the labor of reading them, allowing her to witness the fates of heroes and heroines resolved happily, unaware of the tragic realities awaiting the fictional characters.

On a typical Wednesday, Hans invariably delivered the paper at a precise hour, coinciding with the near-completion of Mab's weekly lesson, claiming he arrived solely to hear Mirah sing. Yet, on this specific Wednesday, after slipping into the parlor with his customary quiet tread and latch-key, he emerged shaking the newspaper aloft with a sharp, crackling noise that brutally interrupted Mab's attempt to render *Lascia ch'io pianga*, mimicking her teacher with distant fidelity. The piano fell silent instantly; Mirah, accompanying the melody, jumped involuntarily, startled by the sound, which, amidst the usual domestic noises, had registered in her ears as thunderous.

"Oh-o-o, Hans!" exclaimed Mab. "Why do you bring a noise more terrible than my singing?"

"What on earth is this wonderful news?" asked Mrs. Meyrick, the only other occupant of the room. "Any report from Italy? Have the Austrians surrendered Venice?"

"Not from Italy, but concerning someone from Italy," Hans replied, his tone and manner bearing a peculiar inflection that prompted immediate interpretation from his mother. Consider how some of us behave when an event, seemingly benign, appears to validate our private suspicions. We adopt a pregnant tone, asking, "What do you think?" of an innocent bystander who hasn't invested their wisdom in the same vessel as ourselves, only to find their response flat and unhelpful.

"No bad news?" Mrs. Meyrick asked anxiously, her thoughts instantly leaping to Deronda; Mirah's heart had already tightened with the same apprehension.

"Not bad for anyone we truly care about," Hans said quickly. "Rather remarkably fortunate, I'd say. I've never known anyone die so conveniently. Given how precious a gazelle I am, I'm constantly surprised to find myself still breathing."

"Oh, me, Hans!" Mab interjected impatiently. "If you must chatter about yourself, do it behind your back. What actually happened?"

"Duke Alfonso is drowned, and the Duchess lives—that's the gist," Hans stated, placing the paper before his mother and pointing to a specific paragraph. "But the most significant detail is this: Deronda was staying at the same hotel in Genoa with them. He witnessed her being carried ashore by the fishermen who rescued her from the water in time to prevent further harm. Apparently, they saw her leap in after her husband—a decision far less prudent than I would have expected from the Duchess. Fortunately, Deronda was there to ensure she received proper care."

Mirah sank back onto the music stool, lowering her eyelids and clutching her hands tightly. Mrs. Meyrick handed the paper to Mab and murmured, "Poor thing! She must have loved her husband dearly to jump in after him."

"It was mere inadvertence—a lapse of attention," Hans corrected, scrunching his face in a rogue's grin and throwing himself into a chair near Mirah. "Who could possibly harbor affection for a jealous baritone, casting freezing glances and singing perpetual asides? That was undoubtedly the husband's role. Nothing could be neater than his drowning. The Duchess is now free to wed a man with a magnificent head of hair and glances capable of melting her heart rather than freezing it. And I shall certainly be invited to the wedding."

At this, Mirah sprang upright from her seated posture. Fixing her gaze upon Hans with an angry gleam, she spoke in a voice deeply shaken by indignation. "Mr. Hans, you ought not to speak thus. Mr. Deronda would not wish you to address matters in this manner. Why attribute luck to him? Why employ such phrasing regarding life and death when what constitutes life for one may be death for another? How can you claim it was fortunate if he loved Mrs. Grandcourt? It could prove a great evil to him. She would tear him away from my brother—I know she would. Mr. Deronda would not consider it 'lucky' to pierce my brother's heart."

All three were struck by the sudden transformation in the atmosphere. Mirah's face, bearing an expression of anger reminiscent of Ithuriel, was pale, even to the lips usually flushed with color. She bore a striking resemblance to Hans, who sat transfixed, blushing furiously as if he were a girl, while stammering, "I am a fool and a brute, and I retract every word. I'll go and hang myself like Judas—if mentioning him is permissible." Even in his moments of sorrow, Hans's improvised words retained an inherent drollery. Yet, Mirah's anger remained undimmed; how could it be appeased? She had erupted in indignant speech much like creatures in acute pain biting their own flesh to make their agony bearable. She said no more, but seated herself at the piano, pressing the sheet music before her as if intending to resume playing.

It was Mab who broke the tension, while Mrs. Meyrick's countenance mirrored some of Hans's discomfort. "Mirah is entirely right to scold you, Hans. You are constantly taking Mr. Deronda's name in vain. And it is horrifying to joke in such a manner about his potential marriage to Mrs. Grandcourt. Men's minds must be exceedingly dark, I think," concluded Mab with considerable scorn.

"Quite true, my dear," Hans replied softly, rising and turning on his heel to walk toward the rear window. "We had best proceed, Mab; you haven't devoted your full time to the lesson," Mirah added, her tone unusually elevated. "Shall I sing this again for you, or would you prefer to sing it?"

"Oh, please sing it to me," Mab requested, eager to ignore the preceding turmoil.

Mirah immediately began *Lascia ch'io pianga*, pouring forth its melodious sobs and cries with renewed fullness and energy. Hans paused mid-stride, leaning against the mantelpiece, deliberately keeping his eyes averted from his mother's. Once Mirah finished her final note and struck the concluding chord, she rose and announced, "I must return home now. Ezra awaits me." She offered her hand silently to Mrs. Meyrick and hesitated slightly, unable to summon the courage to kiss her as was her custom. Instead, the small mother drew Mirah's face down to hers and whispered soothingly, "God bless you, my dear."

Mirah sensed she had committed an offense against Mrs. Meyrick by angrily rebuking Hans, mingled with her suffering was a pang of guilt, feeling she had displayed a kind of proud ingratitude, an unworthy assertion of superiority. Her friend, however, had intuitively grasped this compunction. Meanwhile, Hans had grabbed his wake-up cap and prepared to open the door.

"Now, Hans," Mab said, masking a sister's tenderness with cunning, "you aren't walking home with Mirah. I'm certain she would prefer otherwise. You are dreadfully disagreeable today."

"I shall accompany her if she does not forbid it," Hans replied, opening the door.

Mirah remained silent. When he held the outer door open for her and stepped inside to close it behind him, he walked by her side without invitation. She lacked the courage to initiate conversation again, conscious that her words to him may have been excessively severe, yet finding only harsher sentiments lurking beneath them in her own heart. Furthermore, she was besieged by a swarm of thoughts rushing forward to interpret that consciousness which remained unchanged within herself.

Hans, too, harbored a mind equally preoccupied. Mirah's anger had awakened in him a fresh realization, accompanied by the unpleasant sensation that he had been a fool not to perceive it sooner. He pondered whether Mirah's heart was entirely consumed by Deronda in a capacity other than that of her brother's benefactor. This supposition bred anxieties in Hans's mind that, to his credit, were not wholly selfish. He possessed a strong conviction, unshakable except by direct counter-evidence, that a serious attachment existed between Deronda and Mrs. Grandcourt. Piecing together numerous fragments of observation and gathering knowledge corroborated by what his sisters had heard from Anna Gascoigne, he was convinced not only that Mrs. Grandcourt harbored a passion for Deronda but also that, despite his friend's austere self-restraint, Deronda's susceptibility toward her signaled concealed love.

Some men, holding such a belief, would avoid allusions capable of stirring that susceptibility; but Hans's natural inclination drifted toward mischief, enjoying a form of playful experimentation on living subjects consisting of irritating his friends. These experiments had ultimately satisfied him that his suspicions were correct. Conversely, any sensitivity Deronda showed regarding attentions directed toward Mirah, Hans attributed sufficiently to the alleged reason of her dependent position; he credited his

Something that Mirah had recently watched for as the fulfillment of a threat now appeared as the persistent visit of that familiar sorrow which had returned laden with heavy baggage. Having emerged from Knightsbridge after singing at a charitable morning concert held in a wealthy household—where she had been introduced by Klesmer, and where crowds had gathered outside to bid farewell to the departing guests—she soon felt herself pursued by footsteps keeping a steady, unnerving pace beside her own. Her concert dress was simple black, covered by a dust-cloak; it offered no pretext for unpleasant scrutiny nor rendered walking imprudent. Yet, these rational considerations failed to reach Mirah's mind. A different alarm dominated her thoughts.

She immediately thought of her father. She could no longer glance around as if she were being stalked by a ghost. To turn and confront him would be to voluntarily invite the rush of emotions she had previously deemed unbearable. If it were indeed her father, he must intend to demand recognition, forcing her to face him. She decided to wait for that compulsion. Walking on, she did not quicken her step—for what purpose?—but she pictured the impending encounter with absolute certainty, assuming the man trailing her was her father. Alongside this visualization arose a regret: she had promised Mrs. Meyrick not to conceal anything regarding him. This regret eventually spurred her to attempt preventing a sudden betrayal that might shock her brother unnecessarily. Driven by this motive, she resolved to turn before reaching her own door, choosing to actively summon the encounter rather than passively submit to it.

She had already arrived at the entrance of the small square housing her home and prepared to turn when she felt her embodied presentiment closing in, slipping to her side, grasping her wrist, and saying, with a persuasive curl of accent, "Mirah!"

She paused instantly, without a start. It was the voice she expected, and she met the anticipated gaze. Her face remained as grave as if she were facing her executioner, while his expression was adjusted to soothe and appease her. Once handsome, with bright coloring, his face was now sallow and deeply lined, bearing that peculiar stamp of impudent smoothness that arises from courting favor while accepting disrespect. Lightly built and active, he possessed a semblance of youth that made the signs of age appear like a disguise; in truth, he was barely fifty-seven. His attire was shabby, as she remembered seeing it before.

The presence of this irreverent father struck Mirah anew with a mixture of anguish, shame, grief, repulsion, and pity—feelings intensified now that her own world had transformed into one devoid of comradeship capable of shielding him from scorn and contempt.

Slowly, with a sad, tremulous voice, she said, "It is you, father."

"Why did you run away from me, child?" he began, speaking rapidly in a tone intended as tender remonstrance, accompanied by various quick gestures akin to an abbreviated finger-language. "What were you afraid of? You knew I never forced you to act against your will. It was for your sake I broke up your engagement in the Vorstadt, for I saw it did not suit you, and you repaid me by leaving me to the difficult times that ensued. I had arranged an easier engagement for you at the Vorstadt Theater in Dresden; I withheld this news to surprise you. And you left me stranded there, compelled to vanish because I had breached a contract. That was hard lines for me, after sacrificing everything to secure an education for you, which was to become your fortune. What father devoted himself more to his daughter than I did to you? You know how I bore that disappointment in your silence, making the best of it. When I had no one but you, and was breaking down—as men must who fight their way with their intellect alone—you chose that moment to abandon me. Who else did you owe everything to if not to me? Where was your gratitude in return? For what my daughter cared, I might have died in a ditch."

Lapidoth halted abruptly, not from a lack of invention, but because he had reached a pathetic climax, emitting a sudden sob like a woman's. He hastily produced an old yellow silk handkerchief. He genuinely felt that his daughter had treated him poorly—a sensitivity naturally strong in unscrupulous individuals who demand what is theirs without acknowledging any counter-obligations.

Despite his sob, Mirah possessed enough energy not to allow him to believe he had deceived her. She answered more firmly, though it was the first time she had ever used accusatory words toward him. "You know why I left you, father; and I had reason to distrust you, for I was certain you had deceived my mother. Had I trusted you, I would have stayed with you and worked for you."

"I never meant to deceive your mother, Mirah," Lapidoth replied, tucking away his handkerchief, his voice struggling against further weeping. "I intended to take you back to her, but circumstances hindered me precisely at that time, and then news of her death arrived. It was better for you that I remain where I was, allowing your brother to care for himself. No one had any claim upon me but you. I received word of your mother's death from a particular friend entrusted with managing my affairs, and I sent him money to cover expenses. There is one possibility to consider—"

Lapidoth quickly conceived he must guard against an unlikely yet plausible scenario. "He may have written me falsehoods solely to extract money from me."

Mirah offered no response. She could not bear to utter the only truthful reply: "I do not believe a single word of what you say." Instead, she simply indicated a desire to continue walking, sensing that standing still might attract unwanted attention. As they proceeded, their companionship was sufficient to make a passerby turn back to stare. The figure of Mirah, her beauty enhanced by the quiet, careful dress of an English lady, formed a strange contrast to this shabby, foreign-looking, eager, and gesticulating man. Yet, he retained an indelible jauntness of manner, perhaps due to the bushy curls of his grizzled hair, the smallness of his hands and feet, and his light stride.

"You seem to have done well for yourself, Mirah?" the father asked, examining her with emphatic scrutiny. "You are in no want, I see."

"Good friends who found me in distress have helped me secure work," Mirah replied, hardly aware of her actual words, consumed by what she needed to say later. "I give lessons. I have sung in private houses. I have just performed at a private concert." She paused, then added significantly, "I have very good friends who know all about me."

"And you would be ashamed if they saw your father in this plight? No wonder. I came to England with no prospects, save the chance of finding you. It was a mad quest; but a father's heart is superstitious—it feels a loadstone drawing it somewhere. I might have prospered staying abroad; when I had no one to care for, I could roll or settle as easily as a ball. But it is hard to be lonely in the world when one's spirit begins to break. I thought my little Mirah would repent leaving her father when she looked back. I've had a sharp pinch working my way; I don't know what I'll descend to next. Talents like mine are useless in this country. When a man is reduced to rags, no one believes in him. I couldn't obtain decent employment with my appearance. I've been forced to sink quite low for a shilling already."

Mirah's anxiety was swift enough to imagine her father sinking into further degradation, a fate she felt bound to prevent if possible. Before she could respond to his string of inventive sentences, delivered with the glibness of someone reciting a memorized script, he prompted, "Where do you live, Mirah?"

"Here, in this square. We are not far from the house."

"In lodgings?"

"Yes."

"Anyone to take care of you?"

"Yes," Mirah repeated, fixing her gaze on the keen face turned toward hers— "my brother."

The father's eyelids fluttered as if lightning had flashed across them, accompanied by a slight shrug of the shoulders. After a brief, perceptible pause, he asked, "Ezra? How did you know—how did you find him?"

"That would take long to explain. Here we are at the door. My brother would not wish me to shut it on you."

Mirah was already on the doorstep, yet her face remained turned toward her father, who stood below on the pavement. Her heart began to beat faster at the prospect of introducing him to Ezra. Already, in this posture of granting permission to the father she had been accustomed to obey—seeing him shrink beneath her, indirectly asking for admission—she felt a pang of peculiar, sympathetic humiliation and shame, the stabbed heart of reverence belonging to a nature intensely filial.

"Stay a minute, *Liebchen*," Lapidoth said in a lowered tone. "What sort of man has Ezra turned out?"

"A good man—a wonderful man," Mirah replied with slow emphasis, striving to master the agitation that made her voice trem

"Moses, despite his opposition to art, was himself a great artist and possessed the true artistic spirit. Yet this spirit in him, as in his Egyptian countrymen, was directed solely toward the colossal and the imperishable. But unlike the Egyptians, who shaped their works from brick and granite, he built human pyramids, hewed human obelisks; he took a poor shepherd tribe and forged it into a people destined to withstand the centuries... he created Israel!" — HEINE: *Confessions*.

Consider the shift in Daniel Deronda's inner landscape between the moment he departed England and his return. He had sailed for Genoa shrouded in total uncertainty regarding how his deepest desires and affections would be received, fearing that the claims newly revealed to him might pull him onto paths diverging sharply from the ones his thoughts had recently followed with a hesitant, dangerous consent. He returned, however, bearing something akin to a discovered charter validating the inherited right his ambition had long yearned for. More than mere freedom, he carried a dutiful bond—an obligation his experiences had prepared him to welcome gladly, even if it offered no guarantee of satisfying a secret, passionate longing that had never yet grown into hope. Now, he dared to acknowledge to himself the specific object of his love.

Since the hour he left the Chelsea house in silent devotion, stirred by the exquisite appeal of Mirah's farewell gaze and words, her hold on his heart had fortified his conduct, keeping him blameless amidst difficult circumstances. Though he sensed no likelihood of ever courting a creature who had become dear amidst associations that forbade courtship, she had secured a place in his soul as a beloved archetype. Her presence diminished the power of other fascinations, creating a void where they once stood. This influence grew steadily stronger. It was fate that Gwendolen's dependence on Deronda aroused in him an enthusiasm of self-sacrificing pity rather than personal love, allowing his tenderer feelings to flow freely toward an internalized image entirely unlike Gwendolen. Furthermore, his growing closeness to Mordecai drew him nearer to Mirah, a connection that remained agitating precisely because his outward position toward her appeared unchanged. She was inevitably woven into every thought that made him recoil from outcomes disappointing to her brother.

This transformation was not unconscious in Deronda; he was aware of it, much as one recognizes a covetousness better nullified by distraction than confessed. Yet, jealousy flared at Hans's pretensions, and when his mother accused him of loving a Jewess, every evasion suddenly felt like betrayal. His mother forced a decisive acknowledgment of his love, just as Joseph Kalonymos had compelled a definite statement of his resolve. This new state of decision struck Deronda with a force that surprised him. All the energy previously spent on self-restraint and suppression due to doubtful conditions was released. As he neared England from Mainz, the remaining distance felt increasingly like an obstruction, prompting laughter at his own impetuosity. It was as if discovering his ancestry had granted him an additional soul. His judgment ceased wandering in the labyrinths of impartial sympathy, choosing instead the partiality that is humanity's greatest strength: the closer fellowship that makes sympathy practical. He exchanged the "bird's-eye" reasonableness that soars to avoid preference for the generous rationality of standing shoulder-to-shoulder with men of shared heritage. He craved to be with Mordecai again, to pour forth rather than restrain his feelings, to agree and dissent alike, all while encountering Mirah without the embarrassment of obvious pursuit, interpreting her looks and words from a new foundation. While he was not greatly alarmed by Hans's attentions, he suspected Mirah's feelings had flowed from the start in a channel unlikely to divert into love. To astonish a woman by becoming her lover when she viewed him merely as a Lord Chancellor was a transition men naturally seek to smooth.

No wonder, then, that Deronda saw only one path: to proceed directly from the London railway station to the lodgings in the small square in Brompton. Every argument urged haste. He had promised to visit Lady Mallinger at the Abbey the following day, and it was already sunset. He wished to deposit the precious chest with Mordecai, who would study its contents both alone and in his company; doing so without delay would please Mordecai's heart, and thus, Deronda's. The strongest currents of his nature rushed in one direction—the fervent affectionate delight in fulfilling the wishes of those near him, and the imaginative need for a far-reaching relation to broaden the horizon of his daily acts. In this classical, romantic, world-historic position, bringing forth his hereditary armor from hiding, he wore, as surely as the most ancient heroes did, the summer costume of his contemporaries. He did not reflect that the drab tones suited him, for he rarely indulged in such introspection; yet his own depth of coloring lent the attire an added radiance, a fleeting, returning glow in his skin and eyes, as he entered the house wondering what he would find. He made his entrance as silently as possible.

It was the evening of the very afternoon Mirah had spoken with her father. Mordecai, pierced by her grief and haunted by the sad memories the incident had awakened, had not resumed his task of sorting papers. Some lay scattered on the floor in the initial moments of anxiety, neither he nor Mirah thinking to arrange them again. They sat perfectly still, unaware of the passage of time, while the clock ticked on the mantelpiece and the light faded. Unable to think of the food she should have eaten, Mirah had not moved since throwing off her dust-cloak to sit beside Mordecai, her hand in his. He leaned his head back, eyes closed, breathing with difficulty, appearing, Mirah thought, as one whose soul could no longer inhabit its cramped dwelling. The thought of his imminent death visited her constantly when she saw his face thus devoid of vivid animation. To her existing grief was added the regret that she had failed to curb the violent outburst that had shaken him. She watched him—oval cheeks pale, eyes shining with the sorrowful brilliance of recent tears, curls disordered like a child's just waking—gazing at that emaciated face, where it seemed a veil had been drawn forever, as if it were her dead joy that sustained her strength in sorrow. Life stretched before Mirah with more than a repetition of past sadness; the shadow of the father loomed large, compounded by a double bereavement—one living, one dead.

Then the door opened. No one entered, but a familiar voice asked, "Daniel Deronda—may he come in?"

"Come! Come!" cried Mordecai, rising instantly with an irradiated face and open eyes, seemingly unsurprised as if expecting the visitor in the morning; Mirah jumped up, blushing with confused, half-alarm.

Yet when Deronda entered, his sight was like clarity after rain; no coming clouds could hinder the cherishing beam of that moment. Holding out his right hand to Mirah, who stood near her brother's left, he placed his other hand on Mordecai's right shoulder, standing thus for a moment, holding them both, silent, reading their faces until he asked anxiously of Mirah, "Has anything happened? Any trouble?"

"Speak not of trouble now," said Mordecai, sparing her the need to answer. "There is joy in your face—let the joy be ours."

Mirah thought, *It is for something he cannot tell us.* They all sat down, Deronda pulling a chair close before Mordecai.

"That is true," he said emphatically. "I have a joy that will remain with us even in the worst trouble. I did not tell you the reason for my journey abroad, Mordecai, because—never mind. I went to learn my parentage. And you were right. I am a Jew."

The two men clasped hands in a movement that seemed part of a flash from Mordecai's eyes, passing through Mirah like an electric shock. Deronda continued without pause, speaking from Mordecai's mind as much as his own: "We share the same people. Our souls share the same vocation. Life or death shall not separate us."

Mordecai's reply was uttered in Hebrew, in no more than a loud whisper, using the liturgical words expressing the religious bond: "Our God and the God of our fathers."

The weight of feeling pressed too heavily on that ready-winged speech, which usually adapted itself swiftly to every stir of his fervor.

Mirah fell to her knees beside her brother, gazing at his now illuminated face, which had moments prior been deathly pale. The action was an inevitable outlet for the violent reversal from despair to a gladness that swept over her as solemnly as if witnessing a religious rite. For the moment, she considered the effect on her own life only through its impact on her brother.

"And it is not only that I am a Jew," Deronda continued, enjoying one of those rare moments when yearnings and actions align perfectly, revealing the ideal good as reality; "but I descend from a strain that has ardently maintained our race's fellowship—a line of Spanish Jews that produced many scholars and men of practical power. I possess what will grant us a communion with them. My grandfather, Daniel Charisi, preserved manuscripts and family records stretching far back, hoping they would reach his grandson. Now his hope is fulfilled, despite attempts to thwart it by concealing my parentage. I possess the chest containing them, along with his own papers, located downstairs in this house. I intend to leave it with you, Mordecai, so you may assist me in studying the manuscripts. Some I can read easily—Spanish

"Questa montagna è tale, / Che sempre al cominciar di sotto è grave. / E quanto uom più va su e men fa male." —*Dante: Il Purgatorio*.

It was not many days after her mother's arrival that Gwendolen consented to remain at Genoa. Her desperate desire to flee that jewel of the sea actually served to rally her fading strength and courage. For what place, however idyllic it may appear—a flowery vale of Enna—is not capable of becoming, in the inward sense, a circle of punishment where the blossoms transform into a crop of flame-tongues searing the soles of our feet?

"I shall never like to see the Mediterranean again," Gwendolen told her mother, who seemed to understand her child's feeling perfectly—even in her tacit refusal to allow any direct mention of her late husband. Mrs. Davilow, though formally compelled to regard this season as one of severe calamity, was virtually enjoying life more than she had since her daughter's marriage. It appeared that her darling was returned to her not merely with all the old affection, but with a conscious cherishing of her mother's presence, akin to holding onto a possession one has nearly lost forever.

"Are you there, mamma?" Gwendolen cried out in the middle of the night (a bed had been prepared for her mother in the same room). She spoke with the same instinctive reliance she might have shown in her early girlhood if she had felt frightened lying awake.

"Yes, dear; can I do anything for you?"

"No, thank you; only I like so to know you are there. Do you mind my waking you?" (This question would hardly have been Gwendolen's in her youth.)

"I was not asleep, darling."

"It seemed unreal that you were with me. I wanted to make it real. I can bear things if you are with me. But you must not lie awake, anxious about me. You must be happy now. You must let me make you happy now at last—or else, what shall I do?"

"God bless you, dear; I have the best happiness I can have when you make much of me."

But the next night, hearing her mother sighing and restless, Mrs. Davilow suggested, "Let me give you your sleeping-draught, Gwendolen."

"No, mamma, thank you; I don't want to sleep."

"It would be so good for you to sleep more, my darling."

"Don't say what would be good for me, mamma," Gwendolen answered impetuously. "You don't know what would be good for me. You and my uncle must not contradict me and tell me anything is good for me when I feel it is not."

Mrs. Davilow remained silent, not surprised that the poor child was irritable. Presently, Gwendolen confessed, "I was always naughty to you, mamma."

"No, dear, no."

"Yes, I was," Gwendolen insisted. "It is because I was always wicked that I am miserable now." She burst into sobs and cries. The determination to remain silent regarding all the facts of her married life and its tragic conclusion reacted violently in these escapes of enigmatic excitement. Yet, dim lights of interpretation began to break upon the mother's mind through information arriving from Sir Hugo to Mr. Gascoigne, and subsequently, with some omissions, from Mr. Gascoigne to himself.

The good-natured baronet, while attending to all decent measures concerning his nephew's death and the possibility of the body washing ashore, deemed it the kindest thing he could do to use his friendly intercourse with the rector as an opportunity to communicate the purport of Grandcourt's will. His aim was to spare the rector the additional shock that would await him if he carried his illusions all the way home. Perhaps Sir Hugo would have shared the news regardless of this motive, but he genuinely felt it necessary. He broke the unpleasant news by degrees: at first implying only his fear that the widow was not as splendidly provided for as Mr. Gascoigne, or even as the baronet himself had expected; and only finally, after some vague references to large claims on Grandcourt, did he disclose the prior relations which, in the unfortunate absence of a legitimate heir, had directed all the estate's splendor elsewhere.

The rector was deeply hurt. He remembered, more vividly than ever, how offensively proud and repelling the deceased's manners had been toward him. He recalled also that, in that interesting period just before the new occupant arrived at Diplow, he had received hints of former entangling dissipations and an undue addiction to pleasure, though he had not foreseen that the pleasure which had probably been swept into private rubbish-heaps would ever present itself as an array of live caterpillars, disastrous to the green meat of respectable people. Nevertheless, he did not make these retrospective thoughts audible to Sir Hugo, nor did he lower himself by expressing indignation on merely personal grounds. Instead, he behaved like a man of the world who had become a conscientious clergyman.

His first remark was, "When a young man makes his will in health, he usually counts on living a long while. Probably Mr. Grandcourt did not believe that this will would ever have its present effect." After a moment, he added, "The effect is painful in more ways than one. Female morality is likely to suffer from this marked advantage and prominence being given to illegitimate offspring."

"Well, in point of fact," said Sir Hugo, in his comfortable way, "since the boy is there, this was really the best alternative for the disposal of the estates. Grandcourt had nobody nearer than his cousin. And it's a chilling thought that you go out of this life only for the benefit of a cousin. A man gets a little pleasure in making his will if it's for the good of his own curly heads; but it's a nuisance when you're giving the bequeathing to a used-up fellow like yourself, and one you don't care two straws for. It's the next worst thing to having only a life interest in your estates. No; I forgive Grandcourt for that part of his will. But, between ourselves, what I don't forgive him for is the shabby way he has provided for your niece—_our_ niece, I will say—no better a position than if she had been a doctor's widow. Nothing grates on me more than that posthumous grudgingness toward a wife. A man ought to have some pride and fondness for his widow. _I_ should, I know. I take it as a test of a man that he feels the easier about his death when he can think of his wife and daughters being comfortable after it. I like that story of the fellows in the Crimean War, who were ready to go to the bottom of the sea if their widows were provided for."

"It has certainly taken me by surprise," said Mr. Gascoigne, "all the more because, as the one who stood in the place of father to my niece, I had shown my reliance on Mr. Grandcourt's apparent liberality in money matters by making no claims for her beforehand. That seemed to me due to him under the circumstances. Probably you think me blamable."

"Not blamable exactly. I respect a man for trusting another. But take my advice. If you marry another niece, though it may be to the Archbishop of Canterbury, bind him down. Your niece can't be married for the first time twice over. And if he's a good fellow, he'll wish to be bound. But as to Mrs. Grandcourt, I can only say that I feel my relation to her all the nearer because I think that she has not been well treated. And I hope you will urge her to rely on me as a friend."

Thus spake the chivalrous Sir Hugo, disgusted that the young and beautiful widow of a Mallinger Grandcourt was left with only two thousand a year and a house in a coal-mining district. To the rector, that income naturally appeared less shabby and less accompanied by mortifying privations; but in this conversation, he devoured a much keener sense than the baronet's of the humiliation cast over his niece, and also over her nearest friends, by the conspicuous publication of her husband's relation to Mrs. Glasher. Like all men who are good husbands and fathers, he felt the humiliation through the minds of the women who would be chiefly affected by it; so that the annoyance of first hearing the facts was far slighter than what he felt in communicating them to Mrs. Davilow, and in anticipating Gwendolen's feelings whenever her mother saw fit to tell her of them. For the good rector held an innocent conviction that his niece was unaware of Mrs. Glasher's existence, arguing with masculine soundness from what maidens and wives were likely to know, do, and suffer, and having had a most imperfect observation of the particular maiden and wife in question.

Not so Gwendolen's mother, who now thought she saw an explanation of much that had been enigmatic in her child's conduct and words before and after her engagement. She concluded that in some inconceivable way Gwendolen had been informed of this left-handed marriage and the existence of the children. She trusted to opportunities arising in moments of affectionate confidence before and during their journey to England, when she might gradually learn how far the actual state of things was clear to Gwendolen, and prepare her for whatever disappointment might lie ahead. But she was spared from devising strategies on the subject.

"I hope you don't expect that I am going to be rich and grand, mamma," Gwendolen

"O, welcome, pure-eyed Faith, white-handed Hope, Thou hovering angel, girt with golden wings!"

Deronda did not answer Gwendolen's summons without a certain inner turmoil. It was not his pride that stirred, but his profound empathy. He feared the possibility that another's heart might demand more of him than he could possibly give. He realized, with a clarity that required no debate, that Gwendolen's soul clung to his with a desperate, passionate need. One does not logically argue against the anger or the scorn that vibrates through a voice; one simply feels it, undeniable and absolute. Deronda felt the weight of this woman's fate balancing precariously above a cliff of despair.

To anyone who truly knew him, it would not surprise them to hear his silent admission: if these events had occurred barely a year prior, he would scarcely have questioned whether he loved her. The driving force within him would have been an impulsive urge to shield her from sorrow, to protect her life forever from the terrors of isolation, and to complete the rescue he had begun with the redemption of the necklace. Yet, now, love and duty had woven other chains around him. That initial impulse could no longer dictate his path. Still, it remained within him as a yearning compassion, a painful trembling whenever he imagined facing her gaze and words again. Precisely because the bond was so strong, and his resolve to stand apart so firm, he watched her situation with an ache of pity that grew sharper.

He waited for her arrival in the back drawing-room—a section of the white and crimson space where they had once shared a musical evening. It was there, for the first time, that Gwendolen had declared her destiny hinged on his refusal to abandon her, her plea seeming to dissolve into the melodic cry, *Per pietà non darmi addio*. But that melody had belonged to Mirah's sweet voice. Deronda paced this room, which he had known intimately for years, experiencing a strange sensation of metamorphosis in his own life. The familiar surroundings—from Lady Mallinger's gentle portrait to the human, urbane faces of the lions adorning the chimney-piece—seemed to belong to a past existence, visited only in memory rather than reality. The impressions he had recently endured were so deep and transformative, and his circumstances so altered, that the house he had once considered home now felt foreign. He stood there, hat in hand, awaiting the entry of a young creature whose life was undergoing a tragic transformation toward an uncertain outcome, one in which he felt his own actions were still deeply entangled.

Gwendolen arrived, visibly changed. Beyond her mourning attire, her expression possessed a quiet satisfaction he had not seen in Genoa. This satisfaction stemmed from Deronda's presence, though neither offered a smile as they met and clasped hands; both were heavy with remembrance and anxious foreboding.

"It was good of you to come," she said immediately after sitting in the nearest chair. "Let us sit down."

He positioned himself opposite her. "I asked you to come because I want you to tell me what I ought to do," she began without hesitation. "Do not be afraid to tell me what you think is right, even if it sounds harsh. I have decided to do it. Once, I was terrified of poverty; I could not bear the thought of living under someone else's roof—that was why I married. I have endured worse things since. I believe I could endure poverty if you think it necessary. Do you know the terms of my husband's will?"

"Yes, Sir Hugo informed me," Deronda replied, anticipating her question.

"Should I accept anything he left me? Let me explain my thoughts," Gwendolen continued, her voice gaining a nervous urgency. "Perhaps you do not fully realize how much I contemplated my mother when I married. I was selfish, yes, but I loved her and felt acutely about her poverty. The greatest comfort I found during my misery was knowing she was better off because of my marriage. Now, the hardest thing for me would be to see her impoverished again. I have been considering that if I took enough to support her, and nothing for myself, it would not be wrong. After all, I was precious to her—he took me from her, and he intended... and if she had known..."

Gwendolen trailed off. She had prepared for this meeting by focusing almost exclusively on this question of right regarding her mother, but the issue brought forth thoughts and reasons she could not articulate. These perilous memories swirled between her words, causing her speech to grow increasingly agitated and tremulous. She looked down helplessly at her bare hands, stripped of every ring save her wedding band.

"Do not harm yourself by dwelling on that," Deronda said softly. "There is no need; the matter is straightforward. I doubt I could judge incorrectly. You seek my counsel because I am the only one to whom you have confessed the most painful parts of your experience, and I can understand your scruples."

He paused, allowing her to compose herself. The silence felt to Gwendolen thick with the tenderness in his voice, giving her the courage to lift her eyes and meet his gaze as he spoke. "You are conscious of something you feel to be a crime against one who is dead. You believe you have forfeited all claims as a wife. You recoil from accepting what belongs to him. You wish to avoid profiting from his death. Indeed, your feelings urge you toward self-punishment—a scourging of the self that disobeyed your better judgment, the will that fought against temptation. I have known something similar myself. Have I understood you correctly?"

"Yes—at least, I want to be good, not as I have been," Gwendolen replied. "I will try to bear whatever you think I must bear. I have told you the worst about myself. What ought I to do?"

"If no one else were involved in this question of income," Deronda said, "I would hesitate to urge you against your remorseful impulses. However, I am guided now by your feelings toward Mrs. Davilow, which seem entirely just to me. I cannot believe your husband's obligations to you personally are nullified by your actions. He voluntarily entered your life and profoundly influenced its course. Setting that aside, it was his duty, given his station, to provide for your mother, and he surely understood that if this will took effect, she would share in the provision made for you."

"She receives eight hundred pounds a year," Gwendolen noted. "What I considered was taking that amount and leaving the rest." Having argued internally for so long that this was permissible, her mind struggled instantly to shift to another perspective.

"I do not think it is your duty to set such a limit," Deronda advised. "You would create a painful mystery for Mrs. Davilow; an income from which you exclude yourself would become bitter to her. Furthermore, your own path would become unnecessarily difficult. We agreed in Genoa that the burden on your conscience is one no one should be forced to acknowledge. The future goodness of your life will be best served by sparing others the pain of that knowledge. In my opinion, you should simply follow the provisions of your husband's will, allowing your remorse to influence only how you choose to use your newfound financial independence."

As he finished, Deronda instinctively picked up his hat, which he had placed on the floor beside him. Gwendolen, attuned to his slightest movements, felt her heart leap as if possessing its own consciousness, fearing he might depart. Simultaneously, she rose from her chair, unable to consider that his motion signaled an intention to leave. Deronda, naturally, also stood, stepping forward slightly.

"I will do what you tell me," Gwendolen said hurriedly. "But what else shall I do?"

No other words were possible for her in that emotional state where her proud secrecy had crumbled. As the childlike sentences escaped her lips, they struck her like a mirror reflecting her own helplessness, and she could not stop the sob that brought large tears to her eyes. Deronda, too, felt a crushing pain, yet visible consequences weighed heavily on him, urging him to exert his conscience to the utmost.

Once she had brushed away her tears, he asked gently, "You will likely be traveling with Mrs. Davilow to the countryside soon."

"Yes, in a week or ten days." Gwendolen hesitated, turning her eyes vaguely toward the window as if gazing at some imagined horizon. "I want to be kind to them all—they can be happier than I can. Is that the best I can do?"

"I believe so. It is a duty beyond doubt," Deronda affirmed. He paused, feeling a heavy anxiety settle over his words. "Other duties will arise from this. Viewing your life as a debt may seem the dreariest perspective from afar, but it is not truly so. What makes life bleak is the lack of motive. Once you begin acting with the penitent, loving purpose you hold in your mind, unexpected satisfactions will emerge—newly opening needs that will propel you from day to day. You will find your life growing like a plant."

Gwendolen turned her gaze upon him with the look of one thirsting for the sound of unseen water. Deronda felt this gaze as if she were stretching her arms toward him from a deserted shore. His voice adopted an affectionate, imploring tone. "This sorrow, which has cut down to the roots, has come to you while you are so young. Try to view it not as a spoiling of your life, but as a preparation for it. Let it be a preparation..."

Any listener would have thought he was begging for his own happiness. "See!

"One day still fierce 'mid many a day struck calm."

While Gwendolen remained oblivious to the shifting currents of Daniel Deronda's life, two others—Ezra and Mirah—were drawn inexorably closer to him, their relationship illuminated with painful clarity.

Lapidoth had abandoned his daughter at the threshold, driven by a sudden, desperate gamble. The mere prospect of wagering a sum greater than what was needed to quell immediate hunger had seized his mind, erasing all other considerations. Until he was utterly ruined, he never paused to weigh whether he should seek Mirah again or face his son. In that initial panic, he recoiled from confronting Ezra as one might shrink from any unbearable social constraint; possessing Mirah's purse was sufficient to banish thoughts of future need entirely.

The craving for chance is often more absolute than the need for food. Hunger can be soothed by distraction or excitement, but the obsession with watching odds—the mental suspension required by play—numbs all other impulses. In its final, tyrannical stage, it resembles the aimless wandering of demons seeking amusement on the scorched earth of damnation. Yet, even the most abstract selfishness demands sustenance. Though Lapidoth's appetite for food and drink was naturally modest, he had slid into a squalid existence where survival required cash on hand.

When, during a brief stop at a house marked "Pyramids" on the blind, he had doubled and trebled Mirah's thirty shillings only to lose them all, he stepped out with her empty purse in his pocket. Already, his mind was racing between two options: pawn the purse for an immediate stake, or return it to her with a forced smile, claiming he had used the money to settle a debt. Among the few sensibilities remaining in Lapidoth was a stubborn belief in his own rights; he convinced himself that his claim on his children's possessions outweighed the justice of his son's anger. Ultimately, he reasoned that living with his children was the best path forward. The more he contemplated facing Ezra, the less he flinched, his imagination intoxicated by the fantasy of winning money safely without effort, rather than terrified by the threat of private humiliation.

Luck had recently turned against him, but he expected a reversal. Could not the tide turn with an influx of funds from his daughter's affairs or the generous friends she had mentioned? Lapidoth clung to the illusion of his own cleverness—a habit forged by early experience. It is not only women who fail to recognize their fading allure or pretend they are untouched by exhaustion.

His rapid calculations led him to the small square in Brompton. Hoping to spot Mirah entering or leaving the house, he believed his entry would be smoother. But it was already evening—the day following his first encounter with her. After a short wait, weariness took hold, prompting him to consider ringing the bell and asking when she might return. However, as he approached, he knew she was inside; he heard her singing.

Mirah sat at the piano, pouring out the melody of *"Herz, mein Herz,"* while Ezra listened with his eyes closed. Suddenly, Mrs. Adam opened the door, her voice tinged with embarrassment: "A gentleman below says he is your father, miss."

"I will go down to him," Mirah replied, rising instantly and glancing at her brother.

"No, Mirah, not so," Ezra said firmly. "Let him come up, Mrs. Adam."

Mirah stood frozen, fingers twisting nervously, her stomach churning with anxiety. She watched Ezra, who had also risen, visibly shaken. There was an expression on his face she had never seen before: his brow knitted, his lips set in a severe hardness that mirrored the cold fire in his eyes.

As Mrs. Adam admitted the stranger, she glanced at the tense pair. Comparing the younger man to the older, she murmured to herself as she closed the door, "Father, sure enough."

The resemblance was primarily one of outline, strikingly apparent at first glance. Yet, subtle, hidden differences had carved the strongest contrasts into his features, transforming the spirit of a Cromwell within the visage of a man who had once been merely a respectable parishioner. Lapidoth had adopted a mournful demeanor beforehand, but a genuine wince tightened his frame as he spoke.

"Well, Ezra, my boy, you hardly know me after so many years."

"I know you—too well, father," Ezra replied, his tone slow, biting, and solemn, rendering the word *father* a sharp reproach.

"Oh, you are displeased with me. No wonder. Circumstances have been cruel. When a man falls into straits, he cannot act freely, nor can anyone else control him. I have suffered enough, I know," Lapidoth said quickly.

In speech, he always regained a slickness and boldness. Turning then to Mirah, he extended her purse. "Here is your little purse, my dear. I feared you'd worry about that note. I've emptied it; you'll see. I had a score to pay for food and lodging. I knew you'd want me to clear my name, and here I stand—with not a penny in my pocket—at the mercy of my children. You can turn me out if you wish, without calling the police. Just say the word, Mirah. Say, 'Father, I've had enough of you. You made a pet of me and spent your all on me when I couldn't live without you, but I can manage without you now.' Say that, and I'm gone, vanishing like a spark. I won't ruin your happiness again." Tears welled in his voice before he finished.

"You know I could never say that, father," Mirah answered, her anguish undiminished despite recognizing the falsehood of his words, save for his underlying desire to remain.

"Mirah, my sister, leave us!" Ezra commanded, his tone authoritative.

She looked at her brother vacantly, pleadingly. Awe of his decision paralyzed her, yet she could not depart without making a plea for this father, who felt like a part of her own flesh grown through pain. Moving close to her brother, she placed her hand in his, whispering softly—but loud enough for Lapidoth to hear—"Remember, Ezra, you said my mother would not have shut him out."

"Trust me, and go," Ezra said.

She retreated from the room. A few steps up the stairs later, she collapsed onto a seat, her heart pounding. If her brother's words drove him away...

Lapidoth sensed the storm brewing in his son's mind. He began to adjust, seeking a perspective that granted him a cool superiority over any attempt to humiliate him. This gaunt son, speaking as from a tomb, possessed the jarring incongruity that selfishness learns to admire in suffering. Only when the relentless claws of disease seize its own flesh does such hypocrisy crumble. Any sermonizing delivered must be dismissed as routine, like a passerby ignoring the religious wailing echoing from an open cathedral while seeking shelter from a hailstorm. Lapidoth was not born callous; he had cultivated it.

"This home we have here," Ezra began, "is sustained partly by the generosity of a beloved friend who supports me, and partly by the labor of my sister, who supports herself. As long as we have a roof, we will not eject you. We will not cast you out to the vagaries of your vices. For you are our father, and though you have broken your bond, we acknowledge ours. But I will never trust you. You absconded with money, leaving debts unpaid; you deserted my mother; you stole her child and shattered her heart. You became a gambler, and where shame and conscience once resided, now sits an insatiable void. You were ready to sell my sister—you had offered her, but the price was refused. A man who has done these things can never expect trust again. We will share our food with you; you shall have a bed and clothing. We fulfill this duty because you are our father. But you will never be trusted. You are an evil man; you caused the misery of our mother. That such a creature is our father is a brand upon our flesh that will never cease to sting. Yet the Eternal has decreed it. Even if human justice were to scourge you for your crimes and your body failed beneath public scorn, we would still declare, 'This is our father; make way, that we may carry him out of our sight.'"

Lapidoth, preparing himself for the blow, had not anticipated the precise intensity of the lightning or its trajectory—it would not strike around him but pierce right through his frame. He could not foresee the novelty of this voice emerging from the soul of his son. It tapped into that spring of hysterical excitability Mirah had witnessed when he sat at home sobbing.

As Ezra concluded, Lapidoth threw himself into a chair and wept like a woman, burying his face in the table. Strangely, while this hysterical breakdown was an inevitable physiological reaction to his son's words, it also served as a conscious resource. Just as in his youth, when he was a bright-faced, curly-haired young man, he had learned to exploit this finely tuned physical susceptibility to blunt resentment or disapproval.

Ezra sat silently, exhausted by the shock of his own uncontrollable outburst—the release of feelings he had carried alone and in silence for years. His thin hands trembled on the chair arms; he would barely have found the strength to answer a question. He felt as though he had taken a step toward summoning death.

The divine spark within us often twists our noblest actions into struggles against our own reluctance. For Daniel Deronda, returning from the Abbey to his lodgings in Brompton proved to be an unpleasant surprise: the "undesirable" father had already settled into their home.

Mirah had deemed it necessary to inform her father of Deronda's existence. She had carefully constructed her narrative, detailing the origins of her friendship with Ezra and the deep sympathy that bound them, yet she skirted the specifics of Deronda's aid to her. She omitted entirely the dramatic rescue from drowning and minimized her refuge in the Meyrick household, leading her father to assume Deronda had met her merely through Ezra's circle. Unable to expose the full depth of her connection to Deronda, she shielded her relationship from her father's judgment. Consequently, Lapidoth, though curious about her flight and arrival in England, showed little eagerness to probe further. However, he was deeply intrigued by the news that his children possessed a benevolent friend seemingly of high station.

It was Ezra's brother who informed Deronda of this new development. "I have grown calm observing him now," Ezra concluded. "I strive to believe that my sister's tenderness and her daily taste of peace may eventually win him to distance himself from temptation. I have instructed her, and she has promised, to entrust him with no money. I have convinced her that he would purchase his own ruin with it."

Deronda arrived three days after Lapidoth's installation. His newly tailored clothes were not yet ready, and wishing to make a favorable impression, he declined to appear in his old attire. Watching Deronda depart from the window, Lapidoth was struck by the young man's vitality—a quality Mirah had not emphasized. Having imagined Deronda to be a grave figure engaged in hoary studies alongside the somber Ezra, Lapidoth was surprised by his youth. He quickly surmised that Deronda's true motivation must be love for Mirah. To Lapidoth, this was advantageous; a bond with Mirah offered more promise of leniency for her father than one with Ezra alone. Hoping to recommend himself to Deronda and soften any initial prejudice, Lapidoth behaved with marked amiability. He sought easy domestication, engaging with Mirah's music, submitting to restrictions on smoking in Mrs. Adam's parlors, and strolling the square with his German pipe, using tobacco kindly supplied by Mirah. Acute enough to accept the refusal of funds—which Mirah insisted upon as a solemn duty owed to her brother—he remained comfortably patient.

On his next visit, Lapidoth, freshly dressed and pleased with his reflection, joined Ezra and Deronda. Ezra, performing a severe duty of self-discipline, tolerated his father's presence whenever required. Deronda, however, felt cold and distant. The sight of the man who had blighted the lives of his wife and children created in him a repulsion bordering on physical discomfort. Undeterred, Lapidoth requested permission to stay, listening to papers read from an old chest. He even proved useful, assisting in deciphering a difficult German manuscript. This led him to suggest transcribing the document, offering his services to copy manuscripts and documents in Roman characters. Although Ezra noted his own eyesight failing, he observed that Lapidoth's vision remained sharp. Deronda accepted the offer, interpreting it as a sign of grace in Lapidoth's willingness to be employable. He noticed a gratified expression on Ezra's face, though Ezra soon cautioned, "Let all the writing be done here; I cannot trust the papers out of my sight, lest there be an accident by fire or otherwise."

Poor Ezra felt akin to guarding a convict on leave. Unless he saw his father working, he could not believe he would labor in good faith. Yet, this arrangement fastened the heavy burden of his father's presence upon him. The atmosphere became painful, tainted not only by their deepest historical associations but also by Lapidoth's restless temperament. As Lapidoth grew accustomed to his situation, he shed any lingering awe for his son. In reality, Ezra was placing a strenuous constraint on himself, confining his attention solely to win Deronda's favor. Like a man trapped in an ill-fitting garment, he sought relief at every opportunity: stepping out to smoke, pacing and chatting, or slumping silently in his chair while conducting a mute dialogue of facial expressions and gestures. When Mirah was present, he reverted to his old habits, gossiping with her about past adventures, companions, play adaptations, and plotlines, believing he could summon the vivacity of his younger days. This was a mortal torment to Ezra. Whenever Mirah was home, she attempted to alleviate his suffering by escorting her father downstairs to the parlor, keeping watch over him there.

What duty arises from a single difficult resolve? Its challenge lies in the daily, unflinching endurance of consequences that mar the dawn with prospects of irritation to suppress or shame to endure. Such burdens were borne by these individuals, as by many other heroic children of unworthy fathers, with the hope—at least for Mirah—that their endurance would stretch through the solid years of life.

Meanwhile, Lapidoth's presence erected a new, intangible barrier between Deronda and Mirah. Both dreaded the potentially defiling inferences of his mind, each misinterpreting the heightened reserve and diffidence of the other.

However, clarity soon broke through for Deronda. Shortly after his return from the Abbey, he visited Hans Meyrick's rooms, feeling it a debt of friendship that Hans should be informed of the reasons for his recent journey and the shifts in intention it had wrought. Hans was absent, reportedly in the countryside for a few days. Leaving a note, Deronda waited a week, anticipating a reply. Receiving none, and fearing some erratic mood in the capricious Hans—who had postponed his stay at the Abbey—Deronda made a second call. He was admitted to the painting room, where he found his friend in a light coat without a waistcoat, his long hair still damp from a bath, yet with a face that looked worn and wizened—the antithesis of a country retreat. Hans held his palette and brushes before an easel, but the setup appeared hastily arranged.

As they shook hands, Deronda remarked, "You don't look much as if you'd been in the country, old fellow. Was it Cambridge?"

"No," Hans replied curtly, tossing down his palette with the air of someone who had feigned by mistake. Pushing forward a chair, he leaned back with his hands behind his head. "I've been to 'I-don't-know-where'—No Man's Land—and a mortally unpleasant country it is."

"You don't mean to say you've been drinking, Hans?" Deronda asked, seating himself opposite with an anxious gaze.

"Nothing so good. I've been smoking opium. I always intended to try it sometime to measure the bliss it could yield; finding myself recently devoid of other bliss, I judged it prudent to seize the chance. But I pledge you my word I shall never touch that cask of bliss again. It disagrees with my constitution."

"What has been the matter? You were in good spirits when you wrote."

"Oh, nothing in particular. The world began to look seedy—a sort of cabbage garden with all the heads cut. A malady of genius, you may be sure," Hans smiled grimly. "In fact, I was tired of being virtuous without reward, especially in this stifling London heat."

"Nothing else? No real vexation?"

Hans shook his head. "I came to tell you of my own affairs, but I lack the grace to do so if you intend to hide yours."

"Haven't an affair in the world," Hans said flippantly, "except a quarrel with a bric-à-brac man. Besides, since it's the first time in our lives you've spoken of your own matters, you're only beginning to repay a rather long debt."

Deronda sensed Hans was acting artificially but trusted that old frankness would return if he shared his own confidence. "You laughed at the mystery of my journey to Italy, Hans," he began. "It was for an object touching my happiness at its roots. I knew nothing of my parents until I went to Genoa to meet my mother. My father died when I was an infant. My mother was the daughter of an eminent Jew; my father was her cousin. Many signs had led me to suspect this origin before I left. So prepared was I that I was glad of the confirmation—glad to find myself a Jew."

"You need not expect surprise from me, Deronda," Hans said, shifting his posture to cross one leg over the other and inspecting the heel of his slipper. "You knew it?"

"My mother told me. She visited the house the morning after you were there—both brother and sister informed her. Imagine we cannot rejoice as they do. But whatever you are glad of, I shall come to be glad of in the end—though precisely *when* that end arrives, I cannot predict," Hans murmured in a low, habitual tone, out of humor with his lot yet determined to avoid a scene.

"I quite understand you cannot share my feelings," Deronda said. "Yet I could not allow silence to divide us on what casts such a new light upon my future. I have adopted some of Mordecai's ideas and intend to pursue them as far as one man's effort can go. I daresay I shall soon travel to the East and be gone for years."

Hans said nothing, rising to seize his palette and begin working his brush, facing away from Deronda. The artist's back

"All thoughts, all passions, all delights, Whatever stirs this mortal frame, All are but ministers of Love, And feed his sacred flame." —Coleridge.

Deronda's eagerness to declare his love had reached a fever pitch, fueled entirely by Hans's recent revelation that Mirah suffered deeply from jealousy. Driven by this urgency, Deronda returned to Ezra with a steely resolve: he intended to demand, not merely request, a private audience with Mirah. Should she accept his heart, he felt invincible regarding the consequences; moreover, as her betrothed, he believed he could secure a protective authority over her, shielding her from future conflicts with her father.

Yet, beneath his confidence lay a deep-seated dread. Deronda sensed no overt restlessness in Lapidoth, nor any decline in the father's efforts to court favor, but he foresaw a coming storm—a struggle, a humiliation, or a crescendo of domestic turmoil that might leave Ezra and Mirah helpless. These fears would have been confirmed had he known the turmoil brewing in Lapidoth's mind. What appeared to Ezra as a minor torment was, for Lapidoth, an agonizing submission to restraint, endured only because he viewed it as a temporary measure to secure a future, unrestricted freedom.

Lapidoth had originally planned to wait for a favorable opportunity, perhaps to extract a substantial sum from Deronda. Meanwhile, however, he had begun to scrutinize the household with curious, calculating eyes, hunting for clues regarding where Mirah hid her money and her keys. An insatiable gambling instinct drove him, weaving a mental net that trapped every other thought. Had he possessed a tangible fortune to seize, this single-minded focus might have wavered, but Mirah, possessing a sharp, practical clarity, had thwarted his designs. True to her promise to Ezra, she entrusted all funds except immediate necessities to Mrs. Meyrick's care. Consequently, Lapidoth felt imprisoned in a state of total deprivation, akin to a patient in a lunatic asylum where every avenue of access is secured against him.

To him, prying open a desk or drawer to pocket banknotes found therein would have felt less like theft and more like a domestic right—a gray area of liberty men often claim with others' property, stretching far beyond legal boundaries until the law finally intervenes. In his mind, silverware was a safer investment than mining stocks precisely because the line between "borrowing" and stealing is so delicately drawn. Lapidoth genuinely felt mistreated by his daughter, convinced he deserved access to her earnings as readily as he consumed her apple tarts. Yet, he remained outwardly submissive. The temptation that truly gnawed at him was not direct confrontation with Mirah, but rather a calculated appeal to Deronda.

Clever individuals with nothing else to offer can sometimes command a high price for their absence. Lapidoth's desperate search for an exit strategy led him to believe that his family would be happier without him, and that Deronda, eager to be rid of such a burden, would advance a significant sum. Despite his practiced hardness, however, Lapidoth retained a lingering awe of Ezra's formidable friend, causing him to postpone this scheme indefinitely.

On this particular day, Deronda arrived buoyant with a gladdening certainty that radiated from his very demeanor. This contrasted sharply with Lapidoth, who was at a crisis point of discontent and longing, his mind racing with fantasies of escape. Deronda's newfound amiability only emboldened Lapidoth's schemes. So consumed was he that his usual pretense of interest in the room's activities faltered; he sat listlessly beside Ezra even when reading material flew past his comprehension. Eventually, unable to endure the stagnation, he excused himself to smoke and walk in the square, leaving the two friends to breathe more freely.

Mirah was absent, though Deronda knew she would return before his departure. A secret anticipation warmed his eyes; he imagined that upon seeing her again, he would witness a sweet recognition of his feelings, a connection previously denied to him. His manner toward Ezra grew playfully affectionate.

"This little room is too stifling for you, Ezra," Deronda remarked, breaking off his reading. "The weekly heat here surpasses even that of Genoa, where one retreats into the shaded coolness of vast halls. You deserve a grander home now. As the stronger half, I shall decide our fate."

He smiled warmly at Ezra, who replied, "I am straitened for nothing but breath. You, who might dwell in a spacious palace surrounded by verdant fields, find this a narrow cage. Yet, I cannot say 'go.'"

"Oh, the countryside would feel like exile while you remain here," Deronda countered, rising to pace the cramped double room, fanning himself dramatically with his handkerchief. "This is the happiest room in the world for me. Besides, I shall imagine myself in the East, since I am preparing to travel there someday. Only, I shall not wear a cravat or a heavy ring," he concluded emphatically, pausing to remove these accessories and place them on a small table behind Ezra, whose surface was cluttered with books and papers.

"I have worn my memorable ring since returning home," Deronda continued as he resumed his seat. "But I am such a Sybarite that I constantly cast it off as a burden during any task. I understand why the Romans had summer rings—if they indeed did. Now, I shall perform better."

Soon, they became absorbed in their work. Deronda read passages of Rabbinic Hebrew under Ezra's correction and commentary, paying scant attention when Lapidoth re-entered and settled into a shadowy corner. His wandering gaze quickly fixed upon the ring sparkling on the dark mahogany table.

During his walk, Lapidoth's mind had been rehearsing a scenario: an advantageous proposal abroad requiring immediate cash. If presented privately to Deronda, it might prompt a question about the necessary amount, leading to a generous grant. This calculation was the most contentious part of his fantasy; asking too much risked rejection, while asking too little yielded insufficient reward. His own desires knew no limits, and he lacked any gauge for Deronda's generosity.

Suddenly, amidst these airy preparations for a receipt that remained undefined, the ring—familiar to Lapidoth's envious eye on Deronda's finger earlier—now lay detached and within easy reach. Though its value was modest compared to the colossal sums he imagined, its physical presence sparked an instant leap of desire. A fleeting thought, not yet an intention, formed: *If I quietly pocket this ring and depart, I possess the means for comfortable escape.* It would require no trouble and pose no danger. Any property of Deronda's, accessible without formal consent, was effectively equivalent to his children's property, for a father would never prosecute his son-in-law for taking such a trifle.

These thoughts cascaded rapidly, forming a single, vivid picture. Lapidoth had never committed larceny, yet he reasoned that appropriating this item from a virtual relative—who would otherwise have gifted it generously—did not constitute true larceny. Still, the prospect of a larger gift weighed heavily on him. The internal rhythm of imagining snatching the ring repeated like a haunting tune, eventually sinking into a rejected notion. Instead, he decided to descend, waiting for the moment Deronda departed, intending to invite himself along on a walk and execute his plan boldly.

He rose to stand by the window, watching the street beyond, acutely aware of the short distance separating him from the table where the ring rested. Resolved to go downstairs, yet dominated by a thirst-like compulsion, he moved past the table. His fingers brushed noiselessly against the ring, and he found himself standing in the passage, the stolen object clutched in his hand.

Without hesitation, he donned his hat and fled the house. The possibility of returning to his children faded into an indefinite distance. By the time he stepped onto the square, his frantic energy focused solely on selling the ring and boarding a ship. Deronda and Ezra noticed his sudden exit, but that was all.

Shortly thereafter, Mirah entered, creating a genuine disruption. She had not removed her hat. When Deronda rose to greet her, she spoke in a confused, troubled murmur, "I only came to ensure Ezra has his new draught. I must go straight to Mrs. Meyrick's to fetch something."

"Please, allow me to walk with you," Deronda urged earnestly. "I must not weary Ezra further; besides, my brain feels like it is melting. I wish to accompany you to Mrs. Meyrick's."

"Oh, yes," Mirah replied, blushing intensely, sensing something new and electric in Deronda's presence. She turned away to prepare Ezra's medicine, while Ezra threw back his head, closing his eyes, unable to detach his mind from the swirling ideas generated during their reading session.

For a moment, Deronda stood lost in thought, contemplating the walk ahead. When Mirah returned with the draught, he suddenly recalled setting aside his cravat. "Pray excuse my dishevelment," he apologized, "I did not intend for you to see it." Moving to the small table, he retrieved his cravat and then cried out with violent surprise, "Good heavens! Where is my ring?" He began frantically searching the floor.

Ezra glanced around the corner of his chair. Mirah, swift as thought, approached where Deronda searched and asked, "Did you lay it down?"

"Yes," Deronda replied, unaware of any alternative explanation, assuming the ring had simply fallen into the shadows of the variegated carpet. He shifted furniture and poked through every conceivable hiding

"The human nature unto which I felt / That I belonged, and reverenced with love, / Was not a punctual presence, but a spirit / Diffused through time and space..."

Sir Hugo executed his plan to spend the autumn at Diplow, and by early October, his presence had injected a measure of cheerfulness throughout the neighborhood. From the stately halls of Brackenshaw and Quetcham down to the modest parlors of Wanchester shops, everyone seemed to benefit from his arrival. Sir Hugo was a man of considerable charm, a Liberal of noble birth who believed firmly in reform without fearing it would disrupt the fundamental fabric of English social habits—a habit, notably, of adoring society when it is walled in and adorned with hereditary titles. Consequently, he transformed Diplow into a delightful gathering place. He extended invitations to elderly solicitors and young village curates alike, yet with a shrewd touch regarding the composition of his guest list. He ensured that the "common poultry" of the social sphere were not fed indiscriminately together, lest anyone feel slighted.
