Easy-going Lord Brackenshaw might welcome Attorney Robinson, but Robinson would have been naturally offended to be asked to dine alongside those he considered his social superiors. Sir Hugo, acutely aware of these nuances, effortlessly gained popularity and dispensed pleasure, outcomes that perfectly suited his benevolent disposition.

For the rector of Pennicote, Mr. Gascoigne, the reception at Diplow stood in stark contrast to the haughty tolerance he had endured under Grandcourt's rule. This shift was not born of affection for the baronet, but of Sir Hugo's desire to maintain a cordial relationship with Mr. Gascoigne on behalf of Mrs. Grandcourt. Sir Hugo's chivalry had grown increasingly devoted to her memory. Yet, he guarded the reasons behind this devotion with a rare reticence, refusing to share even with his own wife, Lady Mallinger, what he deemed a woman's secret.

Daniel Deronda, following his engagement to Mirah, felt compelled to disclose his new position and intentions to Sir Hugo via letter. He anticipated that his fatherly friend would feel some degree of dissatisfaction, perhaps even pain, at this turn of fate. Reading unwelcome news offered the advantage of avoiding the hasty, regrettable expressions of impatience that often accompany verbal confrontation. Deronda dreaded the clash of words that could render pardonable feelings permanently offensive.

Sir Hugo, though not entirely surprised, was deeply vexed. His immediate recourse was to show the letter to Lady Mallinger, expecting her astonishment to serve as a buffer against his own discontent. When she expressed shock and distress that Daniel's immense talents and the comfort of his presence had dissolved into madness over Jewish affairs, the baronet could dismiss her concerns with ease: *"Oh, nonsense, my dear! Depend upon it, Dan will not make a fool of himself. He holds large political notions about Judaism which you cannot fathom. No fear but Dan will keep his head above water."*

However, regarding the impending marriage, Lady Mallinger offered no counter-argument. With gentle candor, she admitted she had scarcely imagined such a development when Mirah sang at her parties or tutored Amabel. After a brief hesitation, she confessed that she had briefly entertained the notion that Daniel might eventually marry Mrs. Grandcourt—given his presence in Genoa at that precise moment—and while she personally disliked widows, she conceded that such a union might have been preferable to his total withdrawal into Jewish causes. Sir Hugo, sharing this sentiment so strongly, could not label it a feminine error. Thus, his ill-humor at the refutation of his hopes for Gwendolen remained unvoiced. He instructed Lady Mallinger to keep the matter silent until further notice, reasoning that if the news struck Gwendolen harshly, delay was kinder given her fragile nerves, and ultimately, she deserved to hear it directly from Daniel.

While residing at Diplow, Sir Hugo continued his paternal attentions toward Gwendolen, supported willingly by Lady Mallinger despite her reservations about widows.

The move to Offendene had been completed, and Gwendolen settled there with a calm surpassing her mother's expectations. She experienced a profound, peaceful melancholy stemming from the renunciation of selfish demands and the acceptance of simple existence—even finding kindness in a dog—to be a gift beyond expectation. Could one, nearly lost in a pit of darkness, truly complain of the fresh air and sunlight? Life could be viewed daily as an escape, where the return of morning and evening, and particularly the emergence of pure sympathy or generous impulse piercing inner gloom, served as a salvation reconciling one to hardship. Those possessing the self-knowledge to accuse themselves as Hamlet did understood this habitual sense of rescue. Gwendolen relived the terrifying history of her temptations—from the initial illusion of self-gratification as she fled her conscience, to the later desperate hatred dragging her toward its fulfillment, all while praying for the help of the very conscience she had abandoned. Now, she dwelled on every word Deronda had spoken pointing to her deliverance from her worst evils and the harm she had caused, clinging to his words as anchors against despair.

Yet, she was buoyed by the prospect of seeing him again. She did not perceive him as distant; her supreme need blinded her to the separateness of his life, filling her mental landscape entirely with his connection to her. This was not unique to Gwendolen; we are all prone to this passionate egoism of imagination, projecting our needs onto others and even onto God. The future she faced with willingness was one of continuous assimilation to a type he would hold before her. Had he not first appeared as a corrective presence, initially met with resentment but finally embraced with total love and trust? She could not conceive of ending this reliance, which had become to her imagination the solid ground beneath her feet, the only condition allowing her to walk.

Deronda arrived at Diplow shortly thereafter, a location more accessible from London than the Abbey. He had planned to take Ezra and Mirah to a coastal retreat while preparing a new home for Mirah as his bride, where they could jointly care for her brother. However, Ezra refused to leave unless accompanying them to the East. Outward pressures weighed heavily on him, yet his mind dwelt on the Eastern voyage with visionary joy. Deronda, eager to consult Sir Hugo regarding his resources and affairs in preparation for a marriage he hoped to solemnize within two months, needed a reason to visit Diplow. But he valued another motive equally: his promise to Gwendolen. A strange ache of anxiety persisted in his heart amidst the blessedness of his own lot—a paradox, for lovers are typically hailed as happy, implying an indifference to external sorrow. Yet human experience is often paradoxical. It was no betrayal of Mirah, nor did it diminish his love for her, that his joy could coexist with care for another. What is love itself, for the one we cherish most? It is an enfolding of immeasurable cares that remain superior to any joy existing outside that love.

Deronda visited Diplow twice and saw Gwendolen twice, yet returned to town without revealing the change in his circumstances. He blamed himself. In communications of grave import likely to cause pain, one feels dependent on a preparatory alignment of words and moods, seeking an agreement in the listener's state of mind with the impact of the news. During the first meeting, Gwendolen was so consumed by her own inquiries—about arranging her life, reducing her ignorance, and amending her selfishness—that Deronda recoiled from diverting her attention to his own plight, unwilling to inflict a wound while she leaned on him for support. In the second meeting, determined to steer the conversation, he found her in deep depression, overwhelmed by miserable memories that felt more real than any future materials. She cried hysterically, accusing him of eventual disdain. He could offer only soothing words. As she gradually revived, displaying that pathetic, childlike interest with tear-beaded lashes, it became impossible to burden her further.

Time marched on, making the difficult disclosure a pressing duty. True, Gwendolen never suspected he had affairs, nor did she inquire why he had been in Genoa. Yet, this unconsciousness meant that a sudden revelation of life-altering facts would strike her with greater force. Leaving the truth to be whispered by others would feel like cruel neglect. Writing was impossible; his tenderness could not endure the image of her reading his virtual farewell in solitude, perhaps interpreting his words as cold self-congratulation. He descended to Diplow again, resolved to risk every other peril rather than return leaving her in ignorance.

On this third visit, Deronda found Hans Meyrick installed at Diplow with his easel, painting the three daughters on a bank in the "Gainsborough style," interspersed with sketches of village children at Pennicote to improve his acquaintance with the Gascoignes. Hans appeared recovered in vivacity, though Deronda sensed artifice in it, akin to a lady's bloom that is too high-toned and persistently steady. Despite his gratitude and admiration for Deronda, Hans harbored a certain irritation. Open natures often feel aggrieved when a friend's reserve breaks to reveal a reality not merely unsuspected, but contrary to hopeful conjecture. While Hans had freely poured out his own heart regarding Mirah, he could not suppress the sting of realizing that Deronda had concealed a rivalrous feeling that now emerged as the decisive factor. Friends turning out to be more significant than expected always pose a danger to our pride. Hans must be excused for these stirrings of bruised sensibility; he had acted rightly by his friend, convinced his own character had proven superior. Having declared himself a *dilettante* in virtue—fond of

In the complex tapestry of human life, the seasons often blend together as they did in ancient myths: fruit hangs beside blossoms, and in a single breath, the sickle cuts the grain while seeds are scattered for future growth. One person tends the young vine, another presses the grapes; yet within every individual life, the harvest and the springtime remain eternally intertwined, until the final gathering calls us to be sown anew in unseen fields. Among the gifts of love, none is more exquisite than the profound realization that by joining our lives to another's, we become guardians of their happiness, bringing solace to past hardships and turning memories of suffering into springs of joy.

Deronda's love for Mirah was deeply rooted in this protective instinct. Though she had started her journey across life's rough terrain with steps too small and innocent for the path ahead, the first time he saw her face, it appeared to him as a portrait of youthful despair. Now, however, she glowed like a delicate flower with dark tips and ivory petals, bathed in the warm sunlight of contentment. She viewed every potential sorrow as merely a shadow cast by the bright reality of her life with Deronda—a existence she could name only "good." He watched the dignified joy that animated her movements and the serene stillness of her resting poses with a delight so intense he whispered to himself that saving her from pain was sufficient reward for his own personal happiness.

Mirah remained unaware of Hans's internal struggles or the sharp pang Gwendolen felt. After Deronda revealed the depth of his hidden love for her, she effortlessly interpreted Gwendolen's anxious concern as a sign of gratitude toward Deronda's kindness, a sentiment Mirah herself had experienced. All of Deronda's remarks regarding Mrs. Grandcourt reinforced this perspective, though he spoke of them only in the vaguest terms. Mirah was willing to believe that he had acted as a rescuing angel to many souls, including herself. Her only marvel was that, among all those saved, she alone possessed the privilege of standing constantly by his side.

When the bridal veil finally fell around Mirah, it concealed no trembling doubt, only a thrilling reverence for the immense gift she accepted—one that demanded great purpose in return. Under the velvet canopy, no couple ever stood more worthy of blessing, nor did their people offer a more prudent wish for their progeny. No lips spoke truer vows, and no marriage blessing held a stronger promise of fulfillment than the integrity woven into their mutual pledge. Naturally, they wed according to the Jewish rite.

Since no faith demands that a feast exclude anyone based on social rank, few would likely take offense to learn that Deronda's modest wedding banquet included the entire Cohen family, with one notable exception: the baby, who continued to nurse its teeth intelligently at home. How could Mordecai bear to keep friends from his days of adversity away from sharing in his joy? Mrs. Meyrick understood this heart so completely that she had fully reconciled herself to meeting the Jewish pawnbroker, arriving with her three daughters. Each woman enjoyed the satisfaction of knowing that Mirah's union with Deronda crowned a romance that would forever remain a sweet memory for them. Which of them—mother or daughters—had not played a generous role in it, offering their finest feelings and actions to the girl who needed them?

If Hans had been present, it would have been ideal; but Mab had already noted that men must sometimes suffer for being inconvenient. Perhaps she, Kate, and Amy had all fallen in love with Mr. Deronda? Yet, being women, they were far less ridiculous than men in such matters. The Meyricks were richly rewarded for overcoming their prejudices by hearing a speech from Mr. Cohen, a rarity among public addresses for its lack of conventional patterns. Jacob ate with the appetite of a child, contributing several small, whinnying laughs as a spontaneous accompaniment to his father's words—not irreverently, but from a lively pride that his family was being honored. Meanwhile, Adelaide Rebekah, clad in a new Sabbath dress, maintained a solemn air of responsibility throughout.

Mordecai's brilliant eyes, sunken deep in their large sockets, scanned the room with the cherishing benevolence of a spirit already elevated above worldly concerns, leaving only selflessness and sympathy behind. Yet, whenever his gaze swept across the crowd, it inevitably returned to Deronda, shining with a renewed glow of trusting affection.

The wedding feast was humble, yet Mirah received magnificent gifts. Upon learning of the engagement, friends devised gracious tokens. Sir Hugo and Lady Mallinger arranged a complete outfit for Eastern travel, along with a precious locket inscribed: *"To the bride of our dear Daniel Deronda, all blessings. H. and L. M."* The Klesmers presented a flawless watch, similarly adorned with a tender message. But something far more valuable than gold or jewels arrived for Deronda from the neighborhood of Diplow on the morning of his wedding. It was a letter bearing these words:

*"Do not think of me sorrowfully on your wedding-day. I have remembered your words—that I may live to be one of the best of women, who make others glad that they were born. I do not yet see how that can be, but you know better than I. If it ever comes true, it will be because you helped me. I only thought of myself, and I made you grieve. It hurts me now to think of your grief. You must not grieve any more for me. It is better—it shall be better with me because I have known you. GWENDOLEN GRANDCOURT."*

Preparations for their immediate departure to the East commenced instantly. Deronda could not refuse Ezra's wish to embark on the voyage immediately, rather than delaying them to tend to his failing health. He harbored no hope that Ezra would survive the journey, for symptoms indicated the final stage of his illness had arrived. Yet Ezra himself had declared, "Never mind where I die, so that I am with you."

He did not accompany them. On an early morning, he told Deronda, "Do not leave me today. I shall die before it ends." He insisted on dressing and sitting upright in his easy chair as usual, with Deronda and Mirah seated on either side. For several hours, he remained unusually silent, making no effort to speak, yet occasionally looking at them with eyes filled with a restful meaning, as if to assure them that while this remaining fragment of breathing time was difficult, he felt an ocean of peace beneath him.

It was not until late afternoon, as the light faded, that he took hold of each of their hands. Looking at Deronda, he said, "Death is coming to me as the divine kiss, which is both parting and reunion—it takes me from your bodily sight and grants me full presence in your soul. Where thou goest, Daniel, I shall go. Has it not begun? Have I not breathed my soul into you? We shall live together."

He paused, and Deronda waited, expecting further words. Slowly, with visible effort, Ezra pressed their hands, raised himself, and uttered in Hebrew the confession of the Divine Unity, a prayer spoken for generations by the dying Israelite. He sank gently back into his chair and spoke no more. It was several hours later before he ceased to breathe, enveloped in the arms of Mirah and Deronda.

"Nothing is here for tears, nothing to wail
Or knock the breast; no weakness, no contempt,
Dispraise or blame; nothing but well and fair,
And what may quiet us in a death so noble."
